Finishing the Hypnerotomachia

Robert Dallington's Elizabethan English stops partway through chapter XVII, at the word Mustulento — facsimile page 193 of 467. Everything after it has existed in English only under copyright. This is a new translation of the remainder, made from the 1499 Aldine, set beside the Italian it came from so that any sentence can be checked.

127 of 275 pages englished · 40,954 of 90,646 source words (45.2%)

Method. The Italian is the Wikisource transcription of the 1499 Aldine (scanned from the MIT Press facsimile). That transcription is unproofread, so where it looks corrupt we check the facsimile and the 1545 Aldine reprint before translating rather than rendering a scanner error into English. Readings still in doubt are marked [?like this] and explained in the notes; a page is only verified once none remain.

Register. Modern but formal. We do not imitate Dallington — a reader should be able to tell at a glance which English is 1592 and which is ours. The formality comes from vocabulary and sentence shape, not from archaic grammar.

Rights. The 1499 original is public domain, and a faithful transcription of a public-domain work carries no new copyright. Joscelyn Godwin's 1999 translation is in copyright and has not been consulted, quoted, or paraphrased at any point; every English sentence here was made from the Italian in the left-hand column.

Chapter XVII

Where Dallington stops, mid-sentence, at the word Mustulento.

28 of 32 pages englished

p. 193confidence: highverified

Nel proximo latere, vidi de miranda celatura, una Damigella nel aspecto virgineo, matronale maiestate indicante figmento cum summa laude del artifice. De spice coronata, cum elegante defluxo de capigli et habito Nymphale, tenente cum la dextra una farcita copia de maturo grano, et ne l’altra teniva tre stipule cum aristate spice, et agli pedi uno strophiato fasciculo de spice iacente, cum tale subscriptione. Flavae messi. S.

Nel tertio fronte era uno Divo simulachro nudo, cum l’aspecto, cum miro modo et arte expresso, de uno infante coronato de Botryi de uva, tutto de lascivia ridibondo, uno palmite racemato de uva nella leva teniva, et nell’altra una copia completa de uva, fora degli labii cum le fronde et capreoli dependula, a gli pedi del quale stava uno lanigero hirco, cum tale scriptura insculpta. Mustulento autumno.S. L’ultima parte havea una regia imagine de conspicua exscalptura, rubesto nel aspecto et rigido, nella sinistra tenente uno sceptro, mirava verso el coelo, nel aere scuro turbulento et procelloso, et cum l’altra tangente le grandinose nebule. Da drieto similmente l’aere pluvioso et nymbifero. Vestito de pelliceo tegumento sopra el nudo, cum solee antiquarie calciato, et subscripto. Cum tale titulo. Hyemi Aeoliae. S. * *** *

(This page is the seam. Robert Dallington's 1592 English — the only prior public-domain translation — stops within it, at the word Mustulento below. Everything from that word to the end of the book is here rendered into English for the first time. The whole page is given, so the join can be checked.)

On the next side, I saw, of wondrous carving, a Damsel, virginal in aspect, indicating a matronal majesty in the figment, with the utmost praise of the artificer. Crowned with corn-ears, with an elegant downflow of hair and nymphal habit, holding in the right a stuffed plenty of ripe grain, and in the other holding three stalks with bearded ears, and at the feet a bound little bundle of ears lying, with this subscription:

FLAVAE MESSI · S. — "To the golden harvest. Sacred."

On the third face was a Divine simulacrum, nude, with the aspect — with wondrous manner and art expressed — of an infant crowned with clusters of grapes, all laughing with lasciviousness; a vine-branch clustered with grapes he held in the left, and in the other a full plenty of grapes, out of the lips with the fronds and tendrils pendent; at the feet of which stood a wool-bearing he-goat, with this writing carved:

MUSTULENTO AUTUMNO · S. — "To the must-laden autumn. Sacred."

(— Dallington's translation ends here. What follows is ours.)

The last part had a regal image of conspicuous carving, ruddy in aspect and rigid, holding in the left a sceptre, gazing toward the sky — in the dark, turbulent and stormy air — and with the other touching the hail-clouds. Behind, likewise, the rainy and cloud-bearing air. Clad in a pelt- covering over the nude, shod with antique sandals, and subscribed with this title:

HYEMI AEOLIAE · S. — "To Aeolian winter. Sacred."

Notes

Confidence: high. This is the join between Dallington (1592) and this translation. The page carries the four Seasons as carved figures on an altar: Spring (on the preceding, Dallington- covered page), Summer (Ceres, Flavae Messi), Autumn (the infant Bacchus, Mustulento Autumno), and Winter (a sceptred, pelt-clad king, Hyemi Aeoliae). The Latin tags are read straight from the woodcut. All secure.

p. 194confidence: mediumverified

Ad questo nobile figmento el praestante artifice, electo solertemente el marmoro havea, che oltra la candidecia sua era venato (al requisito loco) de nigro, ad exprimere el tenebroso aere illumino, et nebuloso cum cadente grandine. Sopra la plana della dicta veneranda, Ara rigidamente rigoroso promineva el rude simulachro del hortulano custode, cum tutti gli sui decenti et propriati insignii. La quale mysteriosa Ara tegeva uno cupulato umbraculo, sopra quatro pali nel solo infixi affirmato et substentato. Gli quali pali diligentemente erano investiti di fructea, et florea frondatura, et el culmo tutto intecto de multiplici fiori, et tra ciascuno palo nel lymbo dell’apertura, o vero hiato del umbraculo affixo pendeva una ardente lampada, et in circuito ornatamente bractee d’oro dalle fresche et verifere aure inconstante vexate, et cum metallei crepituli sonante. Nel quale simulachro, cum maxima religione et prisco rito rurale et pastorale alcune amole, o vero ampulle vitree cum spumante cruore del immolato Asello et cum caldo lacte et scintillante Mero spargendo rumpevano, et cum fructi, fiori, fronde, festa, et gioie libavano, hora drieto a questo glorioso Triumpho, conducevano, cum antiqua et silvatica cerimonia illaqueato el seniculo Iano, de reste et trece intorte di multiplici fiori, cantanti carmini ruralmente Talassii, Hymaenei, et Fescennii, et instrumenti rurestri cum suprema laetitia et gloria, celebremente exultanti, et cum solenni plausi saltanti, et voce foemelle altisone, per la quale cosa non manco piacere et dilecto cum stupore quivi tali solenni riti et celebre feste me invase, che la admiratione de gli praecedenti triumphi. *

For this noble figment the outstanding artificer had shrewdly chosen the marble, which — beyond its whiteness — was veined (in the requisite place) with black, to express the tenebrous, unlit and nebulous air, with falling hail. Upon the flat of the said venerable Altar, rigidly rigorous, stood forth the rude simulacrum of the garden-guardian (Priapus), with all his decent and appropriated insignia. Which mysterious Altar a cupola'd little canopy covered, affirmed and sustained upon four poles fixed in the ground. Which poles were diligently invested with fruited and flowered foliage, and the top all covered with manifold flowers; and between each pole, at the rim of the opening, or gap, of the canopy, hung, fixed, a burning lamp; and, in circuit, ornately, gold foils, by the fresh and spring-bearing breezes inconstantly vexed, and sounding with metallic little rattles.

At which simulacrum, with the utmost religion and ancient rural and pastoral rite, certain little flasks, or glass ampullae, with the foaming gore of the immolated Ass, and with warm milk, and scintillating unmixed Wine, scattering, they broke; and with fruits, flowers, fronds, feast, and jewels they libated. Now, behind this glorious Triumph, they conducted, with antique and sylvan ceremony, the little old Janus ensnared, of ropes and tresses twisted of manifold flowers, singing songs rurally — Talassian, Hymeneal, and Fescennine — and rustic instruments, with supreme joy and glory, celebratedly exulting, and with solemn applause dancing, and female voices high-sounding. For which thing, no less pleasure and delight, with stupor, did such solemn rites and celebrated feasts here invade me, than the admiration of the preceding triumphs.

Notes

Confidence: medium. The altar of Priapus under a flower-wreathed, lamp-hung canopy, and the rustic rite performed at it — libations of ass's blood, warm milk and wine, and old Janus led bound in flower-ropes amid Talassian, Hymeneal and Fescennine songs. All secure; the rite is a learned reconstruction of Roman country religion (Priapus, the sacrifice of the ass, the marriage- and-harvest songs).

Da poscia de qui ambo alquanto progressi, cum inexcogitabile solacio et più oltra procedendo, io mirai ancora agli chiari et freschi fonti degli gratiosi et herbidi prati et umbriferi nemori le Cataryte Napee chorigiante, et le piacevole Naiade ludente, et le marine Nereide cantante, indute alcune de pelle de vitulo marino, non laeso da l’ira del supremo Iove, cum cortice grande de Esture in mano farcite di fructi et fiori, dagli glareosi littori convenute, et cum diversi solatii ociosamente festegiante, molte erano nelle sue mano gerule de verdi Tyrsi, gli quali nella summitate erano compacti de fiori de Naranci, cum molle fronde, et de rose aegyptie lutee et persiane, et de fiori de Narciso, cum manipuli de fiori punicei, et odorosi de Amuleto, cum Silvicula Pana Arcado, et gli semidii silvani monticule, et le Drymode, et molte altre, et Zephyro cum la sua amata Chlori. Alla quale lui gli haveva degli belli fiori la potestate conceduta, et molti actuosi et cantanti Pastori, ne le sue cantione experti di certare, et cum gli sui aguli armati, laudavano inseme iubilando et plaudendo, cum serii et ludi et ioci, cum veterrimi instrumenti stipulacei, et Arundinacei, et cum Tubicine Tibie corticee de forma Scytalea, de extraneo sonito, lo amoroso et omnipotente Iove, festabondi glorificando et la sancta ruratione, et facendo divotissimi le florealee feste. Per la quale cosa quanto voluptico piacere io sentisse, lasso iudicare a chi nel cogitato suo el sapesse fingere. Dunque copiosamente stivato d’incredibile laetitia, cum la mia gratissima comite peragrando continuavemo el nostro foelice itinerario et amoroso ambulachro. Diqué alcuna fiata gli ochii dalla sua dolce pregione et ligatura, et quasi proscriptione, dimovendo alquanto, Echo che de sopra le tenelle come et verdissime cime degli lascivienti arbori mirando, io vidi uno excelso Pterygio, sopra apparentimi de uno rotundo fastigio, aestimando quello poco distare dal susuroso littore, verso el quale ella facetosa me menava. Et nel quale gli labenti et acclivi fiumicelli, che circuivano la valle terminavano, et da pedi degli ornatissimi colli, et montagniole parte pratose, et parte arborose, discorrevano cum l’aqua velocissima in chiarissimi canaliculi cum herbido alveo et sabulaceo, uno per lato dissiliendo. Et da poi ancora oltra el dicto Pinnaculo vidi una superba, et eminente cupula, parentimi de livido piombo contecta. La quale nella summitate havea uno cimacio in forma octogonia cum columne, et de sopra un’altra cupula, et poscia octo pille quadrangule, da uno culmo coperte, in figura balaustica, sopra el quale promineva uno stylo cum uno Trigono rotundissimo infixo, praecipue splendente aureo nitore. Diqué molto a grado mi se offeritte tale dimonstratione, per la quale fui non

Then, from here, both of us somewhat progressed, with inexcogitable solace, and proceeding further, I beheld again, at the clear and fresh founts of the gracious and grassy meadows and shady groves, the cascade-Napaeae dancing, and the pleasant Naiads playing, and the marine Nereids singing — clad, some, in the skin of the sea-calf (not harmed by the wrath of supreme Jove), with great bark- vessels of estur in hand stuffed with fruits and flowers, gathered from the gravelly shores, and with diverse solaces idly making festival. Many were, in their hands, bearers of green Thyrses, which at the summit were compacted of orange-flowers, with soft fronds, and of Egyptian roses, yellow and Persian, and of Narcissus-flowers, with handfuls of crimson flowers, and odorous of amuletum; with sylvan Pan the Arcadian, and the demigod sylvan hill-dwellers, and the Dryads, and many others, and Zephyr with his beloved Chloris — to whom he had conceded the power over the beautiful flowers — and many active and singing Shepherds, in their songs expert to contend, and armed with their crooks, praised together, jubilating and applauding, with serious games and jests, with most-ancient instruments of straw and of reed, and with trumpeting Tibiae of bark, of scytala form, of a strange sound, the amorous and omnipotent Jove — festively glorifying — and the holy country-life, and making most-devout the Floralian feasts.

For which thing, how much voluptuous pleasure I felt, I leave to judge to whoever in his cogitation could feign it. Therefore, copiously stuffed with incredible joy, with my most-pleasing companion, traversing, we continued our happy itinerary and amorous walk. Whence, some time, the eyes from their sweet prison and binding — and almost proscription — somewhat removing, Lo! looking above the tender and greenest tops of the wanton trees, I saw a lofty Pterygium (winged pinnacle), above, appearing to me of a round fastigium (gable), esteeming it to be little distant from the whispering shore, toward which she, facetious, was leading me. And in which the gliding and sloping little rivers, that circled the valley, terminated; and from the feet of the most-ornate hills and little mountains — part grassy, part wooded — ran, with most-swift water, in clearest little channels with a grassy and sandy bed, one leaping down to a side. And then again, beyond the said Pinnacle, I saw a superb and eminent cupola, appearing to me covered with livid lead. Which at the summit had a cyma in octagonal form with columns, and above another cupola, and then eight quadrangular piers, covered by a single top, in balustered figure, upon which stood forth a stylus with a most-round Triangle fixed, especially splendent with golden brightness. Whence very pleasingly offered itself to me such a showing, by which I was not —

The turn, within chapter XVII, from the four triumphs to the temple: the water-nymphs (Napaeae, Naiads, Nereids), Pan, the Dryads, and Zephyr with Chloris making a Floralian festival by the shore; then Poliphilo first sees, above the trees, the great lead-domed temple with its octagonal lantern and gold triangle-finial — the Temple of Venus Physizoa, which he reaches on p. 197. mediocremente di ardente desio commosso, quella più proximo perfectamente spectare, suspicando ragionevolmente quivi essere magna et antiqua structura, intanto che per questo quasi voleva la mia benigna Duce precare, che a quella me conducesse, quantunque verso el loco tuttavia peragravemo. Ma tra me tale desio castigando diceva. Heu me, io non audo quella cosa impetrare che cum spinoso stimulo, et cum sedulo impulso vehemente son solicitato, et praecipuamente quella cosa tanto caldamente desiderata, quella che fermamente arbitro consequendola potrei farme sopra qualunque amante contento. Dunque reprimendo, et suffocando, et inane damnando tale inconsulto concepto, non dimandando hogi mai de sì longo tormento, cum diuturna pastura de praecordiali suspiri aiuto. Perché adunque di questa a mi non tanto opportuna debo chiedere? Heu me actuoso core participato et non tutto mio, como volentieri sequi tu el rapace Alieto del vivere tuo? Il quale in questi lascivii laquei et foetosi cogitamenti involupatose causava excitando nel inflammato pecto continuo palpitato, quale el lachrymante Fasciano, giù della fronde dal crudel Falcone al volare impulso el tristo core gli batte. Et cusì per questi tali amorosi corrolarii crebramente agitato, più oltra el moderato grado nostro prosequendo, cum la mia Veneranda Nympha, facondamente confabulando, et delle miravegliose cose per divina gratia chiaramente vise affabile, conferendo, cum eloquio mellifluo, pervenissimo finalmente poco distante dal ripercosso littore, dalle piacevole onde del inquieto mare lavato. In questo loco de sito iocundissimo, trovassemo di arte aedificatoria uno ornatissimo, faberrimo, et vetusto templo, de antiquaria operatura et di maximo censo, sumptuosamente fabrefacto, et alla physizoa Venere consecrato. Questo sacro Templo dunque per architectonica arte rotundo constructo, et dentro della quadrangulare figura nella aequata Area solertemente exacto, et quanta trovasse la diametrale linea, tanta rende la sua celsitudine, et nel circulo nell’area contento, notase una quadratura. Da una pleura della quale sopra la diametrale linea verso la circunferentia, tale spatio divise in cinque partitione, et verso el centro suppliva una sexta. Dalla quale havendo poscia circinato un’altra circulare figura, el docto Architecto questa egregia structura et superbo aedificio havea levato, quanto alle parte principale, cum la commodulatione, dimensione, et de tutto l’ambito et contento potito havea, et la crassitudine degli muri et degli extrinseci Pilli, et tra una circuitione et l’altra, o vero tra lo alamento principale et la columnatione, o vero Peristylio el libero testudinato. Dal centro alla circunferentia poscia in dece radii, o vero partitione le linee deducte, ove

— mediocrely moved by ardent desire, to behold that more nearly and perfectly, suspecting reasonably there to be a great and antique structure; insomuch that, for this, I almost wished to pray my benign Guide that she conduct me to it — although toward the place we were all the while traversing. But within myself, castigating such desire, I said: "Alas, I dare not obtain that thing for which, with a thorny goad and with sedulous vehement impulse, I am solicited — and especially that thing so warmly desired, which I firmly judge that, obtaining it, I could make myself content above whatever lover. Therefore, repressing and suffocating, and vainly damning such inconsiderate conceit, not demanding henceforth of so long a torment help, with the diurnal pasture of heartfelt sighs — why, then, ought I to ask of this, to me not so opportune (a thing)? Alas, active heart, shared and not wholly mine — how willingly dost thou follow the rapacious hawk of thy living?" — which, wrapping itself in these wanton snares and fecund cogitations, caused, exciting in the inflamed breast, a continual palpitating, as the tear-weeping Pheasant, down from the fronds, impelled to flight by the cruel Falcon, its sad heart beats it.

And so, by these such amorous corollaries frequently agitated, proceeding somewhat beyond our moderate pace, with my Venerable Nymph, eloquently confabulating, and of the marvellous things clearly seen by divine grace affably conferring, with mellifluous eloquence, we arrived finally, a little distant from the re-beaten shore, washed by the pleasant waves of the restless sea. In this place of most-pleasant site, we found, of the building-art, a most-ornate, most-excellently-made and antique temple, of antiquarian workmanship and of the greatest cost, sumptuously skilfully-made, and consecrated to the physizoic (life-giving) Venus.

This sacred Temple, then, by architectonic art constructed round, and within the quadrangular figure in the evened Area shrewdly worked out — and as much as the diametral line found, so much did it render its height; and in the circle contained in the area, a squaring is noted. From one side of which, upon the diametral line toward the circumference, such space (was) divided into five partitions, and toward the centre a sixth supplied. From which, having then circled another circular figure, the learned Architect had raised this egregious structure and superb building — as to the principal parts, with the commodulation, dimension, and of all the compass and content he had been able (to make) — and the thickness of the walls and of the exterior Piers, and between one circuit and the other (that is, between the principal band and the columnation, or Peristyle), the free vaulting. From the centre to the circumference, then, in ten radii, or partitions, the lines deduced, where —

Notes

Confidence: medium. Arrival at the round Temple of Venus Physizoa, and the beginning of its geometric construction — a circle inscribed in a square, height equal to diameter, the radius divided 5 (+1), a second circle struck, and ten radial divisions carrying the peristyle and vaulting. All secure; the geometry is a genuine architect's setting-out, of a piece with the Cythera plans.

  • The architectural description of the temple (dome, columns, doors, ornament) fills the pages
p. 198confidence: mediumverified

el circo interstitio secava, ordinò dece archi, residenti sopra columne serpentine, et opposito del substentamento solido, tra uno et l’altro ambiente arco, de latitudine faciale pedi dui, del circulare alamento interiore, resideva promptamente una dolata et expolita columna Corynthia, de celsitudine quanto la Ionica de nove diametri, excepti gli capitelli, de terso Porphyrite, la quale subigeva cum el capitello aeneo, alla trabe recta, circumacta cum el Zophoro et coronice, sopra ducte al solido del fastigio del trabe inflexo, o vero Arco. Gli quali, trabe Phrygio, et coronice, all’ordine del vivo, o vero del firmo della Corinthia, de tanta proiectura eminevano, quanto la linea perpendiculare della praelibata Porphyrica el chiedeva, cum base et capituli de fino metallo causticamente inaurato et perlucido, cum elegante Enthesi, o vero corpulentia. Questo ordine dunque de prominentia regularmente observato era per tutte le Porphyrice aequilibrate et compare. Le quale soprastare al requisito murulo dovevano alla columnatione. Ma el praeclaro Architecto per dare all’area più libera expeditione fece lo intercolumnio pervio. Diqué le trabe inflexe cum gli corni sopra le Ophytice columne terete et tornatile cum frictione illustrabile, residevano, apposito el debito latastro, o vero plintho sopra el capitello per dargli sodo pedamento, et non aereo, per gli operculi insinuati degli dicti capitelli. Et sotto la basi era riservato una semiarula che doveva essere el murulo, sopra le quale semiarule le base de tutte le Ophytice fermamente residevano. Le Corinthie pedavano sopra uno subcolumnio, o veramente uno subbasio di forma de semi Tubulo, de qui et de lì, cum le semi Arule concinnamente colligato. Il quale fue exacto da due quadrature trovate dal diametro della ima crassitudine della Corinthia, restava sexquialtera per el residuo ad undule Thori, Gulule, Nextruli, et Sime, o vero a ssimiglianti liniamenti de sopra et de sotto, conveniente dispensato, decentemente coniugate, cum le base libere sopra deputate. Ciascuno Arco elegantemente del suo cuneo decorato extava, cum puelluli et alternatamente de corticuli cum fogliamento floreo. Et qualunque triangulo specularmente perlustrava uno rotundo diaspreo de colore variato, circinnata egregiamente la sua incasatione, di undiculata fogliatura perlucidamente inaurato.

— cut the circular interstice, he ordained ten arches, resting upon serpentine columns; and, opposite the solid support, between one encompassing arch and the other, of a facial breadth of two feet, of the inner circular band, there resided promptly a hewn and polished Corinthian column, of a height as much as the Ionic of nine diameters (excepting the capitals), of burnished Porphyry — which carried, with the bronze capital, to the straight architrave, carried round with the frieze and cornice, led up to the solid of the fastigium of the inflected architrave, or Arch.

Which architrave, frieze, and cornice, at the order of the live (stone), or of the firm, of the Corinthian, stood forth with as much projection as the perpendicular line of the aforesaid Porphyry asked — with base and capitals of fine metal caustically gilt and translucent, with elegant enthesis, or swelling. This order, then, of projection was regularly observed throughout all the Porphyry (columns), balanced and paired, which had to overstand the requisite little wall of the columnation. But the illustrious Architect, to give the Area a freer expedition, made the intercolumniation passable.

Whence the inflected architraves, with their horns, rested upon the Ophite (serpentine) columns, turned and lathe-round, illustrable with friction, the due little slab, or plinth, set upon the capital to give it a solid footing, and not aerial, through the lids insinuated of the said capitals. And under the base was reserved a half-altar which had to be the little wall, upon which half-altars the bases of all the Ophite (columns) firmly resided.

The Corinthian (columns) footed upon a sub-column, or truly a sub-base, of the form of a half-Tube, here and there, with the half-Altars becomingly joined. Which was worked out from two squarings found from the diameter of the lowest thickness of the Corinthian; there remained a sesquialter (one-and-a-half) for the rest, to little-waves, Tori, Gulets, Knots, and Cymas, or the like lineaments, above and below, conveniently dispensed, decently conjoined, with the free bases assigned above.

Each Arch stood elegantly decorated of its keystone, with little boys, and alternately of little barks with flowered foliage. And every triangle (spandrel) specularly shone with a round jasper of varied colour, its setting egregiously circled, of little-waved foliage translucently gilt.

Notes

Confidence: medium. The temple's inner elevation: ten arches on serpentine columns, with porphyry Corinthian columns (nine diameters) between them carrying gilt-bronze capitals and a full entablature; the columns footed on half-altar sub-bases; and each arch keyed with putti-and-foliage keystones, each spandrel set with a round gilt jasper. All secure; a Vitruvian order-description of a domed rotunda's inner ring.

  • the tenfold division. The "ten arches / ten columns" realise the ten radial divisions set out on
  • Vitruvian vocabulary throughout — enthesis (entasis), sesquialter proportion, tori and cymas,

Nel pillo degli Archi opposito retro alla Corynthia, uno tertio de alveata quadrangula emineva, cum la basi sopra el pavimento extante in facia de un’altra, nel alamento sotto l’arco, che divideva gli fornici infixa, del tutto simigliante. Lo intervallo di queste, da l’una all’altra, gli radii tirati alla circunstantia facevano, sopra la linea degli forinseci Pilli appacte. Sopra gli semicapitelli delle quale ambiva una circumligatione de gratioso operamento. Gli semi Tubuli et hemi Arule erano de luculeo Allabastrite, cum curvescente reste, o vero fasciculi de multiplice fronde et fructi de Lacterii, di Mespili, et Scapi di Papavaro, gravescenti nel suo mediano discenso, di varicante Cymose invinculate sospese et innodate in certi annuli venustamente ornate. Sotto quello circumligamento poco sopra narrato, tra l’una et l’altra striata semiquadrangula nel primario pariete era una quadrangulata fenestra di uno quadrato et semi, come se richiede negli templi antiquarii. La apertione, o vero la luminatione delle quale occupava artificiosamente una speculare, o vero diaphana lamina de petra Sogobrina non temente la vetustate. Diqué in summa octo fenestre erano, perché una parte occupava la porta de esso templo, et per directo del Pronao in fronte un’altra parte della porta cum le valve auree del postico sacello, o veramente del sacro Adyto, la discriptione del quale in altro loco più accommodamente sarà tractata. All’incontro delle sopra recitate columne quadrangule, nel primo murale circuito infixe, gli pilli di fora prominevano, de tanta crassitudine dal muro exacti, quanta quella del muro, ad gli quali la sua latitudine davano le linee degli radii della divisione, dal centro alla circunferentia. La quale latitudine divisa, una portione era per la latitudine del pillastro. L’altra portione ancora in bina partitione deducta, una de qui et de lì l’altra dal Pillo collaterale attribuite, erano per uno arcuare nel solido del muro, o vero concamerato, tra uno et l’altro Pillastro, degli quali Pilli la proiectura, o vero exito, diviso per tertio, usurpava la prominentia del arcuato dal piano del muro scaffato, et gli dui tertii, eminevano gli Pilli, in uno solido muro arcuato et Pilli. Questa exquisita observantia laudavano gli eleganti Architecti, per non dare al muro rude crassitudine tanta, che le fenestre obtuse se accusasseron, cum perspicuo respecto del rude et superfluo solido, et per decoramento exteriore, gli quali arcuati in gyro se involtavano coniugato optimamente l’uno all’altro cum la medesima crassitudine, cum debito illigamento dagli Pilli circunferito sopra el muro per tutto bellamente concincto. Non altramente tra uno et l’altro contrastante arco opportunamente constava.

In the pier of the Arches, opposite, behind the Corinthian, a third of a fluted quadrangular (pilaster) stood forth, with the base upon the pavement, standing in the face of another (in the band under the arch that divided the vaults, fixed) wholly similar. The interval of these, from the one to the other, the radii drawn to the circumstance made, set upon the line of the outermost Piers. Upon the semi- capitals of which went round a circum-ligation of gracious workmanship.

The half-Tubes and half-Altars were of bright Alabaster, with curving ropes, or bundles, of manifold fronds and fruits — of lacterii (spurges), of medlars, and stalks of Poppy, growing heavy in their middle descent, of wavering shoots ensnared, suspended and knotted in certain rings comelily adorned.

Under that circum-ligation a little above narrated, between one and the other striated half-quadrangular (pilaster), in the primary wall was a quadrangular window of one square and a half, as is required in antiquarian temples. The opening, or lighting, of which artfully occupied a mirror-like, or diaphanous, plate of sogobrina stone, not fearing old age. Whence, in sum, eight windows there were — because one part the door of the temple occupied, and, directly opposite the Pronaos in front, another part (the door, with the golden valves, of the rear chapel, or the sacred Adytum, the description of which will, in another place, be more fittingly treated).

Over against the above-recited quadrangular columns, fixed in the first mural circuit, the piers projected outside, worked out from the wall of as much thickness as that of the wall — to which their breadth the lines of the radii of the division, from the centre to the circumference, gave. Which breadth divided: one portion was for the breadth of the pilaster; the other portion, again deduced into a twin partition (one here and one there, the other attributed to the collateral Pier), were for an arching in the solid of the wall, or vaulted, between one pilaster and the other. Of which Piers the projection, or outlet, divided by a third, usurped the prominence of the vault from the plane of the scarped wall; and the two thirds the Piers stood forth, in a solid arched wall-and-Piers. This exquisite observance the elegant Architects praised, so as not to give the wall a rude thickness so great that the windows should accuse themselves obtuse (deep-set), with conspicuous respect of the rude and superfluous solid; and for exterior adornment, which vaults in a ring turned themselves, conjoined most-excellently one to the other with the same thickness, with due binding, from the Piers, carried round upon the wall, throughout beautifully girt. Not otherwise, between one and the other contrasting arch, opportunely it stood.

In questo intervallato vacuo, tra uno et l’altro Pillone, o vero tra le fauce de questi archi prope dicti, sopra el relicto piano murale, excavato era la fenestra. Il quale arcuato tanto quanto egli era distante dal Pillo, tanto circinava sotto la coronice prima extrinseca, ambiente al primo tecto. Dece Pilli, o vero ossi del aedificio erano, et altratanti archi in solido, cum el muro extrinseco, exclusa la facia, ove appacto resideva el sacello. El dicto cornitione amplexando el rotundo sacello, el colligava cum el templo. Oltra questa ligatura ascendeva el suo tecto, che era una caeca cupula dalla maiore per sé distincta et libera. Retorniamo dunque hora allo illigamento interstitio de sopra la circumcolumnatione, o vero Peristylio del Trabe recto, fascia et coronice, porrecto sopra perpendicularemente delle Porphyrice columne, ove nascevano per ciascuna porrectione del illigamento, semicolumnelle de quadratura striate, di egregio Ophytes. Sopra el semicapitello delle quale, ambiva una coronice de liniamento conspicua. Dalla quale el convexo della excelsa cupula, el suo principio sopra incominciava. Infra una semiquadrangula et l’altra de proximo descripte, vidi una proportionata fenestra tempestivamente situata, et de lamina de Bononia de Galia obtusa, constituite in campo deaureato de artificioso Museo. Gli quali spatii cum commensa distributione et aequale partitione, mirai spectatissimamente depicto, de vermiculata tessellatura, la proprietate de ciascuno Mense del anno cum el suo effecto, et de sopra el Zodiaco occurrente cum el Sole operante, et a quello della Luna gli Schematismi, et essa è dita, tra gli menstrui nova cornicula, dividua et praetumida, et el suo circuito, per el quale gli mensi se includeno. Et gli anfracti del Sole indagante, le brume et el solstitio, circuiendo, et della nocte et del giorno la vicissitudine, et la quadrifaria commotione degli tempi, et la natura delle fixe et errante stelle, cum la sua efficacia, suspicai che tale arte fusse quivi ordinata dal nobilissimo mathematico Petosiris, o vero da Necepso. La inspectione delle quale cose, da trahere lo inspectore cum excessivo solacio ad una eximia et miranda speculatione. Non sencia egregio spectaculo, et elegante fictione, et venusta distributione de figure, cum diffinita pictura de coloramento et ombrature, per le quale la commodulatione degli corpi et requisiti lumi, ad complacentia rapraesentantise cum dignissima expressione degli effecti, cum laudabile et iocundo obstento agli sensi del animo. Opera sencia dubio de pensiculatione digna, quanto mai speculare se potesse. Dunque in una divisione el contento del significato delle antedicte operature, de notule antiquarie, elegantemente era inscripto. Gli spatii interpositi tra le semiquadrangule erano circumsepti di fascie, de venusto operamento inscalpte.

In this intervalled void, between one Pillar and the other — or between the jaws of these arches just- said — upon the left mural plane, excavated was the window. Which vault, as much as it was distant from the Pier, so much it circled under the first outer cornice, encompassing the first roof. Ten Piers, or bones, of the building there were, and as many arches in the solid, with the exterior wall — excepting the face where, set fast, resided the chapel (sacello).

The said cornice, embracing the round sacello, joined it with the temple. Beyond this binding ascended its roof, which was a blind cupola, distinct and free of the greater one by itself.

Let us return, then, now, to the interstitial binding above the circum-columnation, or Peristyle — of the straight architrave, fascia and cornice, stretched perpendicularly above the Porphyry columns — where there were born, for each stretch of the binding, little half-columns of quadrature, striated, of egregious Ophite. Upon the semi-capital of which went round a cornice of conspicuous lineament, from which the convex of the lofty cupola began its beginning above.

Between one and the other of the half-quadrangular (pilasters) just described, I saw a proportioned window, timely situated, and of a plate of Bononia (Bologna) of Gaul, opaque, constituted in a field gilt, of artful Mosaic. Which spaces, with commensurate distribution and equal partition, I beheld most- admiredly depicted, of wormwork tessellation: the property of each Month of the year, with its effect; and above, the Zodiac occurring, with the Sun operating; and, to that of the Moon, the phases — and she is called, among the monthly (states), the new little crescent, the halved, and the pre-swollen (full) — and her circuit, by which the months are included. And the windings of the investigating Sun, the solstices and the winter-turns, circling; and of night and of day the vicissitude; and the quadrifarious commotion of the seasons; and the nature of the fixed and wandering stars, with their efficacy. I suspected that such art was here ordained by the most-noble mathematician Petosiris, or by Necepso. The inspection of which things (was) enough to draw the inspector, with excessive solace, to a choice and wondrous speculation — not without egregious spectacle, and elegant fiction, and comely distribution of figures, with defined painting of colouring and shadings, by which the commodulation of the bodies and requisite lights, to complacency representing themselves with most-worthy expression of the effects, with laudable and joyous show to the senses of the soul. A work, without doubt, worthy of pondering, as much as ever one could contemplate. Then, in one division, the content of the meaning of the aforesaid works, of antiquarian letters, was elegantly inscribed. The spaces interposed between the half-quadrangulars were hedged round with bands, carved of comely workmanship.

Gli reliqui parieti del templo, cum multiplice et variforme de Emblemature, erano operosamente, de pretiosi marmori incrustati, quanto meglio el multiscio Architecto, ad tanta magnificentia de structura imaginando sepe applicare, né forsa tale fue facto ad Ammone. Sopra l’ordine delle Corinthie, de perfectissima scalptura el facondo Apolline resideva lyrante. Et sopra ciascuna delle altre in circuito, sopra conveniente Arule, de tutto integro, una Musa de petra Pilates, ad li opportuni gesti et officio, cum summa laude del Statuario exquisitissimamente scalpte, sopralocate al pretenso dicto de sopra dell’ambiente illigamento. La ingente cupula monstrava maximo inditio, più praesto de divina operatione che de humana obstentatione. Ma se humana, non senza stupore et accusatione dell’ambitione de tanto tentato di arte fusoria dalo ingegno humano, perché considerando tanta vastitate, in una sola et solida conflatura, et iacto de metallo, como io arbitrava, io rimansi summopere mirabondo et allucinato, damnandola quasi alla impossibilitate, niente dimeno questo tale Aerificio, era tutto de Vite nascente fora de bellissimi vasi, della aenea materia, al perpendiculo et ordine stanti della columnatione, diffundendo et gli rami, palmite, o vero surculi, et Capreoli vertiginosamente intricantise, cum gratissimo coniugio, in observantia della formatione del convexo della dicta cupula, la una cum l’altra cum decente densitate, cum foglie, racemi, infantuli scandendo decerpenti, Avicule volitante, Lacerte serpente, ad aemulatione della natura, optimamente exacte, et tuto el residuo pervio. Le descripte operature tanto directamente conducte, che proportionate ne l’area quanto el naturale artificiosamente appariano. De perfectissimo oro tutto collustratamente inaurato. Le apertione, cioè lo intervacuo della fogliatione, fructi, et animali convenientissimamente se praestavano, obturati de crystalline plastre, de diverso coloramento tincte, quale perlustrabile gemme. Alla congruentia della structura opportuna è la integritate dela harmonia, imperoché omni cingibile ligamento intraneo, expostula el concincto extraneo. Gli pili externi continuavano el pedamento Areobato cum gli tre gradi impari, colligante tutto lo imo del tempio, tanto dall’area librato, quanto levato era l’interno pavimento, de sopra agli Areobati, o vero Stylobati, o vero Scabelli, in loco de Base, uno ornato, com Thori, Alvei, Fasce, Gule, et quarti di circulo circumiendo, et ancora d’intorno al sacello concinnamente circinava. Accepto lo infimo suo proiecto dal humano pede, et di sopra gli Pilastri. Gli quali in interstitio erano perterebrati, o vero fistulati, et pertusati, cioè lo imbricio, o vero el compluvio delle aque praecipite, per gli Tubuli fina al solo intromessi, se n

The remaining walls of the temple, with manifold and various-form Emblemature, were laboriously incrusted of precious marbles — as best the much-knowing Architect, imagining, could often apply to so great a magnificence of structure; nor, perhaps, was such made for Ammon. Above the order of the Corinthians, of most-perfect sculpture, the eloquent Apollo resided, lyring. And above each of the others, in circuit, upon a fit Altar, of the whole entire, a Muse of pilates stone — at the opportune gestures and office, with the utmost praise of the Statuary most-exquisitely carved — set above the aforesaid, above, of the encompassing binding.

The huge cupola showed the greatest token, rather of divine operation than of human ostentation. But if human, not without stupor and accusation of the ambition of so much attempted of the founder's-art by human wit — for, considering so great a vastness, in one sole and solid casting and pour of metal (as I judged), I remained most-highly marvelling and hallucinated, damning it almost to impossibility. Nonetheless, this such Bronze-work was all of Vine springing out of most-beautiful vases, of the bronze material, standing at the perpendicular and order of the columnation — diffusing the branches, vine- shoots, or twigs, and Tendrils, dizzily entangling themselves, with most-pleasing conjugation, in observance of the formation of the convex of the said cupola, one with the other with decent density; with leaves, clusters, little infants climbing and plucking, little Birds fluttering, Lizards creeping, in emulation of nature, most-excellently worked out, and all the rest passable (open).

The described works (were) so directly conducted, that, proportioned, in the area they artfully appeared as much as the natural. Of most-perfect gold, all shiningly gilt. The openings — that is, the void of the foliation, fruits, and animals — most-fittingly presented themselves stopped with crystalline plates, of diverse colouring tinged, like translucent gems.

To the congruity of the structure opportune is the integrity of the harmony; for every girdable inner binding demands the outer girdle. The external piers continued the areobate footing, with the three uneven steps, binding all the base of the temple — as much librated from the area as the inner pavement was raised — above the areobates, or stylobates, or Scabelli, in place of a Base: an ornament, with Tori, Alvei, Fasciae, Gulets, and quarter-circles circling round, and again round the sacello becomingly circled. Its lowest projection received (worn) from the human foot; and above, the Pilasters. Which, in the interstice, were pierced through, or tubed, and perforated — that is, the guttering, or compluvium of the precipitate waters, through the Tubes let down to the ground, were —

ingurgitavano, de optimo coagmento uno nel lingulato del altro coniuncti. Imperoché negli aedificii subdivo scale, compluvio, o vero gurgiti, non se debi locare. Primo, per el periculo del lapso. Secundo colui che minge proximo ad gli sui pedi, destruge et gli calciamenti sui spurca. Dunque debese tale inconveniente fugire. Lo imbricio discoperto, per inundante aqua l’area excava, et più enorme, si resistentia de subdita petra se oppone, che tanto più l’aqua al pedamento salisce et muri, quanto che dalle gutte, dalle stille resultante se defende. Non sencia grave iniuria, damno, et iactura, et per ventilare impeto, l’aqua per gli muri fundese, reddendoli poscia putri et terricosi, et per fenestre lacescente, exhausto, et evanido omni illimento. Per la expergine della quale, excresce nelle compacture herbacio, cotilidone, o vero cimbalaria Adianto, Digitello, Parietaria, et Polypodio, et a produre arbusculi et caprifici parata, exitio murale, cum populose et redivive fibre, o vero radice, reddendo gli parieti inconstanti et rituosi. Ritorniamo. Lo alamento del primo muro la sua altitudine derivava ad libramento della summitate, della crassitudine degli Trabi inflexi, o vero Archi sopra la columnatione, et quelli del testudinato dal muro a rimpecto della Corinthia columna. Sopra la superficie del quale muro, cioè dello illigamento della circunducta cornitione, era uno canaliolo excavato. Appresso el labro del quale verso el templo se terminava el squammato clivo del aeneo et inaurato tecto. Il supremo del quale culmo supra el camurato, aequalmente principiava, a ricontro della sublime linea della coronice, del Phrygio et Trabe interna. Nel dicto canaleolo sopra el muro nella plana del cornitione excapto, le aque che per piogia per el proclinato tecto scorreano, negli acceptabuli degli Pili infundevanse. Et per questa via gli rivuli della piogia concepta se imbricavano praecipitante al fondo, et per occulte fistule, o vero meati subterranei flueva nella Cisterna. La quale havea uno Voratore, per el quale el superfluo dell’aque se ne usciva, et a sufficientia delle cose sacre rimanea. Il fronte degli Pilastri, tra le undule de optimo liniamento de candelabri, foglie, fructi, fiori avicole et de varia altra eximia operatura, perfectamente se praestava ornata, oltra la fimbria del muro, continuavano dicti pilastri, tanto in altecia, quanto se ritrovava el residuo ascendente, dalla coronice, ove extava el suppedio, o vero Arula subiecta alle Muse, nel primario illigamento interiore, fina alla corona. Sopra la quale principiava la magna cupula a tuberare. Et da questa altitudine alla cima degli Pili

— engulfed, of excellent jointing, one conjoined in the tongued (socket) of the other. For in open-air buildings, stairs, compluvium, or gutters, ought not to be placed: first, for the peril of the slip; second, he who makes water next to his own feet destroys, and befouls his footwear. Therefore such inconvenience ought to be fled. The uncovered guttering, by inundating water, excavates the area — and more enormously, if a resistance of subjacent stone opposes — so that the water climbs so much the more to the footing and walls, as it defends itself from the drops, from the resulting drips.

Not without grave injury, damage and loss (and, by the ventilating impetus, the water pours through the walls, rendering them then putrid and earthy, and through the windows leaking, exhausted and vanished every rendering): by the sprinkling of which, there grows in the joints herbage — cotyledon, or cymbalaria, maidenhair, digitello, pellitory, and polypody — and, ready to produce little shrubs and wild figs, a mural ruin, with populous and reviving fibres, or roots, rendering the walls inconstant and rift.

Let us return. The band of the first wall derived its height to the level of the summit, of the thickness of the inflected Architraves, or Arches, above the columnation, and those of the vaulting, from the wall, opposite the Corinthian column. Above the surface of which wall — that is, of the binding of the circum-led cornice — was a little channel excavated. Near the lip of which, toward the temple, terminated the scaled slope of the bronze and gilt roof. The topmost of which summit, above the curved (dome), evenly began, opposite the sublime line of the cornice, of the Frieze and inner Architrave. In the said little channel, above the wall, in the flat of the cornice excavated, the waters that by rain ran down the sloped roof were poured into the receivers of the Piers. And by this way the rivulets of the conceived rain guttered, precipitating to the bottom; and by hidden pipes, or subterranean passages, flowed into the Cistern. Which had a Devourer (overflow-drain), by which the superfluous of the waters went out, and sufficiently for the sacred things remained.

All secure; Colonna the practical architect.

monstrava tanto discenso, o vero proclinato, quanto quello della tectura inscandulata squammea, et inaurata, che tale non fu quelle del Capitolio inaurato da Catulo, né tale squammeatura hebbe el Pantheon. Gli corni del quale pedavano d’ambidui gli lateri del muro et del Pillastro sopra el capitulo d’una semiquadrangula intrusa nel muro, excluso el tertio obvia della intranea de Ophytes et l’altra nel adiuncto del Pillastro. La facia del quale adiuncto de Pillastro dinanti era uno Nicholo, o vero Solio, et inanti questo Solio, o vero Nichio perpendicularmente sopra la piana dil Pillastro era situata per ciascuno una nobilissima statua, cum multiplici acti. Dal latere dextro et sinistro del quale, adiuncto se vedea tale scalptura, quale nel fronte del Pillastro subiecto. Il quale concincto era una coronatione denticulata et echinata, o vero ovolata, cum el suggrundio gyrulato, o vero voragulato cum le pentafilete tra gli verticuli circunducto, cum le occurrente liniature perfecta. Negli inflexi del volume nascevano silique, o vero teche (pregne del suo parto) fabacee, o vero lupinacee. Il quale cortice era intecto de gratiose squamme. Et sopra el convolvulo resupino serpiva cadendo sopra la undiculatione squammea uno folio di Cynara. Il quale volucro cocleale facilmente a norma del circino acconciamente se involve, fermando il stabile et volvendo semicirculo. — showed as much descent, or slope, as that of the scaled and gilt roof-covering — such as was not that of the Capitol gilt by Catulus, nor such scaling had the Pantheon. In this manner, between the opening of the wall under the cupola, and between this adjunct of Pilaster (founded, level, upon the external mural circuit), was born an arch, gapingly open, contrasting, with the lineament of an Architrave. The horns of which footed on both the sides of the wall and of the Pilaster, upon the capital of a half-quadrangular (pilaster) intruded in the wall (excepting the facing third of the inner Ophite), and the other in the adjunct of the Pilaster. The face of which adjunct of Pilaster, in front, was a Niche, or Solium; and before this Solium, or Niche, perpendicularly above the flat of the Pilaster, was situated, for each, a most-noble statue, with manifold acts. On the right and left side of which adjunct was seen such sculpture as on the front of the Pilaster beneath. The last surface of the thickness of the exterior arch was even with the flat of the topmost Pilaster.

The slip (of the dome), then, from the girdle began, upon which beginning the cupola usurped from without, and fell upon the Pier, with all that lineament binding itself, that was in the girdle circled under the cupola; and under this slip the arch made resistance. Which girdle was a denticulate and echinate, or ovolo, crowning, with the drip-course rolled, or channelled, with the cinquefoils between the whorls circum-led, perfect with the occurring lineaments.

In the plane of the surface of this binding, or crowning, lay the beginning of the cupola; in the projection of which was an excavated trough, into which the waters of the cupola derived, running; and down by the channelled slips they belchingly engulfed themselves into the Pilasters.

Above the slippery (curve) of the cupola, terminating at the Pilaster — that is, at its cyma, or flat — a bark, or little cartouche, covering, made two contrary volutes: the one accosting the cupola supine, and the other, near the Pier, resupine, making the volutes with a snail-line. In the inflections of the volume were born pods, or cases (pregnant of their birth), bean-like, or lupine-like. Which bark was covered with gracious scales. And above the resupine convolvulus crept, falling upon the scaled undulation, a leaf of Cynara (artichoke). Which cochlear (spiral) wrapper easily, by the norm of the compass, fittingly wraps itself — fixing the stable (foot) and turning the semicircle; then placing the stable (foot) between the led semicircle and the point, opening the compass, and coupling the unstable (foot) with the extreme of the semicircle, and turning, and so opening, and moving the point — that —

figura iusta si conduce. Novissimamente sopra la suprema plana de ciascuno Pilastro de mira factura, fue statuito uno candelabro de aurichalco illustre, l’orificio del quale era dilatato a forma conchea, nella quale indesinente di materia inconsumabile uno inextinguibile foco ardeva, il quale, né per venti, né per piogia se poteva extinguere, gli quali mirabili candelabri de una proportionata et compare proceritate vedevanse, opportunamente ansati. Da l’una et l’altra ansa degli quali miravegliosamente una resta pendeva invinculata et disnodata in molte parte cum subtilissimo artificio retinentise, de fronde, et fiori, et diversificati fructi, cum debita pregnatura nel medio curvescente, et lorata, et perviamente excavata. Sopra la cistellata infasciatura degli lori nel Tubulo mediano incubava una vivace et inane Aquila, abranchiata, cum le ale passe, ne l’area al intuito la perfectione del naturale conspicuamente monstrando, della materia degli candelabri, cum saburoso pondo subiecto, tutta vacua et de subtilissima superficie conflata. Fogliamento, fructi inani, et flosculi, et le altre operature subtilmente redacte. Il sufficto del interno camurato, tra el columnamento et el muro externo de vermiculata opera, de inaurata tessellatura cum graphidi de explicatissima arte, cum coloratione concurrente era depicto. Della altitudine tractando non è complebile per havere solamente concluso questa universale regula, tanto uno rotondo templo levarsi, quanto è el suo diametro. Ma concorre regularmente el ritrovare l’altitudine dello illigamento sopra il peristylio, cioè della suprema linea della coronice. Imperoché dal centro deducta la linea alla circumferentia del primo circuito, tanto praestase quella altecia. Diviso poscia tutto il diametro in sei divisione, quatro di quelle rectificate, darano similmente l’ultima superficie del superiore illigamento. La regula del discenso del tecto non si debi negligere, si tuole la intercapedine da muro a muro, ove collocare se debi el culmeo lapso, et riducta in perfecte quadrature bine quanto valeno venire, et extenso il diagonio, secante la linea, gli dui quadri discriminante, d’indi belle se exige el clivo. Universalmente la Symmetria de questa miranda fabrica dal praestante Architecto elegante disposita, cum consentanei illigamenti intrinseci et extrinseci congrui. Molto più diffusamente la regulatione potrebese ad tutte convenientie ad gli correlarii del sodo manifestare, et per quelle figure de l’area ritrovare Altitudine de muri, gli quali quivi recti extavano, quanto meglio fare si potesse sencia obliquitate, o vero restitudine et

— the just figure is conducted.

Newly, above the topmost flat of each Pilaster, of wondrous make, was set a candelabrum of illustrious brass (orichalc), the orifice of which was dilated into a shell-form, in which, unceasingly, of unconsumable material, an inextinguishable fire burned — which neither by winds nor by rain could be extinguished. Which marvellous candelabra, of a proportioned and matching tallness, were seen, fittingly handled (with handles).

From the one and the other handle of which, marvellously, a festoon hung, ensnared and un-knotted in many parts, retaining itself with most-subtle artifice, of fronds, and flowers, and diversified fruits, with due pregnancy curving in the middle, and thonged, and passably (openly) excavated. Upon the little- basket-like binding of the thongs, on the middle Tube, brooded a lively and inane (hollow) Eagle, grasped-fast, with wings outspread, in the area to the sight showing conspicuously the perfection of the natural — of the material of the candelabra, with a ballast weight set under, all hollow and cast of most- subtle surface. Foliage, hollow fruits, and florets, and the other works, subtly reduced. The soffit of the inner curved-vault, between the columnation and the outer wall, of wormwork, of gilt tessellation with graphic (design) of most-explicated art, with concurrent colouring, was depicted.

Of the height treating: it is not completable to have only concluded this universal rule — that a round temple raises itself as much as is its diameter. But it concurs, regularly, to find the height of the binding above the peristyle (that is, of the topmost line of the cornice): for, the line deduced from the centre to the circumference of the first circuit, so much presents that height. Then, all the diameter divided into six divisions, four of those rectified will likewise give the last surface of the upper binding.

The rule of the descent of the roof ought not to be neglected: one takes the intercapace from wall to wall, where the ridge-slip ought to be placed, and reduced into two perfect squarings as much as they can come; and, the diagonal extended, cutting the line, discriminating the two squares — thence, beautifully, the slope is exacted.

Universally, the Symmetry of this wondrous fabric, by the outstanding Architect elegantly disposed, with consentaneous bindings, inner and outer, congruous. Much more diffusely the regulation could, to all conveniences, to the corollaries of the solid, be manifested — and, by those figures of the area, to find the Height of walls, which here stood straight, as best one could do without obliquity, or straightness, and —

Notes

The inextinguishable-fire candelabra on the pilaster-tops (shell-mouthed, festoon-hung, each with a hollow bronze eagle), the gilt-mosaic soffit, and then the proportional rules of the temple (round temple: height = diameter; entablature-height by dividing the diameter in six and rectifying four; roof-slope by the diagonal of two squares). All secure; the rules are a genuine architect's method.

la sua crassitudine, et qualunque minima particula et linea et fina gli recisamenti, sencia errato. O infoelici tempi et aetate nostra, come dagli moderni (usando conveniente vocabulo) sì bella et dignifica inventione, è ignorata? Per la quale cosa imaginare alcuno non se debbi, che Trabi Phrygii, Coronamenti, Base, Capitelli, Columne, Columne semi, Pavimento, Crustati, Alamento, Coassatione, et tutta la Compaginatione, Dimensione, Partitione, se accusasseron sencia inditio degli solerti et praestanti ingenii antiqui et prisci exquisitamente excogitati et digesti. Et de Marmori egregia Xesturgia, quale non rende la spuma del stanno perusto, né ancora la cerusa cremata. Nel centro del dicto Templo promineva uno puteale orificio di una fatale Cisterna, cum una promptissima Chorea de Nymphe in circuito di prominente celatura in defecto solamente del spirito, de Alabastrite, optimo expresso, quanto meglio di scalpro exscalpire si potesse, cum gli volanti habiti et velamini. Nella parte mediana della clausura della intrinseca summitate della excelsa cupula circundava una gioia di dense fronde, delle antinarrate vite metalline, le quale nel profundo de questo coelo finivano tanto perfectamente una cum l’altra stricte implicate, quanto meglio si potesse imaginare. Le quale intermettevano acconciamente tanta circulatione, quanta monstrava di sopra, l’apertura del vaso gutturnio inverso. Il quale circunducto spacio supplendo bellissimamente occupava il viperino capo della furente Medusa, artificiosamente conflata della materia della cupula, che tra gli conglobati serpi la vociferante bucca et manico sembiante et rugata fronte stava nel centro perpendicularemente sopra la Cisterna. Et degli extremi della bucca usciva uno condulo, dal quale innexa una faberrima innodatura perpendicula discendeva, suspensa sopra el puteale orificio. Questa innodatura tutta de perfecto oro, nella inferiore extremitate finiva in uno anello, il quale uno altro egli retinia appacto ad una gracilitate del fundo de una conchula inversa, cioè l’apertura in giù, et el fundo simato de sopra, graciliscente acuminava all’anello, et nel labio resupino di circuitione de uno cubito per diametro, havea appacte quatro armille, o vero fibule. Nelle quale harpavano quatro uncini. Dagli quali invinculate quatro catenule pendevano. Et queste etiam tenendo rapivano una circulare lamina in plano, aequalmente sospesa. Sopra la superiore circunferentia de questo margine in inciso, incubavano quatro monstrificate fanciulle, cum soluta capillatura, cum la fronte redimite de eximia

— its thickness, and whatever least particle and line, and even the cuttings, without error. O unhappy times and our age — how, by the moderns (using a convenient vocable), is so beautiful and worthy an invention ignored! For which thing let no one imagine that Architraves, Friezes, Cornices, Bases, Capitals, Columns, half-Columns, Pavement, Incrustations, Bands, Boarding, and all the Compagination, Dimension, Partition, should accuse themselves without token of the shrewd and outstanding wits of the ancients and the elders, exquisitely excogitated and digested. And of Marbles the egregious xesturgy (polishing-art), such as neither the foam of burnt tin renders, nor yet the burnt ceruse.

In the centre of the said Temple stood forth a well-head orifice of a fatal Cistern, with a most-prompt Chorus of Nymphs in circuit, of prominent carving — in default only of the spirit (i.e. lacking only breath) — of Alabaster, most-excellently expressed, as best with the chisel one could carve, with their flying habits and veils.

In the middle part of the closure of the inner summit of the lofty cupola went round a jewel of dense fronds, of the aforenarrated metallic vines, which in the depth of this heaven ended so perfectly, one tightly entwined with the other, as best one could imagine. Which fittingly admitted (left) so much circulation (opening) as showed above, the opening of the inverted ewer-vase. Which circum-led space, supplying, most-beautifully occupied the viperine head of the furious Medusa, artfully cast of the material of the cupola — which, among the balled-up serpents, the vociferating mouth and handle-like and wrinkled brow, stood in the centre, perpendicularly above the Cistern. And out of the extremes of the mouth came a little conduit, from which, fixed, a most-excellent knotwork perpendicularly descended, suspended above the well-head orifice.

This knotwork, all of perfect gold, at the lower extremity ended in a ring, which another retained, set fast to a slenderness of the bottom of an inverted little conch (that is, the opening downward, and the bottom, sima-moulded, above), thinning, tapering to the ring; and in the resupine lip, of a circuit of a cubit in diameter, it had set fast four little rings, or clasps, in which four hooks harped (gripped), from which, ensnared, four little chains hung. And these too, holding, seized a circular plate in the flat, evenly suspended. Upon the upper circumference of this margin, in incision, brooded four monster- made girls, with loosed hair, with the forehead wreathed of choice —

Et ciascuna di queste, nel suo femine discriminantise, et dissentiente le polpose coxe, vertivano poscia quelle in antiquarie fronde, cum fogliatura Achantinea, obviantise l’una cum l’altra, se colligavano, poscia verso gli sui Ilii, o vero fianchi, gli volubili esse cum stricto pugno le rapivanon, cum le ale harpiiatice extense, verso la catenula. Le quale retro alle sue scapule erano innodate. Nell’ordine ove se ricontravano le sinuose et laciniate fronde, de una et dell’altra puella era impacto a tergo uno inuncato Harpagulo. Gli quali spiramenti nel suo dorso obviantise, si colligavano, et fora del medio della ligatura de sopra uscivano alcune spiche sementate semicrepate. Et de sotto la ligatura tre follicule, quatro ligature, et quatro harpaguli. Da quelli scorpioli pendevano altre quatro catenule, le quale sospensa retinivano la miravegliosa lampada de spherica figura et di ambito ulnale. Nel plano della rotonditate antedicta, nel mediano era circularmente aperta, et dapossa per diametro a llibella da una damigella all’altra, hiavano quatro rotonde aperture, meno di circuitione de dui palmi, in queste quadrine bucce pendevano quatro vacue pile, che cum gli proprii labii, o vero oroli, cum extrinseco reflexo in sé, nella sua apertura, et in quelle bucce retinute, cusì cum artificio diligentemente expresse, che quasi tutta la sua rotunditate era libera, de sotto dependule tutta aparia. Le quale lampadule de pretiosa petra furono excavate, opera incomparabile. Una de Balasso. L’altra de Saphyro. La tertia de Smaragdo. La ultima de Topacio. La maiore lampada, como de sopra è dicto, era spherica de mundissimo crystallo, né al Torno tale iustitia harebbe usurpata, subtilmente exscalpta, opera di grande exquisitione, et factura incredibile. La quale verso l’orificio haveva quatro ansulette, iustamente distribute n iiii

— work, cast. And each of these (girls), discriminating itself at the thigh, and the fleshy hips dissenting, turned those then into antiquarian fronds, with Acanthine foliage; meeting one with the other, they joined themselves; then toward their flanks, or sides, the voluble ones seized them with a tight fist, with the harpy-like wings outstretched, toward the little chain — which, behind their shoulder-blades, were knotted. In the order where the sinuous and jagged fronds of the one and the other girl met, was fixed, at the back, a hooked Grapple. Which shoots, meeting at their back, joined themselves; and out of the middle of the binding, above, came certain seeded, half-cracked ears (of grain). And beneath the binding, three little follicles, four bindings, and four grapples.

From those little scorpions hung another four little chains, which, suspended, retained the marvellous lamp, of spherical figure and of a cubit's compass.

In the flat of the aforesaid rotundity, in the middle, it was circularly open; and then, by diameter, level, from one damsel to the other, gaped four round openings, less than two palms in circuit. In these four little mouths hung four hollow spheres, which, with their own lips, or rims, with an exterior reflex into themselves, in their opening, and retained in those mouths — so, with artifice, diligently expressed, that almost all their rotundity was free, appearing wholly pendent below. Which little lamps were excavated of precious stone, an incomparable work: one of Balas-ruby; the other of Sapphire; the third of Emerald; the last of Topaz.

The greater lamp, as above is said, was spherical, of most-clean crystal — nor at the lathe would such justice have been usurped — subtly carved, a work of great exquisiteness, and incredible make. Which, toward the orifice, had four little handles, justly distributed —

in quatro locatione, per le quale concatenata pendeva la bucca di semibracio aperta, et in questa bucca era intromisso un altro vaso urinaceo, o vero di forma cucurbitacea, similmente di crystallo purissimo. Il quale tanto regularmente intromisso pendeva, che nel centro el lume della lampada ardeva. Poscia tutto el corpo della maiore lampada era completo de aqua ardente, cinque fiate reiterata al stillamento. Perché lo effecto suspicare mi fece, imperoché tutto el sphaerico corpo ardere simulava, per essere locato el lychno nel mediano puncto. Et per questo el viso habilmente non potevasi in quello firmare, come malamente nel Sole, essendo la materia di mira perspicuitate et de factione subtile.

Non meno el liquore inconsumptibile era limpidissimo nel cucurbitaceo fundo per questa dimonstratione. Et similmente de tale liquore le quatro superiore ardevano. Ove reflectevano gli vagi coloramenti delle pretiose petre, nella maiore lampada, et la maiore in quelle, cum inconstante splendore vacilante per tutto el Sacrato Templo. Et per el nitore speculare degli tersissimi marmori, che nel aere tale Iris el Sole dapò la piogia non depinge. Ma sopra tutto miravegliosa cosa questo all’intuito se ripraesentava, imperoché l’artifice scalptore perspicuamente havea in circuito excavato sopra la corpulentia della crystallea lampada, de opera cataglypha, o vero lacunata una promptissima pugna, de infantuli sopra gli strumosi et praepeti Delphini aequitanti, cum le caude inspirantise, cum multiplici et dissimili effecti et fantulinacei conati, non altramente che si la natura ficto havesse, et non excavate appariano, ma di sublevata opera, et sì factamente expresse che l’intento degli mei ochii, via da tanto delectabile obiecto della comitante Nympha

— in four locations, by which, concatenated, hung the mouth (vessel), open of a half-arm; and in this mouth was let in another urn-like vase, or of cucurbit (gourd) form, likewise of purest crystal.

Which so regularly, let in, hung, that in the centre the light of the lamp burned. Then all the body of the greater lamp was full of burning water, five times reiterated at the distilling. For which the effect made me suspect (a marvel), because all the spherical body simulated burning, by the wick being placed at the middle point. And by this, the sight could not ably fix itself upon it — as, ill, upon the Sun — the material being of wondrous perspicuity and of subtle make.

No less the unconsumable liquor was most-limpid in the cucurbit bottom, by this demonstration. And likewise of such liquor the four upper (lamps) burned. Where reflected the vague colourings of the precious stones, in the greater lamp, and the greater in those, with inconstant splendour vacillating throughout all the Sacred Temple. And by the mirror-sheen of the most-terse marbles — such an Iris the Sun, after the rain, does not paint in the air. But above all, a marvellous thing this represented itself to the sight: for the sculptor-artificer had perspicuously excavated, in circuit, upon the corpulence of the crystalline lamp, of cataglyph (relief-hollowed), or coffered, work, a most-prompt battle of little infants riding upon the knotty and swift Dolphins, with the tails inspiring themselves (curling), with manifold and dissimilar effects and infantile efforts — not otherwise than if nature had feigned it; and they appeared not excavated, but of raised work, and so-made expressed, that the intent of my eyes, away from so delectable an object, from the accompanying Nymph, they violated (drew off).

violentavano. Et el vacilamento del lume pareva dare moto alla scalptura. Finalmente per absolutione di questa mirifica structura di Templo, resta a dire brevemente, che ello era compacto de quadrati de Augustea petra parte, et parte del supranarrato marmoro, in contignatione perfecta sencia ferro et lignatura, cum più subtile investigatione di scalptura, che unque al seculo nostro fare, né imaginare si potesse, né tale ad Api Deo, Sannitico aegyptio construsse. Sotto le base degli Pilastrelli, o vero quadrangule, che era imo et continuo illigamento, nel primo alamento, o vero pariete, et el superiore concincto similmente cum gli capitelli ambiente, ambiva nel aequatissimo pavimento, una lista, o vero fascia de finissimo Porphyrite, quanto era la porrectura del quadrato suo, et contigua quasi sencia disceptatione un’altra de Ophytes. Sotto el suppedio delle columne, era circumacta quanta la sua crassitudine, una lista de Porphyrite, cum due collaterale di durissimo serpentino, cum l’ordine del Peristylio in circumductione. Il simile vedevasi l’orificio della cisterna cincto nel pavimento, una lista de Porphyro et un’altra di Ophytes. Il residuo dil spectatissimo pavimento tra l’orificio puteale et il peristylio, era di mirabile emblemature, di minutali, di tassellulato di fine petre circinantisi elegantemente includevano in partitione deducti, deci rotondi, per diametro pedali. Del suo colore et specie l’uno alla linea del altro. Erano dui di rubente Diaspro, di varie macole gratissimamente perfuso. Dui de Lytharmeno de scintule d’oro più pusilli, de atomi rutilanti disseminato. Dui de Diaspro verde, di vene Calcedonice varicato cum rubente macule et giale. Dui di Achates, de fili lactei confusamente undiculato. Dui ultimamente de limpidissimo Calcedonico. Et per l’angustia delle linee verso la cisterna, similmente decrescevano le figure circulare. Sotto el concamerato erano nel solistimo Asaroto di vermiculato emblemate, fogliamento, animali, et fiori tessellulati di minutissimi corpusculi, de recisamenti lapidei diligentemente tessellati depicto, et coaequatissimamente perfricati, o vero scalpturati. Quale arte non hebbe nel pavimentare Zenodoro in Pergamo, né tale fue il lithostrato in Praeneste nel delubro dilla Fortuna. Al cimatio, o vero Pinnaculo della magnifica cupula torniamo, della medesima materia metallina obrizamente inaurata, sopra el coelo della

— violated. And the vacillation of the light seemed to give motion to the sculpture.

Finally, for the absolution of this marvellous structure of Temple, it remains to say briefly that it was compacted of quadrates of Augustan stone in part, and part of the above-narrated marble, in perfect jointing, without iron and timber, with more subtle investigation of sculpture than ever in our century could be done, nor imagined — nor such did they construct for Apis, the Samnitic-Egyptian God.

Under the bases of the little Pilasters, or quadrangulars — which was the lowest and continuous binding, in the first band, or wall; and the upper girdle likewise, with the encompassing capitals — went round, in the most-even pavement, a strip, or band, of finest Porphyry, as much as was the projection of its square, and contiguous, almost without dispute, another of Ophite. Under the sub-base of the columns was circled, as much as its thickness, a strip of Porphyry, with two collateral of hardest serpentine, with the order of the Peristyle in circumduction. The like was seen of the orifice of the cistern, girt in the pavement — a strip of Porphyry and another of Ophite.

The remainder of the much-admired pavement, between the well-orifice and the peristyle, was of marvellous emblemature, of little-minutiae, of little tessellation of fine stones circling themselves, elegantly included, deduced in partition: ten roundels, a foot in diameter, of their colour and species, the one to the line of the other. There were two of reddening Jasper, most-pleasingly suffused with various spots; two of Litharmenon, of little sparks of gold, more minute, disseminated of rutilant atoms; two of green Jasper, variegated with chalcedonic veins, with reddening and yellow spots; two of Agate, confusedly undulated of milky threads; two, lastly, of most-limpid Chalcedony. And, by the narrowness of the lines toward the cistern, likewise the circular figures decreased.

Under the vaulting were, in the floor, an Asaroton of wormwork emblems — foliage, animals, and flowers, tessellated of most-minute little bodies, of stone-cuttings diligently tessellated, depicted, and most- evenly rubbed smooth, or sculptured. Which art Zenodorus had not, in paving at Pergamon; nor such was the lithostroton at Praeneste, in the shrine of Fortune.

Let us return to the cyma, or Pinnacle, of the magnificent cupola — of the same metallic material, refinedly gilt, above the heaven of the —
