Sopra delle quale, cum exigente harmonia sexquialtera de l’altitudine composito et liniamento, circunducti erano, el Trabe, Zophoro, et Coronice, cum gli proiecti a llibella delle subiecte columne. Una squammata cupula resideva. Sopra gli porrecti a llibella delle sustitute columne, residente per ciascuna vidi uno simulachro de vento, cum elegante expressione della natura sua, alati cum le ale passe, et ad le spalle porrecte, in uno instabile Perone, o vero stylo, vertibili artificiosamente infixi, et a qualunque flante vento tutti octo Petasi indagavano, circumacti involventise, la facia a quello vertivano opposita al flato. Nel Praefato culmo della proxima narrata cupula, ancora octo Pilastrelli, cum altecia di dui quadrati rectamente insurgevano, cum uno vaso gutturnio, cum l’apertura sopra quelli inverso. L’ascenso dunque del tuto, cum exquisita commensuratione, et cum obiecto a gli inspectori mathematicamente proportionato. Sopra el fundo del vaso gutturnio (cusì io lo interpreto per la sua forma) circumcirca de scindule peponacee bellissimamente scindulato era inpresso uno stipite del proprio metallo. Il quale principiava dalla latitudine del fundo, moderatamente gracile ascendendo. Et per quanto se trovava la medietate del vaso, el stylo asceso, uno ingente trigone vacuo sustentava, inseme cum el stilo artificiosamente fuso. Nella sumitudine della quale, hiava una apertura circuibile, et nel imo corpulento in quatro locatione era terebrata, coniecturando pensiculai, solerte excogitato del provido artifice, per questo, che aqua intromissa per piogia, o gelo concepto, non la occupasse dal suo officio et per vitare el pondo. Per lo Patore soprano della dicta Pila, libero dagli labri el dicto stylo, o vero fuso transiva nella cima acuatose. Da lo exito in suso, era tanto, quanto dal fundo del vaso alla Pila. In questo fastigato, una aenea luna era confixa, octimera, quanto lei dimonstra, cum gli corni al coelo. Nel colpho, o vero sinuato della quale assideva uno Alieto cum le passe ale. Sotto la luna nel stylo erano quatro solidi et firmi harpaguli, negli quali quatro cathenule del praefato metallo retinute erano et cum el tutto aerificio conflate per indicare el fusore

— stupendous cupola, eight fluted and somphe (spongy), or hollow, columns egregiously rose, with a noble footing, drawn apart by an interjected and encompassing fenestration, with little arches between one and the other, upon the collateral piers overstanding. Above which, with an exigent harmony, sesquialter (3:2) of the height, of composite and lineament, were circum-led the Architrave, Frieze, and Cornice, with the projections level with the subjacent columns. A scaled cupola resided. Above the projections, level with the substitute columns, resident, for each I saw a simulacrum of a wind, with elegant expression of its nature — winged, with the wings outspread, and stretched at the shoulders — on an unstable Peron, or stylus, artfully fixed to turn; and, at whatever blowing wind, all eight Petasi (wind-hats) investigated, circling, involving themselves — the face they turned toward that opposite to the blast.

In the aforesaid summit of the just-narrated cupola, again eight little Pilasters, with the height of two squares, rose straight, with an ewer-vase, with the opening inverted, above them. The ascent, then, of the whole, with exquisite commensuration, and with an object mathematically proportioned to the inspectors.

Upon the bottom of the ewer-vase (so I interpret it, for its form), roundabout, most-beautifully scored of melon-scoring, was impressed a stalk of the proper metal, which began from the breadth of the bottom, moderately slender ascending. And by as much as was found the middle of the vase, the stylus, ascended, sustained a huge hollow triangle, together with the stylus artfully cast. In the summit of which gaped a round opening; and in the low, corpulent (part), in four locations, it was bored — conjecturing, I pondered, the shrewd device of the provident artificer for this: that water let in by rain, or conceived frost, should not occupy it from its office, and to avoid the weight. Through the upper Opening of the said sphere, free of the lips, the said stylus, or spindle, passed, sharpened at the tip. From the outlet upward it was as much as from the bottom of the vase to the sphere.

In this fastigium a bronze moon was fixed, eight-day (crescent), as much as she shows, with the horns to the sky. In the gulf, or sinus, of which sat an Eagle with outspread wings. Under the moon, on the stylus, were four solid and firm grapples, in which four little chains of the aforesaid metal were retained, and with the whole bronze-work cast — to indicate, of the founder-statuary —

— statuary, the large fertility of his wit. Whence one could easily judge that such subtlety the shrewd founder investigated: to found, or cast, an entire little chain without soldering — making a fit mould, quadripartitely cut, in the centre made a pervious hole, into which is let the first ring, and then, the formed parts applying themselves into one — infinitely, one derived from the other, lightly it will be cast.

The said little chains, upon the middle corpulence of the bronze sphere evenly deriving, each in the extreme, ensnared with itself, retained an airy (bronze) Chodon (bell). Which bells, from their middle toward their low (rim), had comb-like fissures, within which a little ball of fine steel resulted, to render enclosed the tinkling sound. These bells were, at exigent proportion, agitated by the blowing winds; upon the corpulence of the hollow sphere they beat, and rendered their tinkle sharp, harmonised, with mixed booms of the metallic triangle — a grateful and sweet and great sound, curiously excogitated and thoughtfully found; and perhaps beyond the sound which, at the summit of the Temple of Jerusalem, the pendent chains with the bronze vases (made), putting the birds to flight.

Lastly, to conclude, remains a rule for understanding all the dimension of the most-celebrated temple. The wall where were the eight windows — its thickness was one and a half feet; as much the scarped (part), or that part which turned itself; that same, the outlet of the —

Pilastri, gli quali oltra el cornicione usurpano el quadrato di tuta questa crassitudine, per omni lato, cioè tre pedi. La porta dunque de questo sancto et stupendo tempio Antipagmentata se offeriva, de forma et operatura dorica tuta di optimo Diaspro. Et nella fascia del suo sublime de litere graece maiuscule antiquarie de puro oro infixe, tale dicto inscripto extava. KYLOPERA Le aurate valve cum perpolito ornato de metallo, della quale porta erano et de bellissima et pervia operatura, tanto più belle, quanto meno le similgliante fare se potrebbe de illustrabile nitella, concluse d’uno pesulo extrinseco. Il quale la Ductrice Nympha non audeva rimoverlo, si prima la Diva Antiste del dicto venerando et sacro Tempio, cum l’altre Phanatice comate, et comptissime virgine (le quale in tuto erano sette) religiosamente non lo reserasse, queste sacre virgine integerrimamente quivi ministravano officiosissime nelle cose sacre alla Antistite Saga de gli sacraficii, et a lei sola concedere lo ingresso convenivase meritissime. Diqué, le sacre virgine havendo nui benigne respectati, domesticamente blandivole, ne acceptorno. Et dalla Nympha mia fida ductrice, la causa del suo et del mio advento audita, ad nui tute agevole et gratiose cum largi vulti, cum esse ne feceron per septe lapidei gradi porphyrici alla egregia porta ascendere, colligati cum el pedamento ambiente de uno magnifico et elegante propylaeo. Quivi trovassemo uno nobile pauso, overo areola di uno quadrato di petra nigerrima et indomabile, che tale non sa ritroverebbe nella patria euganea, aequabile et lucidamente perfricata in bellissime emblemature sepita. Dinanti el sancto limine delle dedicate valve tuto interexcavato, et nelle cavature intersecante cortici di concha cytheriaca de gli più belli intersecti liniamenti, quanto mai agli humani ochii periucundo praesentarse si potesse. In questo loco affirmantise tute, et ambi dui nui, incomincioe allhora di fare una precatione la sacra Antista. Dunque in riverentia inclinatosse la Nympha et io. Quello che lei se dicesse certamente ignorai. Per che flexo alquanto io el capo, sencia mora gli praestissimi, et explorarii ochii, alla invisa albentia et politura de gli vagissimi pedi della comite Nympha riportai. Ove ancora una portiuncula della micante sura dextra era detecta. — Pilasters, which, beyond the cornice, usurp the square of all this thickness, on every side — that is, three feet.

The door, then, of this holy and stupendous temple offered itself antepagmented (framed), of Doric form and workmanship, all of finest Jasper. And in the fascia of its sublime (lintel), of Greek majuscule antiquarian letters of pure gold fixed, this saying stood inscribed:

ΚΥΛΟΠΕΡΑ (KYLOPERA) [?the reading and sense are uncertain; the letters are given as transcribed]

The gilded valves, with most-polished ornament of metal, of which door they were, and of most-beautiful and pervious (open-work) workmanship — so much the more beautiful, as less could their like be made — of illustrious brightness, closed with an exterior bolt. Which the Conductress Nymph did not dare to remove, unless first the Divine Priestess of the said venerable and sacred Temple, with the other frenzied long- haired and most-comely virgins (which in all were seven), religiously unbolted it. These sacred virgins most-integrally there ministered, most-officious, in the sacred things, to the Priestess-Sage of the sacrifices; and to her alone to concede the ingress most-deservedly pertained. Whence the sacred virgins, having benignly respected us, domestically blandishing, accepted us. And, from my faithful conductress Nymph the cause of her and of my advent heard, to us all — easy and gracious, with large (open) faces — with them they made us ascend, by seven porphyry stone steps, to the egregious door, bound with the encompassing footing of a magnificent and elegant propylaeum.

There we found a noble pause, or little area, of a square of blackest and indomitable stone (such as would not be found in the Euganean fatherland), even and lucidly rubbed smooth, hedged in most-beautiful emblemature. Before the holy threshold of the dedicated valves, all inter-excavated (intaglio'd), and in the excavations intersecting little barks of Cytherean conch, of the most-beautiful intersected lineaments that ever to human eyes most-pleasantly could present themselves. In this place, all affirming themselves, and both of us, the sacred Priestess began then to make a prayer. Then, in reverence, the Nymph and I inclined ourselves. What she said to herself, I certainly ignored — because, my head somewhat bent, without delay the most-ready and exploratory eyes, to the unseen whiteness and polish of the most-vague feet of the accompanying Nymph, I carried back. Where, again, a little portion of the glittering right calf was uncovered; for, moderately, by her act moving, the most-tenuous lappet gave way, removed, laying bare the whitest whiteness of the snow-making —

et inhorrescente Borea. Et alhora incontinente una enucleata admiratione nella calda memoria soccorete, che essendo naturale, che la bianchecia disgregi la potentia visiva, questa cum tanta delectatione, in sé tutto el vedere traheva et congregava, non solamente laedendolo, ma a summo solatio et extremo piacere, quel tanto pretioso obiecto ad sé viscido retinendo ligato, et preso, lo occupava. Onde essendo dalla Sacra Donna le sue divote praecatione agli Dii Forculo et Limentino, et alla Dea Cardinea sinceramente finite, rectantise, la bella Nympha (io solamente cum gli ochii fixi alla sua voluptica operatione persistendo) né per tutto ciò unque mosso me sarei, se non che el subtilissimo panno, le dive delitie tornò a recoprire. Daposcia subitariamente dalla simpulatrice donna el Pesulo amoto, quelle gemelle valve, non strepito stridulo, non fremito grave, ma uno arguto murmure et grato, per el testudinato templo reflectendo exsibilava. Et questo animadvertendo cognovi, per vedere sotto la extrema parte delle ponderose valve de una et de l’altra, uno volubile et terete Cylindrulo, il quale per l’axide nella valva infixo, sopra una tersa et coaequata lastra di durissimo Ophytes invertentise et per la frictione faceano uno acceptissimo tintinare. Oltra de questo ragionevolmente me obstupivi, che le valve ciascuna per se medesima, sencia alcuno impulso se aprisseron. Ove da poscia intrati tutti, di subito sencia mirare altronde, quivi affirmatome, volendo investigare, si dicte valve, cusì a tempo et moderatamente, per repenso fusseron tracte, o vero per altro instrumento. Diqué io mirai uno divo excogitato. Imperoché in quella parte, che una cum l’altra, le valve coivano in la lingulata clausura, dalla interna parte, era una lamina de fino calybe sopra el metallo solidata tersissimo. Erano daposcia mirabilmente due Axule di latitudine triente, di optimo Magnete Indico, al quale lo Adamante non dissideva, di Calistone amatore, agli humani ochii praestabile, dal scordeon mortificabondo. Agli navanti singularmente opportuno, le quale del suo conveniente colore monstravano ceruleo, lisse et illustre, affixe perpollitamente nella crassitudine, dilla apertione dil marmoreo muro, cioè nelle poste, alle ante contigue della artificiosa porta. Dunque per questo modo dalla violentia della rapacitate del Magnete, le lamine calybicie erano violentate, et consequentemente per sé le valve cum temporata lentitudine, se reseravano. Opera excellente et exactissima, non solamente de vedere, ma oltramodo di subtile excogitato. Quanta improbitate di investigato di artifice.

— and bristling Boreas.

And then, incontinently, an enucleated admiration succoured in the warm memory: that, it being natural that whiteness disgregates (scatters) the visual power, this (whiteness), with so much delectation, drew and congregated all the seeing into itself — not only not harming it, but, to the utmost solace and extreme pleasure, that so precious object, viscidly retaining it bound to itself, and taken, occupied it. Whence, the Sacred Woman's devout prayers to the Gods Forculus and Limentinus, and to the Goddess Cardea, being sincerely finished, and (all) righting themselves, the beautiful Nymph (I alone persisting, with eyes fixed on her voluptuous operation) — nor for all that would I ever have moved myself, had not the most-subtle cloth returned to re-cover the divine delights.

Then, suddenly, by the libation-woman the Bolt removed, those twin valves — not with a strident noise, not a grave rumble, but an acute and grateful murmur, reflecting through the vaulted temple — hissed sweetly. And, animadverting this, I recognised (it), by seeing under the extreme part of the ponderous valves, of the one and the other, a voluble and turned little Cylinder, which, by the axle fixed in the valve, upon a terse and evened plate of hardest Ophite inverting itself, and by the friction, made a most-acceptable tinkling.

Beyond this, reasonably I was stupefied that the valves, each by its very self, without any impulse, opened themselves. Whence, then, all having entered, of a sudden — without looking elsewhere, there affirming myself — wishing to investigate whether the said valves, so timely and moderately, by a counterweight were drawn, or by some other instrument: whence I beheld a divine device. For in that part where, one with the other, the valves met in the tongued closure, from the inner part, was a plate of fine steel soldered upon the metal, most-terse.

There were then, marvellously, two little Axles of a third (foot) in breadth, of best Indian Magnet (to which the Diamond does not dissent — of Callistone the lover, to human eyes outstanding, by the scordeon made mortal (weakened)) — singularly opportune to navigators — which showed, of their fit colour, cerulean, smooth and illustrious, fixed most-polishedly in the thickness of the opening of the marble wall (that is, in the jambs, contiguous to the antae of the artful door). Then, in this manner, by the violence of the rapacity of the Magnet, the steel plates were violated (drawn); and consequently, by themselves, the valves, with temperate slowness, unbolted themselves. An excellent and most-exact work — not only to see, but beyond measure of subtle device. What improbity (audacity) of an artificer's investigation!

In una tabella di Magnete dextrorso del ingresso inscalpto era, di exquisite litere latine antiquarie, quel celebre Virgiliano dicto. Trahit sua quenque voluptas. Nel levorso la tabella vidi di veterrime maiuscule graece elegante inscripto. Παν δει ποιειν κατα την αυτου φυσιν. In latino. A ciascuno fare gli convene secondo la sua natura.

Solevando daposcia gli ochii curiosamente stimulati alla magnificentia di tanto Tempio, et alla vastitate della spectanda et celificata cupula, cum l’altre exactissime parte, de ambitione, et de praestante artificio. De divo excogitato, et de superba operatura, et mirandi liniamenti, de stupenda ostentatione, condito mirabilissimo. Et molto più mirabile iudicai la incredibile bellecia della diva Nympha, la quale illiceva gli ochii mei inspectanti, et tutto l’animo mio teniva. In tanto che dalla recta disquisitione, de qualunque consideranda parte de esso dimovere valesse, et sola essa trahendo coarctasse, a contemplare cum stupore et miraviglia. Dà venia dunque lectore, si omni particula condecente non havesse perscripto.

Et cusì dunque la sacra Antistite intrando el Templo cum la ingenua et praestante Nympha, et io pertinace sequentila, et cum tutte le altre sacre Damicelle, cum le uberrime capillature per gli lactei colli ornatissime cadente, vestite di electissima purpura, et di sopra riportate le tenuissime Gosapine più breve, o veramente curte del primo indumento. Al fatale orificio della mysteriosa cisterna divote et festive ne condusseron.

Nella quale, como dicto fue, altra aqua non intrava. Se non quella che per gli terebrati Pillastri dagli aquarii et compluvii dal fastigato Templo liberamente, senza pernicie della structura, intro se infundevano. La summa sacerdotessa quivi alle virgine fece nuto, et andorono in uno Adyto sacrario, nui tre soli rimasti.

In a tablet of Magnet, to the right of the ingress, was carved, of exquisite antiquarian Latin letters, that celebrated Virgilian saying:

TRAHIT SUA QUEMQUE VOLUPTAS — "Each is drawn by his own pleasure." (Virgil, Eclogue II.65)

On the left, the tablet I saw, of most-ancient Greek majuscules, elegantly inscribed:

ΠΑΝ ΔΕΙ ΠΟΙΕΙΝ ΚΑΤΑ ΤΗΝ ΑΥΤΟΥ ΦΥΣΙΝ — in Latin, "Each ought to do according to his own nature."

Raising then the eyes, curiously stimulated, to the magnificence of so great a Temple, and to the vastness of the noteworthy and sky-reaching cupola, with the other most-exact parts — of ambition, and of outstanding artifice, of divine device, and of superb workmanship, and wondrous lineaments, of stupendous ostentation, seasoned most-marvellously. And much more marvellous I judged the incredible beauty of the divine Nymph, which enticed my inspecting eyes, and held all my soul — insomuch that from the right disquisition of whatever considerable part of it (the temple) I might move, and she alone, drawing, coarcted (me) to contemplate her with stupor and marvel. Give pardon, then, reader, if I have not perscribed every fitting particular.

And so, then, the sacred Priestess entering the Temple, with the ingenuous and outstanding Nymph, and I pertinacious following her, and with all the other sacred Damsels — with their most-rich hair falling, most- ornate, over the milky necks, dressed in most-choice purple, and above, worn back, the most-tenuous cotton (robes) shorter, or curt, than the first garment — to the fateful orifice of the mysterious cistern, devout and festive, they conducted us.

Into which, as was said, no other water entered save that which, through the perforated Pilasters, from the water-channels and compluvia of the fastigiate Temple, freely, without harm to the structure, poured itself in. The high priestess there made a nod to the virgins, and they went into an Adytum sacristy; we three alone remained.

Et ecco cum summa veneratione maturatamente, una portava cum registrato processo el rituale libro, de villuto debitamente investito, de seta Cyanea, di circulissime unione, in forma de una volante columbina nobilmente di tomentata ritramatura, cum ansulette d’oro. Insignite ciascuna de esse de Pancarpie nel venusto capo. Un’altra portovi due subtilissime suffubule leriate, et dui Tutuli purpurei. La tertia havea el sancto murie in uno vaso aureo. La quarta teniva el secespito cum oblongo manubrio eburneo, rotondo et solido, iuncto nel capulo cum argento et oro et chiovato di ramo Cyprio, et uno ancora praefericulo. La quinta era gerula de una iacynthina. Lepista oculissima di fontanale aqua piena. La sexta baiulava una aurea Mitra, cum richissimi Lennisci dependuli, per tutto ornata copiosamente de pretiose et fulgentissime gemme, tutte queste una sacerdotula cereoferaria praecedeva, cum uno Cereo nunque accenso, de candida, purgatissima, et virgine materia. Queste delicate virgine ad fare le cose sacre et divine edocte, et ad gli ministerii scrupulose, più che la Hetrusca disciplina perite, et ad gli sacrosanti sacrificii, cum prisco instituto apte et observantissime, alla pontificia Antistite, cum obstinata religione riverente, se appraesentorono.

And lo, with the utmost veneration, maturely, one carried, with registered procession, the ritual book, duly invested with velvet of Cyan silk, of most-round pearls-in-union, in the form of a flying little dove nobly of padded re-weaving, with little gold handles — each of them (the virgins) marked with Pancarpies (all-fruit garlands) on the comely head. Another brought two most-subtle little veils, lily-decked, and two purple Tutuli (fillet-caps). The third had the holy brine in a golden vase. The fourth held the secespita (sacrificial knife) with an oblong ivory handle, round and solid, joined at the pommel with silver and gold, and nailed with Cyprian copper; and, as well, a praefericulum (ritual jug). The fifth was bearer of a hyacinthine lepista (bowl), most-eyeing (beautiful), full of fountain water. The sixth bore a golden Mitre, with richest Lemnisci (ribbons) pendent, throughout ornated copiously with precious and most-refulgent gems. All these, a little priestess, candle-bearer, preceded, with a Candle never yet kindled, of candid, most-purged, and virgin material. These delicate virgins, taught to do the sacred and divine things, and scrupulous at the ministries — more expert than the Etruscan discipline — and, at the sacrosanct sacrifices, with ancient institute apt and most-observant, to the pontifical Priestess, with obstinate religion reverent, presented themselves.

Et quivi cum summa devotione, et cum vetusto rito, la saga vate in prima uno Tutulo acceptoe, et la caesarie stricta, poscia soprapose la superba Mitra, et nel medio dilla pretiosa Mitra, soprastrinxe la tenuissima suffubula, coprendo il sancto capo. L’altro Tutulo cum l’altra suffubula alla Nympha dede. Et ella sencia mora la bionda testa dil Tutulo ornatose, soprapose la suffubula, ambe due le suffubule nella crispulatura erano colligate, et coniuncte, cum uno stupendo gioiello, di nitidissimo et pieno di colore di Saphyro il consignato alla Nympha, ma quello dilla Antista di Ananchitides. Indute dunque divotamente sopra lo orificio dilla mysteriale Cisterna, sencia indugio quivi accostare fecime, diqué accepta una aurea clavicula, cum religiosa observantia il Puteale obturato riseroe. Ove la sacrificula ad quella Virgine, che portato havea la Mitra, il candido caereo consignando, il Rituale libro venerabonda tolse, et apertolo, se fece avanti la summa Mantice. La quale incominciò summisso in lingua Hetrusca alquanto di legiere. Poscia scrupulosamente il sanctificato Murie prese, et cum molte sacerdotale signature, cum la mano dextra nella tonante Cisterna il fundette. Poscia fece da l’ardente facola (dilla quale la Nympha era gerula) il puro caereo accendere. Facto questo, fece l’ardente facola rivoltare cum la flammula in giù dentro in medio dil orificio, et alla Nympha interrogando, dice tale parole. Figliola, che petitione et desio è il tuo? Rispose. Sancta Antistite, gratia per chostui io dimando, che inseme pervenire posciamo all’amoroso regno dilla Divina matre, et bevere di questo sancto fonte. Et ad me simigliantemente dixe. Et tu figliolo mio che chiedi? Humilmente resposi. Io sacratissima Madona, non solamente la efficace gratia dilla superna matre supplico, ma sopra tutto, che chostei, la quale ancipite existimo essere la mia desideratissima Polia, obsecro che da lei più ambiguo in tanto amoroso tormento non sia cusì ritenuto. La diva Antistite mi dixe. Apprehendi figlio dunque hora quella facola accensa dille sue pure mano, et tenentila cusì, meco tre fiate sinceramente per questo modo dirai. Così come l’aqua questa arsibile face extinguerà, per il modo medesimo, il foco d’amore il suo lapificato et gelido core reaccendi. Cum il sancto rito, et cum quelle proprie parole, che la Ierophantia mi dixe, dicendo, a qualunque terminato et finire, tutte le perite sacerdote virgine, cum venerabile ministrato, tale responsorio diceano. Cusì fia. All’ultima fiata, la ardente facola nella frigida Cisterna me fece cum reverentia immergere.

And there, with the utmost devotion, and with ancient rite, the sage seeress first accepted a Tutulus, and bound the hair; then set over it the superb Mitre; and in the middle of the precious Mitre, tightened above the most-tenuous little veil, covering the holy head.

The other Tutulus, with the other little veil, she gave to the Nymph. And she, without delay, having ornated the blonde head with the Tutulus, set over it the veil. Both the veils were bound in the crisping (of the hair), and conjoined, with a stupendous jewel — of most-shining and full-of-colour Sapphire, the one consigned to the Nymph; but that of the Priestess, of Ananchitides (a divination-stone).

Vested, then, devoutly, over the orifice of the mysterial Cistern, without delay she made me approach there; whence, having accepted a golden little key, with religious observance she unsealed the bolted Well. Where the sacrificer, consigning to that Virgin who had carried the Mitre the candid candle, took, venerating, the Ritual book; and, having opened it, put herself before the high Prophetess. Who began, low, in the Etruscan tongue, somewhat to read. Then, scrupulously, she took the sanctified Brine, and, with many sacerdotal signings, with the right hand into the thundering Cistern she poured it. Then she made, from the ardent torch (of which the Nymph was bearer), the pure candle be kindled.

This done, she made the ardent torch be turned, with the little flame downward, into the middle of the orifice; and, interrogating the Nymph, said these words: "Daughter, what petition and desire is thine?" She answered: "Holy Priestess, grace, for this one, I ask — that together we may come to the amorous realm of the Divine Mother, and drink of this holy fount." And to me likewise she said: "And thou, my son, what askest thou?" Humbly I answered: "I, most-sacred Lady, not only the efficacious grace of the supernal mother supplicate, but above all — that she, whom, doubtful, I esteem to be my most-desired Polia, I beseech that by her, more ambiguous, in so great an amorous torment I be not thus retained." The divine Priestess said to me: "Apprehend, son, then, now, that kindled torch, from her pure hands, and, holding it thus, with me three times sincerely, in this manner, thou shalt say:

'Just as the water shall extinguish this burnable torch, in the same manner, the fire of love re-kindle her
stone-made and gelid heart.'"

With the holy rite, and with those proper words which the Hierophant said to me, saying — at whatever terminated and finish, all the expert priestess-virgins, with venerable ministry, this responsory said: "So be it." At the last time, the ardent torch, into the frigid Cistern, she made me, with reverence, immerse.

He must quench the torch in the well saying "as the water quenches this fire, so re-kindle her frozen heart," the virgins answering "So be it." All secure; a fully-staged liturgy with dialogue.

Not so soon did I do this holy command, than she, taking the precious hyacinthine lepista, and with a little cord of gold and of crimson and green silk (deputed to such office), infusing it into the cisternal water, drew out of the blessed water, and, with religion, offered it to the Nymph alone. And she, with prompt devotion, drank it. Immediately then the hieratic Priestess, with the golden little key, diligently re-shut the covering of the Cistern; and, somewhat reading over the holy and efficacious prayers and exorcisms, she enjoined the Nymph to say, three times, these words toward me:

"May the divine Cytherea hear thee, to thy vow; and, propitiated in me, may she nourish her son."
Responsory from the virgins: "So be it."

The said ceremonies religiously terminated, the Nymph, in that instant, reverent, at the sandalled feet of purple re-woven with gold, ornated with manifold gems, of the Priestess, having prostrated herself — of a sudden she made her rise up, and gave her a holy kiss. And to me then the Nymph, boldly turned, with her comely presence placid, full of pious semblances, with a sigh come warmly from the base of the inflamed heart, thus said to me:

"Most-desired and my most-cordial Poliphilo — thy ardent and excessive desire, and thy sedulous and pertinacious love, from the chaste college has wholly stolen me, and has constrained me to extinguish my torch. And for this, henceforth — although thou reasonably didst suspect that I was she, notwithstanding that —

fino quivi non me habbi propalata, niente dimeno, non piccolo incendio mi è stato il tenirlo occulto et coelato et cusì diuturnamente soppresso, ma che si sia, io sum sencia dubio quella Polia tua, che tanto ami, congruo diciò si praesta, che tanto digno et cusì facto amore non immune sia di vicissitudine, et di aequivalente reciprocatione et ripenso. Per la quale cosa eccome ad tui inflammabondi optati tota paratissima, ecco che io mi sento lo ignito foco da ferventissimo amore per tota me succrescere, et scintillare. Eccome fine degli tui amari et soventi sospiri. Eccome dilectissimo Poliphilo salubre, et praesentaneo rimedio ad gli tui gravi et molestosi dolori. Eccome alle tue amorose et acerbe poene consorte praecipua et dil toto participe. Eccome cum le mie profuse lachryme a smorciare il tuo cordiale incendio, et per te morire prompta et deditissima. Et per Arra di tuto ciò togli (me stringendo amplexantime) mi dede collabellante uno morsicale et sorbiculoso basio, pieno di divino sucto, et provocate da singulare dolcecia dagli syderei ochii, alcune pluscule perle in forma di lachrymule, intanto che per il suo blandicello parlare, et per il salivoso et gratissimo savio, da capo a gli pedi tutto inflammato me alterai eliquantime in lachryme dulcissime et amorose; et dil toto perdutome. Et il simigliante la sacrificatrice Presule cum le altre astante, da praecipua dolcecia commote, continere non se poteron da lachrymule et dolci suspiruli.

— until here I had not revealed myself to thee — nonetheless, no small fire has it been to me, to keep it occult and concealed, and thus so long suppressed. But be that as it may, I am, without doubt, that Polia of thine whom thou so lovest. Congruous, therefore, it presents itself, that so worthy and so-made a love be not immune from vicissitude, and from equivalent reciprocation and requital. For which thing, behold me, to thy inflaming desires, all most-ready! Behold, that I feel the ignited fire, from most-fervent love, throughout all of me to grow up, and to scintillate! Behold me, the end of thy bitter and frequent sighs! Behold me, most-delightful Poliphilo, a salubrious and present remedy to thy grave and molesting sorrows! Behold me, to thy amorous and acerb pains a consort principal, and of the whole partaker! Behold me, with my profuse tears, to quench thy cordial burning, and, for thee, to die, prompt and most-surrendered!"

And, for the Pledge (arra) of all this — "Take (me)!" — (embracing me, straining me) she gave me, lip-to-lip, a little-biting and sipping kiss, full of divine juice; and, provoked by singular sweetness, from the starry eyes some few little pearls in the form of little tears (fell); insomuch that, by her blandishing speech, and by the salivous and most-pleasing kiss, from head to feet all inflamed I was altered, melting myself into sweetest and amorous tears, and wholly lost myself. And likewise the sacrificing Priestess, with the others standing by — moved by singular sweetness — could not contain themselves from little tears and sweet little sighs.

La quale chosa la infeconda et ieiuna lingua non saperebbe né radunare né tante accommodate parole mendicare, che io condignamente valesse uno pauculo exprimere, quello che ne facesse il succenso core in tanta dolcissima fiamma, quanta che in omni parte lo obtexe. Remansi dunque quale della Epilepsia lapso. Ultimamente questi amorosi et sancti gesti et cerimoniali riti, in tanta singulare et eximia dolcecia et incredibile dilecto da amore expediti, io me ritrovai quasi in una inopinata experientia di morire contento. La Hierophanta disse. Prosequiamo Polia dunche al completorio de gli penetrali sacraficii dil nostro sacrale incepto. Hora verso il rotondo et ciecho Sacello, di directo all’incontro dilla porta dil magnifico tempio situato et cum esso artificiosamente colligato et contiguo, tute compositamente andorono. Di antiquaria et insueta factura et nobile materia fabricato. Il quale tuto de pietra alla forma diligentemente riquadrata era di pretioso Phengite mirabilmente extructo. Cum uno cupulato et rotondo tecto, di uno simplice et solido saxo dilla dicta petra. Quale non fue di tale miraveglia il Sacello di l’insula Chennim di Aegypto. Né quello dil celebre Sacro Ravennate. La quale petra di tale miraculosa natura, che non essendo finestrata ma tuta obtusa, et solamente le valve d’oro havendo per tuto chiaramente era illuminato. (Dalla nostra cognitione secreto absorpto da essa parente) et peroe chusì è denominata. In questo mediato due di quelle virgine exhonerate, et per praecepto ite, portorono cum sincera veneratione, una, uno paro di bianchissimi Cygni mascoli grati negli auspitii. Et una veterrima Irnella cum aqua marina. Et l’altra uno paro di candide Turturine per gli piedi in uno vinculate cum seta Chermea, sopra uno viminaceo Cartallo di vermiglie rose et scorcie di ostrea pieno, et apresso le auree valve sopra una sacra et quadriculata Anclabri disposita, divote et venerante riposino. o ii

Which thing the infecund and fasting tongue would not know how to gather, nor so many fit words to beg, that I could condignly express even a little of what the enkindled heart made of it, in so sweetest a flame as, in every part, o'erwove it. I remained, then, like one fallen of the Epilepsy. Ultimately, these amorous and holy gestures and ceremonial rites — in so singular and choice a sweetness and incredible delight, by love, expedited — I found myself almost in an unthought experience of dying content.

The Hierophant said: "Let us proceed, Polia, then, to the completory of the inner sacrifices of our sacral undertaking." Now toward the round and blind Sacello — situated directly opposite the door of the magnificent temple, and with it artfully joined and contiguous — all, composedly, went. Fabricated of antiquarian and unwonted make, and noble material.

Which was all of stone, diligently re-squared to the form, of precious Phengite marvellously built, with a cupola'd and round roof, of a single and solid rock of the said stone. Such was not, of such marvel, the Sacello of the isle of Chennim in Egypt, nor that of the celebrated Sacred (place) of Ravenna. Which stone, of such miraculous nature, that — not being windowed, but all obtuse (closed), and having only the golden valves — throughout, clearly, it was illuminated (a secret absorbed by it, hidden from our cognition, from its parent (the sun)); and therefore thus is it denominated. In this interval, two of those virgins, unburdened, and by precept gone, carried, with sincere veneration: one, a pair of whitest male Swans, grateful in the auspices, and a most-ancient little urn with sea-water; and the other, a pair of candid Turtledoves, by the feet bound together with crimson silk, upon a wicker basket full of vermilion roses and oyster-shells — and, near the golden valves, upon a sacred and quadrangular anclabris (offering- table) disposed, devout and venerating, let them repose.

Et quivi le valve d’oro reserate, inseme introrono. Ma io me affermai sopra il sancto et riverendo limine. Et cum vigilanti ochii, nel amantissimo obiecto immobilemente infixi respectante, vidi la monitrice iubente, che la mia Polia vero myropolia se geniculasse sopra il sumptuoso pavimento, et cum sincera devotione coricarse. Il quale pavimento era mirabile tuto di gemme lapidoso, orbitamente composito, cum subtile factione, cum multiplice et elegante innodatione politamente distincto, opera ossiculatamente tasselata, disposita in virente foglie, et fiori, et avicule, et altri animali, secundo che opportuno era il grato colore delle pretiose petre splendido illucente, cum perfecto coaequamento, dalle quale geminato rimonstrava quelli che erano intrati. Sopra questo dunque la mia audacula Polia, denudati religiosamente gli lactei genui, cum summa elegantia genuflexe. Più belli che unque vedesse la Misericordia ad sé dedicati. Per la quale cosa isteti sospesamente attento cum gli silenti labri. Et per non volere gli sancti litamenti interrompere et le propitiatione contaminare, et interrumpere le solemne prece, et il mysterioso ministerio, et le arale cerimonie perturbare, gli improbi sospiri da valido amore infiammati debitamente incarcerai. Hora dinanti di una sanctificata Ara, nella mediana dil sacrulo operosamente situata, di divina fiamma lucente, geniculata humilmente se stava.

And there, the golden valves unsealed, together they entered. But I affirmed myself upon the holy and reverend threshold. And with vigilant eyes, immovably fixed on the most-beloved object, respecting, I saw the admonitress bidding that my Polia — true myropolis (perfume-seller/sweet one) — should genuflect upon the sumptuous pavement, and, with sincere devotion, prostrate herself.

Which pavement was marvellous, all of gems, stony, orbitally composed, with subtle make, with manifold and elegant knotting politely distinct — a work bonily tessellated, disposed in greening leaves, and flowers, and little birds, and other animals, according as opportune was the grateful colour of the precious stones, splendid, shining, with perfect evening — from which, doubled, it re-showed those who had entered.

Upon this, then, my little-bold Polia, having religiously denuded the milky knees, with the utmost elegance genuflected — more beautiful than ever Mercy saw (knees) dedicated to herself. For which thing I stood suspensefully attentive, with silent lips. And, so as not to interrupt the holy offerings, and contaminate the propitiations, and interrupt the solemn prayers, and the mysterious ministry, and perturb the altar- ceremonies, the wicked sighs, by valid love inflamed, I duly incarcerated.

Now, before a sanctified Altar — in the middle of the little sanctuary laboriously situated, of divine flame lucent — genuflected, humbly, she stood.

Confidence: medium. The inner rite: the priestess and Polia enter the Adytum (Poliphilo kept at the threshold, watching); the gem-mosaic pavement (leaves, flowers, birds and animals in coloured stones, so polished it mirrors those who enter); and Polia genuflecting, bare-kneed, before the central altar of "divine flame." La dimonstratione dilla quale Ara succinctamente dicendo, mirai uno conspicuo excogitato di insueta factura. Lo imo dilla dicta sopra il gradato et marmoreo pedamento era uno rotondo latastro. Sopra il quale undulava una foliatura auriculare, maxima cum politura laciniata, et eximiamente cauliculata finiendo il mucronato ad una cordicella, overo nextrulo, overo regulo, contento sopra il latastro. Et similmente nel nascimento dil ambiente foliamento, superassideva un’altra cordicella, et tra questa et un’altra era soppressa una troclea modificatamente alveata, et poscia una coronicetta. Sopra la mensula, overo piano dilla dicta Troclea, superastava un’altra rotonditate regulata, poscia alquanto gululata se contraheva verso la superficie plana et expedita. Nella parte mediana dilla quale promineva uno striato stilo, più porrecto nella inferiore parte sopra la piana cum proportionata crepidine. Diviso dunque il diametro dilla interna rotundatione di questo stilo. Partitione una era alla proiectura ambiente consignata. Il superno capo due portione havea cum tornatile gulule et lo imo ancora debitamente riservatose. Sopradicto stipite tegeva una inversa piana rotundata, tanto in proiecto gyrando, quanto l’extremo exito degli labri dilla subiecta Troclea Ornata nella superiore parte, nello extremo circuito in cliva dimonstratione cum una Sima di spectanda folliatura da una egregia coronetta perpollitamente nascendo. Nel circinato contento dunque dilla quale coronetta bellamente occupava la apertura di uno elegante fiore, in balaustico liniamento deformato, cum gli calicei labri sopra il piano lambenti, et quadripartito in periucundo foliamento acanthino fatiscente. Sotto il quale nella laciniata discrepantia subsideva un’altra foglia artificiosamente exscalpta. Sopra il cacuminato dil quale, doppo gli debiti liniamenti egregiamente ritondava uno nodo di exquisito expresso. Al quale infixa apposita dilatatamente promineva, una antiquaria platina d’oro purissimo, cum gli labri largiusculi, et paucolo lacunata. Nel piano orulo de gli labri alternatamente prominevano incomparabili Adamanti et Carbunculi cum praestante deformatione pyramidale, nella circuitione mirabilmente dispositi di incredibile crassitudine. Ceda quivi il Scypho dil fortissimo Hercule. Il Cantharo dil iucundo Baccho, et il Carchesio all’immortale Iove dicato. o iii

The demonstration of which Altar, succinctly saying, I beheld a conspicuous device of unwonted make. The lowest of the said, upon the stepped and marble footing, was a round little slab. Upon which undulated an auricular foliage, most with polish jagged, and choicely cauliculate, ending its point at a little cord, or knot, or fillet, contained upon the little slab. And likewise, at the birth of the encompassing foliage, sat above another little cord; and between this and another was pressed a trochlea (pulley-moulding) moderately channelled, and then a little cornice. Upon the bracket, or plane, of the said Trochlea, over- stood another regulated rotundity; then, somewhat gulleted, it contracted itself toward the flat and unencumbered surface. In the middle part of which stood forth a striated stylus, more stretched in the lower part, upon the flat, with a proportioned base. The diameter, then, of the inner rotundity of this stylus divided: one partition was consigned to the encompassing projection; the upper head had two portions, with turned gulets, and the lowest also duly reserved.

The aforesaid stalk covered an inverted flat rotundity, turning in projection as much as the extreme outlet of the lips of the subjacent Trochlea, ornated in the upper part, in the extreme circuit, in a sloped demonstration, with a Sima of noteworthy foliage, from an egregious little cornice most-polishedly being born. In the circled content, then, of which little cornice, beautifully occupied the opening of an elegant flower, in balustered lineament fashioned, with the calyxed lips over the flat lambent, and quadripartite into most-pleasant acanthine foliage, gaping. Under which, in the jagged discrepancy, subsided another leaf, artfully carved. Upon the point of which, after the due lineaments, egregiously rounded a knot of exquisite expression. To which, fixed, set, dilatedly stood forth an antiquarian platter of purest gold, with the lips broadish, and a little lacunate (hollowed). In the flat rim of the lips, alternately, stood forth incomparable Diamonds and Carbuncles, with outstanding pyramidal fashioning, in the circuit marvellously disposed, of incredible thickness. Let yield here the Scyphus of most-strong Hercules, the Cantharus of pleasant Bacchus, and the Carchesium dedicated to immortal Jove.

Notes

Confidence: medium. A dense architectural description of the central altar of the Adytum — a turned, moulded pedestal (slab, auricular foliage, pulley-moulding, cornice, fluted stem) rising to an inverted dish carved with acanthus and a balustered flower, crowned by a golden platter rimmed with pyramidal diamonds and carbuncles, surpassing the famous cups of Hercules, Bacchus and Jove. All secure; a goldsmith's-and-architect's altar, described moulding by moulding.

p. 283confidence: mediumverified

et lei cum questo alto signore questa continua praeda naviganti dividevano, et impartivano. Dunque questo solo extimai extrema dolcecia di dilecto. Al quale in praesentia la beata celebritate et triumpho me invitavano.

POLIPHILO NARRA CHE LE NYMPHE HAVENDO GLI REMI INFRENATI INCOMINCIORONO SUAVEMENTE DI CANTARE. ET POLIA CUM COMPARATIONE CANTANDO, MAGNA DOLCECIA D’AMORE PERSENTIVA.

CUM SUMMO FAUSTO SUPERBE ET MAgne pompe insolente triumpho, cum inopinabile laetitia et voluptico oblectamento, cum stimulanti dardi saevamente infixi nel cicatricoso core ad gli amorosi ochii di Polia et alla crebritate sagittale di Cupidine obvio et firmatissimo scopo cum ampliato core più fornaceamente ardendo, sempre più avido di augumentare l’ardore, per il solicito ministerio degli insaciabili et impacienti ochii, ad gli quali ragionevolmente io gli perdonava, considerando la causa, che cusì cupidi gli faceva, et in quella summamente applicati et sedulamente intenti. Quale il simulachro di Api, che al sole sempre si volve spectabondo, cusì né altramente gli ochii mei in essa directi, che era uno conspicuo et excitativo obvio da quel spectatissimo volto irradiante, al mondo di aequivalente bellecia proscripto, et per omni modo interdicto. Ma più noxii, et molto più iniqua et vexatrice caede gli furaci et vagi pensieri experiva di questo valoroso signore, optimi di tale materia solerti quaestori, et dil quaesto aptissimi artifici ad fabriculare et componere di foco et di fiamme sì dolce tormento, sì venerando idolo, sì formoso simulachro, sì praestante forma. Nella officina dilla imaginativa et solatiosamente fingere. O quanto, et molesto, et renuente al temperamento recusando di succumbere questi effreni, et publici sicarii dil mio riposo, et quiete se indicavano, et insatiabili et frementi dilla invisa bellecia dilla mia xanthothricha Polia, talvolta dolci, et talhora amari, alcuna volta laeti, et più dille fiate tristibili molte fiati optabili, et sepicule fugiendi se accusavano. Quale dunque validissime forcie hariano potuto gli incontinenti sensi incarcerare, che reluctanti discrepare, et discrepanti repugnare. Et repugnanti abigere, et abigendo respuere, s ii

— and she, with this high lord, this continual prey, navigating, divided and imparted. Therefore this alone I esteemed extreme sweetness of delight — to which, in presence, the blessed celebrity and triumph invited me.

(Chapter argument:)

POLIPHILO NARRATES THAT THE NYMPHS, HAVING RESTED THE OARS, BEGAN SWEETLY TO SING. AND POLIA,
SINGING IN COMPARISON (in answer), FELT A GREAT SWEETNESS OF LOVE.

With the utmost auspicious, superb and great pomp, insolent triumph, with unthinkable joy and voluptuous gratification — with stimulating darts savagely fixed in the scarred heart, at the amorous eyes of Polia, and at the arrow-frequency of Cupid, an opposing and most-firm target — with an amplified heart, more furnace-like burning, ever more avid to augment the ardour, through the solicitous ministry of the insatiable and impatient eyes (to which reasonably I pardoned it, considering the cause that made them so cupidinous, and supremely applied to it, and sedulously intent): like the simulacrum of Apis, which toward the sun ever turns, gazing — so, and not otherwise, were my eyes directed upon her, who was a conspicuous and exciting object, from that much-admired irradiating face, in the world proscribed of equivalent beauty, and by every means interdicted.

But more noxious, and much more iniquitous and vexing slaughter, did the furtive and wandering thoughts experience of this valorous lord — best of such matter the shrewd quaestors, and of the gain the most-apt artificers, to fabricate and compose, of fire and of flames, so sweet a torment, so venerable an idol, so shapely a simulacrum, so outstanding a form, in the workshop of the imaginative faculty, and solacingly to feign. Oh, how much — molesting, and refusing all temperament, declining to succumb — did these unbridled and public cut-throats of my repose and quiet show themselves, and insatiable and raging of the unseen beauty of my golden-haired Polia; sometimes sweet, and sometimes bitter, sometimes glad, and more often sad; many times desirable, and often to be fled, they accused themselves. What most-valid forces, then, could have imprisoned the incontinent senses — reluctant, to dissent; and dissenting, to repugn; and repugning, to drive off; and driving off, to spurn —

qualunque septo et inclusorio per quel amoeno prato diffusamente florigero dille singulare et eximie delicie di Polia (quale susurante ape) uberrimamente infessi racoglievano, tanta dolcecia, et suavissimo dilecto, per le oppresse viscere diffundentilo. Ove le amplissime fiamme serpente, sencia relaxatione insultanti, violentarii invadevano. Per la quale cosa digno non arbitrava essere, né conveniente, che l’amoroso, et carbonculato core, in queste tale opere vigorosamente exercitato, et sustinente di summoverlo, et per molestia debilitarlo. Ma più presto modestissimamente io doveva quello tollerante supportarlo.Il quale tanto voluntiera per mio affabile contento havea operosamente contracto. Hora nella fatale navarchia, sencia amplustre et temone naviganti nui protoploi, et sopra questo impraemiditato navigio, ove tuti gli mysterii d’amore spiravano. Il quale havea per la puppe la prora, et per la prora la puppe, cum il più digno et exquisito artificio, ad Cupidine dalla matre accommodato, che unque una apta et uberrimamente faconda lingua di rotondo eloquio, il sapesse exponendo exprimere, et exprimendo recollere, et distinctamente recollendo percontare. Nel mediostimo dilla quale, cioè nel istopode, era levata una aurea hasta cum triumphale et imperatoria vexillatione, di panno tenue sericeo, di infectura cyanea, nella quale di gemmule dilla coloratione opportune, cum candidissime margarite depolitamente erano picturariamente ritramati d’ambe le facie, cum multiplici foliamenti cum summa deornatione decorissima, tri hieroglyphi. Uno antiquario vasculo, ne lo hiato buccale dil quale ardeva una flammula. Et poscia era el mundo, inseme colligati cum uno ramusculo di vinco ad gli suavi reflati dil verifero et obsequente zephyro perflatile volabile, et eximie inconstante. Lo interpreto degli quali cusì io el feci. Amor vincit omnia. *

— whatever hedge and enclosure, throughout that pleasant meadow diffusely flower-bearing of the singular and choice delights of Polia (like a murmuring bee), most-richly, unwearied, they gathered — so much sweetness, and sweetest delight, diffusing it through the oppressed viscera. Where the amplest serpent- flames, without relaxation insulting, violently invaded. For which thing I did not judge it worthy, nor convenient, that the amorous and carbuncled heart, vigorously exercised in these such works, and sustaining them, should be moved to remove it, and by molestation to debilitate it. But rather, most- modestly, I ought, tolerating, to support it — which so willingly, for my affable content, it had laboriously contracted.

Now, in the fateful ship's-command, without sternpost and rudder navigating, we the first-sailors, and upon this unpremeditated vessel, where all the mysteries of love breathed — which had for its poop the prow, and for its prow the poop, with the most-worthy and exquisite artifice, accommodated to Cupid by his mother — than which never an apt and most-richly eloquent tongue of round oratory could, expounding, express, and expressing recollect, and distinctly recollecting recount.

In the very middle of which — that is, in the mast-step — was raised a golden spear, with a triumphal and imperatorial standard, of thin silken cloth, of cyan dye; in which, of little gems of the opportune colouring, with whitest pearls, were polishedly, picturewise, re-woven, on both faces, with manifold foliage, with the utmost most-comely adornment, three hieroglyphs: an antique little vase, in the mouth- gap of which burned a little flame; and then was the world; joined together with a little branch of osier, at the soft breaths of the spring-bearing and obsequious zephyr wind-blown, flutteringly volant, and choicely inconstant. The interpretation of which I made thus:

AMOR VINCIT OMNIA — "Love conquers all."

Il divino Nauclero io cum riverenti, et cum decenti risguardi volentilo volentiera, cum omni possibile conato speculare, il disproportionato obiecto, il mio debile intuito aconciamente non pativa. Ma le gene connivando, per questo modo alquanto il divino fanciullo pluripharia comprehendeva. Alcuna fiata mi appareva di gemino aspecto. Talhora di triplice, et ancora tal fiata se monstrava cum infinite effigie. Il quale cum Polia lo itinerario nostro facevano foelice, beato, et glorioso. Et per questa via lo amoroso, et proreta Cupidine ventilante le sacre penne, dille perpete ale, nelle quale Canens amante di Pico solaciavase, più che oro obrizo fulgevano di vario, et periucundo coloramine, sopra gli flucticuli in circulo rotante. Più bello et più gratissimo, che il crystalino trigonio columna di Euclide ad gli ochii aproximato dimonstra. Hora le nautice Nymphe deteron principio cum suavissima nota, et cum celica intonatione, da l’humana totalmente devariata, et ultra il credere cum ragione cantionica, di cantare et uno concento dolcissimo, cum voce consona et melodia teretigiare. In tanto che dritamente dubitai di excessiva dolcecia ischiantare, perché quasi dil suo loco dimoto sentiva il resultante et ferito core, et di dolcecia ad me parea ragionevolmente quello per gli mei labri exulare, et elle sequente cum vibrante lingule, nella sonora uvea rompevano crispulando geminando, et triplicando in una le brevissime cromaticule, overo accodate notule, et prima intercepto due a ddue. Poscia trine et trine. Poi ad quatro, ultimo tute sei, gli rosei labrunculi tremuli moderatamente aprendogli, et gratiosamente iungendoli, proferivano gli modulanti spiriti, cum emusicata proportione, cum voce mellea nel caldo core syncopata et stanche prolatione d’amore. Voce agli loci sospirante, suavissimamente gutturando, da fare in oblivione ponere il naturale bisognio et negligere, cum fidici instrumenti canticulavano le dolcece, et qualitate di amore, gli faceti furti dil superno Iove. Le solatiose caldecie dilla sanctissima Erothea, le lascivie dil festevole Baccho. Le foecunditate dilla alumna et flava Cerere. Gli saporosi fructi di Hymenaeo, cum versifico modo exprimendo et rithmiticamente proferendo, et melos emmetron. Per la quale cosa, cum la mente devia firmissimamente teniva tale non essere stato quel dulcisono che Euridice portata nelle volucre trige ad l’infere et s iii

The divine Steersman I, with reverent and decent regards, willingly willing him, with every possible effort to contemplate — the disproportionate object, my feeble sight fittingly did not suffer. But, conniving with my cheeks (half-closing my eyes), in this manner somewhat the divine boy I manifoldly comprehended. Sometimes he appeared to me of twin aspect; sometimes of triple; and yet, sometimes, he showed himself with infinite effigies. Who, with Polia, made our itinerary happy, blessed, and glorious. And by this way the amorous and prow-standing Cupid, fanning the sacred feathers of his perpetual wings — in which Canens, lover of Picus, solaced herself — shone more than refined gold, of various and most- pleasant colouring, rotating in a circle above the little waves. More beautiful and more pleasing than the crystalline triangular column of Euclid demonstrates, approximated to the eyes.

Now the nautical Nymphs gave beginning — with a most-sweet note, and with a heavenly intonation, from the human wholly deviated, and beyond belief, with reasoned song-craft — to sing, and to trill a most-sweet concord, with consonant voice and melody. Insomuch that rightly I doubted I should shatter from excessive sweetness, because almost from its place removed I felt the resulting and wounded heart, and of sweetness it seemed to me, reasonably, that it should exile itself through my lips. And they, following, with vibrating little tongues, in the sonorous palate broke, crisping, doubling, and tripling into one the briefest little chromatics, or tailed notelets — and, first intercepted, two by two; then three and three; then by four; last, all six — the rosy little lips, tremulous, moderately opening them, and graciously joining them, they proffered the modulating spirits, with mosaic proportion, with mellifluous voice, in the warm heart syncopated, and with weary utterance of love. A voice sighing to the places, most-sweetly throating — enough to put into oblivion the natural need, and to neglect it — with stringed instruments they sang the sweetnesses and qualities of love: the facetious thefts of supernal Jove, the solacing warmths of most-holy Erothea (Venus), the lasciviousnesses of festive Bacchus, the fecundities of the foster-mother and golden Ceres, the savoury fruits of Hymenaeus — with versifying manner expressing, and rhythmically proffering, melos emmetron (metred song).

For which thing, with a straying mind, most-firmly I held that such was not that sweet-sounding one which Eurydice, borne in the winged three-team to the infernal (regions), and —

opacissime sedie, dalle aeterne fiamme liberoe. Né ancora cum tale sono Hermete adormentoe lo oculato pastore. Quale per il purgatissimo aire spargentise fora dille pretiose et coralicee bucce spirava. Et per il candido iugulo traiectare vedevasi gli vocali spiriti, cum modulata suavitate diffundevasi. Imperò che quella era coeleste carne, et divo composito transparente, quale crystallina frigidissima, et reficiata camphora di chermeo tincta. Diqué ristato si sarebbe Phoebo di venire ad inrosare la lycophe aurora degli corruscanti radii, et di dipingere sarebbe dimenticato, et fare gli colori ad gli fiori, et di recentarse gratioso dì ad gli mortali. Et per questo sencia dubio la arcigera Diana, gli curvi archi et le volante sagitte, et le sedule venatione, et le dense silve oblite harebbe, et thermato il gelido fonte, et spreto non harebbe la praesentia del incauto venatore, et cornigero cervo, ad gli mordenti cani lacerabondo non l’harebbe convertito. Et il solatioso Baccho harebbe facto resistentia alle lubrice lascivie, et harebbe neglecto gli ogigii colli, Eleo, Naxo, Chio, et Masicho monte, et Mareotis, et harebbe parvifacto le mustulente delicie dil vindemioso Autumno. Et il nubitonante alite fora dilla aduncitate dille inverse ungue non harebbe sentito il rapto pocillatore Phrygio fugirsene, tanto suavissimamente le Nymphe cantavano et concordemente sonavano, et ciaschuna di loro cum la mia Polia cantillante, alle patule urechie coeleste melodie dispensavano. Per le quale sopito se sarebbe il nigro et multiforme et lucubrario Cerbero. Né excubiato harebbe cum immoti ochii le metallacie valve di Tenaro. Et allhora la furente Tesiphone, cum le monstrifere sorore alle misere alme s’haveriano exposte placidissime, et benigne, né unque Parthenope cum le sorore, Leucosia, et Ligia, filiole di Acheloo, et di Calliope, alle Capree insule apresso Peloro cantante, se udirono cum tanta harmonia, cum voce, modo, Lyra, et forabile tibie, d’onde l’alma incendiosamente infiammata dal suo loco summota per gli foelici canti, et soni, effigie bellece, comitato, et maiestate redimere non la valeva né farla sua. Ma strectissimamente ligata il stato suo in le delicate brace commendava, et nel albicante sino di Polia obside perpetuo et dedititia la obligava. La quale poscia cogitando, per delectabile semite et voluptici conducti perveniva alle archane delitie. Et d’indi cum tute mie excitate virtute in me ristrecto non poteva altro reasumere, se non una solacievole imaginativa, et gloriosa.

— from the most-shady seats, from the eternal flames freed (Eurydice). Nor yet, with such a sound, did Hermes lull to sleep the eyed shepherd (Argus). Such (a sound), through the most-purged air scattering itself, breathed out of the precious and coral little lips; and through the white throat one saw the vocal spirits passing, with modulated sweetness diffusing itself. For that was celestial flesh, and a divine composite transparent — like crystalline, most-frigid, and refined camphor, tinged with crimson.

Whence Phoebus would have stayed from coming to bedew the wolf-light dawn with his coruscating rays, and would have forgotten to paint, and to make the colours for the flowers, and to renew a gracious day for the mortals. And by this, without doubt, the bow-bearing Diana would have forgotten the curved bows and the flying arrows, and the sedulous huntings, and the dense woods; and, warming the gelid fount, she would not have spurned the presence of the incautious hunter, and would not have turned the horned stag, to the biting dogs lacerable (i.e. she would have spared Actaeon). And the all-wandering Selene would have withdrawn from illustrating with her splendour the supernal heavens and the shadowed earth. And the frightful Proserpina, in her mournful realm, would not have racked the doleful subjects, had a like tone reached her ears. And the solacing Bacchus would have made resistance to the slippery lasciviousnesses, and would have neglected the Ogygian hills, Elis, Naxos, Chios, and Mount Massicus, and Mareotis, and would have made little of the must-sweet delights of the vintaging Autumn. And the fostering Ceres would have kept the ears of corn ever in green, postponing the abundant realms of Ausonia, nor would have exchanged the fat and four-grained ears with Chaonia. And the cloud-thundering bird (Jove's eagle), out of the hooked curvature of its inverted talons, would not have felt the ravished Phrygian cup-bearer (Ganymede) flee away — so most-sweetly the Nymphs sang, and concordantly sounded, and each of them, with my Polia singing along, dispensed celestial melodies to the open ears.

By which the black and multiform and lucubratory Cerberus would have been lulled; nor would he have kept watch, with unmoving eyes, the metallic valves of Taenarum. And then the raging Tisiphone, with the monster-bearing sisters, to the wretched souls would have shown themselves most-placid and benign. Nor ever were Parthenope, with her sisters Leucosia and Ligeia — daughters of Achelous and of Calliope — singing at the Capri isles near Pelorus, heard with so much harmony, of voice, mode, Lyre, and pierceable tibia; whence the soul, incendiarily inflamed, removed from its place by the happy songs, and sounds, effigies, beauties, retinue, and majesty, I could not redeem, nor make it my own. But, most-tightly bound, it commended its estate into the delicate arms, and in the whitening bosom of Polia, a perpetual and surrendered hostage, obliged it. Which then, cogitating, by delectable paths and voluptuous conduits, arrived at the arcane delights. And thence, with all my excited virtues drawn into myself, I could resume nothing else but a solacing imaginative, and glorious.

Per la quale di inubere pensiculatione la anxia mente cum refocilatione d’animo pabulava, et cum curiosuli risguardi, et petulci appetiti. Le manifeste et perfecte formositate (et raro tale assueto di apparentia) di Polia avidissimamente mirava, omni altra cosa spectanda di tuitione deviabile renuendo. Ma singularmente allhora pergratioso erami il suo micante pecto picturato mirificamente di purpurante rose, et di lactei zigli, nel suo primo aprire, in la lachrymosa aurora sencia obstaculo ad gli ochii mei, placidissimo spectaculo, obvio palesemente conceduto, et sencia impedito medio, ma sufficientemente colorato, ad quello quam spectatissimo obiecto, che era il suo volto tanto illice et mirabilmente bello, legiadro, et perspicuo, che tale non appare cum l’ornato suo Hippe nel puro coelo, cum crinuli capreolati sopra, et la rosea fronte et piane tempore tremululanti, gli quali cum maximo decoramento per il niveo collo, et albicante spalle deflui. La lasciviente et verifera aura eximie reflava. Quanto unque l’altissimo Iupiter, imaginare se pote alla natura dil suo conferire, et di dovere benignamente producere et fabrefare, né mai Apelle harebbe potuto dipingere uno simigliante, et molto meno Aristide, che gli humani animi cum il suo penniculo fingeva. Dil quale intuito non poteva saturarme, né più né meno, che le susurante ape dil olente Thimo et Amello, et le petulce capelle dil florente Cythiso, et dille tenelle fronde non se saturano. Et cum libente animo, et cum incredibile piacere, io il mio amorosissimo core harei riserato, niente grave arbitrando, et tessellato, che ella d’indi prospecta la experientia indicio havesse ricevuto, quale sono le qualitate, che amando se tollera. Quale ad Caesare il stigmato Antipatro, et come l’alma mia fue praestamente dal suo visulo dolce, et insigne figura seducta, et in servitricia deditione redacta, non altrimente pervio facendo il pecto mio lacerando fenestrato, che la pientissima Pelicano Aegyptia, nella solitudine dil turbido et acephalo Nilo habitante, agli fremendi pulli di fame, cum pungente et crudele rostro si sfinde, et ischianta, exviscerando il pietoso et materno core. Il quale non a Dionyso, ma solum a essa perpetuo è dicatissimo excitabile, et diffundentise deflui in me gli insani et lernei amori, et gli focosi disii, et gli pensieri incitativi fingeva nel consentaneo core, componendo a consumarme, et me stesso strugiere, una ardente et peruribile fiamma, per me tuto concepta. Et cum magiore miraculo el lethale et mortifero telo innocuamente transfixo librava nel mio ferito core, che il telo pensile lethale in Epheso sencia laqueo nel tempio di Diana. L’alma perciò interdicta, a vivificarme pienamente non valeva. Dunque per sì facta cagione mortificato oltra mensura ardente, si non gli delectevoli risguardi sui me recentando recreavano, et gli amorosi nuti s iiii

By which, of over-rich pondering, the anxious mind, with a refreshment of spirit, fed itself; and with little curious regards, and lewd appetites, the manifest and perfect shapelinesses (and rarely such a habit of appearance) of Polia most-avidly I beheld, refusing every other thing worth watching, avertible of protection. But singularly, then, most-pleasing to me was her glittering breast, wondrously painted with purpling roses, and with milky lilies, in their first opening — in the tearful dawn, without obstacle to my eyes, a most-placid spectacle, openly conceded, and without an impeding medium, but sufficiently coloured — to that most-admired object which was her face, so enticing and marvellously beautiful, graceful, and perspicuous, that such does not appear, with its ornament Hippe, in the pure sky; with little tendril-curls above, and the rosy forehead and flat temples trembling, which, with the utmost adornment, down the snowy neck and whitening shoulders flowed. The wanton and spring-bearing breeze choicely re-blew them. As much as ever the most-high Jupiter can imagine, to confer on the nature of it, and benignly to produce and skilfully-make — nor ever could Apelles have painted a like one, and much less Aristides, who feigned human souls with his little brush.

Of which sight I could not sate myself — neither more nor less than the murmuring bees do not sate themselves of the odorous thyme and amellus, and the wanton she-goats of the flowering cytisus, and of the tender fronds. And with willing soul, and with incredible pleasure, I would have unlocked my most- amorous heart, judging it no way grievous — and tessellated (it) — that she, thence, having seen it, should have received by proof the token of what are the qualities that, loving, one endures. As to Caesar the stigmatised Antipater (showed his scars), and as my soul was promptly seduced by her sweet little face and notable figure, and reduced into servile surrender — not otherwise making pervious my breast, lacerating, fenestrated, than the most-pious Egyptian Pelican, dwelling in the solitude of the turbid and headless Nile, to its raging chicks, for hunger, with piercing and cruel beak splits and shatters itself, disembowelling the pitiful and maternal heart. Which, not to Dionysus, but only to her, perpetually is most-dedicated, excitable; and, diffusing itself, flowed in me the insane and Lernaean loves, and the fiery desires; and the incitative thoughts it feigned in the consenting heart, composing, to consume me, and to destroy my very self, an ardent and most-burning flame, by me wholly conceived. And with greater miracle the lethal and death-bearing dart, innocuously transfixed, poised itself in my wounded heart, than the pendent lethal dart in Ephesus, without a noose, in the temple of Diana. The soul therefore interdicted, to vivify me fully availed not.

Therefore, for such-made cause mortified, beyond measure ardent — if not her delectable regards, refreshing me, recreated me, and the amorous nods —

Tu alcuna fiata, signore mio, dilla bellissima Psyches te medesmo et cum le proprie crudele sagette vulnerasti, fina alla novissima linea di ardore. Quale gli mortali, essa extremamente amando, et ti piaque lei sopra tute puelle amare. Usa et exercita pertanto verso me pietate, et considera experto la fragile qualitate degli cupidi amanti, et tempera alquanto le tue adurente facole, et modifica l’arme tue nocevole, et il tuo lethifero archo ralenta, perché d’amore tuto me discrucio. Ma per tuto questo non era condignamente reconciliato il mio infocato core, né realmente satisfacto al discuncio appetito mio. — comforted me, and the low and sweet little words vivified me. She soliciting me affectionately, that I should give heed to the sweet songs of such egregious and divine singers, and to enjoy so many marvellous things with the senses — interrupting the fixed regard upon her, and all my cordial intent. Beyond all that one can opine, more pleasing was my most-beautiful Polia to my fervescent burnings, than, by chance, so pleasing and expected would not have presented themselves the rapid waves of Xanthus and of Simois to the Ilian flames (that burned Troy). Nor so pleasing showed itself the honoured gift of the head of the bristly Boar, from Meleager to Atalanta. Nor to the beloved Alcmena the fair gift from the benign Jove. Nor so pleasing and opportune offered itself to Hannibal, in the waters, the elephant — as Polia, most- pleasing to my every delight and content.

Constant, then, to the begun work, between sweetest voluptuousness and odious delay, I persevered — neither more nor less than the ponderous gold persists to the utmost cement, and to the most-subtle liquors. I turned me then to the divine boy. "O flame-bearing Cupid," murmuring I said, "you, some time, my lord, with the most-beautiful Psyche, wounded your very self, and with your own cruel arrows, up to the newest line of ardour — as the mortals — she supremely loving, and it pleased you to love her above all girls. And much it grieved you, the guileful counsel of the envious and fallacious sisters, and upon the cloudy cypress against her, with long-lasting lament racked, you made irate, lamentable complaints, upbraiding her. Use and exercise, therefore, toward me, pity; and consider, expert, the fragile quality of the cupidinous lovers; and temper somewhat your burning torches, and modify your noxious arms, and slacken your death- bringing bow — for of love I am all racked.

"For which I reasonably argue: that if, in your very self, savage and pitiless, you wounded yourself — what equability of soul persuades that I should not fear that, without pity, toward me, more harsh and unbridled and most-savage, you would not show yourself?" And so, exasperated, concitatedly I dared; and with diverse petitions, and prayers, and fabricated complaints, and feigned satisfactions, I somewhat softened the strong invasion, and the frequent impulse of the wicked and eyeless love. But for all this my enkindled heart was not condignly reconciled, nor really satisfied to my disjointed appetite. And that which then I prayed him: only that he put an end, at least, to my tormenting and long-lasting hoping, with the molesting expectation of a prison-like issue.

For, however much the coveted future be much more sweet of avidity than the acquired past delight, yet every hostile love contends toward the expected end. Therefore, abridge, with sudden abolition, the timely hope. My lord, this ungrateful and displeasing deferring — more than to the pure eyes the smoky Cloud, and to the teeth the stupefying sourness, and than the —

— being slow with help to the coveter — because to a concupiscent soul it is a grave torment, the odious day-postponing, and the prolonging of the desired end. Then returning into myself, strongly marvelling. For I could not perfectly know where so much prepared and durable material (like unconsumable Etna) he found, and a fire-place copious of so much fire, within my nursling and burnable heart. At the end, only to guard vigilantly the choice and most-noble adorned, composed, and ornate object, and to exhaust, with my hidden ears, the sweetest consonances with heavenly intonation — from which, receiving ineffable diversion, I tasted, to the senses, a quickening and extreme delight.

Now, in this inexperienced manner, over the placid and levelled little waves of the un-furrowed deep, our speeding six-oar ran like a light water-skater; and the most-comely rowers, most-festively jubilating, sang in the Iasian mode; and the divine Polia, again, without the others, alone — from those minimally dissonant, but comparable — Lydian-wise sang. Not the plaints of raging Tragedy, nor in the cackling Satire, nor in the deceitful Comedy, nor in the tearful Elegies; but with a most-adorned poem, and with elegant little words, the supreme sweetnesses of the holy and fostering Erycina (Venus) they composedly proffered, and the delectable fallacies of the assisting son (Cupid) they eloquently sang. And Polia, affable and comely, of cultivated adornment, polished and elegant, congratulating — the thanksgivings of the obtained graces (provoking to admiration) she sang, with such vehemence and sweet-sound as, of blind Demodocus, at the hearing of the wise Ulysses, with wailing cithara, did not attain. Which most-pleasing companion — feeling herself no less delighted, partaker — speaking jocosely, and blandishing, tempting me: how did the instant things appear to me? Naming to me, of whatever rowing Nymph, the proper name; and with sweet persuasion she affirmed to me that perseverance alone wears the victor's diadem. And in this unbridled avidity wholly slipped down, and most-cast-forth, most-pleasantly we navigated, and prosperous we arrived at the delicious island Cythera.

Chapter XXI
