PERVENUTI LAETISSIMI ALLO OPTATISSIMO LOCO, LA DIGNA AMENITATE DIL QUALE ASSEVERA POLIPHILO DI PIANTE, HERBE, ET AVICULE, ET INQUILINI OPPORTUNAMENTE DISCRIVENDO. MA INPRIMA LA FORMA DILLA NAVICULA, ET COME NEL DESCENDERE DIL SIGNORE CUPIDINE DI RINCONTRO HONORABONDE MOLTE NYMPHE DOROPHORE MATURAMENTE SE APRESENTORONO.

VELIFICANTE IL DIVINO PUERULO CUM le dispanse ale, non dal utre di Ulysse, ma da obsequiose, et rorifere aure filiole di Astreo, et dilla rosea Aurora impulse, di consenso unanimi, Polia, et io ritrovantise, accensi et praecipitatissimi di aviditate di pervenire al destinato termine, cum il maiore dilecto di amore, che unque humano senso il potesse sentire, né praecogitare, et meno divulgare. Il quale quantunque negli praecordii intimamente excessivo fusse. Niente dimeno più promptamente era dalla praesentia deifica vegetato, et da quelle facetissime Nymphe remigabonde, et dal dolcissimo cantare, et dalla mysteriosa forma dilla solida et inconcussa navicula, opportuno instrumento organizato d’amore, et dalla pretiosa materia et dalla dolceza et amoenitate dil loco. Et molto più dalla propinqua fiamma, che Polia cum eximia praestantia exuberantemente nel cremabile core spirava. Perché gli amorosi et praefulgentissimi ochii sui, per gli mei ad gli intimi praecordii fulguraticiamente delapsi, uno acerrimo incendio sediciosamente commovevano. Dal quale ustibile confervefacto, et prostratamente saucio, crebri singulti scaturivano. Non altramente che il coculo sopra l’ardente et excessivo foco per gli labii fora diffunde. Cusì né più né meno gli bullienti anheliti dal fervente core compulsi habondantemente subullivano, manifestantise risonanti, et gli importuni incendii cum la sola bellecia dilla mia venusta Polia ductrice aptamente mitigava. Ma che si fusse io uberrimamente experiva tanta voluptate, che io era totalmente absorpto et absumpto, quanto mai la lingua mia decentissima valesse adaptare ad tale expresso. Finalmente laetissimi, gaudiosi et triumphanti all’insula extremamente desiderata, cum la nostra superba et remivaga exeres, non saburata, ma vacille. La quale di forma cusì era compacta. Di partitione quadripharia due erano consumate in la puppe, et nella

Having come most joyfully to the longed-for place, whose worthy loveliness Poliphilo affirms, fittingly describing its plants, herbs, birds and dwellers. But first the form of the little boat, and how, at the descent of the lord Cupid, many gift-bearing nymphs opportunely presented themselves to meet him.

The divine boy making sail with his spread wings — not from the wineskin of Ulysses, but urged by the obsequious and dew-bearing breezes, daughters of Astraeus and of rosy Aurora, of one unanimous consent — Polia and I, finding ourselves kindled and headlong with eagerness to reach the destined end, with the greatest delight of love that ever human sense could feel, nor foreconceive, nor much less divulge. Which, however excessive it was intimately in the heart, was nonetheless the more readily quickened by the deific presence, and by those most witty rowing nymphs, and by the sweetest singing, and by the mysterious form of the solid and unshaken little boat — a fit instrument organised of love — and by the precious material, and by the sweetness and pleasantness of the place; and much more by the near flame that Polia, with rare excellence, exuberantly breathed into my burnable heart. For her amorous and most refulgent eyes, gliding lightning-like through mine to my inmost heart, seditiously stirred up a most bitter conflagration; by which, heated through and prostrately wounded, frequent sobs gushed forth — not otherwise than the cook pours out over the burning and excessive fire through the lips. Just so, neither more nor less, the boiling gasps compelled from my fervent heart abundantly boiled up, showing themselves resounding; and the importunate fires I fittingly mitigated with the sole beauty of my lovely Polia, my guide. But how it was, I most abundantly experienced such pleasure that I was wholly absorbed and consumed — more than ever my most decent tongue could adapt itself to such expression.

Finally, most joyful, glad and triumphant, to the island extremely desired, with our proud and oar-wandering ship of six banks — not ballasted, but light — which was compacted of a form thus.

Of a fourfold partition, two were used up in the poop and in the prow, thus alike in lineament or form, the one as the other. The two other partitions were dispensed in the remaining hollow body; and —

Le due altre partitione nel residuo inane corpo erano dispensate, et una et l’altra sponda tra la prora et la puppe initiante cum pando acclivo discorrevano gli oruli. Il quale lapso proclinava quadrante, et d’indi paulatine aequale il residuo se prolongava ad l’altro obvio. Le quale prone falcature alte constano da gli constrati bipedale. Et in una et l’altra gli tre transtri trasversariamente erano infixi, hesquipedale dal constrato sublevati. La carina poscia di lame d’oro intecta, et cusì le pande coste, da uno et l’altro extremo ventriculatamente sublevantise. Nel ultimo gracilamento dicto delphino alla deformata similitudine circumacto se aduncava, reddendo uno grato voluto. Nella rotundatione dil quale fulgeva uno ornamento di crasse et pretiosissime gemme. Dalla volutione dil quale, verso la piana dilla puppe et dilla prora, paulatinamente se incaulicava obvertentise in uno foliamento antiquario, amplificato su la piana cum naturale deformatione et exquisita di optimo oro sparsamente serpendo, cum exacti cauliculi, et colphuli lacinii, overo incisure, et fimbriature sopra le piane lambente elegantissimamente expresso. Dal quale volume similmente procedeva bellissimamente dal fastigio mucronato dilla piana descendendo sectario, cum la deformatione de gli oruli, overamente trapheco, overo labio, ove infixi erano gli scalmi, uno mirando frisio di latitudine uno palmo tuto d’oro gemmato d’incredibile impensa egregiamente cingeva cum mirifica, et amicale distributione, et locatione dille gemme, et tuto il ligneo composito tanto diligentemente, et cum eximia politura cum le tabule coassate loricatamente sencia arte stiparia redacto, et sencia alcuno calcamine, apparendo una coaequata compactura, quasi di uno integerrimo solido. Et sopra il linito dilla fragrante et nigerrima picatura nitente et speculare, et di aurea trituratione, di syriaco liniamento per tuto spectatissimamente designata cum tute le altre circunstantie di sopra opportunamente descripte. Cusì era.

— one gunwale and the other, between prow and poop, began and ran with a bent slope. This falling-away sloped a quarter, and thence little by little, level, the rest lengthened out to the opposite end. These bent curvatures stood two feet high from the decking. And in one and the other the three thwarts were fixed transversely, raised a foot and a half from the decking. The keel then was clothed with plates of gold, and so the bent ribs, from one end and the other rising belly-wise. At the last tapering — called the dolphin — bent round in its shaped likeness, it hooked, rendering a graceful volute; in the rounding of which shone an ornament of thick and most precious gems. From the winding of which, toward the flat of the poop and of the prow, little by little it stemmed into a stalk, turning into an antique foliage, amplified upon the flat with a natural and exquisite shaping of the finest gold, sparsely serpentining, with exact little stalks and tendril-lacings, or incisions, and fringings, most elegantly expressed lapping over the flats. From which scroll likewise there proceeded most beautifully, descending inlaid from the pointed summit of the flat, following the shaping of the gunwales — that is, the rail, or lip, where the tholes were fixed — a marvellous frieze, a palm's breadth wide, all of gold and gemmed, of incredible cost, egregiously girding it with a marvellous and friendly distribution and placing of the gems; and all the wooden composite so diligently, and with exceptional polish, with the planks joined plated fashion without the caulker's art, and without any wedging, appearing an even-matched joinery, as of one whole solid. And over the coating of the fragrant and blackest pitch, gleaming and mirror-like, and with a gold trituration, throughout most admirably designed in a Syrian lineament, with all the other circumstances above fittingly described. Thus it was.

Notes

Confidence: medium. This is one of the book's technical set-pieces — the anatomy of Cupid's hexareme — and it is the hardest kind of Colonna to render: a specialist vocabulary (some of it his own coinage) applied to a boat that is half ship and half goldsmith's work. The sense of every clause is recoverable, but a naval historian could refine several terms.

  • The "dolphin" stern-ornament — the aplustre, the curled stern-post of an
  • "without the caulker's art, and without any wedging" — the planks fit so
  • exeres — a hexareme, a galley with six files of oarsmen; kept as "ship of
  • Terms rendered by their standard Vitruvian/nautical equivalents where one exists

Cum questi amorosi triumphi, et euphonice voce dille nautice fanciulle, et oltra dagli marini numini completo era il sereno aere, et il ceruleo pelago di confusi clamori, et multitudine resultante le unde, et feste, et tripudii, et praecipua gesticulatione et festiva iucunditate, et divote veneratione al perameno loco allabenti applicassimo, tanto benigno et quam gratissimo, tanto delectevole et bello, di singulare ornamento arborario agli sensi se offerite, quanto mai cosa excellentissima et voluptuosa cum gli ochii mirare se potesse. Imperò che ciascuna fertile lingua di caritate et parca accusarebese. Et per tale similitudine troppo disconvenevole et abusiva comparatione sareberon gli anticommemorati ad questo. Imperoché sencia existima era quam solatiosissimo, et di delicie loco tuto consito et exornato horto olitorio, et herbario, et fertile pomario, et amoeno viridario, et gratioso arborario, et periucundo arbustario. Il quale era loco non di monti devii, et desueti, eliminata omni scabricie. Ma complanato et aequabile fina ad gli gyrati gradi verso il mirabile theatro exclusivo, gli arbori erano di odore suavissimo, di provento foecondissimi di expansione di rami latissimi. Horto di oblectamento incomparabile affluente, di largissima ubertate, di fiori iucundissimo, et referto, libero de impedimenti, et di insidie tuto ornato, di manali fonti, et freschi rivuli. Il cielo non rigido, ma temprato latissimo, perspicuo, et illustre, non di horrente umbre lochi averni, immune dil variabile, et incostante tempo, che cum insidie lacescente di venti infecti offendesse, non cum moleste et hybernale pruine. Né aestuatione di importuno sole, né invaso et torrido loco di aritudine di aesto. Né di gelatione horrida excocto. Ma tuto vernante et salubre che tale non è l’aere ad gli Aegyptii la Libya spectanti, di lunga salute, et salubritate, et destinato di aeternitate. Loco consito di vireti, di spectanda densitate di frondosi arbori, di gratissima ostentatione, cum venustissima praesentatione di virentia, et per tuto il liquido aere incredibile spiramento di florea ridolentia, cum tuta la area herbescente, et di frescho roramine perfusa, et floridanti prati, et oltra il pensitare di omni piacere faetoso et naturali beni, cum colorati fructi, tra il perenne foliamine virente, cum stabile cohaesione et consenso, cum itione diffinite per le piante, et di multiplici rose arcuatamente contecte, ceda quivi dunque lo irriguo et arborato Thermiscyra campo. Per la quale cosa più che arduo iudico, et difficile arbitro il volere, (et cum acre ingegnio) narrare. Ma, quanto la rapace retinente, et arida memoria nella lauda collocata mi sovenirae, tanto io brevemente me adapterò ad scrivere. Questo sancto loco alla faceta (ad gli mortali et miserabunda) natura dicato alumno degli dii et statione, et degli beati spiriti diversorio, circuiva (come rectamente coniectare valeva) tre miliarii, et da qualunche parte interfluxo di salse

With these amorous triumphs, and the euphonious voices of the sailor-girls, and beyond by the sea-deities, the serene air was filled, and the cerulean deep, with confused clamours, and a multitude resounding — the waves, and feasts, and dances, and especial gesticulation and festive gladness, and devout veneration — as, gliding to the most pleasant place, we put in. So kindly and most gratifying, so delightful and beautiful, offering itself to the senses with singular tree-set ornament, as ever the most excellent and voluptuous thing could be beheld with the eyes. For every fertile tongue would accuse itself of poverty of expression; and by such a likeness the things commemorated before would be too unbecoming and abusive a comparison to this. For beyond estimation it was a most solacing place, and of delights — all planted and adorned as a kitchen-garden and herb-garden, and a fertile orchard, and a pleasant pleasure-ground, and a gracious tree-plot, and a most delightful shrubbery. Which was a place not of trackless and disused mountains, every roughness done away, but flattened and even right up to the circling steps toward the marvellous theatre at its heart. The trees were of most sweet odour, most fertile of yield, of the widest spread of branches. A garden of incomparable delight, affluent, of the largest fruitfulness, of most delightful flowers, and stored, free of impediments, and of ambushes all adorned, of flowing fountains and fresh rivulets. The sky not rigid, but temperate, most wide, clear and bright; not of the horrid shades of infernal places; immune from variable and inconstant weather that might offend with the ambushes of tainted winds harassing, nor with troublesome and wintry frosts; nor the swelter of an importunate sun, nor an invaded and torrid place of the dryness of heat; nor scorched with horrid freezing. But all springlike and salubrious — such as the air is not to the Egyptians who look to Libya — of long health and salubrity, and destined for eternity. A place planted with green things, of admirable density of leafy trees, of most gratifying show, with most lovely presentation of greenery; and everywhere through the liquid air an incredible breathing of flowery fragrance, with all the grassy ground steeped in fresh dew, and flowering meadows; and beyond the imagining of every pleasure, teeming with natural goods — with coloured fruits among the perennial green foliage, in stable cohesion and consent, with paths marked out among the plants, and arched over with manifold roses. Let the watered and tree-set field of Themiscyra yield place here.

Wherefore I judge it more than arduous, and reckon it difficult, to wish — even with keen wit — to narrate it. But as much as my grasping, retentive, yet arid memory, set upon its praise, shall recall to me, so much briefly I will adapt myself to write.

This holy place, dedicated to nature — witty to mortals, and pitiable — nursling of the gods and their station, and lodging of the blessed spirits, encircled (as I could rightly conjecture) three miliaria; and on every side an interflowing of salt —

aque lympidissime. Il quale non era congestitio di tophei scopuli, dalla contumacia dille proturgente et spumicolose onde derosi, pieni di crepidine, quale fragose Plote. Né di vadosi litori praefresi, et exesi da undiculante iniuria né da vesco sale. Né etiam non era composito dilla superba Niobe scrupea, né gli sui acutissimi et durissimi filioli quivi appariscono. Ma tuto mineralmente di nitidissima materia, non fractitia, né freabile, né lutescente, ma translucida, integra, et intemerata, quale perspicace, et artificioso crystallo. Ove cum acurata diligentia explorava da benigna illuvie gli extersi litori lapillosi di sparse gemme, di forma et dil suo coloramento divariate praelucente. Quivi dispersamente ancora habundantia appareva dil fragrante coito dille monstrose Balene, da gli frugi plemmyruli riportato. Ornatissima insula poscia di gratissimo et novello et perhenne operimento di verneo virore per tuto il piano spectatissimo. Ma prima sopra le nude rive litorale attiguo mirai gli aequevi, et procerosi cupressi cum gli sui stiptici et rimosi coni, perseveranti, et durabili ad gli ponderosi tecti. Tigni non saporosi agli rosicanti teredini, et lo intercupressio di passi tre, l’uno separato dal altro. Questo regulare ordine, in orbe gyrato circunducto, era observato per tuto l’extremo circinao de l’insula. Poscia circularmente ambiva uno iucundissimo et floreo myrteto. Amante gli loquacibondi litori. Il quale alla divina genitrice de gli amorosi fochi consta votivo et dicato. Compacto, et densissimamente riducto et deformato in modo di murale septo, uno hesquipasso altiusculo includendo in sé gli troncei stirpi delli dritissimi cupressi, cum exordio dilla sua foliatione subrecto dui piedi dal summo aequato, overo piana dil myrteto. Dunque questa cusì facta viridura obvallava le litorale ripe, cum le opportune itione, agli lochi decenti relicte et distribute. Il quale septo minimo ligno accusava, ma protecti dilla dilectabile et florusa frondatione, che una cima né follio l’altro excedeva, ma cum eximia aequatura derasa conservava la summitate, et la circinatione. Intro da questo circumvallato myrteo, et virente sepe (il quale essere poteva da esso verso il centro dill’insula circa uno semitertio di miliario) vidi per deductione de linee dal centro alla circunferentia litorea, in aequipartitione XX. ciascuna d’imensitate di uno stadio, et adiecta una quinta parte. La extrema clausura dil myrto imitando. In qualunche divisione era uno nemorulo di diversi prati variamente herbanti, et di arborario il simigliante. Distributo specificamente secondo il requisito aspecto dil benigno cielo. Caeda quivi Dodona silva. Le quale divisione, nella figura decangula, opportunamente interponendo per ciascuno intervallo una linea, in vinti multiplica. La quale figura facta in simplice circulo, et sectione facendo mutuamente dui diametri, davano, et il centrico puncto. Uno semidiametro di questi

— most limpid waters. Which was not heaped of tufa crags, gnawed by the obstinacy of the surging and foaming waves, full of cracks like rough Plotae; nor of shallow shores broken and eaten by the wave's harassing injury, nor by the clinging salt; nor was it composed of proud Niobe's rock, nor do her most sharp and hard children appear there. But all minerally of the most polished material — not brittle, nor friable, nor turning to mud, but translucent, whole and undefiled, like keen and artificial crystal.

Where with careful diligence I explored the little pebbly shores wiped clean by a kindly wash, of scattered gems of varied form and colouring, shining. There too, dispersedly, appeared an abundance of the fragrant issue of the monstrous whales, carried in by the fruitful tides. A most adorned island, then, of most gratifying and new and perennial covering of springlike greenery, admirable everywhere over the flat.

But first, upon the bare shore-banks close by, I beheld the coeval and tall cypresses with their close-set and cracked cones, persevering, and durable for weighty roofs — timbers not savoury to the gnawing woodworm — and the spacing between cypresses of three paces, one separated from the other. This regular order, turned round in a ring, was observed throughout the whole outer circle of the island. Then, in a circle, there went round a most delightful and flowery myrtle-grove, loving the talkative shores; which stands votive and dedicated to the divine mother of the amorous fires. Compacted and most densely reduced and shaped in the manner of a walled fence, a pace and a half tall, enclosing within it the trunk-stocks of the straightest cypresses, the beginning of its foliage rising two feet from the levelled top, or flat, of the myrtle-grove. Thus this greenery, so made, walled about the shore-banks, with fitting passages left and distributed at the seemly places. Which fence showed the least wood, but, protected by the delightful and flowery foliage — no top exceeding another, no leaf — kept its summit and its circling shorn with exceptional evenness.

Within this walled-about myrtle and green hedge (which might be, from it toward the centre of the island, about a semitertio of a mile), I saw, by the drawing of lines from the centre to the shore-circumference, in equal partition twenty, each of the immensity of a stadium, with a fifth part added — imitating the outer enclosure of the myrtle. In each division was a little grove of diverse meadows variously grassed, and of tree-plot the like; distributed specifically according to the required aspect of the kindly sky. Let Dodona's wood yield here. Which divisions, in a ten-angled figure, fittingly interposing a line for each interval, multiply into twenty. Which figure, made in a simple circle, and making section mutually of two diameters, gave also the central point. One half-diameter of these —

Et a questo puncto obliquamente trahe una linea recta, verso la summitate suprema dil semidiametro, et a questo supremo puncto, supra questa praefata linea, dal semidiametro signa quanto è una quarta parte di tuto uno diametro. Poscia extendi una linea dal centro secando sopra la signatura alla circunferentia, sarà la divisione dilla figura decangula. Queste .XX. divisione erano per nobilissimi septi, diversamente cancellati cum opportune et conveniente laxatione marmorarie, di crassitudine bipollicaria tra mensurata locatione di pilastrelli perpolitule fabrefacti, di marmoro albente, et il reliquo rubicante luculentissimo, cumvestiti di varia voluptura di serpibile piante, una da l’altra excluse et separate. Nel medio dil septo patevano ad libella in ciascuno una porta, in apertura pedi septeni, alta nove fino al suo arcuare dil supremo convexo. Le quale cancellature et tale rumbee, et quadrule, et tale degenerate dal tetrangulo, et per altri bellissimi expressi. Serpivano quelle tale il periclymeno, altre iossamino alcune di convolvoli, tale di lupuli, et alcune di tanno, overo vite nigra altre di convolvolo, cum le campanule liliacee semiazurine, tale di tuto candido, alcune di momordica, diqué ciascuno era variato. Quale di flammula Iovis, di Smilace, la quale per amor dil formoso Croco, se fece Autophoros ornata di candido flore olente lilio cum sentoso folio et hederaceo, di vitilago, di viticula, cum in triquetro visicaria, cum il seme di albedine maculate, et di molti altri, che in sublime serpeno de la nominatione incogniti. In la prima diloricatione il nemore era daphnona, overo laureto, di multiplice lauri, quivi vidi la Delphica, la Cypria, la Mustace, cum maximo et albicante folio, et la silvestra, Cino, et la Regia, overo Bacchalia, la Taxa, la Spadonica, et Chamaedaphne, quale mai in monte Parnaso spectatissima, et Apolline gratissima. Nobilissimo munere ad gli Romani misso. Né tale la laurifera terra basoe Bruto, gratissima a Tiberio. Né tale vide Drusilla portata dall’albicante Galina. Né cusì facte naqueron dall’aurispico iuso nella villa degli Caesari plantate, ornamento triumphale, praecipuamente la Sterile. Vidi ancora et Daphnoide, overamente Pelasgo, overo Eutale ridolente Thure, né di tanta bella virentia perpetua fece la filiola di Peneo fiume. Dille foglie dilla quale Apolline solito fue, et la cithara, et la pharetra exornare. Ceda quivi dunque gli siculi monti aerii, et quanto ad gli dulcissimi fonti, et quanto ad la amenitate. Quantunque il formosissimo figliolo di Mercurio in quello cum Diana se oblectasse, gli quali non sono prescripti dalla ira dill’altissimo Iove, offerentise cusì grati a coprire la calva di Caesaro, cum peramenissimo solo immixti molti Comari.

— which you will, divide by an equal half, with a prick. And from this point draw obliquely a straight line, toward the topmost summit of the half-diameter; and at this topmost point, upon this aforesaid line, mark off from the half-diameter as much as is a quarter part of a whole diameter. Then extend a line from the centre, cutting over the mark to the circumference: this will be the division of the ten-angled figure.

These twenty divisions were, by most noble fences, diversely latticed with fitting and convenient marble openwork, two inches thick, between the measured placing of most polished little pilasters, of whitening marble, and the rest most lustrously reddening; clothed with a varied delight of creeping plants, one shut off and separated from another. In the middle of the fence there opened, level, in each, a gate — seven feet in the opening, nine high up to the arching of its topmost curve. Which latticings were of such rhombs, and little squares, and such degenerate from the quadrangle, and by other most beautiful devices. Along these serpentined the periclymenon; others the jasmine; some of bindweed; some of hops; and some of black bryony, or black vine; others of convolvulus, with the lily-like half-azure bells; some all white; some of momordica — so each was varied. Some of Jove's flammula; of smilax, which for love of beautiful Crocus made itself Autophoros, adorned with a white fragrant lily-flower, with a thorny and ivy-like leaf; of bittersweet; of the little vine, with the three-cornered bladder-campion, its seed spotted with whiteness; and of many others which, serpentining on high, are unknown to me by name.

In the first partition the grove was a daphnon, or laurel-grove, of manifold laurels: here I saw the Delphic, the Cyprian, the Mustax with its greatest and whitening leaf, and the wild, the Cinos, and the Royal or Bacchalian, the Taxa, the Spadonic, and Chamaedaphne — such as never on Mount Parnassus, most admired and most pleasing to Apollo, most noble gift sent to the Romans. Nor did such laurel-bearing earth kiss Brutus, most pleasing to Tiberius; nor did Drusilla see such, borne by the white hen; nor were such born of the augur's command, planted in the villa of the Caesars, triumphal ornament — chiefly the Sterile. I saw also the Daphnoides, or Pelasgian, or Euthalos, fragrant with frankincense; nor did Peneus's daughter make such a perpetual green of so much beauty, with whose leaves Apollo was wont to adorn both his lyre and his quiver. Let the airy Sicilian mountains yield here, both as to their sweetest springs and as to their loveliness — however the most beautiful son of Mercury delighted himself there with Diana — which are not prescribed by the wrath of most high Jove, offering themselves so pleasing to cover the bald head of Caesar, mixed with many Comari in the most pleasant soil.

Mirai et uno altro nemorulo nel quale cum aequabile locatione era uno pergratissimo querceto di tenelle fronde. Et quivi vidi latifolia et querno et roburi et hemeris producente il medicinale Canchry, et haliphleos salsicortex, cum assai et aesculi, et cerri, et suberi, et fagi, et ilice, overo smilace, overo aquifolia, quivi cum non casure fronde fogliosi, culto dalle Querquetulane Nymphe. In un altro septo seguiva cum aequo ordine uno altro gratissimo boschetto di olenti cupressi silvestri, gilibano, romidascalo, overo iuniperi. Conducti topiariamente in multiplici figurali expressi, cum minute et pongiente fronde, il marito dilla diva genitrice volenteri, conservabile, et altissimi cedri di multiplice utilitate, liquante olio cedreo di fogliatura similgliante al cupresso, dil quale in Epheso constava il simulachro di Diana, ne gli nobilissimi phani per la sua aeterna duritudine summe appretiato. La vetusta Carie et roscicante tinee renuenti. Foeconda alla sua magna Crete bella in Africa, et olente in Assyria, cum venusta intercalatura di vrate, overo savina di perenne virentia noxia ad Lucina deformate, et similmente variabile. Daposcia vidi et uno proceroso et comante pineto di nuce pinee, quivi la tarentina, overo silvestra pino, et la urbana et la pinea picra, overo Apina, et pinastro, et Zapina, et la lachrymante Resina, artificiosamente distribute. Ancora in uno altro claustro permaxime praestava uno copiosissimo buxeto, in marmorei busti rotundi, et quadruli plantati, tra odorabonde holuscule et floride, che simiglianti in Cytero monte Macedonico non se ritroverebeno, densissimi, cum venusta demigratura in acuminato, cum iusto modo et grado diminuentisi, et cum vaga scansione gracilavano, non sencia commertio di molti altri spectatissimi expressi bellissimamente riduti. Ma tute queste operature, una maravegliosa excedeva. Imperò che di questa specie di arbusculi, io vidi le virtute tute dil procero Hercule, industriosamente, et cum antiquaria deformatione composite, non sencia multifario expresso di innumere altre confictione di diversi animali, sempre virenti, cum non decisure fronde, regularmente collocate, et cum proporcionato et congruente intercapedine, per il prato herboso et florulento distribute. Similmente et uno altro era di multiplice arboramento concinnamente consiti. Et quivi il duro corno, cum gli fructi sanguinolenti, et alcune di candido fructo, era et il amarissimo taxo, ad gli instrumenti lethali di Cupidine grati et aptissimi. Daposcia inseme commixti vidi l’ulmo, tilii, et il tenuissimo Phylire, vitici, carpini, et fraxini, et la hasta di Romulo floribonda, et molti mespili, et asperi sorbi. Offerivase ancora, et uno di rectistirpio et sublimi abieti, quantunque

I beheld also another little grove, in which, with even placing, was a most pleasing oak-wood of tender leaves. And here I saw the broad-leaved, and the common oak, and the robur, and the hemeris producing the medicinal canchry, and the haliphloios with its salt bark, with plenty of Italian oaks, and Turkey oaks, and cork-oaks, and beeches, and holm-oak — or smilax, or holly — here leafy with leaves that do not fall, tended by the Querquetulan nymphs.

In another fence there followed, in equal order, another most gratifying little wood of fragrant wild cypresses, savin, romidascalo, or junipers, led topiary-wise into manifold figured devices, with minute and pricking leaves — the willing consort of the divine mother, preservable — and loftiest cedars of manifold use, weeping cedar-oil, of foliage like the cypress, of which at Ephesus stood the statue of Diana, in the most noble temples most highly prized for its eternal hardness; the aged Carie, and refusing the gnawing moth; fruitful to its great Crete, beautiful in Africa, and fragrant in Assyria; with a lovely interspersing of vrate, or savin, of perennial greenery, harmful to Lucina, shaped, and likewise variable.

Afterwards I saw also a tall and leafy pine-wood of pine-nuts: here the Tarentine, or wild pine, and the urban, and the bitter stone-pine, or Apine, and the pinaster, and the Zapina, and the weeping Resina, artfully distributed.

Again, in another cloister, there stood a most copious box-grove, planted in round and square marble tubs, among fragrant and flowering little pot-herbs — such as the like would not be found on the Macedonian mountain Cytorus — most dense, with a lovely tapering to a point, diminishing in just measure and degree, and thinning with a graceful gradation, not without commerce of many other most admirable devices most beautifully reduced. But one marvellous work exceeded all these. For of this kind of little tree I saw all the labours of tall Hercules industriously and with antique shaping composed [in topiary], not without manifold representation of innumerable other feignings of diverse animals, always green, with leaves that do not fall, regularly placed, and with a proportioned and congruent interval distributed through the grassy and flowery meadow.

Likewise there was another neatly planted with manifold trees. And here the hard cornel, with its blood-red fruits, and some of white fruit; and there was the most bitter yew, pleasing and most apt to the lethal instruments of Cupid. Afterwards, mixed together, I saw the elm, lindens, and the finest phylire, chaste-tree, hornbeams, and ashes, and the flowering spear of Romulus, and many medlars, and rough service-trees.

There offered itself also one of straight-stemmed and lofty firs, however —

dill’amplo mare fugitivi se accusano, et il sito suo sia montano, niente dimeno, quivi proceri et in cielo abeunti inseme interpositi ordinatamente gli fungosi et agaricii larici, overo larigni il foco renuenti, et a questi simili cum opportuna et grata collocatione dispositi. Molto spectabile subsequendo se offeriva et uno altro, ove era la iuglande, prima dicta diuglande, quivi cum non infesta umbra, et la persica et la basilica, overo molusca, et la Tarentina, non sencia sociale composito di coryli. Ceda quivi Avellano, et Preneste, et le pontice. Consortiva et ancora cum questi la impatiente Phyllis arborescente, la quale dete il nome phylla alli foglii, prima denominate petale. Essa florulenta offerivase, quale nello advento dil pigritante Demophonte, chiamata, et nuce graeca, et amygdala, et thasia. Non sencia maxima voluptate mirai, et una silvecula di nuce castanee cum il fructo di pungente echinato calice armato, quale mai a gli Sardi primo saritrouorono. Diqué, et per graeco vocabulo Sardiani balani furono chiamati. Agli quali poscia Balano il Divo Tiberio poi gli dete nome. Pensai sinceramente che a queste la parthenia gli cedeva meritamente la tarentina di facillima mundatura et la più facile balaniti, et più rotunda. Queste praestano et alle pure salariane, et alle laudate coreliane, et le coctive, et le tarentine et neapolitane, ove era ancora il sparto, overo miryca, o vero Aspalato. Erano dunque quivi et silvule et di nobilissimi cotonei, overo cydonei, et uno siliqueto, quale Cypri simigliante non produce, et di lente palme se offeriva uno denso palmeto di utilissime fronde cum cultrato mucrone, resistente, et non inclinabile nella sua summitate onerata confertissimamente dil suo polposo fructo. Non squallidi et piccioli quali la Libyca, né quali la interiore Syria produce gli sui dulci carioti. Ma molto più excellenti di magnitudine ancora, et di dolcecia, che Arabia et Babylonia non rendeno, era et uno pervenusto di mali Punici nobilissimi di tute le specie, dolci, acri, mixti, acidi, et vinosi. Agli quali non se compari, né gli Aegyptii, né gli Samii, né gli Cretensi, né gli Cyprii, et Apyrini, et Erythrococomi, et Leucocomi foecundissimi di fructi et balusti. Daposcia vidi uno gratissimo boschetto di lotho, overo agrifolio, faba Syriaca, ciceraso, overo melli, overo celti, cum molto più suave provento, che le syrtice et nasamone, quivi in tuto la Africa superata consta. Non mancava ancora et uno di paliuro cum rubente fructo al vino aemula di suavitate. Ceda quivi la Cyrenaica, et la interiore Africa, et ancora quella che circa il delubro di Hammone nasce, et uno di ambi gli mori, lo uno exprime nel fructo il funesto amore, et l’altro nutrimento alle nostre delitie

— they show themselves fugitive from the ample sea, and its site is mountainous; nonetheless, here tall and reaching to heaven, set among them in order, the spongy and agaric larches, or larigni, that refuse the fire — and others like these, disposed with fitting and gracious placing.

Most notable, following, offered itself another, where was the walnut, first called diuglans: here with no hostile shade; and the peach, and the basilica, or molusca, and the Tarentine, not without the social company of hazels. Let Avella yield here, and Praeneste, and the Pontic nuts. Consorting also with these was the impatient Phyllis grown to a tree — she who gave the name phylla to the leaves, first called petala. She offered herself flowering, as at the coming of loitering Demophoon summoned; and the Greek nut, and the almond, and the Thasian.

Not without utmost pleasure I beheld also a little wood of chestnuts, with the fruit armed in a pricking bristly husk, such as first the Sardians found — whereby, by the Greek word, they were called Sardian acorns; to which afterwards the divine Tiberius gave the name Balanus. I sincerely thought that to these the parthenia rightly yielded, and the Tarentine of easiest shelling, and the more easily balanite, and rounder. These surpass both the pure Salarian and the praised Corelian, and the quick-boiling, and the Tarentine and Neapolitan; where too was the broom, or myrica, or Aspalathus.

There were then here also little woods of most noble quinces, or cydonians, and a carob-grove such as Cyprus does not produce the like; and of slow palms there offered itself a dense palm-grove of most useful fronds with knife-like point, resistant and not to be bent at its summit, laden most thickly with its pulpy fruit — not squalid and small like the Libyan, nor like the sweet dates the inner Syria produces, but far more excellent in magnitude too, and in sweetness, than Arabia and Babylon yield. There was also a most lovely one of most noble pomegranates of all the kinds — sweet, sharp, mixed, acid, and vinous — to which are not to be compared the Egyptian, nor the Samian, nor the Cretan, nor the Cyprian, both the seedless and the red-tufted and the white-tufted, most fecund of fruits and blossoms.

Afterwards I saw a most gratifying little wood of lotus, or nettle-tree, Syrian bean, ciceraso, or melli, or celtis, with a far sweeter yield than the Syrtic and Nasamonian — here in all Africa surpassed, it stands. Nor was there lacking one of Christ's-thorn with reddening fruit, a rival to wine in sweetness. Let Cyrenaica yield here, and inner Africa, and that too which is born about the shrine of Hammon; and one of both the mulberries — the one expresses in its fruit the mournful love, the other affords nourishment to our delights —

si praesta. Mirai et uno di foetosissimo oliveto, et uno ficulno di tute le specie cum copioso provento. Et uno iucundissimo populno, et similmente uno di hippomelides cum le silique aegyptie cum la lachrymante Metropo, quale all’oraculo di Ammone stillante gummo ammonaco non si troverebbe. Gli quali arbusculi cum elegantissimo et artificiosissimo distributo et ordine. Il quale l’aspecto dil coelo quivi non desiderava, ma sencia repugnantia dilla natura omni cosa optimamente locata constava. Diqué essa ingeniosa natura quivi sé manifestamente accusava tute le delicie, che sparsamente per l’universo havea solertemente producto. Quivi congesticiamente, et cum praecipuo studio coniecturare si pole tuto essere creato. Era et il solo herbido et floreo et da surgenti, et umbrati fonti, di vitreo latice perlucidi, madente di aque suavissime più che dil Salmacide fonte. Et quivi non pativano il rigifero Arcto, né il nubigeno Noto, ma l’aire saluberrimo purgatissimo, et quam purissimo, et longe lucidissimo, liberamente ad gli ochii pervio, levissimo et coaequabile, et invariabile cum grande amoenitate, et apricitate moderata dil loco et dil coelo, che mai non patisce turbulenta alteratione, ma dissipato, et omni nubilo dispulso, esso coelo liquido monstrantise exclusi gli procaci venti, gli crepitanti Euri, il sibilante Aquilo, et la malignitate, et la aspritudine cum magno fragore dilla saeviente procella, et qualunque iniquitate di tempi, non subditi agli tumultuosi mutamenti di aque né alla frigida Libra. Ma omni cosa luculente riguardare si pole, et la optabile luce, laeti et pululanti nella statione dil lanoso Ariete siccante le vellere nello illuminoso et heracleo Phoebo existente, cum non caduco, ma perpetuo virore, celebrato da multiplice canto di ucelli, quale volitante nell’aire la galericola, et inseme la cantante luscinia, l’aire tuto cantando personavano. Allo extremo termino di questo semitertio di milliario tuto in silvule distributo verso il centro, perché una circumferentia di circulare figura è di tanto commenso, quanto sono tre diametri sui. Et tanto più quanto che in undeci partitione, deducto uno diametro, sono due portione. Dunque il diametro di questa voluptuosa insula praestavasi uno milliario adiuncte dille undeci partitione due. Ambiva una egregia clausura, di altitudine passi .viii. et di crassitudine pedale tanto condensamente foliosa, che minimo stipite non apparia, cum binate fenestre pervie, et ordinatamente ad gli opportuni lochi, et itione, inarcuava patente porte, facta et compactamente conducta di meli rancii di limonii, et citri, cum illustre virentia nelle mature folie, et di novelle fronde, degli primi et maturi fructi, et degli odoratissimi fiori dispersamente ornate, tanto più all’aspecto grato et spectatissimo, quanto che rarissimamente è usitato di essere concesso agli humani intuiti videre aequivalente factura. [t]

I beheld also one of most teeming olive, and a fig-grove of all the kinds with copious yield. And a most delightful poplar-wood, and likewise one of hippomelides with the Egyptian pods, with the weeping metopon — such as, at the oracle of Ammon, dripping gum-ammoniac, would not be found.

Which little trees were of most elegant and most artful distribution and order — which the aspect of the sky there did not want, but without repugnance of nature everything stood best placed. Whereby that ingenious nature there manifestly avowed all the delights which, scattered through the universe, she had shrewdly produced. Here heaped together, and with especial study, one may conjecture all to be created. The ground too was grassy and flowery and with springing and shaded fountains, of glassy water most clear, moist with waters sweeter than the fountain of Salmacis. And there they suffered neither the frost-bearing Bear nor the cloud-born South Wind, but the air most salubrious, most purged, and most pure, and by far most lucid, freely pervious to the eyes, most light and even, and invariable, with great loveliness and a temperate sunniness of place and sky, that never suffers turbulent alteration; but dissipated, and every cloud driven off, that liquid sky shows itself, the bold winds shut out — the crackling east winds, the whistling north, and the malignity and asperity, with great crash, of the raging storm, and every iniquity of weather — not subject to the tumultuous changes of waters nor to the cold Balance. But everything may be regarded lustrous, and the desirable light, glad and pullulating in the station of the woolly Ram drying his fleece in the illuminating and Herculean Phoebus, with no falling but a perpetual green, celebrated by the manifold song of birds — as, flitting in the air, the little crested lark, and together the singing nightingale, made all the air resound with singing.

At the outermost boundary of this semitertio of a mile, all distributed into little woods toward the centre — because a circumference of circular figure is of as much measure as are three of its diameters, and so much the more that in eleven partitions, one diameter deducted, there are two portions; therefore the diameter of this voluptuous island stood at one mile, with two of the eleven partitions added — there went round an excellent enclosure, of a height of eight paces and of a foot's thickness, so densely leafy that the least stem did not appear, with paired pervious windows, and, in order at the fitting places and passages, arching open gates, made and compactly conducted of orange, lemon and citron trees, with lustrous greenery in the mature leaves, and of new fronds, of the first and mature fruits, and of the most fragrant flowers dispersedly adorned — so much the more pleasing and admirable to the aspect, as it is most rarely wont to be granted to human eyes to see an equivalent work.

In questa iucunda et dilectosa clausura, tra il verdissimo mirteto, et tra questo florido naranceo septo ambiente, innumeri et promiscui animali inclusi vagamente discorrevano, quantunque la natura dissimile repugnasse innoxii et mansueti. Vagavano quivi cum mutua amicitia. Et prima gli caprigeni satyri cum gli pendenti et intorti spirili. Gli bicorni fauni, cum l’altro sexo. Dapoi gli semiferi Cervi, et Capree saxipete, et le pavide Dame, gli maculosi Hinnuli, gli saltanti Capreoli. Gli auriti Lepori. Gli timidi Cuniculi. Le Fele pullarie, candide et lutee Mustelle, et la mendace Gallante, Sciuri inquieti, et somnochiosi Glyri. Gli feroci Alicorni, et Tragope et Tragelaphi. Omni specie leonina sencia alcuna frammea, ma ludibondi. Gli collosi Gyraphi, celere Gavielle. Et infiniti altri animali, intenti ad gli solatii dilla natura. Ultra poscia di questo claustro verso il centro, trovai una magnificentia di uno eximio pomerio, overo delitioso viridario. Quali mai potreberon, non solamente gli humani ordinare, ma mi suado che né pensare. Facile è dunque, che le seconde operatrice faci secundo il primo operante. Et ancora io diciò consento, che né ingegnio tanto foecondo si ritrovasi, che singularmente valesse, di tante excellentissime operature di questo sacro loco dignamente di relato alquanto propalare. Cedano quivi gli pensili horti da Syro re constructi. Per la quale cosa rectamente iudicai, che non da altro, ma solo da divino artifice fue cusì exquisitissimo cogitamento ad contemplatione dill’alma dea dilla natura ad tale ordine, et effecto decentissimamente producto. Il quale spectatissimo giardino verso il centro porrecto, di passi .166. et semi in prati diviso. La quale divisione constava per itione di directo al centro, et circularmente transversarie. Late passi .V. Gli primarii prati nella prima linea dilla sua quadratura verso la clausura, cum le laterale erano passi .50. Ma la quarta linea verso il centro se decrementava, et da questa linea tolleva la dimensione, la prima linea dil secundo prato, et per il medesimo modo il tertio prato se quadrava. Perché la forcia dille linee al centro tendente causava il scambro. Et le angustie di essi prati et dille strate, et però era deformata la quadratura. Le transversarie nella sua integritate rimanente. Le quale strate erano pergulate, et in ciascuna grummia era tuberculato, sopra quatro columne ionice, il scapo overo la sua proceritate dille quale era di nove diametri dilla ima sua crassitudine. Et di qui et de lì dille itione, overo strate, erano altane busteate di finissimi marmori, cum liniamenti decentissimi. Et per omni quatro diametri extava una di sopradicte columne. Tale columnatione, et intercolumnio per tuto observato.

In this pleasant and delightful enclosure, between the greenest myrtle-grove and this flowery orange fence encircling, innumerable and mingled animals, shut in, roamed loosely — however nature, unlike, repugned — harmless and tame. They wandered there in mutual friendship. And first the goat-born satyrs with their pendent and twisted little horns; the two-horned fauns, with the other sex. Then the half-wild stags, and the rock-seeking roes, and the timid does, the spotted fawns, the leaping kids; the long-eared hares; the timid rabbits; the cat-kind, white and yellow weasels, and the lying gallante; restless squirrels, and drowsy dormice; the fierce unicorns, and tragopan and tragelaphus [goat-stag]; every leonine kind without any fierceness, but playful; the long-necked giraffes, swift gazelles; and infinite other animals, intent on the solaces of nature.

Beyond this cloister, then, toward the centre, I found the magnificence of an excellent orchard, or delightful pleasure-ground — such as never could humans, not only order, but, I persuade myself, so much as think. It is easy, then, that the second workers act according to the first worker. And I too consent to this, that no wit so fecund is found that could singly avail worthily to publish somewhat, by report, of so many most excellent works of this sacred place. Let the hanging gardens built by King Syrus yield here. Wherefore I rightly judged that by no other, but only by a divine artificer, was so most exquisite a conception, for the contemplation of the fostering goddess of nature, produced to such order and effect most becomingly.

Which most admirable garden, stretched toward the centre, of 166 paces and a half, divided into meadows. Which division stood by passages straight to the centre, and circularly transverse, five paces wide. The primary meadows, in the first line of their squaring toward the enclosure, with the lateral ones, were 50 paces. But the fourth line, toward the centre, decremented; and from this line took its dimension the first line of the second meadow, and in the same manner the third meadow was squared. Because the force of the lines tending to the centre caused the narrowing, and the straitness of these meadows and of the streets; and therefore the squaring was deformed, the transverse ones remaining in their integrity.

Which streets were pergola'd, and in each ridge was a little dome upon four Ionic columns, the shaft or their tallness being of nine diameters of their lowest thickness. And here and there of the passages, or streets, were tomb-shaped platforms of finest marbles, with most becoming lineaments. And every four diameters stood one of the aforesaid columns. Such columniation, and intercolumniation, was observed throughout.

Out of the little chests, or platforms, beneath the socle of the solid columns, sprouted rose-bushes, their height not exceeding a pace. Which between one column and the other made a delightful fence; to each column, on this side and on that, perpendicularly raised, in the middle of the inner column adhered, most straight, a rod of rose-bush. Which — beyond the most noble architrave extended over the said columniation, of red stone like most gleaming coral — without other support the rose-bushes made the topiary bend. The summit of the said pergola, with the little chests, columns, and straight beam, stood five paces in height. From which summit the little domes began to round, or swell, in bubble-form; these alone covered with yellow roses. The pergolas along their length were roofed with all kinds of white roses, and the transverse ones with vermilion roses of every condition, with perennial fronds and flowering in the utmost multitude, and a breathing of fragrance; and out of the little chests sprouted every generation of flowers and of aromatic herbs.

The first surrounding pergola continued with the cloister of the orange-grove, which had a window of the opening of the arch terminating at the cloister of the pergola, directed toward the centre, less open from the ground up by one pace, equal to the intercolumnar fence.

Each meadow had four gates, in the middle of the enclosing columniation, the little chests left pervious. Which gates, through all the meadows, uniformly corresponded to one another, level.

In the middle of the olive-set and flower-bearing meadows, an elegant work of excellent distribution and especial polish, I saw. First, in the first meadows, I beheld a most celebrated fabric of a gushing fountain, set beneath a little watch- tower of box greenery of shrewd device. Which was found uniform throughout the whole primary order, and in the circuit of the meadows, in this manner.

In the middle of each, with equal distribution, three steps, turned in a rounding, were built up. Of the topmost, in the levelled surface, the diameter was two paces and a half, with a peristyle of eight little columns raised above, with the little bases in the circling round of the upper step, and with the capitals. Which usurped seven diameters in their tallness (Doric, of the lowest thickness) and bellied arches from one to the other. Above the arches went round the beam, fascia and cornice. At the perpendicular of the underset little columns, for each, was superstructed an antique vase. By diameter of its dilated bulk three feet, with the round bottom pointed, and thence little by little dilating, it came to the middle prominence, adorned with an exquisite little girdle. And thence from the rim the moderate slope ascended to the orifice, with lips of great polish about the gap of its opening; and thence to the rim a foot and a half, the rest down to the —

Il supremo di questo quadrato se fastigiava, et sopra l’aculeo sustiniva una deformatione conida, overamente strongyla. Dagli vasi sopra, era tutto dilla viridura stricta, et cohaesa degli buxi negli vasi sati artificiosamente congesto, et optimamente cum densamine conducto, et iustamente decimato tonsile. Che veramente agli ochii cosa più venusta di tale topiario artificio et materia accommodare non si potrebbe. * * * * * * * * * *

— its foot, three feet high, and the foot half a foot, with little channels, or runnels, twistingly, with a most slender beginning, moderately amplifying toward the rim, with two twisted handles, both at the lips and, above the outer slope, oppositely coiled — not without emulation of the lathe. Out of the mouths sprouted straight stocks of leafy box, of the thickness of the underset little columns, the belly removed, with little arches from one stock to the other. The triangles [?eyed — pierced?].

Then, sloping stretched on high, as much as the stocks, from the opening of the vase to its little head, with hairs [thin shoots] that grew in an even line going round the upright over the stocks — among which hairs, from their beginning the separation somewhat dilated, ascending curved they migrated, thinning the discrepancy toward the lineament of this part, which above narrowed, with divided loosening, between the one and the other above inflexed; at the base of each sloped hair, so named, an outstretched branch made a hooking, in the curve of which hung a pommel [ball]. Then bending back, raised toward the top of the hairs, with a ready gaping curve, at the summit it held a free jewel, or circle. Beyond this recounted arrangement, on high there continued six straight stocks, and tall two-thirds as much as the haired part, with little windowed arches; then a little cupola swelled. Above which swelling rose a squared thing, half-and-one pace of an oval, and a four-parted pervious opening; and from the base of the angles issued an outstretched branch, hooked upward. Upon the inverted hooking sat, for each, a flying eagle in the act, with its beak in prospect. The top of this square peaked itself, and upon the point sustained a cone-shaped, or globular, deformation. From the vases upward, it was all of the greenery, close and cohering, of the box set in the vases, artfully heaped and best conducted with density, and justly clipped tithe-close and shorn — so that truly, to the eyes, a thing more lovely than such topiary artifice and material could not be accommodated.

Notes

Confidence: low. This is the hardest page yet: a single continuous description of one topiary-and-vase-and-fountain structure, in Colonna's most compressed technical Italian, with several words that are either coinages or corrupt (gampsado, the "haired" pili, strongyla). The overall shape is recoverable — box trained up out of vases into arches, balls, an oval finial, and eagles — but individual clauses are provisional, and I have rendered them literally with bracketed glosses rather than smoothing. A reviewer should treat this whole page as unsettled and work from the facsimile woodcut, which shows the structure.

Nello intersito mediano dilla coaequata emblematura solistima dil peristylio era uno fonte fundato intro una rotundatione alquanto concheamente lacunata, dal centro dilla quale se attolleva uno balusto inverso, bipedale. Sopra assideva una platina, lo hiato dill’apertione havea per diametro pedi quatro, nel meditullo dilla quale tre varicante caude di tre auree hydre, per il fundo peregrinamente trifarie daposcia in bellissimo nodulo strictamente se intortilando, divise cum il ventre in forma collea una da l’altra, cum repanda discrepantia, et nelle gule anguineamente innodate, cum il capo trifaria dispartito evomabonde nella concha odorissima aqua, aequamente sustinivano erecte bipedale uno vaso di figuramento ovolato. Dil quale nel supernato erano infixe octo fistulete d’oro, dalle quale tenuissimo filo di aqua prosiliva. Et per gli hiati overo intervalli dille buxee columne ussivano tuto il prato roscidamente humectando. Lo interstitio dilla quale specula, era expedito et pervio. La operatura lapidea, tuta era di diaspro finissimo rubente et luculeo, adulterato guttatamente d’infinite macole, di multiplice coloramento, cum elegante et exquisita caelatura ad gli opportuni lochi. Hora in qualunque angulo dil quadrangulato prato, cum proportionata distantia dalle capsule, disposita era una altana di quatro gradi, in forma quadrata. Il primo grado solistimo in fronte pareva bipedale. Nella piana, overo bucca, lato sesquipedale. Et erano vacui busti. Poscia sequiva l’altro di sopra cum ordine gradato, alto quanto la latitudine aperta dil primo, et cusì il tertio, et cusì il quarto. Nel primo herba germinava odorifera, cusì nelle sequente. Quivi dunque erano crispi et minutissimi ocimi, citronei, et cheropholii. Gli quali non praeterivano il frontale mediano dil grado, le fronde aequatamente servate, il simigliante uniforme per tuto constava. L’altra havea olente et minuto thimo gratioso alle mellificante ape. In la tertia, il minuto et amaro gliciacono, overo nectario, overo abrotano, che tale non se offerisce il siculo. Nel supremo spica celtica cum iucundo odore. t iii

In the middle intersit of the levelled, most solid inlay of the peristyle was a fountain, founded within a rounding somewhat shell-like hollowed, from the centre of which rose an inverted baluster, two feet. Above sat a platter, the gap of its opening having by diameter four feet; in the very middle of which, three straddling tails of three golden hydras, through the bottom outlandishly three-forked, then tightly intertwining in a most beautiful knot, divided by the belly one from the other in a bag-like form, with an arching discrepancy, and in their throats snake-wise knotted, with the head three-forked apart, vomiting the most fragrant water into the shell — equally sustained upright, two feet, a vase of ovate figure. Of which, in the part above the surface, were fixed eight little gold pipes, from which a most slender thread of water leapt forth. And through the gaps or intervals of the box columns it issued, dewily moistening the whole meadow. The interstice of which watch-tower was clear and pervious. The stone-work was all of finest jasper, reddening and lustrous, adulterated drop-wise with infinite spots of manifold colouring, with elegant and exquisite carving at the fitting places.

Now in each corner of the quadrangled meadow, with a proportioned distance from the little chests, was disposed a platform of four steps, in square form. The first step, most solid, in front appeared two feet; in the flat, or mouth, a side of a foot and a half; and they were hollow platforms. Then followed the other above, in graded order, tall as the open breadth of the first; and so the third, and so the fourth. In the first, fragrant herb germinated; so in the following. Here, then, were crisp and tiniest basils, citron-scented and chervils, which did not overpass the middle front of the step, the fronds kept even; the like stood uniform throughout. The other had fragrant and small thyme, gracious to the honey-making bees. In the third, the small and bitter glyciacon, or nectary, or southernwood, such as the Sicilian does not offer. In the topmost, Celtic spikenard, of pleasing odour.

Questo tale distributo, in tute le quatro altane collocate agli anguli di questo primo prato, tuto convestito di florigera camaedaphne se praestava. La apertione dil supremo grado era per diametro pedale, et in ciascuna era plantato uno nobilissimo et foecondo fruteto, topiariamente uniformi conducti. Et primo erano pomarii, in uno angulo vidi meli apiani odoratissimi. Nell’altro meli claudiani. Nel tertio meli paradisei. Nel quarto pomuli decii. Ma in qualunque prato di questo primo ordine le specie variavano dil dicto fructo. Arbore quivi di pomifera sobole foeconde, lo odoramento per tuto diffundentise. Cum tanta bellecia di colore, et di tanta suavitate di gustato producevano, quale non produceva lo arbore di Hercule Gaditano. Né tali arbori Iunione commandoe negli sui horti essere inserti. Diqué chiamare si posino pomarii aphyracori.

La topiaria deformatione, et la circulata crassitudine era di corona, cum lo amfracto verso la specula. Gli plutei, overo septi di questa gradata altana extava di bellissimo diaspro speculare, et di scintule auree perseminato, et di maculamento giallo commixturato, et di vene cyanee serpente, et punicee discurrente, et di undatura calcedonica confusamente impentigato, riquadrati cum gratissime undule. Ultra poscia da questo descripto primo ordine di vireto verso all’insulare centro. Nel secondo ordine, nel mediano dill’area, in loco dilla specula. Cum decentissimi liniamenti, l’altecia sua tripedale et in longo passi tre. Alla linea dille strate transversale destinata. Da l’uno et l’altro extremo, meno uno pede era sato uno buxo, alla forma di vaso antiquario, ambi dui aequali, et uniformi egregiamente conducti, cum il pedusculo, corpulentia, et horificio, uno passo sublati, sencia anse. Sopra le sue bucce uno gigante alto passi tre, di qui, et de lì cum il pede calcava cum le crure aperte. Vestito in rotondatione fina alla rota degli ginochii, cincto cum gli brachii in sublime dispansi, et alla statura humana il collo, capo, pecto, cum exigente harmonia deformato. This arrangement, in all four platforms placed at the corners of this first meadow, showed itself all clothed with flowering chamaedaphne. The opening of the topmost step was a foot in diameter, and in each was planted a most noble and fecund fruit-tree, led uniformly topiary-wise. And first they were apple-trees: in one corner I saw most fragrant Appian apples; in the other, Claudian apples; in the third, Paradise apples; in the fourth, the little Decian apples. But in each meadow of this first order the species of the said fruit varied. Trees here fecund with apple-bearing offspring, the fragrance diffusing everywhere — with such beauty of colour, and producing such sweetness of taste, as the tree of Hercules of Gades did not produce, nor such trees as Juno commanded to be grafted in her gardens; whereby they might be called pomarii aphyracori [orchards of golden apples].

The topiary shaping, and the circled thickness, was of a crown, with the winding toward the watch-tower. The parapets, or fences, of this stepped platform stood of most beautiful mirror-jasper, oversown with golden sparks, and mingled with yellow mottling, and with cyan veins serpentining, and reddish ones running, and confusedly dappled with a chalcedonic waving, squared off with most gratifying little wave- lines.

Beyond, then, from this described first order of greenery toward the island's centre — in the second order, in the middle of the area, in place of the watch- tower — I beheld a most admirable device of box, in artful topiary. A stone ark set of precious chalcedony of the colour of soapy water, with most becoming lineaments, its height three feet and in length three paces, destined to the line of the transverse streets. From the one and the other end, less a foot, was set a box in the form of an antique vase, both two equal and uniformly egregiously conducted, with the little foot, bulk and orifice, raised a pace, without handles. Upon their mouths a giant three paces tall, on this side and that trod with the foot, with legs apart. Clothed in a rounding down to the cap of the knees, girt, with the arms spread on high, and to human stature the neck, head, breast, shaped with exacting harmony. He was helmeted; with his arms he sustained two towers, one per hand, four feet wide, six tall, with the base —

Next to the slanted trunk — that is, at the beginning of its exit from the pommel — rose another slender and straight stock, holding a cone-shaped pommel, smaller than the underset. Beneath which summit, in the middle of the convex, fixed supine, was another pommel; like one of the stocks, raised from the said pommel, in the middle bend of the summit a trunk half a foot grew, which sustained a shell somewhat hollowed, and sprinkled from its orifice, a little less than the content of the arch. Out of which platter rose as much of a stalk again as the underset of the shell, which subsided into a form of lily with supine lips about. Out of which lily — that is, basket — grew a box raised in eight suppressed pommels, gradually diminishing toward the top, somewhat separated one from another; all the work from the arch upward being of six feet, the aforesaid box excepted. No indication showed itself in all this excellent topiary of woodwork, except the straight stocks; but all was covered with most propped foliage and most evenly shorn, with diligence and art clipped.

Between one and the other vase, in the chest, was seen a box without a stock, in onion-form [?cepacea], a pace wide, two feet and a half tall. In the middle of which was led a pear-tree, raised four feet, with the pointed top. Above the tapering it held a flat circular figure, its diameter four feet. In the middle of this lens-like round sprang forth a little stock holding an egg-shaped form, tall as the pear beneath.

Ne gli anguli ancora dil praesente, et secundo prato verso il centro, come nel primo constitute erano le capse di quatro gradi cum tuta la regulatione, dimensione, locatione, che hano le altre altane dil primo prato. In the corners also of the present, and second, meadow toward the centre, as in the first were constituted the chests of four steps, with all the regulation, dimension, and placing that the other platforms of the first meadow have — except of the stone, which was of blackest amber, or ambrum. Never did the Phaethontides beside Eridanus pour out such in their weeping, nor would such be found in the Electrid isles, nor does the temple of Ammon produce such — of mirror-smoothness, drawing the straw when rubbed. Which chests were figured circular.

In the lowest germinated the fragrant cassia; in the next grew odoriferous nard. In the third was Mint, the nymph, betokening the fierce hatred of Proserpina. In the fourth grew the unlucky royal Amaracus, extinguished amid his own odour — and such Cyprus does not produce.

In the upper middle likewise, planted, sat a fruit-tree for each. But dissimilar in fruit, and in topiary form, to the first. For [?…] they were four, of a spherical figure most beautifully; they produced kinds of pears — one, musk pears; the other, Crustumian; the third, [?…] and succulent Syrian; the last, tenderest little curmunduli. [?…] in this secondary order of meadows, the others [?…] varied the species of the fruit, with [?…] colour, with most gratifying odour, and with [?…] taste. The ground alone clothed with fragrant and tiniest [?…], and the platforms of [?…] simples.

Following in order, the description also of the third meadow toward the centre, which in its middle had a chest of round content, raised three feet, by diameter two paces the opening, with the accessory lineaments. Out of it exalted itself an artificed box, of the following topiary composite. The chest was of finest litharge.

The stock a foot and a half; above sat an onion-shaping, somewhat exceeding the round of the chest — which was hollow, and open at the top by diameter a pace and a half. Above the lips of which opening a columniation of six greening stocks turned with little arches, four feet high. Then a metal-shaped summit, or shaped into a chalice-foot, ascended; on the summit of which lay a most exact pommel, three feet in its thickness.

Upon the lowest end of the domed summit, at the perpendicular of each stock, there coiled the tail of a serpent, with the belly bent and the spine arching, as much as was the projection of the onion-bulk; adhering with its neck to the pommel existing above, with the head outstretched and jaws open, and through most hidden pipe-like passages it leapt, pouring most fragrant water from the mouth, with the feet extended toward the head, with the wings spread — six in number.

Out of the top of the pommel sprang three branches, three-forkedly divided, and raised two feet bending. Each of which, then, at its summit sustained a little rounded altar, or a little tube, with exquisite diligence attributed above the little coronet, and below the fitting throats, with most even clipping. The height without the lineaments three feet. Above the flat sat an antique water-jar, four- handled, raised three feet. From each of which singly grew a box with two steps of box rounding. La gratiosa venustate di questi quam iucundissimi expressi allo intuito se offeriva tanto acceptissima, quanto che gli corpi, et figuratione di spectatissima virentia, tanto iustamente exacte, quanto che meglio si potrebbe componere, et di tale materia topiare, et in tali liniamenti compacta informare.

In questo dicto prato di omni promiscua herba florigera molto più cha una ficta pictura ancora negli anguli le altane cum la descripta regulatione erano triquetre. Ma di chrysolectro di flavo aureo. Quale da le virgine Hesperide non è collecto, cum più suavissimo perfricato dil citrino odore, che non rende il collecto in Citro insula di Germania translucentissimo et chiaro, né tale sono le lachryme dille Meleagride, germinava nella ima capsa la suavissima saliunca. Nella superiore era Polio montano. Nella tertia erano Ladano et Cistho. Nella suprema la fragrante Ambrosia. Gli fructigeri arbori di forma hemispheria in convexo. Gli quali in questo tertiano ordine, uno l’altro non excedevano, ma di conveniente statura, et di variata specie, et di multiplice fructo. Quivi erano Pistachii, Ameringi, tute maniere di Myrobalani, et Hippomelides, et di tuti pruni damasceni, di molti altri delicatissimi fructi. Oltra quelli, che {{Pt||}}a nui peculiari sono di diversificata specie, di , et forma, et di suavitate di incogniti, et . {{Pt||}}* *

— elegance of its springing. Between this arched trigon, with lovely consent of form, was made a tortoise-shell summit, or shade-canopy; the uprights of the stocks did not rise higher than the summit of the ceiling, but all three by rule met in one height, sustaining a lily-like chalice. Out of which, raised, sat a turbinate, or globular thing, with the slender part downward, or the volute turned down. The gracious loveliness of these most delightful devices offered itself to the sight so most acceptable, as the bodies and figuration of most admirable greenery, so justly exact, as one could best compose them, and inform them of such material for topiary, and compact them in such lineaments.

In this said meadow of every mingled flowering herb — far more than a feigned picture — again in the corners the platforms, with the described regulation, were three-cornered. But of chrysolectrum of yellow gold, such as is not gathered by the virgin Hesperides, with a most sweet rubbing of the citron odour, more than the translucent and clear one gathered in Citros, an isle of Germany, renders; nor are such the tears of the Meleagrides. There germinated, in the lowest chest, the most sweet saliunca; in the upper was mountain polion; in the third were labdanum and cistus; in the topmost the fragrant ambrosia.

The fruit-bearing trees of hemispherical form in the convex, which in this third order did not exceed one another, but of convenient stature, and of varied species, and of manifold fruit. Here were pistachios, tamarinds, all manners of myrobalans, and hippomelides, and of all Damascene plums, of many other most delicate fruits. Besides those which [?…] are peculiar to us, of diversified species, of [?…] and form, and of sweetness of [?…] unknown, and [?…].

Gli quali redundavano gli fructi et flori cum non casure fronde, agli spectanti sensi summa cum iucunditate offerentise. Gli quali ancora non cum obliqui discoli, et intricati rami, contendevano, ma in diverse forme politulamente complexi. Non agli lunarii mutamenti subditi. Né all’impallidire di Phoebo subiecti, ma sempre obnoxii, cum tenella et suchiosa virentia, immutabilmente, et in uno medesimo stato, et producto duravano, et foecondo provento. Et per questa conditione similmente gli flori, et le odorigere herbuscule duravano. Dagli quali per tuto una inexperta fragrantia multo acceptissima diffusamente se diffundeva. Gli rosari poscia tanto più cum magiore gratia se praestavano, quanto erano più diverse, et a me invisitate sorte. Quivi florivano copiosamente le Damascene, Proenestine, Pentaphylle, Campane, Milesie, Rubente, Pestine, Trachivie, et Allabandice, et di tute nobile et laudatissime specie. Le quale dil suo odore suavissimo, et periucundo colore, et quam gratissimo flore, tra le virente fronde, perhenne servabile persistevano. Né più praesto una casitava, che l’altra succedeva. Le capse erano di faberrima arte expresse. La strissatione dille quale specularmente aemulavano in sé havere, et l’aire, et fronde, et gli flori, et foliatura dispensabonde. Sotto le opere topiarie, et pergule, le strate silicate erano di più excellente silicato di factura, che unque accessorio essere potesse nello ingegnio humano et cogitato. Ultra gli memorati aequalmente tripartiti prati. Una maxime magnifica determinatione, di egregio et spectando peristylio eustylo ventriculato bellissimamente clusorio circuiva in circunferentia obvallando. Il murulo dilla quale circinata columnatione, di nobilissima et ordinatamente et daedalice perplexa cancellatura era constructo. Intercalata dalle arule, servabile il solido sotto le base, opportunamente loricate cum

Which fruits and flowers redounded, with leaves that do not fall, offering themselves to the beholding senses with the utmost delight. Which, moreover, did not contend with oblique, wayward and intricate branches, but were neatly clasped in diverse forms. Not subject to the lunar changes, nor subjected to the paling of Phoebus, but ever bound, with tender and juicy greenery, unchangeably, and in one same state, and product, endured, and a fecund yield.

And by this condition likewise the flowers and the fragrant little herbs endured; from which, throughout, an unexperienced fragrance, most acceptable, was diffusedly spread. The rose-bushes then showed themselves with the more grace, the more diverse and, to me, unvisited the kinds. Here flowered copiously the Damascene, Praenestine, five-petalled, Campanian, Milesian, Reddening, Pestan, Trachivian, and Alabandan, and of all noble and most praised kinds — which, with their most sweet odour, and most delightful colour, and most gratifying flower, among the greening fronds, perennially preservable persisted. Nor sooner did one fall than another succeeded.

The chests were expressed with most cunning art. The striations of which mirror-wise emulated to have in themselves both the air, and fronds, and flowers, and foliage dispensing.

Beneath the topiary works and pergolas, the paved streets were of a more excellent paving of make than ever any accessory could be in human wit and thought.

Beyond the aforementioned equally three-parted meadows, a most magnificent determination, of an excellent and admirable eustyle bellied peristyle, most beautifully enclosing, circled in circumference, walling about. The little wall of which circling columniation was constructed of most noble and orderly and daedally-perplexed latticework, interspersed with the little altars, preserving the solid beneath the bases, fittingly plated with —

Lo intercolumnio havea gli spatii alli intervalli di due columne et una quarta parte, et ove le vie al peristylio derivavano, ivi quanto la sua latitudine comperta era, tanto se constituiva, overo se causava infra lo peristylio il laxamento. Il quale la iuge ambientia discontinuava. Et quivi una nobilissima porta era fabrefacta. Lo arco succurvo dilla quale cum gli corni sopra una columna de qui et de lì pedava, quanto dil suo scapo et locatione coniugata uniforme all’altre. Ma dilla crassitudine dissentivano, per essere al superastructo symmetriate. Oltra la incurvata trabe, il fastigio assideva, overo frontispicio, cum tuti gli correlarii ornamentarii expolitissimamente exscalpti. Sopra il circuncolumnamento continuamente in circinao se porrigeva lo epistylio, zophoro et coronice cum mirabili expressi et condecente liniatura.Gli quali cum mirifica operatura bustuaria excavati, et di terra suffarcinati extavano. Questo mirando peristylio havea gli muruli, et supracolumnio tuto di nobilissima alabastrite diaphana, et luculea, sencia fricatione, et expolitione di terra Thebaica, né cum pumice. Ma le columne di colore variavano, perché quelle che extavano in loco diante in uno loco, erano di translucido carchedonio, et le collaterale supra il

— the socle and cymatium, undulate and gulleted correspondingly. The intercolumniation had the spaces at the intervals of two columns and a quarter part; and where the ways derived to the peristyle, there, as much as its found breadth was, so much was constituted, or caused, within the peristyle the loosening — which the continuous surrounding discontinued. And there a most noble gate was wrought. The low-bent arch of which footed with its horns upon a column on this side and that, as much as its shaft and placing conjoined uniform with the others; but in thickness they dissented, being symmetried to the superstructure. Beyond the incurved beam sat the summit, or frontispiece, with all the correlative ornaments most polishedly carved. Above the surrounding columns there stretched continuously in a ring the architrave, frieze and cornice, with marvellous devices and befitting lineation. Which, with marvellous tomb-like work, hollowed and stuffed with earth, stood forth; out of which every sort of most admirable flowers germinated. And at the perpendicular of the underset columns were planted topiary box and junipers. Thus upon the collateral ones, in a preservable order without indication of stock, was a most round pommel of box; and upon the other, so alternating, above a foot of stock rose the juniper with four pressed pommels gradually decrementing, and the flowers interposed.

This marvellous peristyle had the little walls, and the supra-columniation, all of most noble alabaster, diaphanous and lustrous, without rubbing and polishing of Theban earth, nor with pumice. But the columns varied in colour, because those that stood in a facing place in one place were of translucent chalcedony, and the collateral ones, above the —
