murulo di verdissima petra Hexaconthalitho di multiplice coloramento praenitevano. Le altre due de qui et de lì erano di illustre Hieracites cum gratissima nigritia. Daposcia le altre due di albicante Gallatites. Le due sequente l’ordine di Chrysophrasio. Le postreme di fulgente Atizoe di nitore argenteo, et di odore iucondo. Et cusì emusicatamente alternavano, cum incredibile iucunditate di spectatione. Le quale tanto artificiosamente havevano lo enthesi, quanto si al torno exacte fusseron turbinate, cum tale arte, quale Theodoro et Tholo, architecti nella sua officina di turbine a tornare le columne non trovorono. Opera certamente sumptuosa, superba, pretiosa, et elegante. Le columne ionice cum gli capituli, cum gli echini intra lo lanceato. Il quale di corticeo circumvoluto convestito splendicavano cum le base di optimo oro, quale non produce lo aurifero Tago in Hesperia, né Pado in Latio, né Hebro in Thracia, né Pactolo in Asia, né Gange in India. Il zophoro era deornato di antiquaria foliatura in sé convolvula egregiamente excalpta. Et gli cancelli tra le arule circumsepti erano di optimo electro che tale non fue quello, dil quale alla forma dilla mamma ne l’insula Lindo nel tempio di Minerva da Helena fue dedicato. Sopra la plana dil murulo per ciascuno intervallo tra una et l’altra columna era statuto uno vetusto vaso di conveniente operatura al residuo recensito cum faberrima politura variato di petra et di colore, di Sphragide di Colorites, di Calcedonico, di Coaspites, di Achate et di molte altre pretiose et gratissime petre, nel suo terso qualunque obiecto simulando faberrimamente perpolituli et expressi. Cum liniamenti non humani pensai. Fora degli quali vedevasi spectatissimi simplici et plantule, in variata deformatione topiarie congeste. Quivi Amaraco, la aromatica, et crispula Sentonica. Avrotano, Myrtuli, et altri che al contento degli ochii altro più periucundo non si potesse obiecto praestare. Dal praefato peristylio alle rive fina di uno flume il solo citimo era occupato herboso di rosido gramine. Quivi cum gratiosa dispensatione era il floribondo Xiphio, Lavendule, Origani, Pollicaria, Leucorigano, Mente Nympha, che da Plutone il bellissimo munere ricevete. Et ancora florivano le lachryme di Helena, Helenio chiamate, alla facia salubre, et dilla sancta Madre Conciliatrice. Et innumerabile altre plantule celeberrime, aromatice, et di acceptissimo odoramento. Et gli albenti, et cerulei Hiacynthi, et purpurei. Quale in Gallia non è producto. Hora tra le florifere et tenelle frondule, innumeri volatili di eximia pinnatura

— little wall, shone with greenest stone hexacontalithos of manifold colouring. The other two, on this side and that, were of lustrous hieracites with most gratifying blackness. Then the other two of whitening galactites. The two next, in order, of chrysoprase. The last of gleaming atizoe of silvery brightness, and pleasing odour.

And thus they alternated inlay-wise, with incredible delight of beholding. Which had their entasis so artfully, as if they had been turned exact on the lathe — with such art as Theodorus and Tholus, architects in his workshop of the turning-lathe, did not find for turning columns. A work certainly sumptuous, proud, precious, and elegant.

The Ionic columns with their capitals, with the echini within the lanceolate — which, clothed with a bark-like enwrapping, shone with bases of finest gold, such as the gold-bearing Tagus does not produce in Hesperia, nor Padus in Latium, nor Hebrus in Thrace, nor Pactolus in Asia, nor Ganges in India. The frieze was adorned with antique foliage convolved on itself, egregiously carved. And the lattices hedged between the little altars were of finest electrum — such as was not that of which, in the form of a breast, in the isle of Lindos in the temple of Minerva, was dedicated by Helen.

Upon the flat of the little wall, for each interval between one column and the other, was set an ancient vase of fitting work to the rest recounted, with most cunning polish, varied of stone and of colour — of sphragis, of colorites, of chalcedony, of coaspites, of agate, and of many other precious and most gratifying stones — in its burnish simulating any object most cunningly, most polished and expressed, with lineaments I thought not human. Out of which were seen most admirable simples and plantlets, heaped in varied topiary shaping. Here marjoram, the aromatic and crisp sentonica, southernwood, myrtles, and others than which no more delightful object could be offered to the content of the eyes.

From the aforesaid peristyle to the banks, right up to a river, the nearest ground was occupied, grassy, with dewy grass. Here, with gracious dispensation, was the flowering xiphion, lavenders, marjorams, fleabane, white marjoram, Mint the nymph — who received the beautiful gift from Pluto — and there flowered also the tears of Helen, called helenion, salubrious to the face, and of the holy Mother Conciliatrix; and innumerable other most celebrated plantlets, aromatic, and of most acceptable fragrance; and the whitening, and cerulean hyacinths, and purple — such as in Gaul is not produced.

*

Now among the flower-bearing and tender little fronds, innumerable birds adorned with excellent plumage, of small and middling stature, and various little birds and fowls, flitting unstably here and there, wandering, now up, now down, leaping with delightful chatter, the sweet sonority of their song resounding everywhere — which would have had virtue to provoke any woodland and inept heart to pleasure, joy and solace, making festival with their little wings and feathers. Here the plaintive nightingale, Daedalion weeping the death of Lycaon's daughter; the spotted blackbirds, and the singing crested lark, or galerita, and the terraneola, sparrow, or lark; the solitary sparrows; the most eloquent parrot, of manifold clothing — green, white, yellow, crimson, and yellow with green; the singular (but not here) and marvellous Phoenix; most white turtle-doves; Picus, husband of Pomona, betokening the swollen angers of Circe; Aedon, tearful for her beloved husband Itylus; Asterie with feet shod in rose; and the two magpies, Procne the roof-dweller; and pious Antigone, too beautiful, without a tongue; Itys, doleful and funereal table; the gluttonous icteris; Tereus the rock-dweller, keeping the royal pomp in his feathers, seeking pou pou, pu, pu in his song, and in his head a bearer, marked with the military crest; and, by Syrinx, the drowsy shepherd; and the birds of Palamedes, and the teal, and the wanton partridge and porphyrio; Periclymenus, whose form Jupiter freely used for his loves; and the sygolis, or melancoryphos, or blackcap, changeable in autumn; likewise the robin, or redstart, and innumerable others of a lengthy telling.

ONE THIRD, 333 paces

For more evident demonstration: the circuit of this delightful and most pleasant island, by measurement round, stood at three miles. The figure of which gave, for its diameter, one mile. Which, divided in three parts — one third contained 333 paces, one foot, and two palms and somewhat more — from the outer lip of the shore- banks up to the orange-cloister occupied the measure of one semitertio, 166 paces and 10 palms. From this boundary began the meadows, tending toward the centre, another semitertio as much. Thus, one whole third being suitably distributed, there remains one semitertio to dispense up to the very middle, 166 paces and 10 palms. From the aforesaid peristyle, somewhat of space was granted, remaining for the contraction of the aforesaid meadows, to avoid the straitness of the squarings — which did not have their boundary up to the completion of the third; and this shrewdly happened, to proportion somewhat the last square by the lines drawn to the point. Which space, intercalated between the river and the peristyle, all —

gratiosamente intecto, di gratissima herbatura offerivase. Como per avanti sufficiente è descripto, et narrato. Il termine dil recensito spatio finiva in ripe florigere di uno lympidissimo fiume, più che Argyrondes in Etolia, et più che Peneo thessalico. Le sponde dil quale erano di pretiosa petra spartania verdissima, quale Thyberiana Augustea loricatamente contabulate, et tuto circunsepto il flume tra questi limiti marmorei. Le quale ripe non erano implicite, né occupate di Silero, né di Salicta, né di Vinci, né di Canuscula. Ma le purgatissime et argentee lymphe intromisse sepivano. La superficie dille quale, di celebri, et multiplici flori iucundissime et belle appariano. Il quale surgiente fiume et manale, per meati, et fistule subterranee, in diversi et constituiti loci ordinatamente scatevano. Et per aqueducti poscia de finissima petra celeramente discorrendo, questo solatioso et foelice sito per tuto cum piacevole susurro aequamente irrigava. L’aque poscia nel mare contermine fundentise praecipite. Et per questo modo il chlarissimo fluvio il suo incremento per gli emissarii voratori distributi exhauriendo, non superabondava, ma ad uno perhenne coaequamento persistente et contento. Il quale di latitudine era di passi .XII. Le surgibile vene dil quale qualunche celebre fonte et ancora Cabille di Messopotamia excedevano più praestante. Né tale fece la Virgine Castalia il suo. Quale questi rendevano l’aque dolce, odorifere moscate, et sincere, alte palmi .XVI. Che de cusì dulcissima scaturigine ubertosamente non efflueva il fonte di Hercule in Gaditano. Le quale per sì facto modo lympide purificate et subtile erano, che il medio tra il senso et lo obiecto non occupavano, né disproportionato il facevano. Ma omni cosa nel aperto fundo per tuto quale subsideva vedevase perfecta. Et cum aemulatione speculare le cose praesentate integramente rendevano. Il fundo di harenula aurifera era complanato pleno di fine petre calcule di plurifario coloramento lucentissime. Le virente comose, et humide ripe dil quale degli floribondi narcissi, et dil bulbo vomico, overo cepe marino aquicoli erano ornatissime. Non mancavano il hyacintho et gli lilii convallii, et di Xiphion segetale, et Hyllirico. Quivi copiose erano di Caltha, et la Hippotesi, overo cauda equina, et la leonina. Infinite viole tusculane, marine, callatiane, autumnale, et la balsamita, overo Cimiadon, overo trachiotis, et di altri nobilissimi amnici germini. Cum innumere avicule fluvicole. Quivi Halcyone di plumule cyanee, et di altri fluviatili ucelleti subitarii. Quivi gli gulosi, et natanti cygni, nel auspicare grati, cum lo extremo canto dille aque meandre.

— graciously covered, offered itself of most gratifying grassiness, as is sufficiently described and narrated before.

The boundary of the recounted space ended in flowering banks of a most limpid river, more so than Argyrondes in Aetolia, and more than Thessalian Peneus. The banks of which were of precious greenest Spartan stone, like the Tiberian Augustan, planked platewise, and the whole river hedged about between these marble limits. Which banks were not entangled, nor occupied by siler, nor by willow-beds, nor by osiers, nor by little reed — but the most purged and silvery waters, let in, hedged them. The surface of which appeared, with celebrated and manifold flowers, most delightful and beautiful. Which springing and flowing river, by passages and pipes underground, in diverse and constituted places gushed out in order; and then, by aqueducts of finest stone swiftly running, watered this solacing and happy site everywhere evenly with a pleasant murmur.

The waters then pouring headlong into the neighbouring sea. And in this manner the most clear river, exhausting its increment through the distributed devouring outlets, did not overabound, but persisted at a perennial evening-out and content. Which was in breadth twelve paces. The springing veins of which exceeded any celebrated fountain, and even Cabille of Mesopotamia, more excellent. Nor did the Virgin Castalia make such a one of hers. As these rendered the waters sweet, fragrant, musky, and pure, sixteen palms deep — for of so most sweet a source the fountain of Hercules in Gades did not so abundantly flow. Which were purified, limpid and subtle in such a manner that they did not occupy the medium between the sense and the object, nor make it disproportionate; but everything in the open bottom throughout, as it subsided, was seen perfect. And with mirror-emulation they rendered whole the things presented. The bottom was flattened of golden sand, full of fine pebble-stones of manifold most shining colouring.

The greening, leafy and moist banks of which were most adorned with flowering narcissi, and the emetic bulb, or water-loving sea-onion. Nor were lacking the hyacinth and the lilies of the valley, and corn xiphion, and Illyrian. Here were copious the marsh-marigold, and the horsetail, or mare's-tail, and the lion's. Infinite violets — Tusculan, marine, Callatian, autumnal — and the balm-mint, or cimiadon, or trachiotis, and other most noble riverside shoots. With innumerable little river- birds. Here the halcyon of cerulean feathers, and other sudden little water-fowl. Here the gluttonous and swimming swans, pleasing in the auspices, with the last song of the meandering waters.

Sopra le praefate ripe spectatissime, da uno lato, et l’altro erano emusicatamente distributi Meliranci, Citri et Lamonii. Da trunco ad stipite la intercapedine passi tre. Gli quali stirpi uno passo alti dal solo, gli rami a spandere initiavano, l’uno cum l’altro obviantise, et optimamente contricantise rendevano uno arco, da terra al summo inflexo passi tre. Gli residui rami poscia da una ripa ad l’altra sopra il flume all’incontro l’uno dil altro, cum artificioso coniugio, et sotiale complexo camurantise et invilupati. Amoenamente inumbravano, in opera excellentissima pergularia. Il circunflexo dilla quale cum frondea densitate, elegantemente congesto, cum ostentatione di una coaequabile tonsura, overamente decimatura, una fronde all’altra non excedendo se non cum summa gratia, et venustamento, reddendo placidissima ombra, et per il suave flato di Phavonio tremule, et ventilabonde, plene di nutrimento, che dilla sua virentia le faceva illustre, quale nel primo germinare se monstravano, cum gli albicanti flosculi, et praependenti fructi, opportuni latibuli dilla quaerula Philomela, sempre dolcissimamente lamentabonda cantante, perfectamente sencia Echo per la filia Glaucopi dil alto et fulguratore Iove netta et purgatissima risonante. La quale pergulata opera dal supremo convexo giù all’aqua levata superstava passi septe. O quanto cum allectamento et dulcissimo sugesto alla sua contemplatione, questo gli devii ochii facilmente attraheva. Imperò che in esso flume discorrevano alcuni lintri, et scaphidii di materia d’oro delitiosamente contecti cum molte fanciulle caesariate et Euplocame, et Alphesivie, di olenti, et varii flori instrophiolate remigabonde, di limpidi, et crispuli, et crocotuli supari, overamente interule, ovunque orulati d’oro, sopra il nympheo nudo, cum lascivo ornato indute, et cincte, non impedienti all’intuito offerirse voluptuose le rosee carne contecte, immo dalle verifere aure sopra il spectando foemine volupticamente impulse, et presse, la delitiosa formula et qualunque altra parte secondo il moto suo eximie propalava, cum gli bianchissimi, et semipomati pecti, fino al rotundare dille mamillule a maxima et voluptica gratia disclaustrati, cum phrysii gemmati aurei elegantemente ambienti, cum molti adolescenti di omni gente bellicose, gesticulatrice, rixante, intra sé concursante in ludrico, cum navigea pugna ridibonde, contra quelli impetenti pervicace concertavano, intentamente sage alle spolie, subvertivano gli sui lembi, et asportavali, rimanevano spoliati et exuti nelle aque sencia favore, né facevano resistentia contra il suo male, ma festivi solaciavase. Upon the aforesaid most admirable banks, on one side and the other, were inlay-wise distributed bitter-oranges, citrons and lemons. From trunk to stock the interval three paces. Which stocks, a pace high from the ground, began to spread their branches, meeting one with another, and most excellently entangling, rendered an arch, from earth to the topmost bend three paces.

The remaining branches then, from one bank to the other over the river, opposite one another, with artful conjoining and social embrace, vaulting and enveloped, pleasantly shaded — in a most excellent pergola-work. The surrounding of which, with leafy density elegantly heaped, with the show of an even shearing, or clipping, one frond not exceeding another save with utmost grace and loveliness, rendering most placid shade, and, by the sweet breath of Favonius, tremulous and fanning, full of nourishment, that made its greenery lustrous, as in the first sprouting they showed themselves — with the whitening little flowers, and pendent fruits, fit hiding-places of the plaintive Philomela, ever most sweetly lamenting, singing, perfectly without echo, by the daughter Glaucopis of high and lightning Jove made clean and most purged, resounding. Which pergola-work, from the topmost convex down to the raised water, stood seven paces. Oh, with what allurement and sweetest suggestion to its contemplation it easily drew the wandering eyes! For in that river ran certain skiffs and little boats of golden material, delightfully covered, with many long- haired girls, fair-tressed and Alphesiboean, wreathed with fragrant and various flowers, rowing — in limpid and crisp and saffron shifts, or smocks, everywhere edged with gold, over the nymphish nude, clothed and girt with a wanton adornment, not impeding the sight to offer voluptuously the rosy flesh covered; nay, by the spring breezes voluptuously impelled and pressed upon the admirable women, it excellently revealed the delightful little form and any other part according to its motion, with the whitest and half-appled breasts unclosed to the rounding of the little nipples with the greatest and voluptuous grace, elegantly girded with gemmed gold Phrygian bands — with many youths of every warlike nation, gesticulating, brawling, rushing among themselves in play, laughing at the naval fight, contending persistently against those attacking them; intently keen on the spoils, they overturned their skirts and carried them off; the girls remained despoiled and stripped in the waters without favour, nor made resistance against their harm, but festively solaced themselves. These being then left, mocking among themselves, made new fights one with another, submerging the seized little boats; and so the submerged gave work to the rams in the water of —

ricuperare et innovare la iocosa pugna, cum festi et placivoli solatii, cum le gratiose bucce puellaremente ridendo commixti et acuti cridi tripudiante et gestiente. Ancora l’aqua di multiplici pesci, di forma bella, et varia, et praestante, cum il squamato di colore aureo, et glaucopi copiosa, la natura renitente non obstava quivi alcuna cosa, sicuri, et non fugiendo pavidi. Alcuni di grandecia tale, che geruli commodamente alla solatiosa pugna dille damicelle arientantise vehevano, amplexabonde cum le nivee sure, et decori pediculi, gli squamei, et cedenti corpi, sencia pisculento putire sunatavano molliculamente, et de qui, et de lì obliquantise, et cum muliebre conato exequitare volentise, l’una cum l’altra trahentise, inseme accatervati gli candidi cygni, cum la voce deprolata cum le effuse lachryme per lo amato Phaethonte, et lutre et castori cum altri animali aquatici, tuti laetissimamente sotto il velamento topiario a voluptuoso dilecto festigiavano, sencia altro pensiculato, che al suo piacere et solatio molestia causando inducesse, et cosa che obviasse displicibile nell’animo mio uno tacito desiderio repullulando. In questo foelice loco io volentiera ancora cum la mia diva Polia vorei aeternalmente cohabitare. Extincto poscia, et repudiato omni altro vago disio, sencia mora reciduava al mio firmatissimo intento in l’amore unico dilla mia quam amantissima Polia. Niente dimeno hora cosa superexcellente questo sencia dubio istimai, et superamoena, sopra qualunque dilecto, sopra ciascuna dolcecia di placere? Similmente nel primo circulare et arboroso clusorio degli nemori di uno et dil altro sexo sempre in ipso septo, cum quelli animali deputati a placeri cohabitavano. Negli virenti prati dentro dil peristylio, per altra inclinatione vidi innumeri adolescenti, et praestante puelle ad solatii, ad soni, et canti ad choree, et delectevoli confabulamenti, et puri, et sinceri amplexi, intenti al ornato et personale culto, camoene componere, et ad varie operature le damicelle studiose, et dedite. Ove iudicai più la virtute essere affectata, che altro salace piacere. Oltra poscia questo chiarissimo et oblectoso flume, cum continua circinatione era uno herbescente prato, quanto quello, che dal peristylio contiene fina al flume. Il quale flume cum symmetriati ponti era pervio, cum miro exquisito constructi di faberrima scalptura deliniati, di optimo marmoramento alternatamente uno di Porphirite et l’altro di Ophite, cum nitore splendido illucente, servabili il liniamento dille strate, tendente verso il mediano umbilico di epsa mysteriosa, et di omni foeconditate di delitie vernante insula. Dapò il praefato prato incomminciavano septi gradi in piano et in fronte pedali circinanti continui, in altitudine dunque scandevano pedi septe, et in latitudine

— to recover and renew the playful battle, with festive and pleasing solace, with the gracious cheeks laughing girlishly, mingled, and sharp cries leaping and gesticulating.

Moreover the water was copious with manifold fishes, of form beautiful, and various, and outstanding, with their scaling of golden and grey-green colour; nature, reluctant, opposed nothing here, so that they were secure and did not flee timid. Some of such bigness that, as bearers, they conveniently bore the girls, mounted, to the solacing battle — embracing with their snowy calves and comely little feet the scaled and yielding bodies, which without any fishy stink swam softly, and here and there twisting, and willing to do the women's bidding, one drawing the other. Heaped together were the white swans, with the voice sent forth with the tears poured out for beloved Phaethon; and otters and beavers, with other water-animals, all most joyously beneath the topiary covering made festival to voluptuous delight, without other pondering — so that nothing arose to cause molestation to their pleasure and solace, nor anything displeasing to oppose it — a silent desire sprouting again in my soul. In this happy place I would willingly still, with my goddess Polia, wish to dwell eternally. But then, every other roving desire extinguished and repudiated, without delay I fell back to my most firm intent, in the unique love of my most beloved Polia. Nonetheless I judged this without doubt a thing super-excellent, and super-delightful, above any delight, above every sweetness of pleasure.

Likewise, in the first circular and wooded enclosure of the groves, creatures of the one and the other sex, ever within that same hedge, dwelt with those animals appointed to pleasure. In the greening meadows within the peristyle, by another turning, I saw innumerable youths and outstanding girls, intent on solaces, on sounds, and songs, on dances, and delightful conversations, and pure and sincere embraces, given over to adornment and personal cultivation, to compose muse-songs, and to various works the studious and devoted damsels — where I judged virtue to be more affected than any other lascivious pleasure.

Beyond then this most clear and delightful river, with a continuous circling, was a grassy meadow as great as that which stretches from the peristyle to the river. This river was passable by symmetrical bridges, built with wondrous and exquisite skill, delineated of most excellent marble, alternately one of porphyry and the other of serpentine, shining with splendid brightness, preserving the lineament of the roads tending toward the middle navel of that mysterious island, verdant with every fecundity of delights.

After the aforesaid meadow began seven steps, level and pedal in front, circling continuous; in height they climbed seven feet, and in breadth —

altrotanto, di marmoraria operatura, uno di trigle rubente strisso, et uno di petra nigella obstante al celte più nigra, et di magiore duritudine dilla patavia illustrissima di sua natura, sencia fricatione di cylindro, overo cum pulvicula, et abuligine, exclusa la regula dilla crassitudine sextante, overo dodrante, né cum ritractione sesquipedale, né bipedale, ma como sopra è recensito, et cum tale mensuratione tuti gli altri. Sopra il supremo grado dilla nigerrima petra, uno ambiente, et elegante columnulato picnostylo promineva, intercisa la sua continuatione, overo intervallata alla regula dilla latitudine degli ponti observanti quella dille strate. Gli quali egregii ponti debitamente erano tecti dalla intemerata pergulatione supra il fiume, et quantunque directamente le strate tendesseno al centro, non erano per questo gli gradi dal suo integro circinao intersecti. Poscia como di proximo è dicto, sopra lo ascenso degli gradi era dille collumnelle tanta laxatione, quanta la latitudine dille itione praestavano. Ma la regia via de directo alla porta dil mirando circo tendente, dall’angustiare dille linie al centro opportunamente exclusa extava, et sola aequabile, et uniforme in la sua latitudine et il superascenso degli gradi se acclivava commodissimamente scansile. Et peroe in questo loco necessariamente gli scalini se trovavano discontinui. Il quale picnostylo cum duplicate columnule le plinthide delle spire, dille quale a normica distributione la una cum l’altra negli anguli per linea diagonale facevano mutuo contacto, nelle pleure recte intersite cum successiva copula, tra due una di alternata coloratione di praestante diaspro illucentissimo, et tra omni septe una quadrata di rubente resideva, capitata di una perlucida pila aenea deaurata, et le altre parimodo, ligate et concincte cum una trabecula, zophorulo, et coronicula dilla materia et coloramento dilla quadrata, cum decentissimi liniamenti. Supprimente gli capituli dille columnule, dille quale le interstitie di calcedonico constavano, in numero sei, una tra due di virente diaspro, di multiplice maculule pergutato, cum debita et sotiale distantia. Sopra il quale iucundissimo picnostylo vidi lascivamente discurrere et pausare innumeri pavoni, albicanti, rubri, et dil suo colore rotati alcuni, et altri cum le spectatissime pinne demisse, indi et quindi intermixte le specie tute degli psitaci cum non mediocre ornato et delitioso obiecto dilla dicta operatura. Gli fronti degli gradi vedevase exscalpti mirificamente di nobile, et assyriana innodatura, et per suo magiore expresso, lo interscalpitio venustamente era stipato plenamente di materia in rubro azurina, et negli nigri di candidante farcimento.

— as much, of marble work, one of red streaked triglia, and one of blackish stone, blacker than the chisel could withstand, and of greater hardness than the most illustrious Paduan of its own nature — without the rubbing of a cylinder, or with fine powder and soot; excluding the rule of the sextant or the dodrans thickness, nor with a foot-and-a-half's setback, nor two feet, but as is recounted above, and by such measuring all the rest.

Upon the topmost step of the blackest stone stood forward an encompassing and elegant little colonnade of pycnostyle spacing, its continuity interrupted, or intervalled, by the rule of the breadth of the bridges, which observe that of the roads. These excellent bridges were duly roofed by the immaculate pergola over the river; and although the roads tended directly to the centre, the steps were not on that account cut through from their whole circling. Then, as just said, above the ascent of the steps there was, of the little columns, as much opening as the width of the paths afforded. But the royal way tending straight to the gate of the wondrous circus, opportunely excluded from the narrowing of the lines toward the centre, stood alone, even, and uniform in its breadth, and its rise over the steps sloped most conveniently walkable; and so in this place the stairs were necessarily found discontinuous.

Which pycnostyle, with its doubled little columns, the plinths of the bases — of which, by an ant-like distribution, one made mutual contact with another at the angles by a diagonal line, set into the straight faces with successive coupling — between every two, one of alternated colouring of most shining outstanding jasper, and between every seven, one square of red, capped with a translucent gilt-bronze sphere; and the others likewise, bound and girt with a little architrave, frieze, and cornice of the material and colour of the square one, with most decent lineaments. Above the little columns' capitals, the interspaces were of chalcedony, six in number, one between two of green jasper spotted with manifold flecks, with due and companionable distance.

Upon which most pleasant pycnostyle I saw, wantonly running about and pausing, innumerable peacocks — whitening, red, and some fanned with their own colour, and others with their much-admired feathers let down — and there, intermixed here and there, all the species of parrots, with no mediocre adornment and delightful show upon the said work.

The fronts of the steps were seen carved wonderfully with noble and Assyrian knotwork; and for its greater relief, the intercarving was gracefully stuffed full of material, in the red ones azure, and in the black ones with a whitening filling.

Da questo spectatissimo columnato fina ad gli sequenti gradi, era complanato in una marmoraria strata circumgyrante, in latitudine pedi seni. Ove immediate incomminciavano altri septeni gradi a salire, cum la praefata operatura, mensuratione, et materia, et coloramento, et tuto questo negli sequenti era riservato integramente. Sopra il supremo era una capsea excavatura, pedi quatro in apertione. Il patore dilla quale a sufficientia era profundo, et cusì negli sequenti. Nella quale nasceva uno septo buxeo, quale vitrina illustratione gratissimamente virente. Et a llibella degli ponti, et strate, mirai una turre dilla dicta virentia, sublevata pedi nove, et lata cinque, cum una patente porta in hiato pedi tre, et alta sei, et cusì le sequente. Questo primo septo di crassitudine tripedale, et di sei alto, et cusì gli sequenti, vedevasi di foliamento densissimo, et era cusì disposito di pinnatura dilla propria arboratione. Tra una et l’altra turre vidi egregiamente facto uno triumpho cum caballi, una rheda trahenti, et praecedenti lo ovante alcuni militi macherophori, et cum haste triumphale artificiosamente compacti variando bellissime le opere. In uno altro interturrio promineva una navale enyo. Tra due altre turre clasica pugna terrestre. Tra due altre venatione et antiquarie fabule d’amore. Cum diligentissimo expresso, et exquisitissima deformatione. Tale ordine circumveniente variando le sequentie. Intro questo primario circumvallato, dapò una ambiente strata, quale quella inferiore tra il columnulato, et gli gradi di mirabile tessellatura offerivase di grande admiratione, et excessivamente delectabile artificio, sencia dubio di fatichare omni humana intuitione et senso. Il quale nel primo aspecto tapeti charaini dispositi et extensi stratamente alla planitie, facilmente arbitrai, cum tute maniere di coloratione, che a tale ostentatione meritamente expediva exprimere, in modo di gratiosa picturatione conducta in più variate et multiplice ingrupature et figure et signi cum la opportuna diversitate di coloramenti, di holuscule alla requisita distinctione dilla opera ficta. Alcune plene di colore, altre cum obscura coloratione, alcune mediocremente, tale più chiare et festichine, alcune prasine, altre di virore palide, alcune meno, et di subrubicundo coloramento, cum iucundissima conciliatione. Le figure principale continente in sé multiplice designature, era tra due rhombee, una circulare, et una rhomboida tra dui circuli, alternantise continuamente in gyro, exempta quella parte, ove le vie intercallavano providamente relicte. Le quale strate sempre tra due uniforme figuratione passavano.

From this much-admired colonnade up to the following steps, it was levelled into a circling marble path, six feet in breadth. Where immediately began another seven steps to climb, with the aforesaid work, measure, and material, and colouring — and all this was kept entire in what follows.

Upon the topmost was a box-like excavation, four feet in opening. The hollow of which was sufficiently deep, and so in those that follow. In which grew a hedge of box, of a glassy sheen, most pleasingly greening. And, level with the bridges and roads, I beheld a tower of the said greenery, raised nine feet, and five wide, with an open door gaping three feet, and six high, and so the following ones.

This first hedge, of three feet's thickness, and six high — and so those that follow — was seen of densest foliage, and was so disposed of the feathering of its own tree-growth. Between one tower and the other I saw excellently made a triumph, with horses drawing a chariot, and preceding the one who triumphs certain sword-bearing soldiers, and with triumphal spears artfully compacted, varying most beautifully the works. In another space between towers stood forth a naval Enyo. Between two other towers, a fleet-battle on land. Between two others, a hunt, and antique fables of love. With most diligent relief, and most exquisite fashioning. Such an order going round, varying in its sequences.

Within this first rampart, after an encompassing path — like the lower one, between the colonnade and the steps, of marvellous tessellation, which offered itself to great admiration, and an excessively delightful artifice, without doubt to weary all human sight and sense. Which, at first aspect, I readily judged to be charaine carpets laid out and spread flat upon the level, with all manners of colouring, which for such a show it fittingly served to express — in the manner of a gracious painting, conducted into more varied and manifold groupings and figures and signs, with the fit diversity of colourings, of little pot-herbs, to the required distinction of the feigned work. Some full of colour, others of dark colouring, some middling; some clearer and gayer, some leek-green, others of a pale verdure, some less so, and of a somewhat reddish colouring, with a most pleasant reconciling. The principal figures, containing in themselves manifold designs, were: between two rhombs, a circle; and a rhomboid between two circles, alternating continuously in a ring — except in that part where the ways were providently left to intercalate. Which paths always passed between two uniform figures.

Le quale deformatione extavano insepte in uno circulare liniamento imitante (come è dicto) la figura insularia. Et primo erano circundate dalla strata ambiente proxima al buxeo claustro, colligata emusicatamente cum le recte tendente al centro. Le quale strate erano silicate, la mediana parte, di septe partitione, tre aquistava per sé, di nigerrima petra dura et speculare, che di cusì facta nigritudine coticula indice nel fiume Ocho non sa troverebbe, et de qui, et de lì coniuncte immediate erano una partitione di petra lactea, di tale albentia, quale non se vide il composito lacticinio murianense contumacissima et perlucida, gli extremi erano due altre portione, una de qui et l’altra de lì di finissima petra rubentissima più che strisso corallio, et intra la nigra erano impacte faberrimamente le tesellature. Questa venusta dispositione observata se continia per tute le sequente, degli sequenti claustri.

Tra le recensuite strate intersticiamente circumsepte erano le praescripte figure. Dentro le rhomboide, circuli. Dentro gli circuli gli rhombi. Daposcia varie figure implicite di foecundissimo et gratioso cogitato. Nell’ombilico dille rotunde, plantato se attolleva uno alto cupresso. Nel meditullo degli rhombi uno dritissimo et comoso pino. Similmente negli circinanti frigii intra gli limiti dille strate, di uno et l’altro extremo, cum il moderato distributo di varie operature, et figure ovolate, et hemiale, nel puncto mediano insurgevano verdissime vrathe, per lo intermediato cupreseo et pineo corresponsive, et cum il cacuminato aequale, et dilla granditudine uniforme. Degli rami folte et di ordine cupresino, usate dalla divina matre a coelare la calumnia. Daposcia agli convenienti lochi solertemente gli spectatissimi fiori erano communicati di qualuncha coloratione dispensata, cum harmonia elegantissime cum redolente fragrantia. Di uno et l’altro sexo in questi bellissimi et amoenissimi vireti intervallati incollavano essi solamente all’opera dilla foetosa natura dediti, et al culto contenti ad conservatione di tale opere olitore operantise. Né tanta diligentia il iustissimo Re di Pheaci Alcinoo monstroe circa la custodia degli sui horti olitorii, quanta quivi era observata, cum mirifico, et sedulo u iii

Which fashionings stood hedged in a circular lineament, imitating (as is said) the island's figure. And first they were surrounded by the encompassing path next to the box-cloister, joined mosaic-wise with the straight ones tending to the centre. These roads were paved: the middle part, of seven partitions, took three for itself, of blackest hard and mirror-like stone — of such-made blackness that a touchstone in the river Oxus would not be found; and here and there, joined immediately, was one partition of milky stone, of such whiteness as the compounded milk-glass of Murano — most obstinate and translucent — was never seen; the outermost were two other portions, one here and one there, of finest stone reddest more than streaked coral; and among the black were most excellently driven the tessellations. This comely disposition, observed, is continued through all the following ones, of the following cloisters.

Between the recounted roads, hedged round in the intervals, were the aforewritten figures. Within the rhomboids, circles. Within the circles, rhombs. Then various figures interwoven, of most fecund and gracious contrivance. In the navel of the round ones, planted, rose up a tall cypress. In the middle of the rhombs, a most straight and tufted pine. Likewise in the circling friezes between the boundaries of the roads, at the one and the other extreme — with the moderate distribution of various works, and figures egg-shaped and half-round — at the mid-point rose up greenest vrathe (savin), corresponding through the intervening cypress and pine, and with the summit even, and of uniform bigness. Of branches thick and of cypress-order, used by the divine mother to hide the calumny. Then, in the fit places, the much-admired flowers were skilfully imparted, of whatever colour dispensed, with most elegant harmony and redolent fragrance.

Of the one and the other sex, in these most beautiful and most pleasant greeneries, intervalled, they applied themselves solely, devoted to the work of fostering nature, and content with cultivation, labouring at the conservation of such garden-works. Nor did the most just king of the Phaeacians, Alcinous, show such diligence about the custody of his kitchen-gardens as here was observed — with wondrous and sedulous —

IL SECUNDO claustro olitorio sequita immediate dapò la proxima recitata operatura. Nel extremo dilla quale ordinatamente gli altri septeni gradi verso il centro incohavano. Sopra l’ultimo era compacta una variata conclusura di arborario coloramento summamente spectatissima, cum turre, overo specule optimamente congeste di Narancii, et collateraneamente alla porta erano in ordine implantati dui stipiti, cioè tra il pariete dilla turre, et tra l’apertione, overo alamenti dilla porta, quegli fora dil culmo dilla turre extollentise, mutuamente se colligavano in uno redacti, sublati dal supremo dilla turre tripedali. Poscia la fulta frondatione principiava deformantise in uno moderato cupresso. Et cusì in circuito per tutte, di dui passi la sua proceritate. Lo interturrio septo di coloramento variava, et di arboramento, tra due era texuto di iunipero. Tra du’ altre di lentisco. Poscia di Comari, di Ligustro, di Dendro Livano, di Cynocanthe, di Olea, di Lauro. Cum uno modo sempre nel suo recente virore frondeo. Reiterando dopo l’ultimo il primario successivamente bellissime di opera topiaria, immune di ostentatione stipea, cum non periture fronde. Ove tra due turre sopra il planato, in medio uno mirando pinnato emineva. Imperoché interstitiamente dil murale septo, prosilivano piante di buxo, cum exquisito artificio conducti in symmetriate lune cornicularie, a compimento dil spatio interturrio, cum il suo patore, overo hiato supinato, cum singulare diligentia deformate. Nel mediano tra dui corni saliva uno iunipero gradatamente decado pinato, fina alla cima attenuantisi, quale si al torno turbinate fusseron, et coaequate le pungente frondule. La più crassa nel mediano hiato collocata. Tra il cornicio ascendeva surrecto uno stipite transcendendo uno pedi et semisse, ove rotundava una pila buxea iustissimamente proportionata. Intro questo claustramento, tra gli limiti dille vie erano quadrati holuscularii di miranda factura, dissimili di disignatione olearia alternabondo a compimento di tutto questo spectando circuito. Il primo quadrangulo per le strate dal altro discriminato, per le quale illegitimi quadri evadevano, era una innodatura dil liniamento quadrale per fascicule deformato, cum maxima politura, late palmi tre. La prima fascia nel mediano era in circulo demigrata, et dagli dui anguli se ricontravano le fascie al circinare, l’una superna dill’altra. Il quale annulo in sé un’altra fascia innodava, divisi dalla prima inclusive pedi quatro. Et quella parte

— study, every thing applied in pairs, appearing born in its destined place. Illumined by the marbles with distinct splendour, and so the circlings that follow.

THE SECOND kitchen-garden cloister follows immediately after the just-recited work. At the edge of which, in order, the other seven steps toward the centre began. Upon the last was compacted a varied close of tree-colouring, most highly to be admired, with towers, or watch-turrets, most excellently heaped up of orange-trees; and, flanking the door, were planted in order two jambs — that is, between the wall of the tower and the opening, or door-leaves, those rising out beyond the top of the tower mutually joined themselves into one, lifted three feet above the tower's summit. Then the thick foliage began, fashioning itself into a moderate cypress. And so, in circuit through all, of two paces its height. The between-tower hedge varied in colour, and in tree-kind: between two it was woven of juniper. Between two others, of mastic. Then of arbutus, of privet, of dendrolibanum (rosemary-tree), of dog-thorn, of olive, of laurel. Ever in one fashion in its fresh green foliage. Reiterating, after the last, the first successively — most beautiful of topiary work, free of any tow-stuffed show, with never-perishing fronds.

Where, between two towers, upon the level, in the middle a wondrous pinnacle stood out. For, in the intervals of the wall-hedge, sprang up plants of box, with exquisite artifice led into symmetrical crescent-horns, to the completing of the between-tower space, with its hollow, or upturned gap, fashioned with singular diligence. In the middle, between the two horns, rose a juniper, step by step declining in tiers, up to the top attenuating, as if they had been turned whirling on the lathe, and the pricking little fronds made even. The thickest set in the middle gap. Between the horn-work ascended, upright, a stalk, mounting a foot and a half, where a box-sphere rounded itself, most justly proportioned.

Within this close, between the boundaries of the ways, were kitchen-garden squares of wondrous make, dissimilar in the plotting of the herb-work, alternating to the completing of all this admirable circuit.

The first quadrangle, marked off from the other by the roads — by which the illegitimate squares came out — was a knotwork of the square lineament, fashioned by little bundles, with the greatest polish, three palms wide. The first band in the middle passed off into a circle, and from the two angles the bands met again at the rounding, one above the other. Which ring knotted within itself another band, divided from the first, inclusive, four feet. And that part —

Daposcia se causava un altro quadrato aequidistante dal secundo, quanto esso secundo dal primo, et questo similmente il suo angulo se incirculava, verso l’angulo dil secundo, sopra la linea diagonia, intricantise, cum la rotundatione, scandendo et subigendo. Dentro questo novissimo quadrato rhombeava una figura. Gli anguli dilla quale cum stricti voluti innodulavano il mediano dilla fascia dil ultimo interno quadrangulo.

Nel spatio triangulare tra il rhombo et lo intersticio quadrato, sopra le linee diagonie, ad implemento era uno libero circulo, dentro il rhombo era uno circulo per la capacitate dilla figura rhombea disnodato. In medio dil quale circulo, era una octophylla rosa. Nel centro dilla quale era constituita una inane ara, rotundata di petra di flavo numidico cum tre capitale ossature di bove, tra l’una et l’altra di temerario exscalpto pandavano fasciculi di fronde et fructi, nel medio turgescenti, cum vagabondi lori circa gli capi ligando innodati, et cingiendo gli fasciculi, cum eximio liniamento, al socco et all’abaco cum bellissima sima, et altri ornati decorabondi. Fora dilla quale ara nasceva una savina, in forma compacta cupressina, stipata la apertione di l’ara di multiplici cherophile. u iiii

— of the circle that was uppermost was subjected to the other band, and so now one, now the other, alternating under and over. And this second squaring, at its angle, ringed itself, occupying from angle to angle, ever alternately keeping the band-lineament, now uppermost, now undermost, to the regulation of the knots.

The first rings widened out within the second square, making a circling rotundation to the capacity of that square. Then there was caused another square, equidistant from the second as that second from the first; and this likewise ringed its angle toward the angle of the second, upon the diagonal line, entangling itself with the rounding, climbing and passing under. Within this newest square a figure went rhomb-wise. The angles of which, with tight volutes, knotted the middle of the band of the last inner quadrangle.

In the triangular space between the rhomb and the square interval, upon the diagonal lines, as a filling, was a free circle. Within the rhomb was a circle, unknotted to the capacity of the rhombic figure. In the middle of which circle was an eight-petalled rose. In the centre of which was set an empty altar, rounded, of stone of tawny Numidian (marble), with three head-bones of oxen; and between the one and the other, of daring carving, hung little bundles of fronds and fruits, swelling in the middle, with wandering thongs bound in knots about the heads, and girding the bundles, with choice lineament — at the socle and the abacus, with most beautiful cyma-moulding, and other adorning ornaments. Out of which altar grew a savin, in compact cypress-form, the opening of the altar stuffed with manifold chervil.

The kitchen-garden expression of the picture of the preceding square — thus was the colouring distributed. And first, the first band was most densely of marjoram. The second of southernwood. The third of ground-pine. The rhomb of mountain thyme. The rounding contained in the rhomb, of germander. The rose, of amethystine violets. In the circuit of the rose, and outside its bound, were sweet-scented violets, densely flowering. The four circles within the last square, in the triangles made by the rhomb and enclosed by the square, were of melanthium, that is gith (love-in-a-mist), within yellow violets. All the hedging in these triangles, of cyclamen. The circles between the first and second square were filled with rue. The circles of the third, with flowering primrose. In the first bound, between the first and second square, were plotted acanthus-leafings, one opposed between two, of mountain polium, bordered with maidenhair. In the centre of the circles set upon the diagonals, for each, was raised about a foot and a half a sphere, with an even law kept in all — of height and spherical girth and placing — excepting the four caused by the interposed square at the diagonal angles. In which, at the centre, rose up three-cubit stalks of purple and mallow-coloured hollyhock, many-leaved and five-leaved, with a large flowering yield. In the first, santonica (wormwood). In the second, rosemary-tree. In those fashioned by the circular figure contained with the outer and first square band, at the centre were spheres of hyssop. From the encompassing roads, both the straight ones to the centre and the transverse ones near the green hedge, and near the steps the excluded — such was the squaring, and of the rest.

The other companion square of this close, of most pleasant and comely and wondrous work, and device, and marvellous kitchen-garden distribution, and most noble knotwork, distinguished in the colouring of various simples. — a rhombic demonstration knotted, with its angles too, and these — both, and the transverse, and perpendicularly conjoined — and by such mutual commerce, and likewise these, another octagon within the first they prettily formed, companionably encloistering the nine squares.

Wherefore all these figurations, joining one with another, under and over and alternating, rendered graciously an elegant knotwork of manifold figuring. All this square completely figuring. Which fashionings were lined by plates fixed in the ground, of whitest marble, a half-dodrans their surface thickness, and here and there walling round the simples. Within which stony enclosure, between the bounds, the little herb-varieties, most evened and thickly, germinated together to the perfect expression of the figuring; and all this likewise observed throughout every such composite artfully stood. A show, by Jove, conspicuous, and to the senses supremely most pleasant.

The pictorial kitchen-garden distribution stood thus: every free square enclosure was clothed with flowering cyclamen. Its bands, of myrsinites (butcher's-broom). The band-bounds of the others, knotted with the meeting-section, were herbaceous of mountain polium. The four little squares of the crossed section, joined within the square, were all covered with thyme. The octagons walling round the free squares, with the requisite allotment of little herbs, stood thus greening: one of laurentia. One of tarragon. The third of yarrow. The fourth of groundsel. The fifth of diosmo (buckthorn). The sixth of terrambula. The seventh of baccar (sowbread). The eighth of marjoram-gentle. The ninth of maidenhair. These two recounted squares, alternately in a ring, filled up this close most admirably.

DI PROXIMO sequita, et gli altri septi gradi. Sopra il sublime circumcludeva in modo di parietale muro di verdissimo myrto, cum le turre, come le altre designate cum gli cupressi, et cum tuto il residuo, cum pinnatura classica optimamente congesto. Verso il septo, nel primo spatio tra gli circuli tre .GNA. Et nel sequente quatro .DICA.

— in the middle square a porphyry altar resided, at the angles of which, near the little cornice, stood forth four horned wethers' heads, snail-wise and smoothly carved. From which hung down incurving little bundles, with all the accessories recited of the round one. Upon which lay an antique amphora-vase, with four handles equally divided, of finest sardonyx most beautifully conjoined with its familiar agate, expressed with wondrous artifice. Out of which came forth a most-polished box, with its lower sphere, or rounding, of one pace its diameter. On the top of which were four stalks, equally a foot apart and distinct, and each a proportioned sphere; upon each a peacock, with its tail let down, resided, with its head in a salver upon a middle stalk exceeding the four spheres. Out of the salver ascended a stalk again, with four branches, and each sustained a sphere. In the middle, raised, the stalk held another sphere. Upon which grew an egg-shaped circle, spreading two branches to a side, and each had a sphere, and the like at its top. Such a disposition was ordinarily observed in every vase, uniform — the place, the box, altar, vase, lineaments.

NEXT follows, and the other seven steps. Upon the height it encompassed, in the manner of a wall, of greenest myrtle, with towers — like the others fashioned, with cypresses, and with all the rest, with a fleet-like feathering most excellently heaped up. Within this cloister likewise were two squares, alternated of kitchen-garden figuring, with such a plotting. There were two quadrangles banded with symmetrical knotwork, and with the enclosing circle, in the manner of the quadrangle first described. In which circling, egregiously painted, was seen an Eagle with outspread wings, comprehending the circular space. In place of the acanthine foliage, there were majuscule (capital letters); and first, at the left side, between the two bands, in the space excluded by the circles: in the first were two, AL. In the other excluded, four, ESMA. Toward the hedge, in the first space between the circles, three, GNA. And in the following, four, DICA —

The bands, circles, and the interstitial rounding, of rue densely pressed. The eagle, of thyme; what is excluded by the surrounding bounds, of mountain polium. The letters, of marjoram, walled round with iva (ground-pine). The filling of the circles was: of one, flowering violet-blue; one, of yellow; the third, of white, with a great yield of its florets, never falling, but perennially flowering. And the pot-herbs continuously, with uniform evening, greening — forbidden to proceed to the destiny of fecund nature. From the triangle caused by the rounding and by the angles of the inner bands, was for each a little circle of the herbaceous colouring of those enclosing; the excluded, of butcher's-broom. In the centre of which were planted four spherical spheres of most-compressed and most-evened myrtle, with a two-foot stalk, and of the rest.

The other quadrangle — bands, circle, as is now recounted. In the circling were two birds: on the one part an Eagle, and on the other, facing, a Pheasant, with beak straight to beak. Upon the lips of a vase they footed. Which had a scrap of a foot-stalk, and from the back the wings of both raised. Between the volutes of the bands, on the eagle's part, in the first and lower space, were formed three letters, SUP. In the other, three, ERN. In the upper part, in the first space, three, AE·A. In the following, three, LIT. On the pheasant's side, in the upper space, three, IS·B. In the one below, three, ENI. Between the two lowest bands, in the first space, three, GNI. In the following, three, TAS.

(The letters of the second roundel spell, between the eagle and the pheasant:)
SUPERNAE ALITIS BENIGNITAS
— "The benignity of the celestial bird."

Within the circuit, inwardly, beyond the bounds of the figuring, all was of mountain polium; the pheasant of laurentia; the eagle of groundsel; the vase of asarabacca. In the gap between the encompassing lips, of butcher's-broom. The outer bands, with all their course, of periwinkle. The other band, of trinità (heartsease). The circles in the intruded angles, of marjoram-gentle. What is excluded and intruded of these, of digitello. The letters, of thyme; the spaces, of maidenhair. The fields of the round band-work, of santonica. In the centres of the circles, between the triangles, was a sphere — two of sweet-smelling southernwood and two of lavender, raised a foot and a half upon its stalk. In the rest, alternately, a sphere of savin and one of juniper, three feet. All the herbs with most comely thickness, and freshest verdure, and most pleasant look. A wondrous work of exquisiteness, of pleasantness, and delight. Irrigated by narrowest little pipes, orderly distributed, vomiting a most tenuous and droplet-like sprinkling.

FOLLOWINGLY, with the kept rule, ascended other seven steps, upon the topmost of which a much-admired railing encompassed the whole, of red and most illustrious jasper, with elegant open-work, agreeing concordantly to most pleasing patterns, of a sextant's thickness. This railed hedge, and the following, was without any opening, but continuous; and here ended the roads tending straight to the island-centre — but it stood passable only in the triumphal road, and so the following.

In this voluptuous cloister I beheld a grove, conspicuously shady of density, of most-celebrated shrubbery. Here were the two female terebinths, tenacious to old age, of choice and blackish splendour, of pleasant odour; bdellium with oaken foliage; the malus medica (citron), perennially apple-bearing. Precious ebony, the pepper-tree, clove, nutmeg, the threefold sandalwood, cinnamon, the most-praised silphion — which would not be found in the vale of Jericho, or in Egypt at Matarea. Here the whitening costus, which the isle of Patale does not produce. And the shrub nard, with its ears bearded, most praised of spike and of leaf. And the aloewood, of unspeakable sweetness, which the headless Nile does not carry down; and storax, and stacte. And —

l’arbore thurea, et myrthea, quale non germina in Sabea, et infiniti altri arbusculi, et frutici aromatici, cum aequatissimo solo, contecto undique di assaro, quale non è in Ponto, né in Phrygia, né in Illirico, emulante il nardo. Il quale delitiosissimo loco era statione et convento degli più novi, et bellissimi ucelleti, che unque ad gli ochii humani fusseron obiecti, visi, né cogniti, alla opera dil amore intenti, subitarii cum gratissimo garulato, tra la modesta densitate degli rami dilla vivacissima, et nunque cadiva virentia canori. Il quale beato, et foelice, perameno et frondoso nemore, le prorumpente aquule per canaletti et cursivi rivuli dagli liquidi chiarissimi, et sacri fonti, cum soporoso murmure discorrevano. Et quivi sotto le fresche et conserte umbricule, et al suave reflexo tra le novelle fronduscule, il multiplice et arguto garito discorrendo, innumere et illustre Nymphe cum l’altro sexo erano latitante, ad uno discreto piacere da gli altri, et cantante cum antiquarii instrumenti dal suave Cupidine fugitive, et alle opacissime ombre et rurestri facti intente. Vestivano deformemente di sericei habiti sutilli et crispuli et cataclisti, di semicrocea tinctura, et la magiore turbula di olorini, et caltuli et galbani, et alcune di colore colossino, cum crepidule et nymphei calciamini. Hora tutte le inquiline di questi voluptuosi lochi, sentendo il triumphoso advento dil sagittante signore, sencia mora subite obvio festivante venerabonde sa presentorono, le novissime excepte. Daposcia al suo peculiare solatio et continuo oblectamento ritornorono. Postremamente ultra il memorato nemorulo, sencia alcuno intercalato, et l’altra novissima gradatione di septe scalini sequente, cum observata norma haveva sopra il sublime grado una spectatissima conclusura di uno columnulato, quale il primo dopo lo antedicto fluvio di artificio et materia. Et quivi era circumvallato una sectiliata spatiosa, et expedita, et complanata area, cum mirifico invento di tessellato emblematico, et cum innodature di circuleti, triquetri, quadruli, et conoide figure, et almoide, et hemiale, et rhomboide et scaline deformato pulcherrime in multiplice designature coeunte, et cum speculare collustratione, et cum egregio distincto di varia et eximia coloratione. Finalmente il medio tercio dil milliare, dal flume al centro in tale commensione era emusicatamente distributo. Il quale semitertio dunque constava di passi (como praedicto è) .clxvi. et semisso. Diqué al fluvio dati sono passi .xii. al prato ultra il flume .x. Tuti gli gradi occupavano longitrorso passi .viii. et pedi .ii. et in altitudine universale altrotanto. La stratella pedi .vi. Dilla commensuratione insulare, satis.

— the incense-tree, and the myrtle, which does not germinate in Sabaea, and infinite other little trees and aromatic shrubs, with a most-evened soil, covered everywhere with asarabacca, which is not in Pontus, nor in Phrygia, nor in Illyricum, rivalling the nard. Which most delightful place was the station and gathering of the newest and most beautiful little birds that were ever set before human eyes, seen or known — intent on the work of love, sudden, with most pleasing chatter, singing among the modest density of the branches of the most lively and never-falling verdure. Which blessed and happy, most pleasant and leafy grove — the bursting waters, by little channels and running rivulets from the liquid, clearest and sacred founts, ran with a drowsy murmur. And there, beneath the fresh and interwoven little shades, and at the soft reflection among the new little fronds, the manifold and sharp chatter running on, innumerable and illustrious Nymphs, with the other sex, were hiding — at a discreet pleasure apart from the others — and singing, with antique instruments, fugitive from sweet Cupid, and intent on the most opaque shades and rustic doings. They were dressed shapelessly in silken habits, subtle and crisp and pleated, of half-saffron tincture, and the greater crowd of sweet-scented and marigold and galbanum-yellow, and some of colossine colour, with little sandals and nymphish footwear.

Now all the dwellers of these voluptuous places, feeling the triumphant advent of the arrow-shooting lord, without delay suddenly, festively venerating, presented themselves to meet him — the newest excepted. Then to their peculiar solace and continual delight they returned.

Lastly, beyond the recorded little grove, without any intercalation, and the other newest gradation of seven steps following, with the observed norm, had upon the high step a much-admired close of a little colonnade, like the first after the aforesaid river, of artifice and material. And there was walled round a sectile, spacious, unencumbered and levelled area, with wondrous invention of tessellated emblem-work, and with knotworks of little circles, triangles, small squares, and conoid figures, and almond-shaped, and half-round, and rhomboid and scalene, most beautifully fashioned into manifold designs coinciding, and with mirror-like illumination, and with egregious distinction of various and choice colouring.

Finally, the middle third of the mile, from the river to the centre, was in such measurement mosaic-wise distributed. Which semitertio, then, stood (as aforesaid) at 166 paces and a half. Wherefore to the river are given 12 paces; to the meadow beyond the river, 10. All the steps occupied, lengthwise, 8 paces and 2 feet, and in universal height as much. The little path, 6 feet. The first garden, 33 paces; the second, 27; the third, 23. The grove, 25. The area encompassing the theatre, 16. The theatre, from its middle to the centre, contained 16 paces. Of the island's commensuration, enough.

Notes

Confidence: high (for the closing measurement-table, which is arithmetic); medium for the grove and nymphs above it. Chapter XXII

USCITI FORA DILLA NAVICULA ALL’INCONTRO INFINITE NYMPHE VENERON CUM TROPHAEI SUPERBAMENTE INDUTE. POLIPHILO NARRA, ET IL MYSTERIOSO MODO, CHE GLI DIVINI GESTAMINI A CUPIDINE ELLE OFFERIRONO, ET CUM QUALE HONORARIO PROCESSO, POSTOSE A SEDERE SOPRA IL TRIUMPHALE VEHICULO. ET POLIA ET POLIPHILO AMBO LIGATI DRIETO SEQUENTI, CUM MAXIMO TRIUMPHO ALLA PORTA DIL MIRABILE AMPHITHEATRO PERVENERON. IL QUALE, ET FORA, ET INTRO PLENAMENTE ELLO IL DISCRIVE.

SUAVEMENTE CUM MITE AURA SPIrante zephyro vibrate molliculamente le decore et auree pinnule dil divino puello, et cum il suo tranquillo spirito vehente al refluo littore pervenuti molte et infinite semidee dorophore, et insigne Nymphe, cum perspicua pulchritudine, exeunti nui dilla fatale navicula. Di rincontro praestamente, al divino, et aligero puero, cum agregario agmine, cum magno apparato di ornamenti, et di pompe, et sumptuosi vestimenti, cum divo fasto, et culto, più che regio, cum exquisitissimo exornato praecipue et solemnemente venerante, di tenera, et florentissima aetatula quam iucundissime pyrriche, cum virginei allectabuli, et coelesti, et illustri aspecti humilmente, et cum decentissimo famulitio obsequiose tute se dapatice offerirono. Et ante tute le thereutice pastophore, pyrgophore, et le antiludie iubilante praecedevano, cum trophaei di militare decoramenti in haste di oro sicilitate dispositi, cum la thoraca dil furiale Pyroente, cum l’altre armature devicte, et cum l’arco transversariamente pendice retinente la thoraca, et cum la spiculata pharetra et secure alle extremitate dil arco invinculate, et sotto la thoraca explicato lo rete, cum una subiecta facie di puerulo alata, et gemina, et uno pomo suffixo alla facia nella hasta per medio traiectante, et nella summitate la stellata galea. * * * * *

* * *

*

(Chapter argument:)

HAVING COME OUT FROM THE LITTLE BOAT, TO MEET THEM INFINITE NYMPHS CAME,
SUPERBLY ARRAYED WITH TROPHIES. POLIPHILO NARRATES ALSO THE MYSTERIOUS
MANNER IN WHICH THEY OFFERED THE DIVINE INSIGNIA TO CUPID, AND WITH WHAT
HONORARY PROCESSION, HE BEING SET TO SIT UPON THE TRIUMPHAL VEHICLE; AND
POLIA AND POLIPHILO, BOTH BOUND, FOLLOWING BEHIND, WITH GREATEST TRIUMPH
CAME TO THE GATE OF THE MARVELLOUS AMPHITHEATRE — WHICH, BOTH WITHOUT AND
WITHIN, HE FULLY DESCRIBES.

Sweetly, with a mild breeze breathing zephyr, the comely and golden little wings of the divine boy softly vibrating, and by his tranquil spirit conveyed, we came to the ebbing shore. Many and infinite gift-bearing demigoddesses, and notable Nymphs, of conspicuous beauty, came out with us from the fateful little boat. Straightway, to meet the divine and winged boy, with a chosen troop, with great apparatus of ornaments, and of pomps, and sumptuous vestments, with a splendour and cultivation more than regal, with a most exquisite adornment, especially and solemnly venerating, of tender and most flourishing little age — most delightfully in pyrrhic dance, with virginal allurements, and heavenly and illustrious aspect, humbly, and with most decent attendance, obsequious, all bounteously offered themselves.

And before them all, the sacrificing shrine-bearers, the tower-bearers, and the jubilant fore-dancers went preceding, with trophies of military ornaments arranged on gilt spears: with the cuirass of furious Pyroeis; with the other conquered armours; and with the bow, hanging crosswise, retaining the cuirass; and with the barbed quiver and the axe bound to the extremities of the bow; and, beneath the cuirass, the spread-out net; with, set below, the face of a little winged boy, and a twin one; and an apple affixed to the face, transfixed through the middle by the spear; and at the summit, the starry helmet.

Notes

Confidence: medium. The chapter-argument (the whole triumph of Cupid in précis) and the landing: the nymphs come out with trophies to meet the winged boy, and the first trophy of arms is described piece by piece (cuirass of Pyroeis, bow, quiver, axe, net, apple, starry helmet, all on a gilt spear). All secure; the trophy-vocabulary is antique-military.

Un’altra era gestante d’un altro tropheo, nel mucrone era una strophiola di lauro di sotto uno paro di ale, di nigerrima aquila expanse, et poscia subiaceva uno vulto di nobilissimo fanciullo, sequiva et poscia transversariamente dui fulmini conligati cum fascicule di oro, et di seta texute volante, et alla hasta etiam in transverso ligato, uno sceptro suspensa tenendo una soperba veste. Gerula era ancora un’altra d’uno trophaeo. Di una galea, cum uno capo bubalo, et di sotto una toraca antiquaria, cum dui scuti per singulo exito brachiale connodati, tra gli quali due fasciole una per lato perpendevano pensile tenente la cleonea pelle, cum la umbilicata et glandulosamente torosa clava.

Another was bearing yet another trophy. On the point was a little band of laurel; beneath it, a pair of wings of blackest eagle, outspread; and then, set below, lay the face of a most noble boy. There followed, and then, crosswise, two thunderbolts bound together with little bundles of gold and woven silk, flying; and, likewise bound crosswise to the spear, a sceptre, holding suspended a superb robe.

A bearer, too, was another, of a trophy: of a helmet, with a buffalo's head; and beneath it an antique cuirass, with two shields knotted at each brachial outlet; between which two little bands, one to a side, hung pendent, holding the Cleonean skin, with the knobbed and gland-knotted, sinewy club.

Notes

Confidence: medium. Two more trophies of arms: the second Jovian (laurel, black-eagle wings, thunderbolts, sceptre and robe — the disarmed panoply of Jupiter); the third Herculean (buffalo-headed helmet, cuirass, twin shields, and the Nemean lion-skin with the knotted club). All secure; the attributes identify the gods whose arms Love has taken.

Subsequente et una attolleva uno tropheo cum praecipua politura. In uno mucrone demigrava il supremo hastile, subdescendendo uno copertorio sopra una rotunditate semisextante crassa, in modo platineo resupinava, nel mediano insculpta una formula circinata. La quale uno pauculo di vaseo pediculo supprimeva. Poscia una tabella cum tale scriptura maiuscula (QUIS EVADET) subiaceva.

<!--[Iscrizione nella figura:] QUIS EVADET? -->A questa uno pomulo subigeva, et subsequente un’altra rotundatione, quale la superna, ma minore. Da uno alamento circumvallata et ad una solida overo massicia scutella superassidente. Dalla quale continuato descendeva uno longiusculo balusto, et poscia una pileta.

Similmente portava un’altra Nympha una hasta. Nell’acuminato era una figura ovola, cum orulo bullato in circinao, et nel meditullo uno rotundamento saphyreo la figura imitante di crassitudine unciale, subacta una assula, tale cum titulo. NEMO. Et in medio di due ale, la hasta alquanto ballusticata intraiectava. Infimamente una scutella sequiva, quale sopra è recensito.

Next in sequence, one raised a trophy with special polish. On a point passed off the topmost spear-shaft; descending below it, a covering over a rounding half a sextant thick, resupine in the manner of a plate, in the middle of which was carved a circled formula. Which a small vase-stalk suppressed. Then a tablet with this majuscule writing subjacent:

QUIS EVADET?
— "Who shall escape?"

To this an apple was set below, and next another rounding, like the upper but smaller. Walled round by a rim, and superimposed upon a solid, or massy, little dish. From which, continued, descended a longish baluster, and then a little basin.

Likewise another Nymph carried a spear. On the pointed end was an egg-shaped figure, with a bossed little rim in a circle, and in the middle a sapphire rounding, imitating the figure, an inch thick; set under it, a small board, thus with the title:

NEMO
— "No one."

And between two wings the spear, somewhat balustered, passed through. At the bottom a little dish followed, as is recounted above.

Notes

Confidence: high (the two inscriptions are read straight from the woodcut; the trophy- description is secure). Two emblematic trophies bearing the paired mottoes QUIS EVADET? ("Who shall escape?") and NEMO ("No one") — one of the most famous devices in the whole book, and one Colonna clearly means to be read as a single sentence.

Consequent was another most noble trophy, borne aloft. On the high spear-shaft a sphere stood forth upon a pyrronium, between an inflected lunar-crescent of two little wings, subtly leafed with gold foil, one leaf pressed page-wise upon the other.

The remainder of which, forced into a circling, rendered a crown held by a little band, through the middle of which the spear, slightly balustered, passed perpendicularly. Under the crown a little basin, with the bottom of an ewer-vase descending upon the oarage of two conjoined wings. Then an egg-shaped figure, with a byssus-boss coruscating in the navel. Fixed under this was a sphere, scored melon-wise, with flying little bundles opportunely bound in.

Many others, of prolix telling: the standard-shafts of which — some of ebony, others of red, citrine and white sandalwood, and of whitest ivory, and gilt, and covered with silver, and of other precious woods. Every thing skilfully fashioned of most tenuous gold, silver, and of polished material, and formed of green silk, and of every other gracious colouring, with pleasant flowering. Multiply adorned with gems in the fit places, every thing harmoniously destined and conspicuously applied, with pendent vertebrae —

— or little whorls of precious stones, pierced, through which was drawn a golden filament; and every thing requisitely depicted with most pleasant colouring, and shining with a scintillating illumination. And the gesticulating dancers gloved with gloves of needle- texture, in manifold little knots and florets of gold and silver thread, and of silk of various colouring artfully interwoven, with a purse-like closure in a jewelled phrygium (embroidered band) at the rosy and fleshy arms, with little gold cords beautifully mingled, elegantly heaped, of various colouring of elegant silk.

Before all these trophy-bearers ran an outstretched and displayed one: the standard-bearing of the little boat's conveyance ran ahead with prompt bearing. And immediately, in imitation, followed another, with a triumphal celibar (standard), which at the top had a winged Cupid, unveiled, in the act of drawing with the bow, treading upon an apple; at the top, inflected, a jewel of laurel-foliage of foil. Which was upon an inverted bottom of an ewer-vase. And there it was ensnared: the little bands, licking above the interval of the jewel, burst out flying. Within the content of the circle was a tablet, through the thickness of which the spear bored, and through a sphere superimposed on the tablet in the middle of the outer part; and so in its lowest part another, subjacent, penetrated. Likewise in the abacus-work, on both the left and right side, a little spear-shaft jutted somewhat, passed through the jewel, and stood forth outside. From the one and the other jutting pendant were two little cords twisted of gold and silk, threaded through berries of precious stones. Under the reverse of the jewel, an ewer-vase with its bottom uppermost pressed on; and its orifice, in a balustered opening, bit an egg-shaped figure, with lineaments imitating the form. With two little apples above the circumference, one to a side, in the middle of the diameter; at the bottom another sphere. Lastly a golden little knot, with varied texture, fluttering. On the tablet, on the one and the other face, was, in Greek majuscules, this title:

ΔΟΡΥΚΤΗΤΟΙ (DORYKTETOI)
— "The spear-won" (captives taken by the spear).

Et molti altri falerati cum molto polimine di varii flori, fronde, et fructuli, non sencia sotiatione di bractee d’oro, et argento, et di omni coloramento enchausticamente infecte, cum infiniti ornamini et exquisita elegantia di politura, progrediente laetissime, cum altre assai insigniture et victoriale notule, et di exuvie et spolie et manubii trophaeali decoramenti, et dal potente et divino fanciullo sagittabondo, devicte praecedente cum moderata distantia di triumphale processo, et cum molto plauso et divo thiaso, et cum soni et gutturiali iubili dille pheretrie gestante. Daposcia prima apresentatovise la sua diva uxore Psyche, cum regilla veste, cum fulcimento villoso, et chermeo oro collustrante di textura aurea triliciata et tramata, di lucente serico induta. Quale Hermaria et Malicha di Hesperia non produceno. Et subsequente le comite di sericie veste di discreta et versicolore infectura laute et genialemente indute, cum habiti superbi invisitati et novi, opulentissimamente dialithi. Quali la natura nostra ad gli humani ingegni novercamente fare interdice, cum crispule pliche rugosamente sopra gli delicati et quam mundissimi corpusculi, et sopra le verginale anche, al suave impulso dille fresche aure inquietule. Alcune cum inhaerenti pectorali di squamea operatura aurea, cum venusto et sotiale ornato di lucentissime gemme. Et in colore phoeniceo, aliquantulo quella incochleatura di vernanti x ii

And many others, trapped-out with much polish of various flowers, fronds, and little fruits, not without the company of gold and silver foil, and infected with every colouring encaustically, with infinite ornaments and exquisite elegance of polish — proceeding most joyously, with many other insignia and victory-marks, and with trophy-ornaments of exuviae and spoils and plunder, conquered by the potent and divine arrow-shooting boy, preceding with moderate distance of triumphal procession, and with much applause and divine revel-rout, and with sounds and guttural jubilees of the quiver-bearers.

Then, first, there presented herself his goddess-wife Psyche, in a queenly robe, with a villous underlay, and shining with crimson gold, of gold texture triple-twilled and wefted, clad in lucent silk — such as Hermaria and Malicha of Hesperia do not produce. And, in sequence, the companions in silken robes of discreet and versicolour dye, sumptuously and genially clad, with superb habits, unwonted and new, most opulently jewelled — such as our nature interdicts the human wit to make, step-motherly — with crisp pleats wrinkling over the delicate and most-clean little bodies, and over the virginal hips, at the soft impulse of the fresh, restless breezes.

Some with adhering breastplates of scaled gold-work, with comely and companionable adornment of most-lucent gems. And others had set their fitted garments over the whitest breasts, whiter than the winter frosts of Capricorn, with adornment and delight at the first swelling of the untamed little breasts — like little rounded apples, upright-nippled, with a half-cluster put forth and affixed — and above them, up to the breast-band, in a cyanean depilate colour, a sumptuous phrygium wound spiralling, stuffed with oriental pearls, somewhat swelling, occupying bounteously the whole diaphragm, or all that part above the girdle, of artful volutes, of egregious foliage, such as was never invented in Phrygia by the Phrygian embroiderers. And in a Phoenician (crimson) colour, that spiralling somewhat of springing —
