Which silken garments were some shorn, others villous, and some in triple and quadruple little gradations of pile, with work in most-pleasing figures of flowers and little animals excellently woven. Others with silken cloth of silk warp and weft of gold and silver in every colouring and figuring, interwoven with the utmost disquisition of art. Some with zoned vestments alternately — now a gold, now a cyan, then green, and consequent silver — and with the remaining colourings distributed amicably and correlatively, not without comely texture, and voluptuous show. Some had oyster-purple investitures, and twice-dipped Tyrian; and others the saffron-mantles, such as were never in Scythia made, of the arboreal down (cotton), diversely segmented and variegated. Others, with a thin cotton pall, shadowed the noteworthy belly, closely adhering.

Accessorily, on their tufted heads, ornamental nymphish fillets, gemmed with wondrous congruity, wormwise worked in most-exquisite little nets of choice invention; and with ribbons with golden filament squared in rhombic figure and reticulated, and covered with golden tiaras — so that the twisted and doubled threads were distinct one from another, with a triple association, one gold twist standing forth above two silken, with exquisite little knotting where they met. Among which the circular and resplendent gems were variously sewn in order, according as the variety of the garments is told. Others, a golden diadem divinely binding the head.

— some with twisted little knots, with a tight swelling prettily arranged, the shapely nape gathered nymph-wise into a little clump, and bound again with little cords of silk and gold, twisted. Others had the most-tenuous hair over the flat temples most ornately woven in. And some then held part of the festive forehead comely-occupied with hair curled, twisted and crisped, like illustrious golden threads, and with narrow tendril-like curls circling round; and, for some, the fore-blowing little locks covered the rosy forehead, and, at the moderate breezes delightfully inconstant, lay decorously about the head and neck. The rest, thence, of the copious and abundant hair, fluctuating with the vaguest little waves, measured from the most-delicate shoulders down past the round calves, above and down from the firm and freshest buttocks, most profusely wandering, ran.

Some, wantonly, the cleanest hair let down from the ornate nape, and hanging from the occiput, with even discrepancy from a golden binding, dividing here and there over the milky neck. Then beautifully, and with choice polish, sinuated and balled up into an egregious whirl, they elegantly met at the topmost crown, thinned to a point, where a most-precious tuft of pearls, to the highest voluptuousness, bound both the braidings; and beneath, the little breezes masterfully conducted, passed over the flat temples, and arrived, with even thinning, at their seat and voluptuous meeting.

For some the hair was reddish, with much-admired wrappings girdling round the head with an amicable springing floret. Some golden, more than leafy orpiment of outstanding lustre, and of colour, with a decorous whirl composed tendril-wise to the nymphish and voluptuous adornment, with a pretty adjunction of various and precious little stones, and to the expression of extreme delight; some blackish, more than the anthracite plumes of Aesacus, in comely volume shining, covered with limpid golden veils, and above the parting, held with hairpins, with a conspicuous and gracious flight, with manifold curled knots, with much resplendence, with art and artifice that exceeded every wanton study and nymphal voluptuousness — combedly combed, and most-decorously composed. Little snares baited with the retaining, biting, and noxious birdlime of amorous hearts. With whorls of inestimable value hung at the pierced ears, and with most-precious necklaces and segments torqued and girt the straight and snowy throat, with wicked collars sumptuously begemmed, with more excellent research of adornment, and in decorating so much womanly affectation as could never subtly be excogitated.

Ove uno gentilicio ritramato di policaria latitudine circundava di petre pretiose mirificamente exornato, cum laccioli d’oro et di seta. Alcune a pede nudo gli vermiglianti evandii di seta, cum voluptica vanitate gestavano. Molte calciavano soccoli di coreo aureo suppressamente caelati molti eleganti expressi. Assai le crepidule di rosaceo corio portavano orulate d’oro. Tale exquisitamente di expolito et lunato calciamine calciate, overamente cum recurvata apertura, et di amentate solee, cum più nove et maravegliose ligature, ligule et coregie, che mai dir se potesse, di seta caesia et di filamine d’oro, cum gli più vagi et grati implicamenti circa il polposo talo innodantise, che divisare nella mente se valesse, et dall’angusta solea il laqueolo in nodulo bellulamente intricato, di armenica textura usciva impedito tenendo il police digito. Et d’indi verso il minimo demigrava più bianchi che lla calcinatura ossea. Poscia supra le tuberule suffragine scandendo bellissimamente se coniugavano, cum una lingula demissa dal circundante ligamine dil calcaneo supra il culmo dill’eburneo pede, di vermicularia operatura aurea di lucente gemme decorato. Alcune di panno sericeo de raso cum figure per tale artificio congeste, cum variato coloramine volupticamente calciavano, discreto da quello dil superbo et nympheo induto. Concluse decore cum fibule auree, cum adunco morso et illaqueate. Sopra le exquisite crepidule gli tumiduli pecioli suppressi dagli eleganti strevli ansulati d’oro, et gli obstraguli margaritati, et cum nextruli di colorita seta et d’oro cum gli capi di terso argento infixi, erano decentemente innexi ritenuti, cum sì bello decoramento da cecare et exoculare gli effrenati tori. O quanta politura, bellitudine, et nitella, quanto perspicace ornamento, quale insuete operature. Cum quanta acre diligentia vedevase excogitatamente reperto artificio di dare ad gli intuenti dolci, et incentivo piacere, et morire opiculo. Nel vestire similmente concinate oltra la superba et delitiosa inventione tanto decore, quanto condignamente il foelice loco expostulava, cum artificiose fimbrie et lancinule, overo fracte cum subtile intercisura di versicolore et cum mirabile et vermiculario suticio decorissime. Et dal stricto cincto in giù dinanti ordinatamente propendevano gli aurei porpici di vermiculario lavorio. Alcune in loco degli piruli havevano margarite elenchi di crassitudine avellanea. Nel imo cum longitudine fastigiata di alabastrea figura, et similmente nel loco degli porpici aurei, che era una textile lista, overo fasceola aurea di latitudine sextante, di operamento vermiculario. Dal suo sinuato sina all’ambiente fimbria copulantise tra le minute

Beyond this, some, with most-purple buskins on the little feet, with gold ankle-pieces, shod themselves trimly. Which terminated upon the white pulp of the calves, where a genteel re-woven band of a policaria's breadth went round, wondrously adorned with precious stones, with little laces of gold and silk. Some, barefoot, bore the vermilion silk sandals with voluptuous vanity. Many were shod with soccoli of golden leather, closely chased with many elegant figures. Very many wore crepidae of rosy leather, edged with gold. Some exquisitely shod with polished, crescent-shaped footwear, or with a recurved opening, and of thonged soles, with more new and marvellous ligatures, laces and thongs than could ever be told, of blue-grey silk and of golden filament, with the vaguest and most-pleasing interlacings knotting round the fleshy heel — more than one could devise in the mind — and from the narrow sole the little snare, prettily entangled in a knot, of Armenian texture, came out, impeded, holding the great toe. And thence toward the least (toe) it passed off, whiter than bone-lime. Then, climbing over the little swelling insteps, most-beautifully they conjoined, with a little tongue let down from the surrounding binding of the heel, over the top of the ivory foot, adorned with wormwork of gold and of lucent gems.

Some, of silken satin cloth, with figures heaped by such artifice, with varied colouring voluptuously shod, distinct from that of the superb and nymphish garb. Concluded comely with gold buckles, with hooked clasp, and ensnared. Over the exquisite crepidae, the little swelling insteps pressed by the elegant little gold-eyeleted straps, and the pearled instep-covers, and with little knots of coloured silk and of gold with the ends of burnished silver fixed, were decently interwoven, retained, with so beautiful an adornment as to blind and put out the eyes of the unbridled bulls. Oh, what polish, beauty and brightness; what perspicacious ornament; what unwonted works! With what keen diligence was seen the thoughtfully-found artifice of giving to the beholders sweet and incentive pleasure, and a little death.

In the dressing, likewise, they were arrayed, beyond the superb and delightful invention, as comely as the happy place condignly demanded, with artful fringes and slashes, or breaks, with subtle intercutting of versicolour, and most-decorously with wondrous wormwork stitching. And from the tight girdle downward, in front, hung in order the golden little tags of wormwork labour. Some, in place of the little pears (tags), had chains of pearls of hazelnut thickness. At the bottom, with a fastigiate length of alabaster figure; and likewise in place of the golden tags, was a woven strip, or golden band, of a sextant's breadth, of wormwork. From its sinuous bosom to the encompassing fringe, coupling among the minute —

Et tale quale le pirulate margarite havevano gemme turbinate cum le complanate inquadrature, et cum le terete cum iucundissimo commercio et elegante consenso dil suo colore et deformatura variatamente dispensate, et summa cum venusta congruentia. Alcune etiam mirai cum superbo et divo culto, cum insigne et egregio, et sumptuosissimo et incredibile exornato, et di pretiositate incomparabile. Le quale sopra gli fianchi indute erano di torace sinuate sericee di amethystino raso luculeo. Et d’indi retro al ultimo spondilo, et al foemine, cum moderata curvescentia dependente, di tomentata foliatura antiquaria, et di mediocre perle contecte. Gli vertigini dilla quale cum nodulature supra le mamillule terminando, et sopra l’ombilico diffinivano in albente. Negli quali erano di pretiosissimi lapilli rosule, et altri mirabili expressi, in oro inclaustrati, et cum inchaustico operamento eximie deornati, et vermiculariamente depoliti. Gli labri dille quale thorace di miro efferato di syrmata textura aurea, gli phrygii iugimente ambivano, et nello infernate extremo alternatamente crassissime perle, et piruli cum moto inconstante ludibonde dependevano. D’indi poscia una investitura di seta verde cum tramatura aurea descendeva, et ad gli genui fina derivava. Di sotto questa fina ad gli tali ultra, un’altra era demissa, di Chermeo intramato d’oro. Le quale cum frequentario pliculamento erano inphimbriate, di latitudine semi et unciale, cum binato ordine negli extremi di crasse margarite. Et nel contento dil phrygio dilla prima inextimabili rhomboidi di gemme occupavano. Tra le quale cum partiario coloramento le circulare interposite lucevano et in campo aureo artificiosamente deputate. Ultra gli labri extremi, erano meruli serratamente formati, et in omni angulo degli quali uno ritondato lapillo pendeva. Et nel imo tra gli meruli instabilmente straleti d’oro ventilabondi. Dal quale phrygio fili d’oro in forma rhombea innodulati descendevano, et da una pillulata gemma retenti. In ciascuna dunque dille sectione interposita era una gemma, per la quale transversariamente uno syrmato filo aureo traiectava. Sopra la prope dicta gemma, nel limbo dil phrygio appacta assideva una circulare simile alle collaterale di forma et crassitudine, cum auree barbule pendente, nel narrato filo transversario, nel mediostimo dilla figura rhombea, uno mirabile lapillo ovolato era intromisso. Et nel mediano dille pleure, similmente infilata era una pillulata gemma, ciascuna cum recta distributione et elegante ordine, et dil coloramento variate aequalmente obviantise. Tutti gli infernati anguli dilla narrata figura intercalariamente, in uno praependeva una inextimabile tabellula quadrata pretiosa, sotto la quale et dalla quale suspeso pendeva uno baccato lapillo. — slashes distributed. And such as the little pear-shaped pearls had turbinate gems with the flattened squarings, and with the rounded ones, with most-pleasant commerce and elegant consent of their colour and fashioning, variously dispensed, and with the utmost comely congruity. Some, too, I beheld with a superb and divine cultivation, with notable and egregious and most-sumptuous and incredible adornment, and of incomparable preciousness. Which, over the flanks, were clad in sinuate silken cuirasses of amethystine bright satin; and thence, behind, to the last vertebra and to the thigh, hanging with moderate curving, of padded antique foliage, and covered with middling pearls. The whorls of which, with little knots ending above the little breasts, and above the navel, defined themselves in white. In which were little roses of most-precious stones, and other marvellous devices, encloistered in gold, and choicely adorned with encaustic work, and wormwork-polished. The lips of which cuirasses, of a wondrously wild train of golden texture, the embroidered bands continuously went round; and at the lower extreme, alternately, thickest pearls and little pears, with inconstant motion, hung playfully.

Thence then a green silken investiture, with golden weft, descended, and reached down to the knees. Beneath this, down past the ankles, another was let fall, of crimson wefted with gold. Which, with frequent little pleating, were fringed, of a half-inch's breadth, with a doubled order at the extremes of thick pearls. And in the field of the first embroidered band, inestimable rhomboids of gems occupied. Among which, with distributive colouring, the circular ones interposed shone, artfully assigned in a golden field. Beyond the outermost lips were merlons serratedly formed, and in every angle of which a rounded little stone hung. And at the bottom, among the merlons, unstably, gold darts fluttering. From which embroidered band gold threads knotted in rhombic form descended, and were retained by a pellet-gem. In each, therefore, of the interposed sections was a gem, through which crosswise a trained gold thread passed. Above the just-said gem, in the border of the band, set fast, sat a circular one like the collateral in form and thickness, with little gold barbs pendent; on the said crosswise thread, in the very middle of the rhombic figure, a marvellous egg-shaped little stone was let in. And in the middle of the sides, likewise threaded, was a pellet-gem, each with straight distribution and elegant order, and of the colouring varied, evenly meeting. All the lower angles of the said figure, intercalated, in one there hung an inestimable square precious little tablet, under which, and from which, suspended, hung a berried little stone. l’altra assula gemmale appensa era una oblonga overo ovola, tutte di mira crassitudine. Il phrygio dilla secunda tra le margarite in forma hemiale erano tabellule di pollicaria longitudine et di semi la latitudine, intervallate tra due circulare, et daposcia tra due quadre, cum altri additamenti di lapilli, negli vacui venustamente appacti et di ornaculi ad delitia et gratia optimamente inserti, da obstupire essa natura, cum iugi fulgetri praelucentissimi fulguranti, et di convenientia dil coloramine iucundissimi, cum le maniche bellissime, et superbi brachiali cum ornato thoracale, et allo exito brachiale et al collare di tali recensiti phrygii, et ornati circundavano bellissime.

— the other appended gemmed board was an oblong, or egg-shape, all of wondrous thickness. The embroidered band of the second (nymph), between the pearls in half-round form, were little tablets of a thumb's length and half that breadth, intervalled between two circular ones, and then between two square ones, with other additions of little stones, comelily set fast in the voids, and of little ornaments to delight and grace most excellently inserted — to stupefy nature herself — with continual most-lucent flashings coruscating, and most-pleasant in the convenience of their colouring; with most-beautiful sleeves, and superb arm-pieces with an adorned breast-work; and at the brachial outlet and at the collar, of such recounted embroidered bands, and ornaments, most-beautifully they went round.

The arms were bipartitely covered, from the outlet to the elbow; and from the elbow to the grasp of the hand was the little sleeve, of all-golden texture, woven with choice low-relief work, walled round by a little embroidered band of nymphal study diligently invented, prettily laced at the elbow-division, with gold cords, or with little twisted cordlets. In the divisions was an elegant outlet of a copious little cluster, with a curved little folding of the snowy cotton chemise, most-tenuous and shining, bound in the decent part, with silken little snares doubled into armlets, or let into flattened gold circlets, and pointed with pure gold, with pendent little knots, with exquisite virginal (art). For there desire, and the thing coveted, with the knowing and the power, reconciled itself with the will. So much, by Jove, to the amorous eyes was it most-pleasant, that one would truly desire nothing else but to be able perpetually to gaze — and Nymphs so comely, and of such shapeliness and delight, outstanding, with their bright-eyed aspects so sumptuous, and wantonly comely, and so, with snowy garments of the utmost polish, pre-shining.

Cum tanta provocatione, che all’aspra morte lo homo ultroneamente se offerisca et volente. O contagioso artificio. O allectivo ministerio, o propugnante machine. Qualunque core sano tutissimo, valido et quieto, et libero, et renitente, di facilmente inficere, repente di subvertire et depopulare, sencia mora prosternere, sencia indusio perturbare, totalmente subiugare, et sencia relucto trahere. Di prostrare discussamente omni moderata, incredibile et pervicace continentia. Questi sono quegli veraci et infallibili sateliti dil operoso amore cum ampliabile numero di immanissimi carnifici, le immune remote, et disiuncte pensiculatione insidianti, et da tale inclinante subiectione exclusi di potere per alcuno modo, né cum audente animo prevalerse, et dalle praesentate inevitabile, et trahente dulcitudine, cum tanto solerte et diligente studio illaqueabile, et cum subtile ingegnio invento tanto exquisitissimo operamento et ad tanto bella et formosissima opera per sé dalla foecunda natura, artificio da distrugere ritrovato, et eximio adiuncto et accessorio di cruciare. Omè il quale ragionevolmente fingere se doverebbe, et usare per le marmoree statue, et non per gli humani et fragili cori. Imperoché ancora quel nobile et divo sexo, spoliato et denudato summamente contorque et infice periclitabondi, non che cum questo voluptico additamento di laquei pernitioso investigato. Ma solo questo accede, che elle non pensiculano essere assai il d’amore naturalmente perire, sencia tanto aucto incremento di excogitati modi adiungere gli miseri et molliculi amanti allo interito dil cordolio più facilmente di conducere, et continuamente excitare scintillule extremamente incendiose da vegetare alla consumptione le amorose et fervide flammule, divi obiecti di rimpire et confundere il caldo pecto di bullienti suspiruli, et di fermentare il core ad amore. Hora io non saperei debitamente exprimere per quale modo uno tanto fundamine iacto d’amore stabile et sincero fermamente in Polia collocato, che alquanto quassare il sentisse da queste inevitabile et parate insidie et impetuose violentie. Omè poscia tra me tacitamente replicava. O prophilea Pollia bellatula mia, custodi la tua adepta praeda. Imperoché grande periclitamento, è il transito per tante pyratice fallacie, et manifesti sicarii et praedonuli insidiarii. Gli quali contra omni sincera rectitudine sono dil suo amoroso offendimine commendati et laudabilmente approbati et dagli tristi offensi, tanto più desiderati et riverentemente più requisiti et più dolce amati. Hora nella sua laetissima fronte, sotto a due hemicycle, quale filo di seta subtile, et nigerrime ciglie più che illustrante electro, dui sagittabondi ochii festevolmente lucevano più che irradiantissime stelle nel lympidissimo coelo splendescente. Et nel aspecto suo più belle che incarnate, et ricente rose, et le gene non miltate, ma cum più gratissima et genuina rubedine, che degli

With such provocation that to bitter death a man would offer himself, of his own accord and willing. O contagious artifice! O alluring ministry, O assaulting siege-engines — which any heart, however sound, most safe, valid and quiet, and free, and resisting, you easily infect, suddenly subvert and depopulate, without delay prostrate, without respite perturb, totally subjugate, and without struggle drag away. To lay low, scatteringly, all moderate, incredible and stubborn continence. These are those true and infallible satellites of laborious Love, with an amplifiable number of most-monstrous executioners, ambushing the immune, remote and disjoined reflections, and excluding those inclined to such subjection from being able by any means, nor with a daring spirit, to prevail — and, by the presented, inevitable and drawing sweetness, with so shrewd and diligent study ensnaring, and with subtle wit invented so most-exquisite a working, and to so beautiful and most-shapely a work by fecund nature herself, an artifice found for destroying, and a choice adjunct and accessory of torment. Alas — which reasonably ought to be feigned, and used, for marble statues, and not for human and fragile hearts. For even that noble and divine sex, despoiled and denuded, supremely torments and infects the imperilled, let alone with this voluptuous addition of pernicious snares devised. But this alone accrues: that they do not consider it enough to perish of love naturally, without adding so much increased increment of excogitated modes, to conduct the wretched and soft lovers to the death of heart-grief more easily, and continually to excite little sparks extremely incendiary, to quicken the amorous and fervid little flames to consumption — divine objects, to refill and confound the warm breast with bubbling little sighs, and to ferment the heart to love. Now I would not know how duly to express by what means so great a foundation of love, stable and sincere, firmly placed in Polia, felt itself somewhat shaken by these inevitable and ready ambushes and impetuous violences. Alas, then, within myself I silently repeated: O my dear little beautiful Polia, guard your won prey! For a great peril is the passage through so many piratical fallacies, and manifest cut-throats, and little ambushing plunderers — which, against all sincere rectitude, are for their amorous offence commended and laudably approved, and by the sad, offended ones so much the more desired, and reverently the more sought, and the more sweetly loved.

Now, on her most-joyous forehead, under two half-circles like threads of subtle silk, and blackest brows more than shining amber, two arrow-shooting eyes festively shone, more than the most- irradiating stars resplendent in the most-limpid sky. And in her aspect more beautiful than incarnate and fresh roses; and the cheeks not rouged, but with a most-pleasing and genuine redness, than which —

meli decii nel vinifero autumno vermigliante et più lucide che il bianchissimo eburo perfricato, ove il Sole se dimonstrava più chiaro forsa che tale ad gli Achimenii Titano non apparisse. Et nel sacro Antro Mitra ad gli Persii, quale exponevase nell’advento dil suo signore le delitiose et dive Nymphe cum venerandi sembianti conspicue, cum ornati gesti morate et morigere, cum non visitata bellecia spectatissime, et cum praestante ligiadria eximie, cum elegante honestate praeclare et decore, et cum integra procacitate ornate et comptissime.

Ad questo puncto meritamente cum veneranda caterva valante, et cum frequente comitato prosequente la belissima Psyche gratificabonda riverendo il Carissimo Marito tutta agevola et blandicella ricevete, et cum summo venerato, nel capo una pretiosissima corolla poseli, che tale non fue la votiva di Hiero. Daposcia distinctamente sequendo molte altre laetissime trine et trine, cum solemne processo, et ordine, et honorificentissima pompa, et decente veneratione, et cum Nymphale geniculatione ormomene veneron.

Et primo vene Toxodora, la quale il sinuato, et vulnifico arco accortamente gli offeriva. Il quale rigorosamente era extento. Questa era in medio di due altre, Ennia una, che nelle tuberule mano gerulava uno dedolato vasculo Amphoe di colorissimo saphyro cum iucundissima splendescentia, cum latulo orificio emusicatamente exscalpto. Dal quale alquantulo di clivulo di multiplice florato cum maxima politura expresso, alla dilatata corpulentia moderatamente descendeva, sopra lo initio dil gracilarse dalla tornata corpulatura recedente verso lo orificio le ansule adhaerescente, in vipereo effigiato diligentissime deformate. Il limbo elegantemente gululato mordicavano. Daposcia il circuncincto corpulescente nel extrinseco di miri simulachruli coelato, et praepolitulamente conficto, et ove il corpulamento incominciava demigrare in angustia dil gracile et oblongo fundo, era di

— apples of Decius (?) in the wine-bearing autumn, reddening, and more lucid than the whitest ivory rubbed smooth, where the Sun showed itself more clear, perhaps, than such a Titan appeared to the Achaemenians (Persians), or to the Egyptians Osiris, or Isis, or Serapis with the calathus, or the three-headed simulacrum, or Mithra in the sacred cave to the Persians — such did the delightful and divine Nymphs expose themselves, at the advent of their lord: conspicuous with venerable semblances, mannered and well-mannered with adorned gestures, most-admired with unvisited beauty, and choice with outstanding gracefulness, illustrious and comely with elegant honesty, and adorned and most-comely with entire boldness.

At this point, deservedly, with a venerable band flying, and with frequent retinue following, the most-beautiful Psyche, full of gratitude, revering her dearest Husband, all easy and blandishing received him; and with the utmost veneration set on his head a most-precious little crown, such as was not the votive one of Hiero. And two of the Nymphs, honourably accompanying her — Imeria the one — placidly received Polia; and the other, Erototimoris, most facetious, took me by the hand. Then, distinctly following, many others, most joyous, three and three, with solemn procession, and order, and most-honorific pomp, and decent veneration, and with nymphal genuflection, came in motion, revering.

And first came Toxodora (Bow-giver), who cunningly offered him the sinuous and wound-dealing bow, which was rigorously extended. She was in the middle of two others: Ennia the one, who in her little swelling hands bore a chiselled little amphora-vase of most-coloured sapphire, with most- pleasant resplendence, with a broadish orifice mosaic-wise carved. From which a little slope of manifold flowering, expressed with the greatest polish, descended moderately to the dilated corpulence; and above the beginning of the tapering, receding from the turned body toward the orifice, the little handles adhering, most-diligently fashioned in a viper-effigy. They bit the gullet-moulded rim elegantly. Then the girt-round swelling body, on the outside carved with wondrous little images, and most-politely fixed; and where the body began to pass off into the narrowness of the slender and oblong bottom, it was of —

canaliculi obliquiscenti, cum moderati rivuli inalveati excavato, finivano supra uno nodulo, cum temperato tumore al solerte reperto, cum uno subacto pedulo, stipato di bellissimi et multiplici flori, quegli disseminante. — little channels obliquing, excavated with moderate rivulets in-channelled, ending upon a little knot, with a temperate swelling to the shrewd device, with a subjected little foot, stuffed with most-beautiful and manifold flowers, disseminating them. And her consort Phileda (Love-of- pleasure) gathered up in her lap those which Ennia, solacingly scattering, disseminated.

Likewise, between two other most-adorned little Nymphs, came forward Velode; she festively presented to her lord a marvellous quiver, superb of artifice and of egregious invention, with two most-swift arrows — one with the shaft of pure silver, and the other with the pointed shaft of black, heavy, cold and inauspicious lead. That with this he might gird his tender and divine flank, or his shoulders, with the flying weapons. And her companions Omonia (Concord) and Diapraxe (Accomplishment), with two balls, joyfully played, one to the other alternating. But of shining gold was Omonia's, and of fragile crystal her Diapraxe had; and when one of them caught up the gold one, she sent back the crystalline, with a shrewd avoidance of their meeting together.

To these, then, in order, the others followed: the most-beautiful and reverent Typhlote (Blindness), with religious and deserved honours and dignification — she offered, bowing, a most- tenuous veil, that with it he should band and cover his unveiled eyes. She had with her two wanton damsels, of immodest semblance. Of which one, named Asynecha (Heedlessness), turning now to the right side, now to the left, inconstant; and Ardelia Comazista (Busy-Reveller) danced. And like Thymele the histrionic, leaping, a jester, and a notable lamia.

And the other, Aschemosyne (Shamelessness), among all the clothed ones naked, undaunted and salacious, presented herself — neither more nor less than if she had drunk from the fount of Salmacis. And in her left hand she held a sphere formed of golden plates, with its centre. And with her right she would not consent to let her long hair, taken up, extend over the fleshy and quivering buttocks, with unashamed petulance, like a wanton huckstress — but indicating a most-obscene Tribade insolence, with an extolling of her inconstant, blue-grey eyes, and with a lustful act, like a bold Gaditanian (Cadiz dancing-girl), gesticulating with too much lasciviousness, ineptly, and more than the most-filthy Hostius had in himself, gazing in his hollow mirrors.

Newly, three other unpremeditating matrons presented themselves: Teleste (Initiatrix) the first, clad in ardent purple, with her loosed and abundant hair, and on the fillet-bound forehead little curls hanging in front — she, elegant, gave to her god a kindled torch. And a companion, Vrachivia the bearer, gave of an emerald —

— little urn, most-excellently expressed, and of antique artifice, and of an audacity insolent for human workmanship. Its orifice had contorted striations, which striae, or rivulets, ending moderately in the middle amplitude of the vase, with most-even distribution somewhat dilated. Then the comely girt-work descended toward the round bottom, the corpulence little by little thinning, clothed with an inestimable making of celery-leaf foliage, standing forth from the solid with egregious separation; and, continued from the lips of the orifice toward the down-slope, twisting themselves into exquisite foliage, two little handles they rendered, with wondrous relief, and with a little foot.

Out of which broadish orifice, flying and crackling, with a most-pleasing pop, sparks leapt out, running lucent through the air; which then, extinguished, fell as ashen sparks.

The other companion, Capnolina (Smoke-girl), bore a little goatish, or earthen, vase, with a narrow orifice, raised somewhat high and thinning at the bottom; and above its dilation, under the handles, it had circularly, with even distance, thirteen most-measured Greek letters diligently impressed:

ΠΑΝΤΑ ΒΑΙΑ ΒΙΟΥ (PANTA BAIA BIOU)
— "All the things of life are little [trifling / short-lived]."

With many other ornaments and obliquing little channels. Which, from the letters toward the orifice, was polished-smooth. Out of which, and through the vent-holes, leapt forth a misty and sweet-smelling smoke, resolving into nothing through the air.

Recepte finalmente le oblate mysteriose et fatale erotenara et convenienti gestamini. Il divino puello ad sedere se pose sopra uno aureo et antiquario carpento, overo vectabulo ivi triumphale praeparato, tutto di lamine d’oro per tutte le fimbrie circumambiente uno phrygio dodrante in latitudine. Nel quale solariamente lucevano inclaustrate ostentuose gemme di granditudine et inopinabile pretio, et di artificio imperceptibile et di invento rarissime, divinamente disposito, et ordinatamente destinato. Cum due vehiculare rote habente la circunferentia d’oro, et gli radii procedenti dal centrico axe, nel quale il polo iaceva, et infixi nel meditullio erano deformati longiusculi in balaustico figmento di petre pretiose varie di coloramento fulgurante. SEDENDO dunque ello sencia protracto di tempo prensi et capti ambi dui fussimo, Polia, et io dalle praestante Nymphe Plexaura et Ganoma, per lo imperatorio nuto dil triumphante puellulo dominante, et reiecte da retro di ambi dui le brace, et al tergo restricte le mane, come captivi Polia et io fussimo illaqueati et vincti, cum trece et serticuli di rose et vario floramine connexi et resticulati. Et retro alla pomposa, et divinata rheda dil athlophoro, et maximo triumphatore eramo tracti molliculamente voluntabondi da Synesia Nympha praestantissima. Per la quale cosa incomminciai quasi di trepidare. Ma per che le Nymphe cum la mia cosmoclea Polia facetamente si ridevano di subito me assicurai. Sequiva immediate dapò nui la curiosa Psyche. Et retro lei erano sequace le puere damicelle, che venerante havevano offerto. La quale Psyche era patagiata, overo superinduta di culto matronale et ambitiosissimo manto d’oro overo chlamyda, che tale a Dario non donoe Silofonte. Né cusì facta Numa Re primo inventore poté usare. Supra lo humero dextro complicatulamente iniecta et in orbiculi suppressi d’oro ansulata, ove tra crassi carbonculi purgatissimi paragonii, et di splendore corruscanti teniva inclaustrato uno adamante sesquidigitale longitrorso et complanato, et in latitudine policari, et di ferrineo scintillare, completo di mirando stupore, cum inextimabile decoramento prominente, che di tanto pretio non fue da Gige il dono dato ad

(The inscription on the smoking vase, continued:)
PANTA [BAIA BIOU]

Received finally the offered mysterious and fateful love-tokens and fitting insignia, the divine boy set himself to sit upon a golden and antique car, or triumphal vehicle, there prepared — all of golden plates, an embroidered band a dodrans wide going round all the fringes. In which, sun-like, shone encloistered ostentatious gems of bigness and unthinkable price, and of imperceptible artifice and rarest invention, divinely disposed, and orderly destined. With two vehicular wheels having the circumference of gold, and the spokes proceeding from the central axle, in which the pole lay; and, fixed in the middle, they were fashioned longish, in balustered figment, of precious stones various of fulgurant colouring.

Sitting, then, he — without protraction of time — both taken and captured were we, Polia and I, by the outstanding Nymphs Plexaura and Ganoma, at the imperial nod of the triumphing, dominant little boy; and, both our arms thrown back behind, and our hands bound at the back, like captives Polia and I were ensnared and bound, connected and re-netted with tresses and little wreaths of roses and various flowering. And behind the pompous and divined car of the prize-bearer and greatest triumphator we were drawn, softly and willingly, by Synesia (Understanding), a most-outstanding Nymph.

For which thing I began almost to tremble. But because the Nymphs, with my world-key Polia, laughed facetiously, of a sudden I was reassured. There followed immediately after us the curious Psyche. And behind her were following the girl-damsels who, venerating, had made offering. Which Psyche was bordered, or clad over, with a matronal and most-ambitious mantle, or chlamys, of gold — such as Silophontes did not give to Darius, nor could Numa, first king and inventor, use such-made. Over the right shoulder, foldedly cast, and eyeleted in pressed gold circlets, where among thick carbuncles most-purged and touchstone-tried, and coruscating with splendour, she held encloistered a diamond an inch-and-a-half long lengthwise and flattened, and a thumb in breadth, and of iron-like scintillation, full of wondrous stupor, with inestimable adornment standing forth — such that of so great a price was not the gift given by Gyges to —

Apolline Phithio. Nel quale, non dal nobile Pyrgotele, ma più praesto divinamente era Cupidine faberrimamente exscalpto se medesimo crudelulamente lancinante, et Psyche incauta pertractante cum la sagitta cum punctura laetale, ove il brachio soluto portava essa la volante et vehemente sagitta d’oro, o vero il suo pyrovolo, et ne l’altra mano, elegantemente reiecta cum gratiosa complicatura sopra la sinistra spalla la chlamyde inversa et di aurea et tenuissima villatura fultrata gerente una hyacinthina lucerna di antiquario operamento et di perspicuo effigiato, accensa, scintilabonda. Questo divino triumpho et tutte le triumphale Nymphe praecedevano, como avanti è descripto le pastophore, poscia le trophigere, daposcia le faceferarie, di fascicule d’oro infasciate le face, consequente le splendofore cum auree lucerne, tede, et cerei di albicante, et purissima materia, cum chiaro candore luminosi. Alcune cum vasculi aurei cum angustissimo exito, liquore odoratissimo inconstante sopra tutti guttatamente excussi imbrefacendo conspergevano. Altre assai cum caelico ordine che nelle sue longiuscule mane sonanti instrumenti tenivano. Symphonie suave, fistule forabile, Tibie, cum suavi moduli consonavano, et bucine tortile auree, et pretiosissimamente gemmate. Alcune inseme cum dulcissimo consenso harmonicamente cum lidia tonatione cantante, cum angelice prolatione, et voce non reddendo familiare sono, et iterante gli venusti Carmini. Alcune altre cum gli tintilanti Cymbali. Altri cum increpitanti et rauci Tympani. Sospesi dalla sinistra mano,

— Pythian Apollo. In which — not by the noble Pyrgoteles, but rather divinely — was Cupid most- excellently carved, cruelly lancing himself, and incautious Psyche handling him with the arrow, with a lethal puncture; where the loosed arm bore the flying and vehement golden arrow, or her firebrand; and in the other hand, elegantly cast back with gracious folding over the left shoulder, the chlamys reversed and stuffed with a golden and most-tenuous pile, bearing a hyacinthine lamp of antique workmanship and of perspicuous effigy, kindled, scintillating. Which most-sumptuous chlamys was most- opulently fringed with marvellous precious stones, over a most-exquisite and pleated amice of springing silk and wefted with purest gold, girt under the little swelling breast, with divine and delightful make.

Towing, then, this superb vehicle of triumphant Love drew two scaly serpents, skinks, beyond their nature great in this place, for this amorous office and mystery and conveyance valid and vigorous, inflamed with three-forked and quadruped vibrations; with their necks stretched out, becomingly entwining their scaly breasts with marvellous knotwork of thongs and retainers interwoven of gold train, with little swelling bosses standing forth, of various and excellent gold chasing alternated with gems, most-excellently, with irradiating coruscation, buckled in gold buckles and most-choice clasps they passed through; and thickly adorned with thick little stones, at the drawing not swiftly, but with a temperament of triumphal procession.

This divine triumph all the triumphal Nymphs preceded, as before is described: the shrine-bearers, then the trophy-bearers, then the torch-bearers, the torches banded with little bundles of gold; next the light-bearers with golden lamps, brands, and tapers of whitening and purest material, luminous with clear candour. Continuing, followed the scent-bearers (Osmiferae) and Sweet-smellers (Euosmiae), with golden scent-vessels of unseen make, and with golden censers, or incense-caskets, scattering wondrous fragrance beyond that which the happy place diffusedly breathed everywhere. Some with little golden vases, with the narrowest outlet, sprinkling a most-odorous inconstant liquor drop by drop, bedewing all. Very many others, with a heavenly order, held in their longish hands sounding instruments: sweet symphonies, pierceable pipes, tibiae, sounded with sweet measures; and twisted gold horns, most-preciously gemmed. Some together, with sweetest consent, harmonically, in the Lydian mode, singing, with angelic utterance, and a voice rendering no familiar sound, and reiterating the comely songs. Some others with the tinkling Cymbals. Others with rattling and raucous Drums, suspended from the left hand —

Cum gratiosa tuberatione pomululando le colorate buccule. Et daposcia cum la dexterula, la tirata et extenta pelle, cum pulsatoruli di bianchissimo ebure percotevano, cum mensurata participatione et emusicata, cum lene et grato susurro et raucitate, cum il tibiale sibilo concordi risonanti. Redimite di diademate di gioie di verno coronamine florente, tra gli variati flori bractee auree deornatamente intercalate, praecipuamente di amethystine viole, et di flori cumatili, et di purpurante amarantho, et holochryso et di cyclamino, et cum strophiole di mellilotho, cum viole luteole et candide, mensuratamente composite, et commixtamente inserte, et di altri coronarii flosculi, et cum candicante margarite et altre gemme, accessoriamente le ornate caesarie circundate et innexe, et alcune elegantissime callicridene tanta demulcente harmonia ad gli auditori communicando dispensavano, quanto forsa unque Apolline, immo sencia haesitare, la lyra pulsante alle helicone Muse non rende, né porge. Né tale et tanta suavitate di sono gli tyrreni navicularii sentirono. Né Arione tanto cum sono supra il delphino vectore ad Tenaro fece il traiecto. Le quale sonatrice, non in uno erano, ma in ordinato processo disposite, et divisione processionale al suo requisito, et constituto loco ad magna extollentia et triumphale adoria et victorine laude, in redundante turme. Per le quale dive cose fermamente mi suado inane praestarse omni foeconda et facunda lingua exprimere satagendo particula degli suavissimi soni, degli dulcissimi cantici, degli solemni et iocosi tripudii et feste, dille dive Nymphe et insigne puelle, dille sue singulare et incomparabile bellece. Dillo excellente et illustre et elegantissimo decoramento, da interrumpere et amputare la gratiosa vita, et da dissipare et distrahere omni duro et obstinato core laetante cum vario et triumphale gestamine et cum alacre

— and, with little cords of gold and silk twisted, hanging at the fleshy arms near the grasp of the hand. And then, with their turned and longish fingers, solicitously they forbade, and in time yielded, upon the pierced tibia, leaping — not such as was found by Mercury — the whistling voice which, among the most-purple lips, received composedly the sonorous breath. With gracious swelling, apple-rounding the coloured little cheeks. And then, with the little right hand, the drawn and stretched skin they beat with little beaters of whitest ivory, with measured and mosaic participation, with a soft and grateful murmur and raucousness, resounding in concord with the tibia's whistle.

Some with twin tibiae — to the invention of which let Marsyas yield, and the Phrygians likewise. Some lyring with garrulous lyres, and some citharising with citharas, exciting upon the trained brazen strings, with tender and delicate fingers, or with plectra, the brazen sound, striking it together; and again with other most-noble and marvellous and organed instruments, with golden and rattling sistra clattering with a sharp tinkle; and with steel triangles, with playful rings tinkling, soft and sharp, at the sweet stroke; and others conformably with hooked little horns, diffused an unheard harmony, with a crackling trumpet fore-singing.

Wreathed with diadems of jewels, flowering with a spring-crown, among the varied flowers gold foil becomingly intercalated — chiefly of amethystine violets, and of sky-blue flowers, and of purpling amaranth, and everlasting, and of cyclamen, and with little wreaths of melilot, with yellow and white violets, measuredly composed, and mixedly inserted, and of other coronary florets, and with whitening pearls and other gems, accessorily their ornate locks girdled round and woven in. And some, most-elegant, of beautiful voice, dispensed so much soothing harmony, communicating it to the hearers, as perhaps Apollo — nay, without hesitating — playing the lyre to the Heliconian Muses does not render, nor offer. Nor such and so great a sweetness of sound did the Tyrrhenian sailors hear; nor did Arion, with so much sound, upon the dolphin his conveyer, make the crossing to Taenarum. Which women musicians were not in one place, but disposed in ordered procession, and processional division at their requisite and constituted place, to great extolling and triumphal glory and victorine praise, in redundant troops.

Through which divine things, I firmly persuade myself, every fecund and eloquent tongue would prove vain, striving to express a particle of the sweetest sounds, of the sweetest canticles, of the solemn and playful dances and feasts, of the divine Nymphs and notable girls, of their singular and incomparable beauties. Of the excellent and illustrious and most-elegant adornment — enough to interrupt and amputate gracious life, and to dissipate and distract every hard and obstinate heart — rejoicing with various and triumphal insignia, and with alacrity —

gesticulatione incedente, et di tanto sumptuoso et superbo triumpho, et di tante delitie et amoenitate, et immensa amplitudine di pretiose divitie et maxime opulentie più praesto, che dalla natura, divine agli ochii mei per la Cythareida gratia et cupidineo privilegio palesemente di videre, et chiaro et realmente mirare praecipuamente indulto.

Nel ultimo loco immediate, dinanti proximo gli trahenti serpi, dui aegipani, et procaci satyri, cum hircino barbitio, cum gli caprei, et bisulci pedi procedevano. Gli quali petulci laetabundi, cum strophiole di florido satyrione, et di flori di Helenio et di Cynosorchi supprimente gli incompti capili sopra la caperata fronte incoronati. Ciascuno gestava uno monstro rudemente exciso in ligno, et inaurato, effigiato humano vestito. Dal tricapo fina alla diaphragma solamente il residuo in quadrato acuminantise alla parte infernate, demigrava in una gulatura basiale, cum uno latastrello, cum una antiqua foliatura nel sito brachiale, cum uno pomo al pecto. Et nel medio dil quadrato nella parte più lata appareva lo ithyphallio signo. Dinanti agli quali praecedeva immediate una politula Nympha, nel volto niveo dilla quale le gene porphyriace rubevano, cum la hederata fronte di corymbi, induta di segmentato palio, vibrati de qui et de lì da suavi reflati zephyrei, gli lacinii dil reflato sino, baiulava uno vasculo aureo, in modo di papila rotundato, lacte per angustissimo meato aspergendo libava. Similmente ancora due Nymphe praecedente redimite l’una di folii dilla foemina Linozoste, et l’altra di Hermopoane masculo, erano gestatrice. La una di uno integro puerulo inaurato nella dextra, et ne l’altra uno altro dil capo, brachioli, et dil collo mutilato. L’altra cum praecipuo honore et obstinata superstitione el simulachro dagli Aegyptii di Serapi venerato portava. El quale era uno capo di leone. Alla dextra prosiliva uno capo di cane blandiente. Et dalla laeva, uno capo di rapace lupo. La quale effigie era tuta in uno volumine di draco contenta et circundata, radii praeacuti emittente. Il quale draco cum la testa alla dextra parte del simulacro derivava faberrimamente deaurato.

— gesticulation advancing; and of so sumptuous and superb a triumph, and of so many delights and pleasantnesses, and immense amplitude of precious riches and greatest opulences — rather divine than of nature — to my eyes, by the Cytherean grace and cupidine privilege, openly to see, and clearly and really to behold, especially indulged.

In the last place, immediately, in front, next to the drawing serpents, two aegipans and wanton satyrs, with goatish beards, with goat-feet and cloven, proceeded. Which lewd ones, joyous, with little wreaths of flowering satyrion, and of flowers of helenium and of cynosorchis (orchid), pressing down the unkempt hair over the wrinkled forehead, crowned. Each bore a monster, rudely cut in wood, and gilt, effigied human, clothed: from the three-heads to the diaphragm only; the rest, in a square, tapering to the lower part, passed off into a basal gullet-moulding, with a little slab, with an antique foliage at the arm-site, with an apple at the breast. And in the middle of the square, in the broadest part, appeared the ithyphallic sign.

Before whom, immediately, preceded a polished Nymph, on whose snowy face the porphyry cheeks reddened, with the ivy-crowned forehead of corymbs, clad in a segmented pall, quivered here and there by the soft zephyr-breaths, the lappets of the reflexed bosom; she bore a golden little vase, rounded in the manner of a nipple, from which, by the narrowest channel, sprinkling milk, she libated.

Likewise, again, two Nymphs preceding, wreathed the one of leaves of the female Mercury-plant, and the other of male Hermopoane, were bearers: the one, of a whole gilt little boy in the right hand, and in the other another, mutilated of the head, little arms, and neck. The other, with special honour and obstinate superstition, bore the simulacrum venerated by the Egyptians, of Serapis. Which was a head of a lion; to the right jutted a fawning dog's head; and to the left, a head of a rapacious wolf. Which effigy was all contained and surrounded in a coil of a dragon, emitting fore-sharpened rays; which dragon, with the head, derived to the right part of the simulacrum, most-excellently gilt.

Onde cum emusicata distantia, et cum longo et solemne progresso, comitato era il triumphante fanciullo, et ambidui Polia et io invinculati di florenti serticuli et viminali rosei festabondi. Le Nymphe di faceta natura molto benignamente active a nui gratificantise amorosi blandimenti prolectante facevano, cum festivi volti et genuina hilaritudine, et amoroso solamine praemulcente. Finalmente cum questo triumphale et mysterioso discorso, et cum le pheretrie pompe et amorose niciterie, tra ridolenti flori successivamente procedendo, praecellente la vexillatione dilla insigne victoria, cum gli anticantamenti dilla laetissima iuventute Nymphale, cum incesso perfluo et antiludii, et cum corusce face praelucente et cum chiaro lume praemicante praecedevano, et tra fragrante olere, olente et vive arbore, odoriferi fructi, odoroso aire et clemente et liquidissimo coelo. Sepita la strata cum omni generamento di fruteti consita et in omni parte contecto di herbule, et di virente gramine. Non vacando a passo a passo le rose, et la copia di flori, omni cosa fragrante et loco foelice, beato, delitioso, et amoenissimo. Et cum tanta et diva sequentia di turba miscellanea di Nymphe, cum peculiare pompa, cum religioso progresso immo triumphale, la una da l’altra, cum statura et determinata distantia comitato incedeva, sotto la rosaria pergula di omni manera, cum novelle, et verne fronde et foglie. La quale da dolci strepiti obsibilava. Et quivi tutto il solo ubertosamente obruto et coperto era di sternate foglie di rose, et di flore naranceo, di amethystine viole, et matronale luteole et di bulle albe, et di pulliphure purpurante et di flori di Iosamino di lilii et altri conspicui et olentissimi flosculi. Whence, with mosaic distance, and with long and solemn progress, was accompanied the triumphant boy; and both, Polia and I, bound with flowering little wreaths and rosy withes, festive. The Nymphs, of facetious nature, most benignly active, gratifying us, made alluring amorous blandishments, with festive faces and genuine hilarity, and soothing amorous solace.

Finally, with this triumphal and mysterious discourse, and with the quiver-bearing pomps and amorous enticements, among fragrant flowers successively proceeding — the standard-bearing of the notable victory preceding, with the fore-singings of the most-joyous nymphal youth, with flowing gait and fore-dances, and with coruscating torches pre-shining, and with clear light pre-glittering, they went before — and among fragrant pot-herbs, sweet-smelling and living trees, odoriferous fruits, odorous air and clement and most-liquid sky. The road hedged, planted with every kind of shrubbery, and in every part covered with little herbs, and with green grass. Not lacking, step by step, the roses, and the plenty of flowers — every thing fragrant, and a place happy, blessed, delightful, and most- pleasant. And with so great and divine a following of the miscellaneous throng of Nymphs, with peculiar pomp, with religious progress — nay, triumphal — the one accompanied the other, with stature and determined distance, advancing under the rose-pergola of every manner, with new and spring fronds and leaves. Which, with sweet noises, whistled softly.

And there all the ground was fertilely overwhelmed and covered with strewn leaves of roses, and of orange-flower, of amethystine violets, and matronal yellow ones, and of white bells, and of purpling pulliphure, and of flowers of jasmine, of lilies, and other conspicuous and most-odorous florets. And singularly was disseminated the seed of the single-stalked marsh-mallow, and little branches of flowering myrtle — upon the most-even paving, of most-lucent marbles in infinite works tessellately compacted. Many bore thyrses of manifold flowering heaped together; others, with branches of olive; some, of laurel; many, of myrtle, and of other celebrated trees, with intrepid little birds familiarly perching upon them, singing with canorous chatter and sweet concerts, with the —

cantante Nymphe hymni et cantici, et cum dulcissimi moduli psallente per tutto adsonavano, cum caeleste plauso laetissimamente festigianti cum hilare cerimonie et cum delicato et virginale tripudio ardeliamente, et alcune cum saltatione pyrrhica, et altisone laude extollendo la divina genitrice et il potente filiolo, cum festivi spectaculi cum maximo triumpho, et superba pompa paulatinamente pervenissimo ad uno proscenio, ove era una conspicua, et faberrima, et scitissima porta hiante, di materia, et di operatura di uno mirabilissimo amphitheatro sublime instructo di fabrica, pleno di artificio di ornamenti et arte non viso mai tale, né in Atella né in qualunque altro famoso loco exquisitissimamente fabrefacto et perfectamente absoluto di lunga narratione explicabile, et quasi non cogitabile. Quale dire si potrebbe non humana, ma più praesto divina operatione, et ostentamento maximo di structura.

GIUNTI dunque cum solemnissimo gaudio, et incredibile laetitia, et solatioso dilecto per la triumphale via cum distributa aspergine indi et quindi per alcune strictissime auree fistulete, irrorante di odoratissime aquule gli processionarii, et tutta la triumphatrice turma rosidulamente perfusa alla porta dil ingresso, mirai che l’era stupendo artificio. La quale constructa era di orientale litharmeno, nel quale infinite scintule, quale scobe disperse se cernivano di fulgurante oro. Et di questo puro metallo erano

— singing Nymphs, hymns and canticles, and with sweetest measures psalming, everywhere resounded; with celestial applause most-joyously making festival, with hilarious ceremonies and with delicate and virginal dance, busily; and some with pyrrhic leaping; and, with high-sounding praise extolling the divine Genetrix and her potent little son, with festive spectacles, with greatest triumph and superb pomp, little by little we came to a proscenium, where was a conspicuous, and most-excellently made, and most-cunning gate, gaping — of material and of workmanship of a most-marvellous amphitheatre, sublimely built of fabric, full of artifice of ornaments and art never so seen, neither in Atella nor in any other famous place; most-exquisitely wrought and perfectly absolute, of long narration explicable, and almost not conceivable. Which one might call not human, but rather divine, operation, and greatest ostentation of structure.

ARRIVED, then, with most-solemn gladness, and incredible joy, and solacing delight, by the triumphal way — with distributed sprinkling, here and there, through certain narrowest golden little pipes, irroring the processionaries with most-odorous little waters, and all the triumphing throng dewily suffused — at the gate of the entry, I beheld that it was of stupendous artifice. Which was constructed of oriental litharmenon (a golden marble), in which infinite sparks, like scattered filings, were discerned, of fulgurant gold. And of this pure metal were —

dille exacte columne le base, gli capituli. Il trabe, zophoro, coronice, et fastigio limine et ante, et omni altro operamento dilla recensita materia vedevase, renuente il duro et tenace chalybe et aspernabile la toreumata antiquariamente variata, gratioso elegante et spectatissimo expresso, et structura oltra modo magnifica. La quale io penso da gli terriculi non factibile, cum summo impendio et longanimitate, grave et diutina faticha, et cum non mediocre ingegnio, cura et industria, et diligentia, che ad tale ostento fusse absoluta et adfabrefacta era nella clusura di tutto l’arco di ophitea petra, et le collaterale columne ambe porphyrice. Poscia l’altre variando, et ophitea una, et l’altra porphyrica. Le mediane superastante alle porphyrite, ambe ophite, et le supernate quadrangule mediane di porphyrite, et poscia contrariando l’una all’altra, et cusì per il contrario mutamine erano capituli base et arule.

Dinanti la quale uno per lato, era uno pretiosissimo vaso, uno di saphyro, l’altro di smaragdo, di maximo et obstinato artificio faberrimamente daedale facti. Pensai degli vasi all’ingresso dil templo di Iove in Athene collocati. A questa descripta porta mirabile dil triumphale et volucre vehiculo il signore Arquite discese. Lo amphitheatro era di incredibile invisitata et inaudita structura. Imperoché il pedamento elegante, et gli emusicati concincti, y ii

— of the exact columns the bases, the capitals; the architrave, frieze, cornice, and pediment, threshold and antae, and every other working, of the recounted material was seen — refusing the hard and tenacious steel, and spurning it, the toreutic (relief-work) antiquely varied, gracious, elegant and most-admired in expression, and a structure beyond measure magnificent. Which I think not makeable by earth-dwellers, save with the utmost expense and long-suffering, grave and lasting labour, and with no mediocre wit, care and industry, and diligence — that to such a marvel it should be perfected and skilfully-wrought. In the closure of the whole arch it was of ophite stone, and the collateral columns both porphyry. Then the others varying — one ophite, and the other porphyry. The middle ones, superstanding upon the porphyry, both ophite; and the overstanding quadrangular middle ones of porphyry; and then, contrarying one to the other, and so by the contrary change were capitals, bases and little altars.

Before which, one to a side, was a most-precious vase: one of sapphire, the other of emerald, of greatest and obstinate artifice most-excellently and daedally made. I thought of the vases set at the entrance of the temple of Jove in Athens.

Il residuo tutto di alabastryte diaphano, et di collustrante nitella, et le ante cum gli archi, overo trabi inflexi. Né tale opera M. Scauro fece nella sua aedilitate. Il quale dalla parte extima havea dui aequali ordini di pervii archi intercalati tra le columne. Uno ordine all’altro supposito de hemicyclo il suo inflexo cum lo additamento. Et tra le apertione degli quali nel solido late perpendicularmente eminevano appacte semi columne striate, cum il tertio suo rudentate cum nextruli, overo reguli. Alcune cum aequa alteratione et distributo referte di signi et di imaguncule, quale in Epheso nunque furono vise. Ad gli anguli dille quale appacti pendevano dui ossi di capo di ariete, uno di qui, et l’altro di llì, cum gli rugosi corni incochleati, overo cum intorta vertigine, per le quale uscivano certe cymose inseme innodate, una frondea gioia cum suppresso foliamento, et di prominenti fructi retinente et illaqueante, nel contento dilla undulatione quadrata di l’arula. Alcune altre columne di questa medema forma, cum gli dui tertii di alveatura torqueata, et lo infimo arulato come è dicto, mutavano geminate di liniamento. Tale havevano tra tuberate reste di fronde et fructi incurvescente pueruli ludibondi. Alcune multiplici trophaei scalpture egregiamente facte molte havevano exscalpte congerie di exuvie. Altre occupati di signi appacti plaudente dee, et puelluli et victorie copie et tituli et altri ornamenti congruentissimi.

— or bindings, and the symmetrical colonnade in a circle. Beams, friezes, and cornices, all exclusively, were of cast bronze, encaustically over-gilt with fulgurant gold. The rest, all of diaphanous alabaster, and of shining brightness; and the antae with the arches, or inflected beams. Nor such a work did M. Scaurus make in his aedileship.

Which, on the outermost part, had two equal orders of passable arches, intercalated between the columns; one order superposed on the other, of hemicycle, its inflexion with the addition. And between the openings of which, in the solid, broadly and perpendicularly, stood forth set half- columns, striated, with their lower third fluted with little knots, or fillets. Some, with even alternation and distribution, filled with figures and little images, such as in Ephesus were never seen. Set under the bases of which, becoming little altars lay, and with the requisite lineament. At the angles of which, set fast, hung two bones of a ram's head, one here and the other there, with the wrinkled horns spiralled, or with twisted whirl; through which came out certain shoots knotted together — a leafy jewel with pressed foliage, and retaining and ensnaring prominent fruits, in the content of the square undulation of the little altar. Within the compass of the jewel, egregiously carved, was a satyric little sacrifice: with a little altar set under a tripod, with an antique boiling cook-pot, and two nude Nymphs, one to a side, with a little pipe blowing on the fire; and, next to the altar, two little boys, one to a side, with a little vase apiece. Likewise two lascivious Satyrs, with a token of vociferating, with a fist tightly raised toward the Nymphs, with snaky entanglement. Which (Nymphs), with the free arm, gripped those of the satyrs — who, with the hand of the other arm, stopped the orifice of a leaky vase, forbidding the touch — and, inclined, with the other held the little pipe, intent and unmoving at its office. Some other columns of this same form, with the two-thirds of twisted fluting, and the lowest one altared, as is said, changed, doubled in lineament. Such had, among swelling ropes of fronds and fruits incurving, playful little boys. Some, manifold trophies, sculptures egregiously made; many had carved heaps of spoils. Others, occupied with set figures of applauding goddesses, and little boys, and copies of victories, and titles, and other most-congruous ornaments.

Et de qui et de lì iaceva uno cornuto bove prostrato cum gli pedi pretensi al vaso, et cum il capo elevato, et uno nudo quello aequitante cum la dextera elata, et impugnato multiplice virgule, indicava percussuro, cum l’altra il paleario collo amplexando. Above the topmost shafts sat the choice capitals of the recounted columns, with artful abaci, or lids, under the projections; beneath which subsided the most-polished greater helices (volutes), the trabeate binding, and of frieze and crowning, circling round, with the projections of the live (stone) congruently to the perpendicular of the columns.

Between one projection and the other, in the order of the door, in the middle part of the frieze, stood artfully expressed a most-noble device and invention: of a most-ancient vase, stuffed, having the orifice of antique and prependent fronds. And here and there lay a horned bull, prostrate, with its feet stretched toward the vase, and with the head raised; and a nude one riding it, with the right hand lifted and gripping manifold little rods, indicating he will strike; with the other embracing the dewlapped neck. Next to which a nude girl sat, dorsally, with the arm toward the solid embracing the nude one over the paunch, and with the other, snatched, held a veiling cloth above the filleted head; impeded, it came out under her seat, over the embracing arm. In prospect, a satyr, the bull's horn grasped with the inner hand, and with the other, turned with his back to the bull, raised toward the woman, held a balled-up serpent. mano dill’altro satyro. La parte posteriora degli bovi migrante verso le prominentie in nobilissime spire di antiquarie fronde transformavase. Per questo medemo modo variando il zophoro venustamente inscalpto era decorato.

Ultra questo primario illigamento sequiva ascendendo ordinatamente uno simigliante di columne in omni cosa conveniente et in niuna parte discrepante. Et quantunque l’arte aedificamentaria appetisca che le superapposite columne più breve il quarto dille substitute essere debono, dille quale il perpendiculo deveniva sencia lo arulato supra el centro dille subdite cum la sequentia. Et le tertie il quinto. Niente dimeno in questo elegante, et symmetriato aedificamento, questo non era observato, ma di una proceritate, et le supernate cum le inferne. Le tertie quadrangule nel tertio ordine obedivano. Havevano ancora queste superassidente columne lo illigamento ambiente, quale le subiecte. Ultra questa concinctura, le quadrangule, overo pilastrelli sulcati salivano. Et tra uno et l’altro dal solido uno suo tertio evulse, nella aequata intercapedine hiava una finestra, non quadrangula al modo templario, ma quale è requisito nelle profane aedificature obliquate, overo inarcuate. Supremamente in orbe gyrato sopra le quadrate columne, la regia coronice sencia proiecture, cum omni ornato et requisitissimo liniamento et cum il praecipuo in essa dovuto suggrundio, cum omni proportione harmonica circumligava. Oltra la dicta, sublato circinava uno nitido et expedito alamento alto uno passo et semisse. Tutto questo celebre, illustre, superbo, et summamente approbato aedificamento di finissimo alabastryte indico, di vitrino nitore artificamente constructo optimamente decorato, et egregiamente absoluto sencia illimento di calce pulte, overo cemento, ma cum stabile cohaesione et contracto consenso perfecto. La quale praenitente materia non era dedignata da maculabile fumo, non palescente per unctura di olio illibuta, né lutea per infusione di vermigliaceo vino, ma in omni parte obnoxia sencia alcuna infectione, nella sua nitella superba, luculenta riservato. La

— hand of the other satyr. The posterior part of the bulls, migrating toward the projections, transformed itself into most-noble spirals of antique fronds. In this same manner, varying, the frieze was comelily carved and decorated.

Beyond this first binding followed, ascending in order, a similar order of columns, in every thing conformable, and in no part discrepant. And although the building-art appetites that superposed columns ought to be shorter by a quarter than those set below — of which the perpendicular came, without the altar-work, above the centre of the columns beneath, with the sequence, and the third (order) a fifth (shorter) — nonetheless in this elegant and symmetrical building this was not observed, but of one height, the overstanding with the underlying. The third quadrangular ones obeyed in the third order. These superseated columns had also the encompassing binding, like the subjacent. Beyond this girdle, the quadrangular ones, or little pilasters, furrowed, rose. And between one and the other, a third of it emerging from the solid, in the evened intercapace gaped a window — not quadrangular in the temple- manner, but as is requisite in profane buildings, obliqued, or arched.

Supremely, in a turned orb above the square columns, the royal cornice, without projections, with every ornament and most-requisite lineament, and with the special due drip-moulding in it, with every harmonic proportion, went round. Beyond the said, raised, circled a clean and unencumbered band, a pace and a half high.

All this celebrated, illustrious, superb, and supremely-approved building, of finest Indian alabaster, of glassy sheen, artfully constructed, most-excellently decorated, and egregiously perfected — without any smearing of lime-paste, or cement, but with stable cohesion and contracted consent, perfect. Which pre- shining material was not disdained by staining smoke, nor paling by the anointment of oil smeared on, nor muddied by the infusion of vermilion wine, but in every part unliable, without any infection, kept in its superb, luculent brightness.

La partitione dilla columnatione ambiente, era quadrifaria divisa sopra la circunferentia. Tra una et l’altra partitione interiecte constavano cum aequa discriminatione octo divisure, ove constituite et apposite erano le columne. Dalle quale il solido verso il centro procedeva, et per il recto, et per circuitione transversarie in circinao, extavano commensuratamente le suffulture columnarie, tra una et l’altra partitamente gli hiati dille apertione, correspondentisi linialmente pervii, et intervacui, et artificiosamente testudinati gli portici o vero fornici, le linee angustiantise cum mira convenientia, le recte et le circinante transversale l’altitudine reservata, et omni normico alla harmonia accessorio. The Area contained, by diameter, 32 paces. The thickness of the composite (the built ring), 8 paces.

The partition of the encompassing colonnade was quadrifariously divided over the circumference. Between one and the other interjected partition stood, with even discrimination, eight divisions, where the columns were constituted and set. From which the solid proceeded toward the centre; and, both by the straight and by circuit, transverse in a ring, stood commensurately the columnar supports; between one and another, partitely, the gaps of the openings, corresponding linearly, passable, and inter-void, and artfully vaulted the porticoes, or fornices — the lines narrowing with wondrous convenience, the straight and the circling transverse, the altitude reserved, and every rule accessory to the harmony. The pavement floor was egregiously sectile, of marvellous invention and of conspicuous art —

cusì el superno, quale lo inferno di petre bellissime, di coloratione variate optimamente, et cum coaequatissima cohaesione, una integritate mentiente, cum speculare strissatione et venustissima politura spectabile. Gli suffiti cum elegante operatura il solerte et calotechnio architecto, havea historie inducte di spectatissimo museaco cum elegantissima pigmentura summamente deornati. Le quale historie erano tutti gli effecti da Cupidine facti. Dunque in questa miranda aedificatura diligentemente la expressa peritia et la acuta et magna solertia dill’artifice, la praestante arte dil significo, et la forcia dillo ingegno dil venusto Thesellatore explicata considerai. Alla quale structura ceda il mirabile Templo Ephesio, il Romano Colosseo, et il Veronense Theatro, et omni altro, perché le Columne, Capitelli, Base, Corone, Crustatione, Pavimenti, Statue, Signi, et omni altro accessorio, non sencia magnificentia et diva operatione, mirificamente composite, et coordinate, et perfectamente castigate, et faberrimamente consumate, cum summa admiratione spectatissime constavano. Ceda quivi la superba fama et admiratione dille imagine dil Divo Augusto, et degli quatro Elephanti nel tempio dilla Concordia dicati. Né a questo aequiparare si pole la magnifica imagine di Menelao, et omni altro stupendo ostento. Fora dunque di questo maraveglioso introito, o vero porta mediana et Regia. Tutte quelle officiose Nymphe si rimanseron excluse. Et il divino signore, et la sua gratissima Psyche, et nui inseme, cum quelle due che ne ligorono laetissimamente, et cum extrema voluptate intrassemo. Quivi in questo adito solamente non erano gli laxati spatii, ma parietato et sepito et obturatamente tabulato dilla nobile petra ante insinuata. Indi pervenissimo per sotto il testudinato ad un’altra obvia porticula. La summitate dilla quale era contermine sotto ad una aequatura d’una perguleta, che se dirae. Quivi pervenuti essendo fora dilla interstitia apertura dil testudinato adito, et nella complanata Area dil Theatro, di admiratione summopere digno, introeunti. Io nel primo aspecto, uno miraculo molto et grandemente stupendo vidi. Imperò che tutto il pavimento dil spatio dilla mediana Area dil consepto dilla cavea dil Theatro silicato il vidi di una solida et integra petra Obsidiana, di extrema nigritia, et di duritudine indomabile, tersa et tanto illustre, che io abstracto sopra di quella, nel primo ponere dil mio dextro pede, in quello instante in Abysso inconsideratamente, et me tutto d’amore, et da dolcecia occupato veramente moribondo dubitai praecipitare. Ma la obvia resistentia mi restituite repente gli commoti, et territi spiriti cum laesione dil decepto pede. Nella quale petra chiaro vedevase, et perfectamente cernivasi, quale in placido et flustro mare la lympitudine

— so the upper as the lower, of most-beautiful stones, of varied colouring most-excellently, and with most-even cohesion, feigning one integrity, with mirror-like striation and most-comely polish, noteworthy. The soffits, with elegant work, the shrewd and beautiful-crafting architect had induced with histories of most-admired mosaic, most-highly adorned with most-elegant pigmentation. Which histories were all the effects done by Cupid. Thus, in this marvellous building, I diligently considered the expressed skill, and the acute and great solertia of the artificer, the outstanding art of the maker, and the force of the wit of the comely Tessellator, explicated. To which structure let yield the marvellous Ephesian Temple, the Roman Colosseum, and the Veronese Theatre, and every other — because the Columns, Capitals, Bases, Cornices, Incrustations, Pavements, Statues, Figures, and every other accessory, not without magnificence and divine operation, wondrously composed, and coordinated, and perfectly castigated, and most-excellently consummated, with the utmost admiration, most-admired, stood. Let yield here the superb fame and admiration of the images of Divine Augustus, and of the four Elephants dedicated in the temple of Concord. Nor to this can be equalled the magnificent image of Menelaus, and every other stupendous marvel.

Out, then, of this marvellous entrance, or middle and Royal gate, all those officious Nymphs remained excluded. And the divine lord, and his most-pleasing Psyche, and we together — with those two who bound us — most-joyously, and with extreme voluptuousness, entered. Here in this entry-way only were not the loosened spaces, but walled, and hedged, and closedly boarded of the noble stone before insinuated. Thence we came, by under the vault, to another facing little door, the summit of which was conterminous, under an evening of a little pergola, which shall be told.

There arrived, being out of the interstitial opening of the vaulted entry, and into the levelled Area of the Theatre — most-highly worthy of admiration — entering, I, at the first aspect, saw a miracle greatly and grandly stupendous. For all the pavement of the space of the middle Area of the enclosed cavea of the Theatre, paved, I saw of a solid and integral Obsidian stone, of extreme blackness, and of indomitable hardness, polished and so lustrous, that I — abstracted upon it, at the first placing of my right foot — in that instant, into an Abyss inconsiderately (and I, all occupied with love and sweetness, truly moribund), feared to precipitate. But the facing resistance suddenly restored to me the shaken and terrified spirits, with the hurt of the deceived foot. In which stone clearly was seen, and perfectly discerned — as in a placid and calm sea the limpidity —
