dil profundo caelo. Et similmente tutte le cose quivi in gyro existente reflectevano, molto più di mundissimo speculo, et cusì le soprastante. Nel medio dilla quale Area nel centro, era il sanctissimo et delicioso fonte dilla divina Genitrice et domina di esso amore artificiosissimamente constituito. Dil quale sacratissimo fonte ante ch’io ne parli, primo tracterò dilla inaudita dispositione et foelicissimo intersito Theatrale. Il quale semota omni dubitatione, sopra il capto dil nostro imaginare fue composito, et miraculosamente definito, in forma (como ho dicto) di Theatro. La gradatione dil quale dall’area dilla lucidissima petra principiava. Non solidi, né massicii gradi, ma inani, cioè vacui, quatro cum il scalinato ascenso, uno sopra all’altro, quale et degli subsellii la gradatione. Ne l’altecia dil sedere palmi sei, et nella latitudine gemino pede, et semisse, o vero nell’apertura. Gli quali erano in ambito circundante capsule di diversi fiori constipate, gli quali la medietate dilla sequente bustula non excedevano. Il quarto et supremo grado coaequavasi la sua apertione cum li labri ad una interposita stratula, quini pedi lata. La quale intorniando era coperta d’una perguleta, alta semisse et uno passo. Ultra poscia il culmo dilla inflexura di tale pergula. L’altro ordine di altri tanti gradi ordinatamente sequiva. Il primo inferiore tanto più alto nell’alamento initiava, o vero pariete, circumvallatamente alla parte intrinseca, che la sublime incurvatura dilla pergula non occupava, il vedere dil fronte di esso primo grado. Tale Symmetria directamente era observabile agli subsequenti. Poscia la quarta gradatione, di questo secondo ordine, et cusì dilla tertia, quale la primaria, era di strata, et di pergula, et di commenso uniforme. Gli tabulati parieti, o vero dille dicte pergulature le sponde in circuito, di nigricante petra di nitore speculare erano. Il primo pariete dilla inferiore pergula, era coaxato di petra spartopolia. Il secondo di Hieratites, la tertia suprema di Cepionide. La nitella dille quale sotto le pergule, una apertura dil sereno aere, che in quella reluceva simulava, et non clausura. La quale petra in elevato sotto l’opera pergulare era tanto, quanto incominciava il flexo di essa pergulatura alla interna parte, ultra la nigra tabulatione, al primordio fina dil primo grado era parietato coassatamente dilla materia di esse capselle. Le quale tutte cose cum tanta solerte peritia, et speculato exquisito di arte, et contumace artificio incomprehensibile, et sapientia investigabile furono, cum divo invento, mirificamente conducte, extremamente contemplabile. Imperoché omni cosa perfectamente distincta et explicata (et non una da l’altra confusa et occupata) aptamente et limitata fina dalla extrema summitate allo infimo prono et singularmente

— of the profound sky. And likewise all the things existing there in the ring reflected, much more than a most-clean mirror, and so the overstanding ones.

In the middle of which Area, at the centre, was the most-holy and delicious fount of the divine Genetrix and mistress of that Love, most-artificially constituted. Of which most-sacred fount, before I speak of it, I shall first treat of the unheard disposition and most-happy theatral interposition — which, setting aside every doubt, was composed above the grasp of our imagining, and miraculously defined, in the form (as I have said) of a Theatre.

The gradation of which began from the area of the most-lucid stone. Not solid, nor massy steps, but inane — that is, hollow — four, with the stepped ascent, one above the other, like the gradation of the benches. In the height of the seat, six palms; and in the breadth, two feet and a half, or in the opening. Which were, in their encompassing circuit, surrounded by boxes stuffed with diverse flowers, which did not exceed the middle of the following little box. The fourth and supreme step levelled its opening, with the lips, to an interposed little walkway, five feet wide. Which, encircling, was covered by a little pergola, a pace and a half high.

Beyond then the summit of the inflexure of such pergola, the other order of as many steps followed in order. The first, lower, began so much higher in the band, or wall, encompassingly at the inner part, that the sublime incurvature of the pergola did not occupy the seeing of the front of that first step. Such symmetry was directly observable in the subsequent. Then the fourth gradation of this second order, and so of the third — like the first — was of walkway, and of pergola, and of uniform commensuration.

The boarded walls, or the sides of the said pergolas in circuit, were of blackish stone of mirror sheen. The first wall of the lower pergola was boarded of spartopolia stone; the second of hieratites; the third, supreme, of cepionis. The sheen of which, under the pergolas, simulated an opening of the serene air, that shone within it, and not a closure.

se cerniva, per linea directamente sopra gli anguli degli gradi deducta. Il pariete al quale la ultima pergulatura cingeva appacta, tanto sublevata era, che lo archuare di essa pergula ad gli inspectanti nell’area non obstava integramente videre. Intro il quale pariete era uno alveo, o vero canale intervacuo capace fabricato, quo al fondo et quo all’apertura, di passo uno et semisse inclusive la sua capacitate, cum lo aestimo alamento nitido et spatiato sopra l’ordine fenestrale. Di tutta questa disertata fabricatura il cincto interiore, cioè gli vacui gradi constavano facti di optimo diaspido, ponderoso, continuo, et orientale, et forsa Cyprico, di coloramento confuso, cum varicante undicule diversissimo. Gli labii dille quale capsule, et il socco, bellissimamente erano infimbriate in circuito continuo di Oro obrizo, in undule et gululatione, et sopra le cohaesione dilla iusta divisione dille tabelle, cum operatura transcendente lo humano cogitato, et exactissimo expresso, et ultimo conato. Quivi perire debi la luxuria deli vasi aurei di Basso, et quella di Antonio, ceda et la gloria di Nerone che inauroe il Theatro Pompeiano. Sopisca Gorgia leontino cum la sua statua. Né mai tale ad gli Scythe dalle formice, et Gryphi, et cusì praestante fue eruto. Il supremo alveo, o vero capsa in canale ducta, et di terra completa, et farcinata, haveva complantati cupressi bini, et bini uno proximo (ma intacto) all’altro, passi trini distante la binatione. Gli quali cum aequa forma et iustamente, cum Idonea norma implicando mutuamente le sue summitate et propilato inseme, cum egregio coniugio se inflectevano, cum sì facto connexo, che uno medemo appariano. Cioè la acuminatione dil quarto, cum il quarto. Gli dui poscia di medio, quello dalla dextera, se copulava cum il quarto al dextro ordine. Et il sinistro per il simigliante se coniungea cum il quarto al sinistro lato, alternando uno di sotto, et uno di sopra innodantise, tanto meglio, quanto dire non se potrebe. Tra uno et l’altro degli inflexi, o vero proclinatione degli collaterali cupressi, resideva uno densissimo Buxo, di pile ordinariamente cum aequario decremento minorantise fastigiato, cioè in multiplice rotundatione decimate, tanto optimamente tosate (quale di forficula) che una foglia non se monstrava il suo ordine offendere. Et tra gli vicinati et curvi cupressi, se attolleva uno rectissimo stipite di Iunipero, in iustissima pila, cum vernale frondatura topiato, il quale ornava il vacuo triangulare degli archi cupressini. Il risiduo dill’altana habundante germinava varii et exquisiti Olusculi aromatici copiosi degli sui odorifici flori, come appare nel supremo dil Theatro designato.

— was discerned, by a line directly deduced over the angles of the steps.

The wall which the last pergola girded, set fast, was so much raised that the arching of that pergola did not, to those inspecting in the Area, wholly obstruct the seeing. Within which wall was a trough, or channel, inter-void, capaciously fabricated — both at the bottom and at the opening a pace and a half, inclusive, its capacity — with the estimated band, clean and spaced, above the fenestral order.

Of all this discoursed fabric, the interior girdle — that is, the hollow steps — stood made of best jasper, ponderous, continuous, and oriental, and perhaps Cyprian, of confused colouring, with wavering little waves most-diverse. The lips of which boxes, and the socle, were most-beautifully fringed, in continuous circuit, of pure refined Gold, in little waves and gullet-mouldings; and over the cohesions of the just division of the tablets, with a working transcending human thought, and most-exact expression, and utmost effort. Here must perish the luxury of the golden vases of Bassus, and that of Antony; let yield also the glory of Nero, who gilded the Pompeian Theatre. Let Gorgias of Leontini be put to sleep with his (golden) statue. Nor ever was such (gold) dug up, so outstanding, by the Scythians, from the ants and Griffins.

The topmost trough, or box, led in a channel and filled and stuffed with earth, had planted cypresses, two and two, one next (but untouching) to the other, the pairing three paces distant. Which, with even form and justly, with a fitting rule interlacing mutually their summits and fore-joined together, with egregious conjugation inflected themselves — with such-made connexion that they appeared one and the same. That is, the point of the fourth (cypress) with the fourth. The two then of the middle: that on the right coupled with the fourth on the right order; and the left, by the like, joined itself with the fourth on the left side, alternating, one below and one above, knotting themselves, so much the better as could not be told. Between one and the other of the inflected, or the leaning-together, of the collateral cypresses, resided a densest Box, of spheres in order, diminishing with even decrement, fastigiate — that is, decimated into manifold rounding — so excellently shorn (as with little shears) that not one leaf showed itself to offend its order. And between the neighbouring and curved cypresses, rose a straightest stalk of Juniper, in a most-just sphere, topiaried with spring foliage, which adorned the empty triangle of the cypress-arches. The remainder of the terrace-top abundantly germinated various and exquisite aromatic little pot-herbs, copious of their odoriferous flowers, as appears designed at the top of the Theatre.

La prima pergula dunque inferiore di odorifero myrto intecta et florulento operosamente a virgule d’oro sopra in arcuale curvatura compacte alla coronice circunducta supra gli arculi dille columnelle auree. Le quale residevano sopra cum le basule la superficie di labro interno dilla quarta capsula circunducta di exquisitissima caelatura. Il complanato sotto dilla prima pergula, non tanto allo intellecto ostupire, ma il sentimento insensare. Era tutta dunque pavimentata in circo di olente ladano, cum gradata mixtura di Ambra, Mosco, Styrace, et Benzuì in fusco coloramento condensato. Nella quale fragrantissima mixtura elegantemente erano poscia albentissime margarite infixe, quale tessellatura riducte in uno circumvoluto phrygio in foliatura antiquaria, cum fructificatione olearea, et intro gli lovi gli flori, overo intro gli sui calici animaletti et avicule, operamento divino et incomparabile, et da divini pedi pressabile compavito, quale Zenodoro unque valse trovare. La tectura dilla secunda coaequatura dilla planitie sotto la pergula di multiplice rose opulentemente disseminate, et cum gli accessorii dilla prima, era di pastamento di Coralio, il quale nel contrito la rubedine havea riservato. Et in questo fulgurava venustamente depicto una mirabile phrygiatura di veterrime fronde di finissimi smaragdi, et gli flori saphyrei coaequatissimi. La tertia strata similmente di minuto, et floribundo myrtho era la pergula di uno pretiosissimo composito di lapislazuli comminuto, ponderoso cum il suo Cyaneo colore alquanto adulterante al verde, mirabilmente lastracato. Nel quale cum optimo glutino, commixti erano fragmenti, overo minutie immassate, overo recisamenti, di tutti gli gioielli universalmente che la foetosa natura ha saputo producere et cum pontiture d’oro bellissimamente in coaequamento ad libella tersissimo praenitente pavimentato. Pensa quanta voluptate, quanto placere, et solacio, et allectamento di tante delitie ad gli humani sensi incredibilmente conferiva, che ad gli beati spiriti mareveglioso offerivase. Queste pergule nel fronte anteriore, le columnule d’oro, cum gli arculi substentavano, quale uno peristyllio in circinao disposito bellissime continuavano. Ma lo intervacuo triangulare tra uno et l’altro arco ravo, overo obtuso era di Calcedonii, di Achates, di Iaspide, et di altre pretiose petre complanatamente tabulate, sencia alcuno liniamento praelucentissime. Et lo internato dilla pergula non arcuata, ma di linea in longitrorso recta assideva nel pariete affixa sopra una

The first, then, lower pergola, was covered of odoriferous myrtle and full of flower, laboriously compacted of little gold rods above, in arched curvature, led round to the cornice, upon the little arches of the golden columnlets. Which resided above, with the little bases, on the surface of the inner lip of the fourth box, led round with most-exquisite chasing.

The levelled ground under the first pergola — not so much to stupefy the intellect, as to un-sense the sense. It was, then, all paved, in a ring, of odorous ladanum, with a graded mixture of Amber, Musk, Storax, and Benzoin, condensed in a dusky colouring. In which most-fragrant mixture were then, elegantly, whitest pearls fixed, like tessellation, reduced into a circumvolved embroidered band of antique foliage, with olive-fructification; and within the loops the flowers, or within their calyxes little animals and little birds — a divine and incomparable working, and pressable by divine feet — pavemented as never Zenodorus availed to find.

The covering of the second levelling of the flatness under the pergola, of manifold roses opulently disseminated, and with the accessories of the first, was of a paste of Coral, which in the grinding had kept its redness. And in this fulgurated, comelily depicted, a marvellous embroidery of most-ancient fronds of finest emeralds, and the flowers of most-even sapphires.

The third walkway, likewise of minute and flowering myrtle was the pergola, of a most-precious composite of lapis lazuli ground small, ponderous, with its cyan colour somewhat adulterating toward green, marvellously flagged. In which, with excellent glue, were commixed fragments, or minutiae massed, or cuttings, of all the jewels universally that fostering nature has known to produce; and with points of gold most-beautifully, in an evening to the level, most-terse, pre-shining, pavemented. Think how much voluptuousness, how much pleasure, and solace, and allurement of so many delights, it incredibly conferred on the human senses — that to the blessed spirits it offered itself marvellous.

These pergolas, on the front anterior, the little gold columns with their little arches sustained, which, like a peristyle disposed in a ring, most-beautifully continued. But the triangular inter-void between one arch and the other, grey, or obtuse, was of Chalcedonies, of Agates, of Jasper, and of other precious stones, flatly tabled, without any lineament, most-pre-shining. And the inner part of the pergola, not arched, but of a line lengthwise straight, sat fixed in the wall, above a —

coronice ambiente cum zophoro et trabecula, cum miro et absolutissimo expresso cum le subiecte mensule obvio alli capitelli dille columnule appacte. Quivi sotto inquietamente chorea ducevano et continue molte laetissime Nymphe sempre emusicatamente nel medio dill’arco dile columne cum elegante continentia se attrovavano, et verso il fonte cum mensurato tempo inclinantisse riverente tutte. Daposcia la reverente inclinatione, tutte inseme da quel medio dispartentise migravano nel altro, cum successiva duratura. Gli dui extremi chori contra il choro mediano gyravano, cum temperatissimi salti et elegante rivolutione sempre nel medio arcuale. Cum superbo sono concordemente di quatro ductile tube d’oro, et quatro suavissimi epiphonii, antiphonii, messophoni, et chamaephoni. Tornati di sandalo rubente, citrino et candido, et di nigerrimo Hebeno, non sencia multiplici ornamenti d’oro, et di gemme. Cum sonoritate dulcissimamente unitoni participata, et cum brevissime prolatione, una explicata symphonia in questa summamente delitiosa, et foelicissima conclausura, concinnamente reflectendo inseme, et le choristrie non cum refracto tumultuando iubilavano. Ove se persentiva una varietate mirifica di voce, et una inexperta aequabilitate di proportione, et suavitate et harmonia. La quale extremamente oblectava la mente mia, et rapta demulcente retiniva. Le Nymphe mediane nude, cum gli albicanti et formosissimi corpi procedevano. L’altre exquisitissimamente decore, cum spectatissimo exornato degli divi corpusculi induti di byssine veste, et velante subucule, cum gesti virginei et moti, et cum puellare dilecto. Altretante nel pariete specularmente mentiendo la nigerrima petra. In prospecto dunque dilla porta dil nostro ingresso correspondeva una scala di septe scalini dilla petra dille capsule. Per gli quali si ascendeva al piano dilla prima pergulatione, et de contra alla scala sotto la pergula, astructa era nel pariete una exigua porta. Per la quale poscia potevasi, per scale interstitie, et meati, et itione pervagare et ire per gli fornici et testudinati, et commodamente per tutto lo aedificio. Et sotto etiam all’altre pergulature, a llibella dilla prima erano similmente porticule di oro faberrimamente caelato valveate. Il primo ordine, overo imo degli gradi infernati era dimidiato dil suo continuo ambire per la porta dill’introito, et dalla recensita scala. Il primo grado, overo capsula inferiore sopra l’area tra la scala, et la porta constipate di terra era germinosa di viole Ianthine, overo amethystine aeternalmente

— encompassing cornice, with frieze and little architrave, with wondrous and most-absolute expression, with the subjacent brackets facing the capitals of the little columns, set fast.

There, beneath, restlessly, choral dances they led, and continually many most-joyous Nymphs — ever mosaic-wise — in the middle of the arch of the columns, with elegant continence, found themselves, and, toward the fount, with measured time, inclining themselves, all reverent. Then, after the reverent inclination, all together, departing from that middle, migrated to the other, with successive continuance. The two extreme choruses gyred against the middle chorus, with most-tempered leaps and elegant revolution, ever in the middle of the arch. With superb sound, concordantly, of four ductile golden trumpets, and four sweetest epiphonia, antiphonia, messophona, and chamaephona (upper-, counter-, middle-, and ground-toned instruments), turned of red, citrine and white sandalwood, and of blackest Ebony, not without manifold ornaments of gold and of gems. With a sonority most-sweetly united-in-tone, and with briefest utterances, one explicated symphony, in this supremely-delightful and most-happy enclosure, becomingly reflecting together — and the choristers, not with a broken tumult, jubilated. Where was perceived a wondrous variety of voice, and an inexperienced equability of proportion, and sweetness, and harmony, which extremely delighted my mind, and, rapt, held it soothing.

The middle Nymphs, nude, with their whitening and most-shapely bodies, proceeded. The others, most- exquisitely comely, with much-admired adornment of their divine little bodies, clad in byssus robes and veiling shifts, with virginal gestures and motions, and with girlish delight. As many again, in the wall, specularly feigning, the blackest stone.

In prospect, then, of the door of our entrance, corresponded a stair of seven steps, of the stone of the boxes. By which one ascended to the plane of the first pergola; and, over against the stair, under the pergola, was built into the wall a slender door. Through which, then, one could — by interstitial stairs, and passages, and goings — wander and go, through the fornices and vaults, and conveniently through all the building. And under the other pergolas too, level with the first, were likewise little doors of gold, most-excellently chased, valved. The first order, or lowest, of the lower steps was halved in its continuous going-round by the door of the entry, and by the recounted stair. The first step, or lower box, above the Area, between the stair and the door, stuffed with earth, was germinous with Ianthine, or amethystine, violets, eternally —

Il secundo grado era florido di Cyaneo segetale. Nel tertio copiosamente florivano purpurissimi cheropheli, et densamente foliosi. Nel novissimo densissimi meliloti. Nel tertio Cheropheli olorini et pentaphylli et multiphylli. Il supremo in dece partitione aequalmente diviso omni partitione cum harmoniata sortitione era di varii et spectatissimi flori. La prima continiva la primulaveris. La secunda Heliochrysso. La tertia amarantho. La quarta la matronale viola russa. La quinta Passerina Ianthina. La sexta la opula, overo bulla alba. La septima Pulliphura luteola. La octava Lilii convallii di candifico flosculo cum gli Calathuli Inversi negli ramusculi odoratissimi dependuli. Nella nona multiplici lilii. Lo uranio, et hyreos candido et Hiacyntho, et gialli, et rubenti. Nella novissima la fiorita Aquilegia azurei, candidi et russi flori germinante era. Questa delitiosa et eximia dispositione degli spectatissimi flori non quivi succumbeva ad alcuno temporale mutamento né ordine. Né quale in Memphi. Ma sempre irrorati et freschi in uno essere verneo, et mai decidui existenti. Per la quale cosa tale maiestate di loco, cum tanta gratia obstupente contemplando, cum questa elegantia ordinata et sortita, et iucundissima harmonia di variatione dil florulento colore, quale nella rosida aurora recenti se praestano. Cum tutti gli disertati miraculi attonito et exanimo io totalmente restai. Gli mei sensi interni et externi occupati, et di ineffabile voluptate et dilecto circumvenuti. Oltra lo excessivo amore, che tutta via nel mio lancinato core fortemente ardeva, et lo assedio indesinente dille incomparabile bellecie dilla mia decorissima Polia, mee delicie. In tanto che io non sapeva in quale essere me fusse. Finalmente in questo foelicissimo loco, et beatissimo sito introducti. Di subito le due Nymphe che nui ligorono, quivi ancora ne solveteno da gli impexi serticuli. Et la veneranda Psyche honorificentissima al suo dilecto Marito, la sagittula d’oro ridibunda gli restituite.

— flowering, and so in the other. In the second order the boxes abounded with whitest violets. In the third order were xanthe, or yellow, violets. In the fourth flowered narcissus, such as would not be found in the mountains of Lycia, with more flower-yield than herbage.

Beyond the first pergola, in the first circularly-continuous box, germinated the cyclamen, with the reverse of the leaves vermilion, of an ivy colour, with its inverted flower most-richly appearing, most- odorous. The second step was flowering with cyan corn-flower. In the third copiously flowered most-purple chervils, densely leaved. In the newest, densest melilots.

In the third order, beyond the second pergola, grew, in the first step, the grey-green and most-beautiful flowers of the herb tora. In the second, reddening Anemones. In the third, sweet-scented chervils, five- leaved and many-leaved. The topmost, into ten partitions equally divided, every partition with harmonised allotment, was of various and much-admired flowers. The first contained the primrose. The second, everlasting (heliochrysum). The third, amaranth. The fourth, the matronal russet violet. The fifth, Ianthine passerina. The sixth, the opula, or white bell. The seventh, yellow pulliphura. The eighth, lily-of-the-valley, of whitening floret, with the little inverted calathi (bells) hanging most-odorous on the little branches. In the ninth, manifold lilies — the heavenly, and white iris, and hyacinth, and yellow and reddening ones. In the newest, the flowered Columbine, germinating azure, white and russet flowers.

This delightful and choice disposition of the much-admired flowers succumbed here to no temporal change, nor order — nor such as in Memphis — but, ever irrigated and fresh, in one vernal being, and never deciduous, existing.

For which thing, contemplating such majesty of place, with so much grace astonishing, with this elegance ordered and allotted, and most-pleasant harmony of variation of the flowering colour — such as, in the dewy dawn, they present themselves fresh — with all the discoursed miracles, astonished and lifeless I wholly remained. My internal and external senses occupied, and surrounded with ineffable voluptuousness and delight — beyond the excessive love, which all the while in my lanced heart strongly burned, and the unceasing siege of the incomparable beauties of my most-comely Polia, my delight — insomuch that I did not know in what being I was. Finally, into this most-happy place, and most-blessed site, introduced — of a sudden the two Nymphs who bound us, there, again, unloosed us from the entangled little wreaths. And the venerable Psyche, most-honorifically, to her beloved Husband, laughing, restored the little golden arrow.

Chapter XXIII

11 of 11 pages englished

p. 358confidence: mediumverified

Dal quale appresentati fussimo dinanti al sacro sancto fonte cythereo.

POLIPHILO IL MIRABILE ARTIFICIO DIL VENEREO FONTE DESCRIVE NEL CENTRO DILLA THEATRALE AREA EXISTENTE, ET COME FRACTA FUE LA CORTINETTA. ET VIDE LA DIVINA MATRE IN SUA MAIESTATE, ET COME ESSA SILENTIO ALLE CANTANTE NYMPHE IMPOSE. DILLE QUALE TRE PER UNO A POLIA ET A LLUI GLI CONSIGNOE. DAPOSCIA CUPIDINE AMBI DUI GLI FERITE, ET LA DEA CUM L’AQUA DIL FONTE GLI IMBREFECE, ET POLIPHILO FUE REVESTITO. POSTREMO VENENDO MARTE IMPETRATA LA LICENTIA SE PARTIRONO.

CUM DECENTISSIME VENERATIONE ET summa honorificentia la Eutrapela Polia et io affectuosamente dinanti al mysterioso fonte dilla divina genitrice congenulati, io da una imperceptibile dulcedine diffusamente invadere vexabonda me sentiva, che in quel puncto che fare non sapeva. Imperò che per lo amoenissimo et incredibilmente delitioso sito, et ultra omni credito dilectoso di virentia et vernale decoramento. Et per le avicule per il purissimo aere discorrendo, et per le novelle fronde garriendo volante ad gli forinseci sensi, quam iucundissime, et cum le decorissime Nymphe cantante melode inseme cum gli sui insueti soni audiendo, et degli sui divi acti, et modestissime moventie videndo ardentemente impulso ad extrema voluptate, et de sì facta fabricatura di tanta dignitate di concepto, et dilla elegantissima dispositione solertemente et curioso pensiculando, et di tanta inexperta fragrantia avidamente hauriendo. Per lo immortale Iove rectamente ignorava ad quale sensitivo potere, lo intento obiecto mio da tanto diverso dilecto distracto, et excessivo et foelice solatio et voluptico placimine firma, et stabilmente applicare, et nescio me accusava. Le quale tutte belle, et dulcissime cose tanto più gratioso et desiderabile allectamento ad me allhora plenamente causando offerivano, quanto che io conosceva la Uranothia Polia participe placidamente delectarsene. In questo loco, at etiam di questo ammirando fonte la novitate et excellentia mirando.

(the last line of the preceding chapter) …by which we were brought before the holy and sacred Cytherean fountain.

Notes

Poliphilo describes the marvellous artifice of the Venerean fountain standing at the centre of the theatre's floor, and how the little curtain was torn away; and he saw the divine Mother in her majesty, and how she imposed silence upon the singing nymphs, of whom she assigned three apiece to Polia and to him. Afterwards Cupid wounded them both, and the goddess sprinkled them with the water of the fountain, and Poliphilo was clothed again. Last of all, Mars coming, they obtained leave and departed.

With the most fitting reverence and the highest honour, the graceful Polia and I knelt devotedly before the mysterious fountain of the divine mother, and I felt an imperceptible sweetness spreading through me and troubling me, so that in that moment I did not know what to do. For what with the delightful place, incredibly pleasing and beyond all belief lovely in its greenness and spring adornment; and the little birds coursing through the purest air and chattering as they flew among the new leaves, most joyfully, to the outward senses; and the hearing of those most beautiful nymphs singing their melodies together with their unfamiliar instruments; and the sight of their divine gestures and most modest movements, which drove me ardently toward the extremity of pleasure; and the careful, curious weighing of a fabric of such dignity of conception and such elegant arrangement; and the greedy drinking-in of a fragrance I had never known — by immortal Jove, I truly did not know to which of my senses I should firmly and steadily apply the object of my attention, distracted as it was by so many different delights, by so excessive and happy a solace and voluptuous pleasing; and I accused myself of not knowing.

And all these beautiful and most sweet things offered me an enticement so much the more gracious and desirable, in that I knew that Polia — heavenly Polia — was placidly taking her share of the delight in them.

In this place, then, and marvelling too at the novelty and excellence of this astonishing fountain.

---

Confidence: medium. The chapter argument is secure. The body is one enormous periodic sentence — a chain of per… clauses hung on "I did not know to which sense to apply my attention" — and English will not hold a period that long, so I have broken it with semicolons. The sense is not in doubt; the shape has been compromised, and a reviewer might well restore more of it than I dared.

  • Eutrapela — Greek εὐτράπελος, "graceful, ready in speech, well-turned."
  • Uranothia — a Colonna coinage from οὐρανός, "heaven." Rendered "heavenly
  • The chapter argument is set in full capitals in the original, as the running
  • The final sentence is deliberately unfinished: the argument runs straight on
  • per il purissimo aere discorrendo — discorrere here in its older sense

Il quale nel mediostimo di questo inhumano aedificamento divinamente constructo et expresso per questo modo. Della nigerrima petra, che sola integramente era tutto il silicato, overamente il pavimento di l’area di essa propria nel mediano di uno murulo sublato pedale, cum egregia politura reducta cum omni ornato opportuno di forma extrinseca heptagono, et della interstitia rotunda. Cum l’ambiente cimasula et socco et arulette, et undiculatione fabrefacte et ordinariamente supposite alle base, sopra il mediano puncto degli anguli, dil quale per ciascuno era superastructa una enthesiata, overo ventriculata columna in numero septe, cum summa exquisitura turbinate. Dille quale due correspondevano aequale ex adverso dell’ingresso. In conspecto di questo ove stavamo proni geniculati. Una dille quale tornatile columne, alla dextera parte cyanava praefulgente di finissimo sapphyro, et dalla sinistra vernava virente smaragdo di praestantissimo colore più lucentissimo che gli affixi per gli ochii al Leone al tumulo di Hermia Regulo. Né tale fu donato da Ptolemaeo ad Lucullo. Né di tanta pretiositate fue il praesentato da Re di Babylonia al Re Aegyptico, né di tanto aestimamento furono quegli dil obelisco nel Tempio di Iove. Né di tanta miraveglia fue la statua nel templo di Hercule in Tyro, quale questo admirando se praestava. Proximo ad questa sequiva una columna di petra turchinia di venusto Ceruleo coloratissima, cum la virtute gratiosamente donata. Et quantunque caeca, niente dimeno illustrissima et specularmente praefulgeva.

Contigua alla sapphyrica columna assideva una pretiosa di petra caeca etiam di iucundissimo colore, quale il Meliloto, et di lustro quale lo interlucido floreo dil vatrachio. Adhaeriva a questa una di Iaspide di colore hyalino, et l’altra di topatio fulgurante colore aureo. La septima sola et singularmente era hexagonia di lympidissimo berillo indico di oleaceo nitore in contrario gli obiecti reddendo. Et questa per medio dille due prime correspondeva, per che omni figura dispare angulare, uno angulo obvia nel mediano dell’intercalato di dui. Dunque il circulo obducto del suo diametro semisse, ivi uno triangulo aequilatero constituito, et poscia dal centro una linea, nel medio dilla linea

Which fountain, at the very middle of this more-than-human building, was divinely constructed and set out in this manner.

Of that blackest stone which alone, and entire, made the whole paving — that is, the floor of the area — there rose at its centre a little wall a foot high, brought to an excellent polish and furnished with every fitting ornament, heptagonal on the outside and round within. It had its running cymatium and socle, its small plinths and its wave-mouldings, wrought and set in due order beneath the bases; and over the middle point of each angle there was built up a column swelling in the shaft — that is, bellied — seven of them in all, turned with the utmost exquisiteness. Two of these stood answering one another, equally, opposite the entrance, in full view of where we knelt face down.

One of these lathe-turned columns, on the right hand, shone blue with the finest sapphire; and on the left one glowed spring-green with an emerald of a colour so surpassing, and more brilliant, than the stones set for eyes in the lion on the tomb of Hermias the prince. Nor was such a one given by Ptolemy to Lucullus; nor was that of so great price which the King of Babylon presented to the King of Egypt; nor were those of the obelisk in the Temple of Jove of such estimation; nor was the statue in the temple of Hercules at Tyre so great a marvel, as this one showed itself to be.

Next to this followed a column of turquoise stone, most beautifully coloured a lovely cerulean, endowed graciously with its virtue; and though it was an opaque stone, it was nonetheless most brilliant, and shone back like a mirror.

Touching the sapphire column there stood another precious one, opaque also, of a most delightful colour like melilot, and with a lustre like the translucent flower of the buttercup. Adjoining this was one of jasper of a glassy colour, and the other of topaz flashing with a golden colour. The seventh, alone and singularly, was hexagonal, of the most limpid Indian beryl with an oily sheen, giving back whatever stood before it reversed.

And this one answered to the midpoint between the first two — because every figure of an odd number of angles has one angle facing the middle of the interval between two others. So then: the circle drawn about its half-diameter, an equilateral triangle set up within it, and then a line from the centre, and in the middle of that line —

Notes

Confidence: medium. The gem-lore and the column order are secure — Colonna names each stone plainly and the Plinian comparisons are identifiable. Two weaker areas: the mouldings of the kerb (cimasula, socco, arulette, undiculatione) are a technical vocabulary I have rendered by their standard Vitruvian equivalents without being certain Colonna means exactly those members; and the geometrical construction in the last paragraph is compressed to the point of obscurity, so I have translated it literally rather than smoothing it into a proof. A reviewer should look hardest at the last paragraph.

  • The seven columns and their stones, in the order Colonna gives them:
  • enthesiata, overo ventriculata — entasis, the swelling of a column
  • petra caeca — literally "blind stone," the period term for an opaque
  • vatrachio — βατράχιον, the buttercup, whose petals have exactly the

sopra la circunferentia adiacente deducta, tanto è la septenaria divisione dilla dicta circulare figura. In medio del scapo della septima columna beryllia, dalla parte intranea era mirificamente della propria petra quasi di scalptura divulsa, uno puerulo Hermaphrodito da uno cotylidone ritinuto. Le tre praelucente columne all’ordine dextero similmente per ciascuna haveano in miro modo uno infantulo capto in certi acceptabuli. Et cusì nel scapo di ciascuna dille pretiose columne sinistre appendeva per ciascuna infixo il foemello sexo. Et questo de artificio mystico nel medio dille columne era naturalmente expresso. Cum tanto scintulamento di lustratione, quale non rende la corrosione dil cotes, overo smirillo cum la lambente Tripolea creta. Le base, gli capitelli, il trabe Phrygio et coronice extavano di mundissimo oro. Gli archi cum tutto il solido tra una columna et l’altra era della subacta petra di una delle columne per ordine ambiente, cioè di saphyro verso il smaragdo et il smaragdo verso la turchinia. Et cusì subsequentemente tutto l’arcuato era mirabilmente constructo. Negli anguli dilla corona sopra la viva et centrica linea perpendiculare di qualunque substituta columna, una Aruleta, et di supra excitata una imagine di planita cum il suo appropriato attributo promineva. La sua grandecia dal tertio dilla subiecta columna exacta symmetricamente di purissimo oro. Nel fronte anteriore alla dextera il falcifero Saturno assideva, et alla sinistra la noctiluca Cynthia, per ordine incominciando dal primo circinanti terminavano ad Selene. Sotto agli quali nel zophoro in circuito cum maximo exquisito di artificio elegantemente celati vedevase gli duodeci signi zodiaci, cum le superiore impressione, et charactere, cum eximia scalptura expressi. Il culmo poscia di questo mirabilissimo fonte et tectorio fulgeva di una insolente cupula di optimo et disvenato crystallo mundissimo et perspicuo. Né tale vide Xenocrates, né reperto simile in Cypro, né producto in Asia. Né in Germania, sencia rubigine et scabricie, sencia nube maculosa, né centro sale, né alcuno capillamento vedevasi. Né tale franse Nerone. Ma puro praestante et asyntheto, incincta di una sublata operatura cum aequa convenientia di ligatura di fronde procedente da alcuni monstriculi cum pueruli per quelle ludibundi maravegliosamente implexi. La quale era di egregia corpulentia et convexo. Nel gracilamento del summo cacumine havea infixo uno miraculoso ostentamento in uno aureo et faberrimo Lovo, di uno undique fulgitritio carbunculo di forma ovola et di crassitudine strutiocamela. Nelle facie dil murulo dilla fuscatissima petra, sopra il quale emusicatamente

— drawn upon the adjacent circumference: such is the sevenfold division of the said circular figure.

In the middle of the shaft of the seventh, the beryl column, on the inner face, there was marvellously carved out of that same stone — as though wrenched free of the sculpture — a little Hermaphrodite child, held in a socket. The three shining columns of the right-hand order had likewise, each of them, an infant boy caught in a kind of receptacle; and so too on the shaft of each of the precious columns of the left there hung, set into it, the female sex. And this, by a mystical artifice, was expressed naturally at the middle of the columns, with such a scintillation of polish as neither the biting of whetstone nor emery with licking Tripoli chalk can give.

The bases, the capitals, the Phrygian architrave and the cornice stood out in the purest gold. The arches, and all the solid between one column and the next, were of the wrought stone of one of the columns of the surrounding order — that is, of sapphire toward the emerald, and of emerald toward the turquoise — and so on in sequence, the whole arcade was marvellously constructed.

At the angles of the crown, above the true central perpendicular of each column beneath, there stood a small altar, and rising from it projected the image of a planet with its proper attribute, its size exactly a third of the column below it, symmetrically, and of the purest gold. On the front face, at the right, sat scythe-bearing Saturn, and at the left the night-shining Cynthia; and beginning from the first and going round in order they ended at Selene. Beneath these, in the frieze running round, chased with the most exquisite artifice, were to be seen the twelve signs of the zodiac, with their governing impressions and characters, expressed in outstanding carving.

The crown then of this most marvellous fountain, and its roofing, blazed with an extraordinary cupola of the finest crystal, veinless, utterly pure and transparent. Xenocrates saw none such; none like it was found in Cyprus, nor produced in Asia, nor in Germany — without rust or roughness, without a clouded speck, no core of salt, nor any hairline flaw to be seen. Nor did Nero shatter such a one. But pure, surpassing, and unalloyed, it was girdled with a raised work, in a fitting and even binding of foliage issuing from certain little monsters, with small boys wonderfully interwoven playing among them; and it was of excellent bulk and convexity.

At the tapering of its topmost peak was fixed a miraculous show: in a golden and most cunningly wrought [?setting], a carbuncle flashing lightning on every side, egg-shaped, and of the thickness of an ostrich's.

On the faces of the little wall of that darkest stone, upon which —

Diqué essendo ambidui Polia et io supra gli vertibili popliti expositi cernui, il divino signore Cupidine, dede alla Nympha Synesia la sagitta d’oro et accortamente gli fece nuto che ad Polia essa la offerisca. Et che quello che la mellea et integerrima Polia fare non audeva, che io thelithoro et avidissimo di mirare la Sanctissima genitrice exequire dovesse. — the ready columns were raised, there were cut, perfectly excavated, letters of the most ancient Greek, the shaft being three-quarters of a foot. In the hollows of these there shone, laid in, words of refined silver. On the front face only two letters were to be seen, intersected with companion emblems of gold and elegantly polished; and then following, on the other faces, three and three, they said this:

ΩΣΠΕΡ ΣΠΙΝΘΗΡ ΚΗΛΗΘΜΟΣ

as a spark, so enchantment

Each face was three feet, and from the golden bases up to the architrave the height stood at seven feet. Of the artifice of this thing, marvellous and most highly finished, I judge that its dignity will be more fittingly preserved by my keeping silent than by my discoursing of it poorly and meagrely.

There, between the sapphire and the emerald column, hung on bent rings with knotted loops the most beautiful little veiling curtain that fertile nature could ever have devised to produce as a thing more pleasing to the gods — so beautiful a weaving, and of such a material, that I should never know how to describe it; but of a sandalwood colouring, woven with the loveliest flowering, and with four Greek letters of gold subtly embroidered over it, ΥΜΗΝ, stretched out most becomingly and heavenward. Let the marvellous curtain the Samians sent to Delphi rightly yield place here. This appeared to my Polia as a most precious treasure, and gracious; and it was this that veiled and hid the majestic and divine presence of the venerable mother.

And so, both of us — Polia and I — kneeling face down upon our bending knees, the divine lord Cupid gave the golden arrow to the nymph Synesia, and shrewdly made her a sign that she should offer it to Polia, and that Polia with that fearful arrow should rend and split the most noble curtain. But Polia, as though grieved at the command for such a cutting and breaking, however subject she was to that divine authority, seemed unpractised, and refused to consent. The lord in that same moment, smiling, charged the nymph Synesia that she should hand it to the nymph Philedia, and that she should then present it to me; and that what the honeyed and most upright Polia did not dare to do, I — [?spellbound], and most greedy to look upon the most holy mother — should carry out.

Whereupon no sooner had I taken hold of the divine instrument than, ringed about with a blind flame and not refusing, but rather with an urgent and headlong passion, I struck the little curtain. And as it split I saw that Polia was almost saddened by it; and the emerald column, cracking, seemed as though it must shatter entirely.

And behold, all at once I plainly see the divine form in the salt fountain, brought forth out of that venerable majesty from which all beauty deliciously flowed. Nor sooner had that unlooked-for sight —

divino ad gli ochi mei spirando scorse, che ambi dui da extrema dolcecia excitati, et da novello et da tanto diutinamente concupito piacere impulsi et velitati, cum divoto timore inseme quasi in extasi rimansimo. Per la quale cosa cusì ritrovantime incomminciai pur ragionevolmente expaventarme alquanto dubitando dilla visione in la valle Gargaphya che hebbe il filio di Aristeo, in uno momento maraveglia et terriculo mi misse. In medio la divina Venere stavasi nuda nelle perspicue, et limpidissime aquule insino supra ad gli ampli et divi fianchi. Le quale non crasso, non gemino, non disfracto, non breve il cythereo corpo reddendo, ma integerrimo et simplice, quale era cusì in ipso perfectamente se cerniva. Et circumcirca all’infimo grado suboliva uno spumamento che referiva olido musco. Cum tanto numine trasparente il divino corpo, cum praecipua perspicuitate quella maiestate et venerabile aspecto obiectantise. Quanto pretioso et corruscante carbunculo agli solarii radii fulgura. Cum facteze et mirando composito tra gli humani né viduto né unque meditato. La quale havea, o quanta cum venustate la sua obaurea caesarie amoena et delicatamente compta, supra la lactea et candifica fronte concinnamente irriciatula et concrispulata cum erranti, et inquietuli vertigini, che di extendirsi erano capreolamente impediti. Dalle quale pendevano due ostentose margarite, quale ad essa nel Pantheon il dissecto Unione a Roma non pendeva. — that divine sight breathed upon my eyes and passed into them, but both of us, roused by an extreme sweetness and driven and harried by a new pleasure so long desired, remained together in a devout fear, almost in ecstasy.

And finding myself so, I began with some reason to be afraid, misdoubting the vision that the son of Aristaeus had in the vale of Gargaphie; and in a moment it put both wonder and terror into me.

In the midst, the divine Venus stood naked in the clear and most limpid little waters, up to her ample and divine flanks — waters which gave back the Cytherean body neither thickened nor doubled nor broken nor foreshortened, but wholly and simply, so that it was perfectly discerned in them just as it was. And round about, at the lowest level, there rose a foaming that gave off a fragrance of musk. With such divinity was that body transparent, with such particular clarity did that majesty and venerable aspect present itself, as a precious and coruscating carbuncle flashes in the rays of the sun — with features and a marvellous composition neither seen nor ever imagined among human kind.

She had — oh, with what loveliness — her golden hair pleasantly and delicately dressed, neatly curled and crisped above the milky whitening brow with wandering and restless whorls, which were hindered from spreading out like vine-tendrils; and from her rosy shoulders they were becomingly kept from falling free by the most beautiful little waves. The face rosy and snowing. The eyes starry and luminous, with an amorous and most holy regard. The honeyed cheeks purpled. The small narrow mouth most purple, of coral — the dwelling and the little estate of every fragrant bud. The breast more than snowy and treasured up, with two small swelling paps that resisted every inclination. The body most ivory and smooth. Divine semblances. Ambrosial — breathing, rather, a spirit of musk.

Her hair then, most beautiful, like the finest golden threads trailing, lay upon the purest waters, not sinking, but scattered in a gyre and floating out very long: in that show the rivals of long-haired Phoebus in the clear Olympus, radiating illuminating rays. And above the twisted little locks, over part of the loveliest brow, with a dense growth and an airy heaping, they shone out before her and covered her as with a little shade, down to the small ears. From these hung two ostentatious pearls, such as the divided Union did not hang for her in the Pantheon at Rome; nor did the island of Taprobane ever produce their like for conspicuous whiteness. And a circlet went about her, or rather a plaited band of vermilion-whitening and pleasant roses, springlike interwoven, and it flashed with little gems.

Notes

Confidence: medium. This is a blazon — a head-to-toe catalogue — and the sense of every clause is clear; the difficulty is that Colonna coins a diminutive or a compound in nearly every one (melule gene, eburissimamente glabello, torcularei crinuli), and English has to choose between keeping the strangeness and keeping the sentence readable. I have kept the strangeness where it carries meaning and let it go where it was only ornament, which is a judgement another translator could reasonably make differently. Nothing here is doubtful; a good deal is debatable.

Et nel ambito internate del sacratissimo fonte, fora dille haesione de gli sumptuosi gradi. Il floribundo et purpureo Adone germinava tra le sue amnice fronde purpurigiante dall’aqua exclusivo. Et al lato sinistro similmente cum le sue pallide uve thelygono floriva. Et alla dextera Arsenogono, spectatissime herbe et sempre floribonde. Et in circo alla dea, alcune candide columbule volitavano, moratamente obsequibile ministrante. Cum gli aurei rostri nelle mundissime limphe immersi. And within the inner circuit of the most sacred fountain, clear of the join of the sumptuous steps, flowering, purple Adonis sprang up among its kindred leaves, purpling, kept just clear of the water; and on the left side likewise thelygonon flowered with its pale grape-clusters, and on the right arsenogonon — most admirable herbs, and always in flower. And in a circle about the goddess certain small white doves fluttered, dutifully and decorously ministering: dipping their golden bills in the most spotless waters, they mysteriously bedewed the little Cytherean body, and the droplets showed on her translucent flesh as nothing other than orient pearls set there. Close by stood the nymph Peristeria, promptly and even boldly ready, attending with a whole mind upon the services of Venus.

Likewise outside the fountain, upon the stone floor where Peristeria stood, at the right side, three other divine girls stood naked in this manner, inseparably embraced: two of them, Eurydomene and Eurymone, showing their maiden faces toward us; the third, Eurymeduse, turned with her whitest shoulders to us, her hidden hips veiled by the long outpouring of her most golden hair. These were the gracious daughters and handmaids, prompt to the effect of the goddess mother. And she held, lastly, in her divine hands an opened oyster-shell packed with fresh spring roses, and in the other a burning torch.

Now from the topmost step, upon which the columns stood, six little steps more went down to the lip of the fountain, of dark agate; and the flat floor beneath the water was wave-worked with the loveliest and most graceful milky veining, variously spiralling — as delightful an object as could ever be set before the senses. The fountain's water reached to the edge of the fourth step; the others stood clear of it.

And above the upper step there sat at his ease a wanton figure in the shape of a man, the god Nyctelius, whose look was that of a pert and striking girl, most witty, the breast bared by the parting of his garment. His horned head was bound with a vine-band of writhen tendrils, adorned with savoury grape- clusters. He leaned upon two most swift tigers.

And on the left in the same manner sat, at her ease, a most beautiful and nourishing matron, her broad and curled brow wreathed with a blond garland of wheat-ears. That renowned lady rested upon two scaled serpents. And each of the two held a spherical ball of some thin and yielding stuff in their laps; and with these, at measured intervals, through a cunning and fateful nippled opening, they dripped into the fountain, drop by drop, a most sweet, foaming and potent liquor. advertivano de intingere nel salso fonte gli sui belluli pedusculi. Gli quali degli prompti digituli più excedente quello indicavano, che propinquo è al magiore. D’indi gli altri gradiculatamente cum venusto moderamine verso il rotundo talo, cum grato ordine alla parte extima declinando minuentise. Per questa divina dispositione dunque la sanctissima maiestate dilla Dea resideva volupticamente nel mediastimo del fonte. Et la parte che del divino corpo tra l’aque stasea, cusì né più, né meno, che radio overo splendore del Sole in expolitissimo crystallo praelucente. Quivi congenulati divotamente perseverando, cum la mente oltra modo, et excessivamente mirato vacillava. Non valendo fixamente el nume divino dovunque spirante mirare. Et la cagione non meno pensiculatamente recogitando, cum quale dolcecia di sorte et fede, cum quale modo et merito, tali mysterii ad me fusse concesso chiaramente di cernere, et cum gli ochii mei ad tale obiecto indispositi. Ma solamente iudicai essere stata degli immortali Dii la libera voluntate, et il benigno consentire di Polia et fidele oratione. Ma supra tutte cose ad me praestavasi displicibile, che tra tante coeleste et dive persone solo contemptibile et exotico, et de gli decoti habiti atriti et frustrati, et di qualunque altra maniera dissimile istava abiectissimo et pauperrimo, et allhora mi sarebbe stato il modo di coprire la mia deformitate quam acceptissimo. Quale Erichthonio per caelare gli viperini pedi. Imperò di incredibile miraveglia stupefacto nell’animo mio summamente la benignitate divina laudava. Che permesso haveva, che terrigeno homo le opere divine et il thesoro dilla fermentosa natura palesemente contemplasse. Per la quale cosa, quelle insigne Nymphe che di sotto le pergule, cum gli sui plausi et cantici et suave harmonia laetabonde festizavano per la victa praeda, che triumphante consequire doveva il pinnato et temerario Cupidine che più perspicace dil Lynceo et di Argo oculeo, era vigorosamente cum l’arme prompto. Diqué parv’hora consumpta dalla dea Matre, ad gli coelesti soni, et cantici alquanto de silentio intervallato ad ambi nui, cum divina facundia et lepore mulcente cum blando affamine tale suaviloquio la sanctissima bucca produsse parole argute et sencia dubio, da soporare et la vigilante custodia adormentare dil fatale thesoro di Colcho. Et da rivocare in benigno effigiato Aglauros filia di Cecrope. Et al grato armento di ristituere Daphni Idaeo, cum la forma humana. Et Cadmo et Hermione dalla sibilante voce rivocare, et dal squammeo corpo, ad Polia cusì dicendo. Pulchella Polia cultrice mia, gli tui sancti libamenti saeduli obsequii,

— they took care to dip their little rosy feet in the salt fountain; and of their ready little toes one stood out beyond the rest, the one nearest the great toe. Then the others, step by step, with graceful measure, sloped down toward the rounded ankle, diminishing in good order toward the outer edge.

By this divine arrangement, then, the most holy majesty of the goddess sat voluptuously in the very middle of the fountain; and the part of the divine body that stood among the waters showed neither more nor less than a ray or splendour of the sun shining through the most polished crystal.

Kneeling there and devoutly persevering, my mind wavered, gazing beyond all measure and to excess, unable to look fixedly upon the divine spirit breathing everywhere. And no less thoughtfully I turned over the cause — by what sweetness of fortune and faith, by what means and merit, such mysteries were granted me to see clearly, and with my eyes unfit for such an object. But I judged it to be only the free will of the immortal gods, and the kind consent and faithful prayer of Polia. Above all things it displeased me that among so many celestial and divine persons I alone stood there contemptible and foreign, in worn and threadbare clothes, unlike them in every way, most abject and most poor; and I would then most gladly have had some means of covering my deformity — as Erichthonius had, to hide his viper feet. And so, stupefied in my mind with incredible wonder, I praised the divine benignity to the utmost, that it had allowed an earth-born man openly to contemplate the works of the gods and the treasure of fermenting nature.

Whereupon those illustrious nymphs, who beneath the pergolas rejoiced with their applause and songs and sweet harmony for the prey that winged and rash Cupid — keener-sighted than Lynceus and eye-covered Argus — was vigorously ready with his weapons to take in triumph: after a little hour had been spent by the mother goddess, and the celestial sounds and songs were broken off a while into silence, to both of us, with divine eloquence and soothing charm, in a gentle address, that most holy mouth uttered such sweet-speaking words — words shrewd and beyond doubt able to lull to sleep the wakeful guardian of the fatal treasure of Colchis, to call back to a kindly shape Aglauros the daughter of Cecrops, to restore Daphnis of Ida to human form and to the grateful flock, and to recall Cadmus and Hermione from the hissing voice and the scaled body — to Polia thus saying:

Lovely Polia, my worshipper, your holy libations, your assiduous observances —

religiosi ministerii propiciantime, te dignificata hanno facto dille nostre dolcissime et fructifere gratie. Et per le tue sincere supplice et illibate litatione et solemne cerimonie commendata alla placatione, et cum votato core, et cum observabile tirocinio precata inclinarme voglio benifica, et favorigiante et munificamente munerabonda sospitatrice. Et che il tuo individuo Comite Poliphilo quivi dil tuo amore candescente astando, parimente annumerato sia tra gli veraci et foelici amatori. Et dille plebee et vulgarie sorde quivi remundato, et da omni spurco impiamento si forsa casitato fusse, dil mio rore perfuso expiato se purifichi, et ad te incessante esso deditissimo sia. Et ad gli tui placidi desii praesto et saedulo et che egli non recusi a qualunque tua voluntate. Et tutti dui aequalmente amantise ad gli mei amorosi fochi ultroneamente, et cum integerrimo consenso servirete, amplificabondi. Et la succisiva vita sotto la mia tutela protegente beati et gloriosi vitulanti. Al praesente acioché il vostro tanto amore sortisca foelice successo, Poliphile, quatro praeclare virguncule donare, et ad te consignare io voglio et dille sue ornate virtute dotarte, al tuo excellente animo, et generoso amore ad decorare molto conforme. Et cum esse tu sii observatore pervicacemente, più che il firmo Pico verso la sua Canente, et di Polia cultore. Et giù sencia morula dalle pergule ad sé chiamata la praestante Nympha Enosina gli dixe. Tolle teco la fanciulla, singulare Monori, et la vigile Phrontida et la sua silente sorore Critoa, et comite siate inseparabile di questo nostro Athleta et servitore amante Polia. Et per mio fatale iusso cagione siate che tutti dui siano aequivalente di mutua dilectione. Et sencia intercalato fora dilla cortice ostrea dui annuli trasse cum una pretiosa gemma violacea inclaustrata anterota in ciascuno et ad Polia uno gli dete et a mi l’altro, cum effabilissimo Imperio, che sempre di tale divino munere decorati devessimo perdurare, et che penitamente sempre delitiscente lo edicto suo servare dovessimo, cum fronte serena, et propiciato vulto commulcente. Et rivoltata in uno momento, ad te Polia dixe. Similmente quatro altre ingenue et scitissime virgine apresento nel tuo consortio indesinente. Le quale debino dignificarte, et in questo tuo celeberrimo amore elegantemente honorarte. Da quello loco chiamate ancora vacando dagli cantici sui et soni. Adiacorista cum le tre ingenue sorore, Pistinia, Sophrosyne, et Edosia gli impose dicendo. Hor non lasciate costei unquantulo di mora sencia vui, aciò che essa, cum aequa legie il suo Poliphilo amando, cum herculano nodo vivi, ornata del più scitissimo, et bellissimo amore, che unque nel z iii

your religious ministrations propitiating me, have made you worthy of our sweetest and most fruitful graces. And for your sincere, suppliant and unspotted offerings and solemn ceremonies, commended to my appeasing, and with a devoted heart and observant novitiate praying, I am willing to incline myself, kind and favouring and munificently bountiful, your preserver — and that your own companion Poliphilo, standing here glowing with your love, be likewise numbered among the true and happy lovers; and cleansed here of the plebeian and vulgar filth, and if by chance he was chastened of any foul defilement, let him be purified, expiated, sprinkled with my dew; and let him be unceasingly devoted to you, and prompt and assiduous to your gentle desires, and let him refuse nothing of your will. And loving one another equally, of your own accord and with wholest consent, you shall both serve my amorous fires and increase them; and for the rest of your life, protected under my keeping, you shall live blessed and glorious and exulting.

Now, so that so great a love of yours may have a happy outcome, Poliphilo, I will give and consign to you four illustrious little maidens, and dower you with their adorned virtues, well suited to your excellent spirit and generous love; and with them be you a keeper more steadfast than firm Picus toward his Canens, and a worshipper of Polia.

And without delay, calling to her from the pergolas the excellent nymph Enosina, she said to her: take with you the girl, matchless Monori, and watchful Phrontis and her silent sister Critoa, and be inseparable companions of this our athlete and loving servant of Polia. And by my fated command be the cause that both of them are of equal and mutual affection. And without pause she drew from within the oyster-shell two rings, each with a precious violet gem set turning in it, and gave one to Polia and the other to me, with most eloquent command that we should endure forever adorned with such a divine gift, and should keep her edict deep and always hidden, with a serene brow and a kindly, soothing face.

And turning in a moment, to you, Polia, she said: likewise I present four other noble and most accomplished maidens into your unceasing company, who shall make you worthy and elegantly honour you in this most celebrated love of yours.

Calling them too from that place, at leisure from their songs and sounds, she charged Adiacorista with the three noble sisters, Pistinia, Sophrosyne and Edosia, saying: now let these not leave her for the least while without you, so that she, loving her Poliphilo by equal law, may live bound in a Herculean knot, adorned with the most accomplished and most beautiful love that ever in her —

suo seculo celebre, et di memorato dignio se sentisse. Al genio indulgendo et nunque defraudando. Offerentise hostia iniuge cum sincera et mera fede verso a lui, et labante firmare, anxio et solicito beneficamente recevere, et gratificabonda, cum tenacissimo vinculo amplexare. Tutte queste dive Nymphe lo imperio dilla suprana dea sencia inducie incominciorono ciascuna il consignato, et credito suo amorosamente cum hianti osculi consaviare in stricti amplexi, cum molti Nymphaei blandimenti et attractive charitie, et promiscuamente alacre, et saepicule praessulamente amplexandone saviate, cum debite et conveniente inclinatione annuendo alla Divina Matre, il credito overo commisso principiorono sedule et cum praecipua comitate a famulare. Et al promptissimo filiolo, non prima quasi le sancte parolette prolate, et finito il divo rationamento, che lui cum genuina licentia procace sencia pietate severo, non della gortiniaca, ma dilla volante sagitta d’oro non dal Ithyreo arco ma divino directa et non più praesto dal impeto dilla rigente cordula lo amento fue expulso, che per medio trasvecta dil mio dissaveduto core repente confixe. Et d’indi properamente cusì cruorosa tincta nel mio inflammato pecto, et dal caldo cruore fumidula uscitene del sagittario vulnere, non risanabile dal trago frutice cretense. Et sencia morula trasfixe, et quello dilla mia pyrrothricha Polia, et nel suo palpitante pecto dilla propria illibata alma retincto, ello la vulnerante et cruentata sagitta riassumpse, et di subito nel materno fonte lavatola la repose. O me che incontinente di sentire principiai essere nelle penetrale, et intime viscere le mellite uredine di una exuberante flammula diffusa, et lernicamente disseminata, et tutto me occupare et d’amorosi ardori obducto tremiculo, et di offuscare gli ochii meii. Et sencia pausula lo incenso pecto reseratose ad sé più harpylatica et mordacemente attraxe, che le anguinee thriche del Polypo, et che il typhone sorbiculabundo l’aqua, et intromisso l’amore pretioso et il divulo effigiato di Polia nunque obliterabile, cum le ingenue caste et dulcissime conditione nel praeparato et amorosamente disposito subiecto informantise, ove aeternalmente dominabonda rimanse, et quel coelico, et inelluibile simulachro firmatissimamente impresso speciosissimo. Quale arefacta et siccissima palea rimasto in subitaneo et violente foco adusta, et come facole di arsibile teda, non servata capillare particula, che d’amorosa flamma non penetrasse. Et quasi ad me parve d’altra forma acconciamente immutarme. Cum grande vacillamento et carivatione dillo intellecto di non valer comprehendere se non per quale assimilitudine che Hermaphrodito cum Salmace nel vivo, et fresco fonte amplexantise advene, quando elli nel promiscuo sexo

— famous age she might feel herself, and worthy of memory; indulging her genius and never defrauding it. Offering herself a yoked sacrifice with sincere and pure faith toward him, and steadying what would falter, anxiously and carefully and kindly to receive him, and gratefully, with the most tenacious bond, to embrace him.

All these divine nymphs, at the command of the sovereign goddess and without delay, each began lovingly to kiss with open kisses the one committed and entrusted to her, in close embraces, with many nymphish blandishments and attractive endearments; and promiscuously eager, and often pressingly embracing and kissing us, with due and fitting inclination assenting to the divine Mother, they began assiduously and with especial courtesy to serve the charge committed to them.

And to the most prompt little son — the holy little words scarcely uttered and the divine discourse hardly finished — he, with native licence, bold and pitiless and severe, with the flying golden arrow (not the Gortynian, and drawn not from the Ityraean bow but the divine one), no sooner had the string's [?loosing] driven it out with the force of the taut cord than it struck, carried through the middle of my unwitting heart, and suddenly pierced it; and then, so quickly, dyed with gore, smoking from the hot blood issuing out of the arrow-wound — a wound not to be healed by the Cretan dittany shrub — without delay it transfixed also the heart of my red-haired Polia, and in her throbbing breast, stained with her own unspotted soul, he took the wounding and bloodied arrow up again, and at once, washing it in the maternal fountain, laid it back.

Ah me, that straightway I began to feel in the innermost and deepest bowels the honeyed burning of an exuberant little flame, spread and sown far and wide, and occupying all of me; and clouded over with amorous ardours I trembled, and my eyes were darkened. And without pause my kindled breast, opening to itself, drew her to it more harpy-like and more bitingly than the snaky hair of the polyp, than the whirlwind swallowing water; and the precious love and the divine and never-to-be-blotted image of Polia being let in — with its noble, chaste and sweetest conditions, informing the prepared and lovingly disposed subject where it remained eternally ruling, that heavenly and unwashable likeness most firmly imprinted and most beautiful — I remained like dry and parched straw kindled in a sudden and violent fire, and like the torches of burnable pine, not a hair's particle preserved that the amorous flame did not penetrate. And it almost seemed to me that I was fittingly changed into another form, with great wavering and faintness of the intellect, unable to comprehend it except by that likeness of Hermaphroditus embracing Salmacis in the living, fresh spring, when he, in the mingled sex —

vedese, in la forma unica transformarse. Né più né meno, che la infoelice Biblis le sue lachryme sentite farle nel liquido fonte dille naiade Nymphe. Dunque morando io già nelle dulcissime flamme nun più vivo che mortuo sencia vivido pulso. Et da non impedito meato, da summa dulcitudine permitteva liberamente et exalare il spiritulo mio pensando che da Epilipsia, cum genu replicato cadendo fusce stato invaso. La pientissima Dea repente cum la diva vola lacunata, deposita la ostrea stringendo la intervallatura degli longiusculi digiti, dille salsule aque exhaurite divinamente supra nui humectando asperse. Non quale la indignabonda Diana, il sfortunato venatore imbrificò dilacerando a cani in belva vertite, ma sencia haesitare per lo opposito imbrefacto transmutando alle sacre Nymphe gratificabondo et amplexando. Né più praesto benignamente facto hebbe, et io di rore marino asperso et delibuto, che in me immediate excitati gli clarificati spiriti furono più intelligibili. Et sencia praestolatione se convertirono nel pristino stato li adusti et concremati membri et me senza fallire di digne qualitate ricentarme sentendo. Conobi veramente per assimile modo Eson rinovato non essere, né altramente in me regresso mi apparve, che alla optata luce il virbio Hippolyto revocato per grande precatione di Diana cum la herba glycyside ad la appetibile vita. Et a me affectuosamente le plebarie toge dalle assignate Nymphe exute, di candida et lautiuscula veste di novo me officiosamente vestirono. Et facti tranquillamente del nostro amoroso et corroborato stato securi, et iucundissimamente rifocillati consolabondi et di gaudio subitario et laetitia commoti et delibuti repente ne feceron cum mustei osculamenti, et cum linguario vibramine suavemente basiare et strictamente amplexare. Et cum simile modo l’uno et l’altro le iucunde et festose Nymphe, nel suo sacro collegio novo tirocinio et officio dilla foecunda natura recevendo nui tutte dulcicule lepidamente ne basiorono. Diqué la Dea genitrice, cum elegantissimo affamine, et placido colloquio et cum maiestale obtuto propitiata ratiocinando, et cum divino flato spirante geniale Balsamo dispensando cose illicite di propalatione et agli vulgari homini, non di relato effabile, dando opera diutine di stabilire, et di fermentare gli nostri accensi amori, et di unire unanimi gli nostri cori sotto alle sue fructuose et dolce legie cum extento aevo, et negli stabili et parili amori magnanimi essere ne fece. Et per tanto sempre pia di porgere et munificamente favore conferire et munimine ad omni occurrente perturbatione ultronea et largamente promisse, et in tale colloquio mitissima la gratia sua lepidissimamente conferendo. Advene dunque che uno viriato milite nell’aspecto divo giù per gli z iiii

— is seen to transform into a single form. Neither more nor less than unhappy Byblis, feeling her own tears become for her a liquid fountain of the Naiad nymphs. Dwelling then already in the sweetest flames, no longer alive than dead, without a living pulse — and by an unimpeded passage, out of the height of sweetness, I freely let my little spirit exhale, thinking I had been seized by epilepsy, falling with buckled knee.

The most merciful goddess, suddenly, with her cupped divine palm — having set down the oyster-shell and closing the spaces of her longish fingers — divinely drew up the salt waters and, moistening, sprinkled them over us. Not as indignant Diana rained upon the unlucky hunter, tearing him to a beast for the dogs, but on the contrary, unhesitating, sprinkling to transmute — gratifying and embracing the sacred nymphs. And no sooner had she kindly done it, and I was sprinkled and anointed with the sea-dew, than in me at once the clarified spirits were stirred and grew more intelligible; and without waiting my burnt and consumed members reverted to their former state, and I felt myself, without fail, renewed with worthy qualities. I knew truly that I was renewed in a like manner to Aeson, nor did my return to myself seem otherwise than when twice-living Hippolytus was called back to the longed-for light, by Diana's great prayer, with the herb glycyside, to desirable life. And the assigned nymphs affectionately stripped from me the plebeian robes, and dutifully clothed me anew in a white and somewhat elegant garment. And being made tranquilly secure of our amorous and strengthened state, and most delightfully refreshed, consoled, and moved and anointed with sudden joy and gladness, at once they made us kiss with new-wine kisses, and with the vibration of the tongue sweetly to kiss and closely to embrace. And in like manner both of us, the joyful and festive nymphs, in their sacred college receiving us to the new novitiate and office of fruitful nature, all sweetly and charmingly kissed.

Whereupon the mother goddess, with most elegant address and placid discourse, and propitious with a majestic gaze, reasoning, and with divine breath dispensing a genial balsam — things unlawful to publish, and to vulgar men not to be told — labouring long to establish and to ferment our kindled loves, and to unite our hearts as one under her fruitful and sweet laws for a long age, made us great-souled in stable and equal love. And therefore, ever devoted to offering and munificently conferring favour and defence against every chance perturbation, she largely and freely promised; and in such discourse, most gentle, most charmingly conferring her grace.

It came to pass, then, that a manly soldier, divine in aspect, down the —

graduli fora dille forule sotto la prima pergula, valerosamente al sacrosancto fonte adventando, et nella maiestale facia vehemente, et di formidabile ferocitate pectoroso et cordato, cum ample spalle lacertoso et procero, cum gli lumi degli ochii acri et atroci, ma di una veneranda dignitate. Cum adornato sumptuoso, et superbo et divo cum faberrimo clypeo argyraspida, quale non fece Bronte, cum gli compagni all’exule Troiano contecto il capo di fulgente galea, instrophiata di olenii flori. Cum prominente crista et insigne apice aureo sopra il cono induto di thorace d’oro. Quale il divo Iulio di Britannia non portoe, né dedicoe alla genitrice nel suo templo. Né tale unque fece Didymao eximio artifice, cum il pendente Succingulo, overo Balteo transversario incincto, cum sumptuosissima acinace connodulata aurea. Cum tutti gli decorissimi gestamini militari, robustissimamente uno flagello in mano tenendo, comitato era dal suo fremente Lycaone. Gionto hogi mai al delectabile et delitioso fonte exponevase voluptabondo di deponere l’armature, et alla dea amata intrare dearmato. Etquivi ello et essa, non cum humani blandimenti et charitie, ma cumdivini gesti et affecto se implicatamente amplexavano cum innodanti abraciamenti. Per la quale cosa le Nymphe questo advertente, cum humile et reverente parlare licentia impetrorono, et io ancora, et la mia activa Polia il simigliante facendo, cum quel modo possibile, che allhora nui potevamo le immortale gratie dicte, ne dispartissemo. Rimanseron dunque solamente cum la divina Matre e cum il filio, et cum li continuamente al fonte circumastanti, et il venuto armigero ad divini et solatiosi oblectamenti abiecti tutti gli amiculi.* * ** ***

— steps, out of the recesses beneath the first pergola, valiantly making for the sacred fountain, with a majestic and vehement face, and breasted and stout- hearted with formidable ferocity, brawny and tall with broad shoulders, with the lights of his eyes keen and terrible, but of a venerable dignity. Sumptuously and proudly and divinely adorned, with a most cunningly wrought silver-gleaming shield such as Brontes and his companions did not make for the exiled Trojan; his head covered with a shining helmet wreathed with olive flowers, with a prominent crest and a noble golden apex above the cone; clad in a golden cuirass, such as the divine Julius did not bring from Britain nor dedicate to his mother in her temple; nor did the excellent craftsman Didymaon ever make such a one; with a pendent baldric, or cross-belt, girt about him, with a most sumptuous golden scimitar knotted to it; with all the most beautiful military accoutrements, most robustly holding a scourge in his hand, and attended by his growling Lycaon.

Come now at last to the delightful and delicious fountain, he showed himself eager to lay down his armour and, disarmed, to go in to the beloved goddess. And there he and she embraced one another intricately, not with human blandishments and endearments but with divine gestures and affection, in knotting embraces. Whereupon the nymphs, perceiving this, with humble and reverent speech obtained leave, and I too, and my lively Polia doing the like, in what manner we then could, having spoken our thanks to the immortal graces, departed. There remained, then, only the divine Mother with her son, and with those continually standing about the fountain, and the armed one newly come, cast down to divine and solacing delights — all the attendants withdrawn.

Chapter XXIV

The polyandrion — the ruined temple and its tombs.
